Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Trace ideas about mental illness from supernatural explanation to moral treatment, and explain why moral treatment declined.
  2. Assess the contributions of Wundt, Galton, Cattell, Binet and Terman to the emergence of clinical psychology.
  3. Why is Lightner Witmer regarded as the founder of clinical psychology, and how far does the modern field follow his conception?
  4. Explain how the two world wars turned the clinical psychologist from a tester into a therapist.
  5. Compare the scientist-practitioner, practitioner-scholar and clinical scientist models of training, and relate them to Indian training.
  6. Critically evaluate Eysenck's 1952 critique of psychotherapy and the response to it.
  7. Describe the development of clinical psychology in India from 1916 to the Rehabilitation Council of India Act of 1992.

3 The models clinicians think with

Every clinician looks at a suffering person through a model: a set of assumptions about what human beings are, why they come to suffer and what helps them change. This chapter teaches the main models of clinical psychology (psychodynamic, behavioural, cognitive-behavioural, humanistic and existential, family systems and biological) and the integrative frameworks that join them. For each you will learn the same six things: its view of human nature, what it says causes problems, how it assesses, how it treats, its strengths and its criticisms.

One person, Ananya, a young woman with panic attacks, is formulated through every model, and the last section combines those formulations into one integrated picture. Both clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) treat this as foundational ground, and it underlies most long-answer questions on theoretical orientations.

3.1 Why models matter

The historian of science Thomas Kuhn, in The Structure of Scientific Revolutions (1962), called the shared framework inside which scientists work a paradigm: it decides which questions are worth asking and what counts as an answer. In clinical psychology "model", "paradigm" and "theoretical orientation" name such a framework, and it does three jobs. It decides what the clinician looks for: repeated relationship patterns, what happens just before a behaviour, or sleep and family history. It decides what the clinician asks: "What was your childhood like?" and "What were you saying to yourself at that moment?" come from different models. It decides what the clinician does: interpret, expose, dispute, reflect, restructure a family or prescribe.

There is no theory-free look. That a client's heart races is a fact; whether it signals buried anger, a conditioned response, a misread sensation, a family's distress or a surge of noradrenaline is supplied by the model. The model's practical product is a case formulation: a working hypothesis about how this person's problems arose and what keeps them going, which guides treatment. Programmes recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India expect students to formulate in more than one model and then integrate, and because Indian hospital wards speak the medical model, a clinical psychologist must be fluent in diagnoses and in psychological mechanisms.

Why it matters. When asked to compare models, build every answer on one skeleton: human nature, causes, assessment, treatment, strengths, criticisms. Each model below is taught on that skeleton.
Case. Ananya, 23, moved from a small town to Bengaluru eight months ago for her first software job. Three months ago, on a crowded metro in a deadline week, after two short nights and much strong coffee, she was suddenly overwhelmed by a pounding heart, breathlessness, dizziness, tingling hands, a sense of unreality and the certainty that she was dying. Emergency tests, including an electrocardiogram, were normal. She now has about two attacks a week; she avoids the metro, sits near doors, carries water and checks her pulse constantly. Her uncle died of a heart attack last year. Her mother, a lifelong worrier, phones several times a day; her strict, distant father wants her to come home and marry. As a child she cried for weeks when left at school. A diagnostician would call this panic disorder with developing agoraphobic avoidance.

3.2 The psychodynamic model: Freud's foundations

Who gave us this. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), a Viennese neurologist who founded psychoanalysis, the first systematic talking cure. Key works: The Interpretation of Dreams (1900), Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905) and The Ego and the Id (1923).

View of human nature. Freud's view is deterministic and somewhat tragic. Nothing in mental life is accidental (psychic determinism): dreams, slips of the tongue and symptoms have hidden causes. People are driven by sexual (libido) and aggressive drives, mostly unconsciously, and live in permanent conflict between desire, conscience and reality.

The topographic model (1900) has three levels: the conscious (what you are aware of now), the preconscious (what can easily be recalled, such as a phone number) and the unconscious (wishes and memories actively kept out of awareness because they would cause anxiety). The unconscious leaks out in dreams, which Freud called its royal road, and in slips, free associations and symptoms.

The structural model (1923) describes three agencies. The id, present at birth, contains the drives; it obeys the pleasure principle, demanding immediate gratification, and thinks in primary process, the wishful, illogical thinking of dreams. The ego develops to deal with the outside world; it obeys the reality principle, delaying gratification until it is safe, and uses secondary process, realistic thought. The superego forms as the child internalises parental and social standards; its conscience punishes with guilt and its ego ideal holds the person one ought to be. The ego serves three masters at once: the id, the superego and reality.

Trap. The two maps are not the same thing drawn twice. The id is wholly unconscious, but the ego and superego stretch across all three levels: much defensive work and much guilt are themselves unconscious.

Anxiety and defence. Later (1926) Freud treated anxiety as a signal that a forbidden impulse threatens to break into awareness, distinguishing realistic anxiety (about real dangers), neurotic anxiety (fear of one's own impulses) and moral anxiety (guilt). The ego responds with defence mechanisms, unconscious operations that reduce anxiety by distorting or excluding part of reality, systematised by his daughter Anna Freud in The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936). Everyone uses defences; pathology lies in rigid reliance on primitive ones.

DefenceWhat it does, with an example
RepressionPushes a threatening memory out of awareness: no recall of a humiliating childhood event
DenialRefuses an external reality: chest pain insisted to be "only gas"
ProjectionAttributes one's own feeling to another: a jealous wife accuses her husband of jealousy
DisplacementRedirects a feeling to a safer target: scolded by the boss, a man shouts at his child
Reaction formationTurns a feeling into its exaggerated opposite: resentment shown as excessive sweetness
RationalisationSupplies a good reason for the real one: "I never wanted that job anyway"
RegressionReturns to earlier behaviour: bedwetting when a sibling is born
SublimationChannels a drive into a valued activity, the mature defence: aggression into sport

The psychosexual stages (1905) propose that libido centres on a different bodily zone at each stage of childhood. Too much frustration or gratification at a stage leaves a fixation that shapes adult character. The oral stage (roughly the first eighteen months) centres on the mouth and on dependence. The anal stage (roughly eighteen months to three years) centres on control, played out over toilet training; fixation was linked to orderliness and stubbornness. In the phallic stage (roughly three to six) the child's desire for the opposite-sex parent and rivalry with the same-sex parent form the Oedipus complex, resolved by identifying with the same-sex parent, which creates the superego. The latency period (about six to puberty) is quiet, and the genital stage brings adult sexuality.

Causes, assessment and treatment. Symptoms are compromise formations that partly express and partly defend against a forbidden wish, growing from childhood conflicts revived by adult events. Assessment relies on long unstructured interviews that attend to what is avoided as much as what is said, and on projective tests such as inkblot tests and the Thematic Apperception Test (see the assessment chapter). Classical psychoanalysis met four or five times a week for years, the client on a couch. Its tools are free association (saying whatever comes to mind), dream analysis, analysis of resistance (avoidance of painful material) and of transference (redirecting onto the analyst feelings that belong to early figures), and interpretation, which makes the unconscious conscious; the analyst's own reactions are countertransference. The goal is insight, worked through until the personality changes.

3.3 After Freud: later psychodynamic schools, evaluation and Ananya

Freud's followers kept the unconscious and the power of childhood but moved from sexuality towards relationships and culture. Carl Jung added a collective unconscious of inherited archetypes and the distinction between introversion and extraversion. Alfred Adler placed feelings of inferiority, and the striving to overcome them, at the centre. Karen Horney traced neurosis to basic anxiety produced by an insecure childhood and rejected Freud's idea of penis envy as male bias. Erik Erikson, in Childhood and Society (1950), proposed eight psychosocial stages across the whole life span, each a crisis such as trust versus mistrust or identity versus role confusion.

Four later schools followed. Ego psychology, led by Anna Freud, studied the ego's adaptive strengths. Object relations theory held that the deepest human need is for relationship, carried inside us as images of self and others: Melanie Klein described splitting (seeing people as all good or all bad), and Donald Winnicott the good-enough mother and the holding environment. Attachment theory, founded by John Bowlby, proposed an inborn system that keeps an infant close to a caregiver and builds internal working models of whether others can be relied on; Mary Ainsworth's Strange Situation procedure classified infants as secure, avoidant or resistant, and a disorganised pattern was added later. Self psychology, founded by Heinz Kohut (1971), held that a cohesive self needs caregivers who mirror the child and can be idealised, and that repeated empathic failures leave a fragile, narcissistic self.

Contemporary brief psychodynamic therapy works face to face, weekly, for a limited number of sessions, with an active therapist and a single focus, usually a repeating relationship pattern. David Malan taught therapists to trace a triangle of conflict (hidden feeling, the anxiety it arouses, the defence against it) across a triangle of person (current relationships, the therapist, past figures). Barbara Milrod and colleagues developed and trialled a panic-focused version. The evidence base is real but smaller than that for cognitive behaviour therapy.

Strengths. Psychoanalysis invented psychotherapy. Its claims that much mental life is unconscious, that early relationships shape later ones and that people defend against pain are widely accepted in modified form, and every modern therapy attends to the relationship. Criticisms. The philosopher Karl Popper argued that the theory is unfalsifiable, since any outcome can be explained after the event. Its evidence came from case studies of middle-class Viennese patients. Hans Eysenck (1952) claimed that neurotic patients improved no more with psychotherapy than without it; his analysis was flawed, but it launched modern outcome research. Classical analysis is long and costly, and its universality is doubtful: Girindrasekhar Bose, who founded the Indian Psychoanalytical Society in 1922 and corresponded with Freud, argued that his Indian patients showed conflicts the Oedipal scheme did not fit.

Case. The first attack came at the point of greatest separation in Ananya's life, alone in a new city while her father pressed her to return and marry. Her childhood distress at school suggests an insecure attachment in which independence feels dangerous. The hypothesis is that anger at her father and guilt about wanting her own life are unacceptable to her superego, so they are repressed and covered by reaction formation (she is excessively dutiful on the phone), returning disguised as bodily terror; her uncle's death adds an unmourned loss. Treatment would link attacks to separation and anger and watch for the same pattern with the therapist, such as fearing disappointment if she disagrees.

3.4 The behavioural model

View of human nature. John B. Watson's manifesto "Psychology as the Behaviorist Views It" (1913) declared that psychology should study observable behaviour, not invisible mental events. People are seen as shaped mainly by their environment and learning history, and the same laws of learning produce normal and abnormal behaviour. A phobia is not a symptom of something deeper; it is the problem, a learned response that can be unlearned.

Classical conditioning. Ivan Pavlov, a Russian physiologist who won the Nobel Prize in 1904 for research on digestion, found that a neutral stimulus (a bell) repeatedly paired with food, an unconditioned stimulus that naturally produces salivation (the unconditioned response), comes to produce salivation on its own. The bell is now a conditioned stimulus and the salivation a conditioned response. Presented repeatedly without food, the response fades (extinction), though it can briefly return after a rest (spontaneous recovery). Similar stimuli also trigger it (generalisation) unless the animal learns to tell them apart (discrimination).

Little Albert. Watson and his student Rosalie Rayner (1920) conditioned fear in Albert, an infant of about eleven months, by striking a steel bar loudly behind his head whenever he reached for a white rat. Soon the rat alone made him cry, and the fear generalised to a rabbit, a dog, a fur coat and a Santa Claus mask. The study showed that an emotional disorder could be learned, and it is also a textbook ethical failure: Albert was never deconditioned, and even his later identity is disputed. In 1924 Mary Cover Jones showed the reverse, removing a boy called Peter's fear of rabbits by bringing a rabbit gradually closer while he ate food he liked.

Operant conditioning. Edward Thorndike's law of effect (late 1890s) stated that behaviour followed by satisfying consequences becomes more likely. B. F. Skinner developed this in The Behavior of Organisms (1938): behaviour is shaped by its consequences. Reinforcement increases behaviour: positive reinforcement adds something pleasant (praise), negative reinforcement removes something unpleasant (a tablet ends a headache, so you take tablets more). Punishment decreases behaviour. Behaviour rewarded unpredictably (a variable-ratio schedule, as in gambling) is hardest to extinguish, and complex behaviour can be built by shaping, reinforcing successive steps towards it.

Trap. Negative reinforcement is not punishment. Both kinds of reinforcement make behaviour more likely; "negative" means only that something is taken away. Escape and avoidance, which remove fear, are the classic negatively reinforced behaviours.

This explains why fears last. O. Hobart Mowrer's two-factor theory holds that fear is acquired by classical conditioning and maintained by operant conditioning: avoidance brings relief, relief reinforces avoidance, and because the person never stays long enough to learn that nothing terrible happens, the fear never extinguishes.

Systematic desensitisation. Joseph Wolpe, a South African psychiatrist, argued in Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (1958) that a person cannot be deeply relaxed and anxious at once. His method has three steps: the client learns progressive muscle relaxation; client and therapist build an anxiety hierarchy of situations ranked by distress on a 0 to 100 scale; then, while relaxed, the client imagines each item from the bottom up, moving on only when it no longer causes anxiety. Later research showed that the essential ingredient is exposure, contact with the feared stimulus without escape, the basis of real-life exposure and of exposure with response prevention for obsessive-compulsive disorder.

Social learning. Albert Bandura showed that people learn by watching others. In his Bobo doll studies of the early 1960s, children who saw an adult attack an inflatable doll copied the aggression. His social learning theory (1977) names four processes in observational learning (attention, retention, reproduction and motivation) and adds vicarious reinforcement, seeing a model rewarded. Self-efficacy (1977), the belief that one can carry out a behaviour, makes Bandura the bridge to the cognitive model; his therapeutic legacy is modelling.

Assessment and treatment. The core of behavioural assessment is the functional analysis: the Antecedents that trigger a behaviour, the Behaviour described precisely (frequency, duration, intensity) and the Consequences that maintain it. Data come from behavioural interviews, observation, self-monitoring diaries and approach tests (how close can the client come to the feared object?). Treatments follow directly: exposure, contingency management and token economies, behavioural activation for depression, modelling and social skills training.

Strengths. Behaviourism made therapy testable, measurable and brief, and exposure-based treatments are among the best-supported interventions in clinical psychology. Criticisms. It ignores thoughts, meanings and feelings. Martin Seligman's concept of preparedness (1971) showed that people readily learn to fear snakes and heights but rarely electric sockets, so conditioning has evolutionary limits, and many people with phobias recall no conditioning event at all. Its history also includes aversion therapy used to "cure" homosexuality, now recognised as unethical and harmful.

Case. Ananya's first attack was an unconditioned fear response to intense arousal from caffeine, sleep loss and the crush of the train. Both the metro and her own quickening heartbeat became conditioned stimuli (interoceptive conditioning), and the fear generalised to lifts and closed rooms. Functional analysis: Antecedent, a crowded space or a fast heart after climbing stairs; Behaviour, escape, pulse checking, phoning her mother; Consequences, immediate relief (negative reinforcement) and her mother's attention (positive reinforcement), while in the long run the fear never extinguishes. Treatment: interoceptive exposure (running on the spot or breathing through a narrow straw to produce the feared sensations until they lose their power), then a graded hierarchy from one metro stop with a friend to a rush-hour journey alone, without safety behaviours.

3.5 The cognitive and cognitive-behavioural model

View of human nature. People are meaning-makers: they react not to events themselves but to their interpretations of them, so emotional problems come from biased, rigid or unrealistic thinking. The idea goes back to the Stoic philosophers; what was new in the 1960s was turning it into a testable therapy.

Who gave us this. Aaron Beck, a psychoanalytically trained psychiatrist at the University of Pennsylvania, set out to test the idea that depression is anger turned inward and instead found depressed patients' thoughts full of loss, defeat and worthlessness (Cognitive Therapy and the Emotional Disorders, 1976; Cognitive Therapy of Depression, 1979). Albert Ellis, also trained psychoanalytically, developed rational therapy in the mid-1950s; it was renamed rational emotive behaviour therapy (REBT) in 1993.

Beck's model. Beck described three levels of thinking. Automatic thoughts are quick, plausible, unexamined thoughts that pop up in a situation ("I will make a fool of myself"). Beneath them lie intermediate beliefs, rules and conditional assumptions ("If I am not perfect, people will reject me"). Deepest are core beliefs, organised into schemas: rigid beliefs about self, others and the world formed in childhood ("I am unlovable"). Schemas can lie dormant until a matching stress activates them, so Beck's theory is a diathesis-stress model. In depression the content forms the cognitive triad: a negative view of the self, the world and the future. Thinking is bent by systematic cognitive distortions.

DistortionExample
All-or-nothing thinking"If I don't top the exam, I have failed"
Overgeneralisation"I was rejected once; nobody will ever want me"
Mental filter and disqualifying the positiveRemembering only the one critical comment; "they praised me to be polite"
Jumping to conclusions (mind reading, fortune telling)"She didn't reply, so she is angry with me"
Catastrophising"This headache is a brain tumour"
Emotional reasoning"I feel stupid, so I must be stupid"
Should statements and labelling"I should never feel tired"; "I am a loser"
Personalisation"My son failed because I am a bad mother"

Beck's therapy rests on collaborative empiricism: therapist and client work as a research team and treat thoughts as hypotheses, not facts. Tools include Socratic questioning ("What is the evidence? Is there another explanation?"), thought records, behavioural experiments that test a prediction in real life, and homework, within structured sessions that begin with an agenda.

Ellis's ABC model. An Activating event does not directly cause the emotional Consequence; the Belief about it does. Healthy beliefs are flexible preferences ("I would like to pass"); irrational beliefs are rigid demands ("I must pass"), with three derivatives: awfulising ("it would be terrible"), low frustration tolerance ("I couldn't stand it") and global rating of worth ("I would be worthless"). Therapy adds D, disputing the belief for its logic, evidence and usefulness, and E, an effective new belief. Ellis was more directive and confrontational than Beck, attacking beliefs head on; Beck tested them collaboratively.

Trap. Two different ABCs are examined. Ellis's is Activating event, Belief, Consequence. The behavioural functional analysis is Antecedent, Behaviour, Consequence. Only Ellis's has a belief in the middle.

Integration into CBT. Cognitive and behavioural methods merged in the 1970s because each needs the other: experiments change beliefs, and changed beliefs allow new behaviour. Donald Meichenbaum's Cognitive-Behavior Modification (1977), with self-instructional and stress inoculation training, was a landmark. The result, cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT), developed disorder-specific models. David M. Clark's cognitive model of panic (1986) proposes that panic arises when normal bodily sensations are catastrophically misinterpreted as signs of disaster, raising anxiety, which produces more sensations, in a vicious circle. A later "third wave", including acceptance and commitment therapy (Steven Hayes) and dialectical behaviour therapy (Marsha Linehan), aims to change one's relationship to thoughts through mindfulness and acceptance rather than their content.

Assessment combines structured interviews, questionnaires such as the Beck Depression Inventory and thought diaries. Strengths. CBT has the largest evidence base of any psychotherapy, is brief, structured and teachable, and adapts to groups and digital formats. Criticisms. The link between negative thinking and disorder is partly correlational: the thoughts may be a consequence as much as a cause. In a dismantling study, Neil Jacobson and colleagues (1996) found behavioural activation alone about as effective as full cognitive therapy for depression, questioning whether changing thoughts is the active ingredient. It is also called rationalist and individualistic: a belief that looks "irrational" in one culture may be a shared, meaningful framework in another, hence culturally adapted CBT for South Asian clients.

Case. Through Clark's model, Ananya's problem is not the palpitations but their meaning: "My heart is racing, I am having a heart attack like my uncle"; dizziness means "I will faint or go mad". The fear makes her heart race faster, confirming the danger, and her safety behaviours prevent disconfirmation: she believes she survived because she got off the train. Beneath lie an assumption ("If I lose control of my body, something catastrophic will happen") and a core belief ("I am fragile"). Ellis would frame one episode as A, heart racing in a meeting; B, "I must not have an attack in front of colleagues, it would be awful"; C, panic and shame. Treatment draws the vicious circle from her own attacks and then tests it: two minutes of deliberate over-breathing reproduces her symptoms and shows their harmless cause, and a metro ride without holding the pole tests her prediction that she will faint.

3.6 The humanistic and existential model

View of human nature. In the 1950s humanistic psychology presented itself as a "third force" against psychoanalysis and behaviourism, which it saw as reducing people to drives or reflexes. People are basically trustworthy, growth-oriented, free and responsible, and their subjective experience is the proper starting point. Problems arise from blocked growth, not sickness.

Who gave us this. Carl Rogers (1902 to 1987): Client-Centered Therapy (1951), the 1957 paper on "the necessary and sufficient conditions of therapeutic personality change" and On Becoming a Person (1961). He was also among the first to record and research therapy sessions.

Rogers. Every organism has an actualising tendency, an inborn drive to realise its potential. As the child develops a self-concept, it comes to need positive regard from others. When love is given only if the child thinks and acts in certain ways, the child absorbs conditions of worth ("I am worthwhile only if I am never angry") and begins to deny or distort experiences that contradict the self-concept. The gap between self-concept and actual experience is incongruence, the root of anxiety. The healthy end point is the fully functioning person, open to experience.

Person-centred therapy is non-directive: the client is the expert on their own life. Change occurs when three core conditions are present. Congruence (genuineness) means the therapist is real rather than hiding behind a professional façade. Unconditional positive regard means accepting the client without conditions, which dissolves conditions of worth. Accurate empathy means grasping the client's inner world as if it were one's own and communicating that understanding, often by reflecting feelings.

Maslow. Abraham Maslow's hierarchy of needs (1943; Motivation and Personality, 1954) ranks physiological, safety, love and belonging, and esteem needs, the deficiency needs, below self-actualisation, a growth need. Self-actualising people, he said, are accepting, spontaneous, autonomous and prone to peak experiences. He used the phrase "positive psychology" in 1954, long before the movement of later chapters.

Existential themes. Viktor Frankl, a Viennese psychiatrist who survived Nazi concentration camps, wrote Man's Search for Meaning (1946) and founded logotherapy. Its central motive is the will to meaning, its typical problem the existential vacuum, and its claim that even in extreme conditions people keep the freedom to choose their attitude. His paradoxical intention, asking the client to wish for the very thing they fear, was designed to break anticipatory anxiety. Irvin Yalom's Existential Psychotherapy (1980) named four givens (death, freedom, isolation and meaninglessness), so some anxiety is the normal price of being free and mortal.

Gestalt. Fritz Perls's Gestalt therapy (1951) emphasises awareness in the here and now, attention to the body and the completion of unfinished business, asking "what" and "how" rather than "why", sometimes by having a client speak to an absent person in an empty chair.

Assessment is wary of diagnosis and tests, which can turn a person into an object; the aim is to understand the client's own frame of reference. Strengths. The model put the relationship at the centre, and alliance and empathy have since been shown to predict outcome across therapies; it shaped counselling worldwide, motivational interviewing and positive psychology. Criticisms. Its concepts are vague and hard to measure; the core conditions help but have not proved sufficient; the evidence for severe disorders is weaker; Maslow's hierarchy is not supported as a strict sequence; and its individualism is culture-bound. In a family-centred society such as India, duty to family may be a genuine value rather than an imposed condition of worth.

Case. A Rogerian sees a self-concept of the "good daughter who never causes trouble", built on conditions of worth. In the city Ananya is having experiences that contradict it: ambition, resentment of her father, a wish to choose her own partner. Denied awareness, the incongruence surfaces as anxiety; the therapist offers the core conditions so she can own these feelings without losing her worth. An existential therapist sees a young woman confronted at once with freedom (she must now author her own life) and with death (her uncle's heart attack). Frankl might prescribe paradoxical intention: "Try right now to make your heart beat as fast as it can", which usually deflates anticipatory fear. A Gestalt therapist might invite an empty-chair conversation with her father.

3.7 The family systems model

View of human nature. This model moves the unit of analysis from the individual to relationships. From the 1950s, drawing on Ludwig von Bertalanffy's general systems theory, therapists began to treat the family as a system: a whole greater than the sum of its members, in which each part affects every other. Systems regulate themselves to maintain balance (homeostasis), resisting change even when the balance is painful. They contain subsystems (the couple, the siblings) separated by boundaries, and they pass through a family life cycle of transitions such as launching young adults.

Cause of problems. The model replaces linear causality (A causes B) with circular causality: a mother nags because her son withdraws, and he withdraws because she nags. The person with the symptom is the identified patient, the member who expresses a problem belonging to the system, and the symptom often has a function, such as uniting parents who would otherwise fight. Gregory Bateson's 1956 "double bind" theory, that contradictory family communication causes schizophrenia, belongs to this early period and has been abandoned.

Trap. Circular causality does not mean the family is to blame. It means no single member is the cause; the pattern is. Blaming the parents is linear thinking in systemic clothing.

The main schools. Salvador Minuchin's structural family therapy (Families and Family Therapy, 1974) holds that healthy families have parents in charge and clear boundaries. Boundaries may be enmeshed (members over-involved in each other's lives) or disengaged (members isolated); the therapist joins the family, has it act out its conflict in the session (enactment) and redraws boundaries. Murray Bowen's theory centres on differentiation of self, staying connected to family while thinking and acting independently. Poorly differentiated families manage anxiety through triangles (two anxious people drawing in a third) and pass patterns down generations, mapped on Bowen's three-generation genogram. Strategic therapy, associated with Jay Haley, focuses on the present problem, power and hierarchy, and uses directives, including paradoxical ones that prescribe the symptom. The Milan group led by Mara Selvini Palazzoli added circular questioning (asking each member about the relationships between others) and positive connotation of the symptom.

Assessment means interviewing the family together, noticing who speaks for whom, drawing a genogram and locating the family in its life cycle. Strengths. The model sees problems in context and treats the family as a resource. Research on expressed emotion (criticism, hostility and emotional over-involvement from relatives) found that high levels predict relapse in schizophrenia, and family interventions that reduce it lower relapse. In India families have long been partners in care: in the 1950s the psychiatrist Vidyasagar at the Amritsar mental hospital had relatives stay with admitted patients. Criticisms. Early theories blamed families, especially mothers, for severe illness; feminist therapists argued that neutrality obscures real imbalances of power, most seriously in domestic violence; many concepts are hard to test; and the individual's biology and inner life can vanish from view.

Case. This family is launching its first child, and the attacks began just as Ananya separated. Since then her mother calls several times a day, parents who used to quarrel are united in worry, and the marriage question is postponed. Systemically, the symptom keeps her connected, steadies the parents' relationship and lets everyone avoid the question of her independence: homeostasis, with Ananya as identified patient. In Bowen's terms differentiation is low within an anxious triangle; in Minuchin's, mother and daughter are enmeshed and father is peripheral. Circular questions open it up: "When Ananya has an attack, who is most upset? What does your father do then? If the attacks stopped, what would the family talk about?" Family sessions, perhaps online, would reframe the calls as love needing a new form and agree a schedule of contact.

3.8 The biological and medical model

View of human nature. The person is a biological organism, and mental disorders are disorders of brain and body, in principle like physical illnesses. The medical model follows a sequence: symptoms are grouped into a diagnosis, which points to a cause, which suggests a treatment and predicts a course.

Causes. Genetics: family, twin and adoption studies show that most major disorders run in families and are substantially heritable. Heritability is the proportion of variation in a trait within a population associated with genetic variation; it says nothing about how "genetic" one person's illness is. Disorders are polygenic, involving many genes of tiny effect that interact with environments. Neurotransmitters: dopamine has been linked to psychosis, serotonin and noradrenaline to depression and anxiety, and gamma-aminobutyric acid, the main inhibitory transmitter, to anxiety. Brain structure and function: the amygdala in fear, the prefrontal cortex in regulating emotion, and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis in the stress response.

Assessment includes medical history, physical examination, laboratory tests to rule out physical causes, family history and a diagnostic interview based on the DSM or the ICD; brain imaging is mainly a research tool. Treatment means medication (antidepressants such as the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, antipsychotics, mood stabilisers such as lithium, and benzodiazepines for anxiety), electroconvulsive therapy, transcranial magnetic stimulation and lifestyle measures. In India clinical psychologists do not prescribe; they work alongside psychiatrists.

Strengths. The model has produced real advances for severe mental illness, brought rigour and large-scale research, and can reduce the moral blame placed on sufferers. Criticisms. The central charge is biological reductionism: explaining suffering entirely by brain chemistry loses meaning, relationships, culture and choice. That a drug helps does not show what caused the problem; aspirin relieves headaches, but headaches are not caused by a lack of aspirin. An umbrella review led by Joanna Moncrieff (2022) found no consistent evidence that depression is caused by low serotonin, undermining the popular "chemical imbalance" story, and reliable biological tests for most disorders do not exist. Thomas Szasz, in The Myth of Mental Illness (1961), argued that most "mental illnesses" are problems in living. Purely biogenetic explanations appear to reduce blame but increase pessimism about recovery, and the model can neglect poverty, discrimination and violence.

Trap. "Biological" does not mean "untreatable by psychology", and "psychological" does not mean "not in the brain". Psychotherapy changes brain function too, and a brain difference found in a disorder does not prove it caused the disorder.
Case. Panic disorder runs in families, and Ananya's mother's lifelong anxiety suggests an inherited sensitivity of the brain's fear circuits; in laboratory challenges, people with panic disorder react more strongly than others to breathing air enriched with carbon dioxide. Caffeine and sleep loss were the physiological triggers. Assessment must rule out physical mimics: an overactive thyroid, heart rhythm disturbances, anaemia, low blood sugar, asthma and stimulant use or withdrawal. Treatment would consider a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor, a first-line medication for panic disorder, with benzodiazepines used briefly if at all because of dependence and because they can undercut exposure, plus less caffeine, regular sleep and exercise.

3.9 Integrative frameworks

Who gave us this. George Engel, an American physician and psychiatrist, published "The need for a new medical model: a challenge for biomedicine" in Science in 1977, arguing that the biomedical model was too narrow even for physical medicine.

The biopsychosocial model. Engel pictured a hierarchy of systems from cells and organs through the person to family, community and culture, and argued that every illness involves all levels at once: two people with the same disease can have very different illnesses depending on their beliefs, relationships and circumstances. It became the standard framework for formulation, but it is a framework rather than a theory and can become a checklist that calls everything relevant without saying how factors interact.

The diathesis-stress model specifies one interaction. A diathesis is a predisposing vulnerability (genetic loading, sensitive temperament, early adversity, a dysfunctional schema); a stress is a precipitating event or strain. Neither alone is enough: disorder appears when the combined load crosses a threshold, so a strong diathesis needs only minor stress. Paul Meehl (1962) applied this logic to schizophrenia, and Joseph Zubin and Bonnie Spring (1977) proposed a vulnerability-stress model in which vulnerability is lasting and episodes are triggered by stress. Later work added protective factors that buffer vulnerability.

Eclecticism versus integration. Integrative or eclectic orientations are among the most commonly reported in surveys of clinical psychologists. Picking whatever technique feels right on the day is incoherent; three disciplined routes exist.

  • Technical eclecticism borrows techniques from any school on evidence of what works for whom, without adopting their theories. Arnold Lazarus's multimodal therapy assesses seven modalities, summarised as BASIC I.D. (Behaviour, Affect, Sensation, Imagery, Cognition, Interpersonal relationships, Drugs or biology).
  • Theoretical integration fuses theories into a new one. Paul Wachtel's Psychoanalysis and Behavior Therapy (1977) showed how inner conflicts produce behaviour that provokes reactions from others which confirm the conflicts.
  • The common factors approach looks for what all effective therapies share. Saul Rosenzweig (1936) suggested that therapies work equally well through shared ingredients, quoting the Dodo bird in Alice in Wonderland: "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes." Jerome Frank's Persuasion and Healing (1961) named a confiding relationship, a healing setting, a rationale that explains the problem and a ritual both parties believe in. Edward Bordin (1979) defined the therapeutic alliance as agreement on goals and tasks plus an emotional bond.

The "Dodo bird verdict" remains contested, because specific treatments clearly outperform others for some conditions, exposure for anxiety disorders being the standard example. Common factors also take cultural shapes: Jaswant Singh Neki (1973) proposed the Indian guru-chela (teacher and disciple) relationship, more directive than the Rogerian ideal, as a therapeutic paradigm for Indian clients.

Key. Eclecticism mixes techniques; integration mixes theories; common factors look beneath both. The biopsychosocial model organises causes, and the diathesis-stress model explains how vulnerability and stress combine.

3.10 Ananya through every lens

Side by side, the formulations of Ananya look less like rivals than views of one house from different windows: biology explains why her body reacts so strongly, learning how her heartbeat became a trigger, cognition why it means death to her, the psychodynamic and humanistic lenses why this moment of independence was charged, and the family lens why the system sustains the symptom. The standard integration is the "four Ps" grid, crossing biopsychosocial levels with the time course of the problem.

FactorBiologicalPsychologicalSocial
Predisposing (why her)Family history of anxietyChildhood separation distressProtective, anxious parenting
Precipitating (why now)Caffeine, sleep loss, crowdingDeadline stress; uncle's deathMove to a new city; marriage pressure
Perpetuating (why it continues)Conditioned arousal to bodily cuesCatastrophic misinterpretation; avoidanceMother's frequent calls; isolation from colleagues
Protective (what helps)Good physical healthIntelligent and motivatedA supportive friend at work; financial independence

The integrated plan: cognitive behaviour therapy for panic as the core, with interoceptive and real-life exposure; a psychiatric review for medication if attacks stay frequent; a few family sessions to renegotiate contact; and room to explore what independence, anger and her uncle's death mean to her, all carried by the alliance.

Recap.

A model decides what a clinician looks for, asks and does, so every clinical judgement rests on one. Freud explained suffering as unconscious conflict among id, ego and superego, managed by defences and rooted in childhood, and later schools shifted the focus to relationships, ending in brief focused therapies. The behavioural model treats problems as learned through classical and operant conditioning and modelling, and treats them through exposure guided by functional analysis. The cognitive model locates the problem in interpretation, from Beck's automatic thoughts and schemas to Ellis's irrational demands, and merged with behaviourism into cognitive behaviour therapy. The humanistic and existential model sees blocked growth, incongruence and the anxieties of freedom and mortality, and heals through the relationship. The family systems model moves the problem into the pattern, and the biological model into the brain, each powerful and each incomplete. Engel's biopsychosocial model, the diathesis-stress model and disciplined integration let a clinician formulate a person like Ananya at every level and build one coherent plan.
