Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. What is a theoretical model in clinical psychology? Explain how a clinician's model shapes what they observe, ask and do.
  2. Describe Freud's topographic and structural models and the role of anxiety and defence mechanisms in symptom formation. How did later psychodynamic schools modify his ideas?
  3. Explain classical and operant conditioning, show how Mowrer's two-factor theory accounts for persistent avoidance, and critically evaluate the behavioural model.
  4. Compare Beck's cognitive therapy with Ellis's rational emotive behaviour therapy, and show how cognitive and behavioural techniques were integrated into cognitive behaviour therapy.
  5. Discuss Carl Rogers' theory of personality and therapy. How do existential approaches such as Frankl's logotherapy differ from it?
  6. Explain circular causality and the identified patient, and compare the structural, Bowenian and strategic schools of family therapy.
  7. Critically evaluate the biological model and explain how the biopsychosocial and diathesis-stress models address its limits, illustrating with a formulation of panic disorder.

4 Defining abnormality, and how diagnosis works

Every clinical psychologist, every working day, makes a judgement that sounds simple and is not: is what this person is experiencing a disorder, or part of the ordinary range of human life? A widow who still sets a place for her husband a year after his death, a woman who hears her late mother's voice comforting her: some of these people need clinical help and some do not, and the difference cannot be read off the behaviour alone.

This chapter teaches how psychology and psychiatry have tried to draw that line, why every attempt has a flaw, and how the modern diagnostic systems, the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM) and the International Classification of Diseases (ICD), turn it into working rules. It ends with culture and with the difference between a diagnosis and a case formulation. The clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) treat this as foundational: every later topic assumes you can say what a disorder is.

4.1 The four classic yardsticks of abnormality

"Abnormal" means "away from the norm", but "norm" has several meanings: what is statistically common, what society expects, what feels comfortable to the person, and what lets a person function. Each gives a different definition, and each, used alone, fails.

Statistical infrequency says behaviour is abnormal if it is rare. When scores on a characteristic fall on a normal (bell-shaped) curve, anyone far out in either tail is statistically abnormal; a common convention treats scores more than two standard deviations from the mean as unusual. Its value is an objective cut-off: intellectual disability has traditionally used an intelligence quotient about two standard deviations below the mean (around 70) as one part of its definition, alongside problems in adaptive functioning. The flaw is that rarity says nothing about harm. An intelligence quotient two standard deviations above the mean is just as rare, yet nobody calls giftedness a disorder, while depression and anxiety are among the commonest health conditions in the world. The cut-off is also arbitrary: why two standard deviations and not one and a half?

Violation of social norms says behaviour is abnormal if it breaks the unwritten rules of a society. The flaw is that norms differ between cultures and change over time, and can turn prejudice into medicine. In 1851 the American physician Samuel Cartwright proposed "drapetomania", a supposed illness that made enslaved people run away. Homosexuality was listed as a disorder until the American Psychiatric Association voted to remove it in 1973. India's Mental Healthcare Act 2017 builds the lesson into law: mental illness shall not be determined on the basis of non-conformity with moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs.

Personal distress says behaviour is abnormal if it makes the person suffer. This fits depression and panic well and respects the person's own view. But some serious conditions involve little distress: a person in a manic episode may feel wonderful, and a person with antisocial personality disorder may be comfortable while harming others. Clinicians call such features "ego-syntonic" (felt as acceptable, part of the self) as opposed to "ego-dystonic" (felt as alien). And much distress, such as grief or fear before surgery, is appropriate rather than disordered.

Disability or dysfunction says behaviour is abnormal if it interferes with work, study, self-care or relationships. Almost every DSM diagnosis requires "clinically significant" distress or impairment. The flaw is that impairment is judged against expectations: a hunger striker is impaired but not disordered.

Trap. Examiners ask which single criterion is "correct". None is. Each captures part of what we mean by abnormality, each fails alone, and clinicians combine them with judgement.

4.2 The four Ds, Wakefield's harmful dysfunction, and the manual's definition

Abnormal psychology textbooks, notably Ronald Comer's, summarise the practical approach as the "four Ds". Deviance: thoughts, feelings or behaviour differ markedly from what the person's culture accepts. Distress: the experience is upsetting to the person. Dysfunction: it interferes with daily activities. Danger: it risks harm to the person or others. The four Ds are a checklist of what clinicians notice, not a theory of disorder, and they inherit the yardsticks' flaws. Danger misleads if taken as typical: most people with mental disorders are not dangerous, and are more often victims of violence than perpetrators.

Who gave us this. Jerome Wakefield, an American philosopher of psychiatry, in "The concept of mental disorder: On the boundary between biological facts and social values", American Psychologist, 1992.

Wakefield argued that a mental disorder is a harmful dysfunction, and both words matter. "Dysfunction" is a factual claim: an internal mental mechanism is failing to perform the function natural selection designed it for. The fear system is designed to respond to danger; if it fires violently with no danger present, as in panic disorder, it is failing. "Harmful" is a value judgement: the failure must harm the person, judged by the standards of their culture. A disorder needs both. This handles cases the older yardsticks got wrong. Grief is distressing and impairing, but the sadness mechanism is doing what it was designed to do after loss: harm without dysfunction, so not a disorder. Drapetomania fails because fleeing slavery involves no failed mechanism; the "harm" was only to the owner. The main criticism is that we rarely know the evolved function of a mental mechanism, so the factual half often cannot be checked.

The DSM-5 (2013) definition, paraphrased, calls a mental disorder a syndrome of clinically significant disturbance in cognition, emotion regulation or behaviour reflecting a dysfunction in the psychological, biological or developmental processes underlying mental functioning, usually with significant distress or disability. It excludes an expectable or culturally approved response to a common stressor or loss, such as a death, and socially deviant behaviour unless it results from a dysfunction in the individual.

Key. A defensible definition of disorder combines a factual element (something inside the person is not working as it should) with a value element (the result is harmful), and excludes expectable reactions to life events and mere social deviance.

4.3 The positive counterpart: Jahoda's ideal mental health, and cultural relativity

All these definitions treat health as the absence of disorder. Marie Jahoda argued that psychology also needed a positive account of healthy functioning.

Who gave us this. Marie Jahoda, an Austrian-born British social psychologist, in Current Concepts of Positive Mental Health (1958).

Jahoda drew six criteria from the literature. Positive attitudes towards the self: self-acceptance and a realistic sense of identity. Growth, development and self-actualisation: moving towards one's potential. Integration: balance among the parts of the personality and a unifying outlook that withstands stress. Autonomy: some independence from social pressure, regulating behaviour from within. Accurate perception of reality, including seeing others with empathy. Environmental mastery: the ability to love, work and play, meet situational demands and solve problems. The list is an ancestor of positive psychology; Carol Ryff's model of psychological well-being, taught later, shares self-acceptance, autonomy, environmental mastery and personal growth. As a definition of abnormality it fails, because few people meet all six criteria, so almost everyone would be "abnormal"; it describes an ideal to move towards. It also reflects a Western, individualist picture of the good life, in which autonomy is prized more than in cultures that understand a person through family and community.

That point generalises into cultural relativity: what counts as normal depends on the culture in which behaviour occurs. Trance at a temple festival may be expected in one setting and read as a symptom in another. A strong relativism, that abnormality is nothing but cultural judgement, cannot be right, since schizophrenia and dementia are recognised across very different cultures. The balanced position: the core of many disorders is universal, while their expression, explanation and the help people seek are shaped by culture.

4.4 Why classify and diagnose at all, and what it costs

Classification sorts cases into groups by shared features, producing a taxonomy or, in medicine, a nosology; diagnosis assigns a person to a group. There are five good reasons to do it. Communication: "bipolar I disorder" tells another professional a cluster of facts about symptoms, course and risk. Research: shared definitions make studies comparable. Treatment selection: exposure-based therapy works for obsessive-compulsive disorder, lithium has a specific role in bipolar disorder. Prognosis: a diagnosis indicates likely course. Services, insurance and law: health systems, disability certification and courts run on categories, and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 requires Indian insurers to cover mental illness on a par with physical illness, which presupposes coded diagnoses.

The costs are real. The first is stigma: Erving Goffman, in Stigma (1963), described a deeply discrediting attribute that reduces a person in others' eyes from a whole person to a tainted one. Psychiatric labels bring discrimination in work, housing and marriage, a sharp concern in Indian families where a diagnosis may affect even a sibling's marriage prospects, and can be turned inward as self-stigma. The second is described by labelling theory: Thomas Scheff, in Being Mentally Ill (1966), argued that a labelled person is treated according to the stereotype and may come to act out the expected role, so the label helps create the behaviour it names. The strong version fails, since symptoms of serious disorders usually precede any label, but the milder claim, that labels shape how others read behaviour and how people see themselves, is well supported. The third is reification, treating an abstraction as a concrete thing: forgetting that a category is a human agreement and treating "borderline personality disorder" as an entity in the brain, like a tumour. Steven Hyman, a former director of the National Institute of Mental Health, argued in 2010 that DSM categories had become reified this way. The fourth is loss of individual detail, which is why diagnosis must be joined to formulation (Section 4.12).

Who gave us this. Thomas Szasz, a Hungarian-born American psychiatrist, in "The myth of mental illness" (American Psychologist, 1960) and the book The Myth of Mental Illness (1961).

Szasz argued that in medicine a disease is a demonstrable bodily lesion, while most mental illnesses are defined by behaviour departing from social, ethical or legal norms. Calling them illnesses is therefore a metaphor; what people really have are "problems in living". Medical language, he held, hid psychiatry's role in controlling troublesome people, and he campaigned against involuntary hospitalisation. He was right that diagnosis can serve social control, and he pushed the field towards patients' rights. But medicine has always recognised diseases before finding their cause (migraine is still defined by symptoms), brain changes have since been found in conditions such as dementia, and calling severe schizophrenia a "problem in living" can deny people effective treatment. Most clinicians take from Szasz a warning, not a conclusion.

4.5 Rosenhan's "On being sane in insane places" and its unravelling

Who gave us this. David Rosenhan, an American psychologist at Stanford University, in "On being sane in insane places", Science, 1973. The journalist Susannah Cahalan later investigated it in The Great Pretender (2019).

In the study as published, eight mentally healthy people, including Rosenhan, went to twelve psychiatric hospitals in the United States complaining of a voice saying words such as "empty", "hollow" and "thud". Otherwise they gave true histories under false names. All were admitted, then reported no symptoms and behaved normally. All but one were diagnosed with schizophrenia and discharged with "schizophrenia in remission" after stays of 7 to 52 days, averaging 19. No staff member detected them, though in the first three hospitalisations 35 of 118 fellow patients voiced suspicion. Normal behaviour was read through the label: one pseudopatient's note-taking was charted as "patient engages in writing behaviour". In a follow-up, a hospital told to expect pseudopatients over three months judged 41 of 193 new patients, with high confidence by at least one staff member, to be fakes; none had been sent. Rosenhan concluded that diagnosis could not separate sanity from insanity and that labels were "sticky". The paper added pressure for the explicit criteria of the DSM-III.

Critics at the time, most prominently the psychiatrist Robert Spitzer in 1975, argued it showed less than claimed: a convincing liar about symptoms would fool any branch of medicine. Spitzer also argued from hospital records that "in remission" was an unusual discharge diagnosis, suggesting clinicians had noticed the absence of symptoms. The follow-up showed only that warned staff became over-suspicious.

Graver doubts came in 2019. Cahalan, who had herself been misdiagnosed with a psychiatric condition before an autoimmune brain inflammation was found, examined Rosenhan's private papers. She reported that his own hospital records described more serious symptoms than the paper stated, and that she could trace very few other pseudopatients. One she found had had a largely positive hospital experience, and his data had been left out of the published study. Her findings raised the possibility that parts of the study were misreported.

Why it matters. Examiners now expect both halves: what Rosenhan claimed, and why his method is doubted. The lessons that survive are modest: diagnosis depends on self-report and can be fooled; labels do colour how staff interpret behaviour; and a famous study is not trustworthy until checked and replicated.

4.6 How a classification is built: categories, dimensions and prototypes

A categorical system treats disorders as distinct kinds: you have it or you do not, like a broken leg. The traditional DSM and ICD are largely categorical, descending from the German psychiatrist Emil Kraepelin, who in 1899 separated "dementia praecox" (later schizophrenia) from "manic-depressive insanity" by how they unfolded over time. Categories suit yes-or-no clinical decisions such as whether to treat. But most psychological characteristics vary continuously, so cut-offs are partly arbitrary: a person meeting four of the nine criteria for major depression is "not depressed", one meeting five is, though they may barely differ.

A dimensional system places each person on continuous scales, such as severity of anxiety. Blood pressure is the analogy: it varies continuously, and "hypertension" is a cut-off where risk rises. Statistical tests of whether a condition is a true category (taxometric analysis) have, on the whole, found that common conditions such as depression and anxiety behave dimensionally. Dimensions keep more information but are harder to use in a busy clinic.

A prototype approach draws on the cognitive psychologist Eleanor Rosch, who showed in the 1970s that people form concepts around typical examples: a robin is a "better" bird than a penguin. The clinician judges how closely a person matches the typical case; the ICD clinical guidelines lean this way. The modern DSM uses a polythetic hybrid: a set number from a list of criteria, none essential. Borderline personality disorder needs any five of nine, allowing 256 combinations, so two people with the same diagnosis may share only one criterion.

Trap. Do not write that the DSM-5 "became dimensional". It remains mainly categorical, with dimensional add-ons such as severity specifiers and a dimensional personality model kept in a section for further study.

4.7 Reliability, validity and comorbidity

Reliability asks whether a classification is applied consistently; validity asks whether its categories correspond to something real and useful. A system can be reliable without being valid, but not valid without being reliable: a diagnosis that depends on which clinician you see cannot be tracking anything real.

The key form is inter-rater reliability: do two clinicians independently reach the same diagnosis? Before 1980 often not. A 1962 study led by Aaron Beck found experienced psychiatrists agreed on specific diagnoses only about half the time, and the United States and United Kingdom Diagnostic Project around 1970 found New York psychiatrists diagnosing schizophrenia far more often than London psychiatrists, who diagnosed mood disorders in similar patients. The difference lay in the clinicians, not the patients.

Raw percentage agreement misleads, because some agreement happens by chance. The standard statistic is Cohen's kappa, introduced by the American psychologist Jacob Cohen in 1960: observed agreement minus chance agreement, divided by one minus chance agreement. A kappa of 1 is perfect; 0 is chance level. Suppose two clinicians assess 100 people, each says "depressed" to 50, and they agree on 80. Observed agreement is 0.80; chance agreement is 0.5 times 0.5 plus 0.5 times 0.5, or 0.50; kappa is 0.30 divided by 0.50, which is 0.60, decent. Now a rare condition: each says "yes" to only 10, and they agree on 84 cases, mostly because both said "no" to the same 82 people. Observed agreement looks better, 0.84, but chance agreement is 0.1 times 0.1 plus 0.9 times 0.9, or 0.82, so kappa is 0.02 divided by 0.18, about 0.11: almost no real agreement. Roughly, kappas above 0.8 are called excellent, 0.6 to 0.8 good, 0.4 to 0.6 moderate, and below 0.4 poor. The DSM-III raised reliability considerably. The DSM-5 field trials drew criticism when major depressive disorder showed a reported kappa of around 0.28 and generalised anxiety disorder around 0.20, though their stricter method makes comparison with older studies difficult.

For validity, the American psychiatrists Eli Robins and Samuel Guze proposed in 1970 that a category needs a distinct clinical description, supporting laboratory findings, delimitation from other disorders, a characteristic course on follow-up, and a pattern in family studies. Psychologists also ask about predictive validity (does the diagnosis predict course and treatment response?) and construct validity (does the category behave as its theory predicts?). In 2003 Robert Kendell and Assen Jablensky argued that most psychiatric categories are not proven valid in the sense of natural boundaries, but many have high utility for prediction and treatment.

Comorbidity means two or more disorders present in one person at once, and it is extremely common, as with depression and anxiety. The disorders may share causes, one may cause the other (panic leading to depression, anxiety to drinking), or the system may have split one underlying problem into several categories. High comorbidity is a strong argument that categories are drawn in the wrong places.

4.8 The Diagnostic and Statistical Manual, edition by edition

The DSM is published by the American Psychiatric Association, covers only mental disorders, and dominates research and training worldwide, including many Indian programmes. DSM-I (1952) grew out of United States Army and Veterans Administration classifications after the Second World War; it gave brief psychodynamic prose descriptions and called many conditions "reactions". DSM-II (1968) dropped "reaction" and aligned with the ICD-8 but kept "neurosis"; vague definitions kept reliability poor.

DSM-III (1980) was a revolution. A task force led by Robert Spitzer replaced prose with explicit diagnostic criteria: symptom lists, required counts, minimum durations and exclusion rules, building on research criteria from Washington University in St Louis (1972). It was atheoretical about causes, which in practice ended psychoanalytic dominance; the approach is called "neo-Kraepelinian". It introduced the multiaxial system. In its best-known (DSM-IV) form: Axis I, clinical disorders; Axis II, personality disorders and intellectual disability; Axis III, general medical conditions; Axis IV, psychosocial and environmental problems; Axis V, a Global Assessment of Functioning score from 0 to 100. DSM-IV (1994), whose task force was chaired by Allen Frances, based changes on literature reviews, data reanalyses and field trials, and added a clinical significance requirement to many diagnoses.

DSM-5 (2013) switched to Arabic numerals so updates could be numbered like software. It dropped the multiaxial system, abandoning the unreliable Global Assessment of Functioning, reordered chapters along the lifespan, merged autistic disorder and Asperger's disorder into autism spectrum disorder, and removed the "bereavement exclusion" for depression, which critics including Wakefield and Frances feared would medicalise grief. Section III held the Cultural Formulation Interview, an alternative model of personality disorders, and conditions for further study. DSM-5-TR (2022) added prolonged grief disorder: intense yearning for or preoccupation with the deceased, with symptoms such as identity disruption or emotional numbness, lasting at least 12 months after the death in adults (6 in children and adolescents), with significant impairment.

EditionYearWhat changedWhy it mattered
DSM-I1952Brief prose; disorders called "reactions"First official American clinical manual; psychodynamic
DSM-II1968Dropped "reaction"; aligned with ICD-8Still vague; homosexuality removed in later printings
DSM-III1980Explicit criteria; atheoretical; multiaxial systemTransformed reliability and research
DSM-III-R1987Corrected inconsistenciesRefinement, not a new direction
DSM-IV1994Evidence reviews; clinical significance criterion; Outline for Cultural FormulationStandard for two decades of research
DSM-IV-TR2000Text updated, criteria almost unchangedKept the manual current
DSM-52013Multiaxial system dropped; lifespan order; dimensional add-onsTowards dimensions without abandoning categories
DSM-5-TR2022Text revision; prolonged grief disorderCurrent edition
Trap. The multiaxial system arrived with the DSM-III (1980), not the DSM-IV, and left with the DSM-5 (2013). Prolonged grief disorder entered with the DSM-5-TR (2022), not the DSM-5.

4.9 The International Classification of Diseases

The ICD is the World Health Organization's classification of all diseases and health conditions. The World Health Organization took it over in 1948, and its sixth revision was the first with a section on mental disorders. Every member state reports mortality and illness statistics in it.

The ICD-10, endorsed by the World Health Assembly in 1990 and used from 1994, placed mental disorders in Chapter V, "Mental and behavioural disorders", coded F00 to F99, hence "F codes": F20 is schizophrenia, F32 a depressive episode, F33 recurrent depressive disorder. Clinicians used the Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Guidelines (1992), the "blue book", with prose descriptions and flexible guidelines; researchers used the stricter Diagnostic Criteria for Research (1993).

The ICD-11, adopted in 2019, came into effect on 1 January 2022. Mental disorders are in Chapter 6, "Mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders", with codes beginning with 6. New diagnoses include complex post-traumatic stress disorder (core trauma symptoms plus disturbed emotion regulation, self-concept and relationships), prolonged grief disorder (at least six months after the death) and gaming disorder. Personality disorder became dimensional: one diagnosis graded mild, moderate or severe, optionally described by trait domains (negative affectivity, detachment, dissociality, disinhibition, anankastia) and a borderline pattern specifier.

FeatureDSMICD
PublisherAmerican Psychiatric AssociationWorld Health Organization
ScopeMental disorders onlyAll diseases and health conditions
Main purposeClinical diagnosis and research, especially in the United StatesGlobal statistics, public health and clinical use in every member state
StyleOperational criteria with counts and durationsDescriptions closer to prototypes, more room for judgement
AccessCommercialFreely available

The ICD-10 required schizophrenia symptoms for about one month, the DSM at least six months of disturbance in total, so a three-month illness could be schizophrenia in one system and not the other. Grief: six months in the ICD-11, twelve in the DSM-5-TR.

India, as a member state, records and reports health data in ICD codes, and government hospitals and national statistics follow it. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 provides that mental illness shall be determined by nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, including the latest edition of the ICD, and defines mental illness as a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgement, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life. An Indian psychologist often uses the DSM in teaching and research and the ICD in hospital records, and needs both.

4.10 Newer alternatives: RDoC, HiTOP and the alternative model of personality disorders

Limited validity and heavy comorbidity have prompted alternatives, though none has replaced the DSM or ICD in the clinic.

The Research Domain Criteria (RDoC) were launched in 2009 by the National Institute of Mental Health in the United States under its director Thomas Insel, who announced in 2013, just before the DSM-5 appeared, that the Institute would steer research away from DSM categories because they lacked validity. It is a research framework, not a diagnostic manual, built from basic dimensions of functioning present in everyone and arranged as a matrix. The rows are domains: negative valence systems (threat and loss), positive valence systems (reward and motivation), cognitive systems, social processes, arousal and regulatory systems, and, since 2019, sensorimotor systems. The columns are units of analysis, from genes, molecules, cells and circuits through physiology and behaviour to self-report. A researcher might study threat responses across people diagnosed with panic disorder, post-traumatic stress disorder and phobia together. Critics find it heavily biological and far from the clinician's needs.

The Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology (HiTOP) is a research consortium, with leading figures including Roman Kotov, Robert Krueger and David Watson, which set out its model in 2017. It builds a dimensional hierarchy from how symptoms statistically cluster: symptoms and traits group into syndromes, syndromes into subfactors such as "distress" (depression, generalised anxiety) and "fear" (panic, phobias), these into broad spectra (internalising, thought disorder, disinhibited externalising, antagonistic externalising, detachment, somatoform), and at the top perhaps a general "p factor". Comorbidity is expected here: depression and anxiety co-occur because they sit on one internalising spectrum. The model is data-driven but has few everyday clinical tools yet.

The DSM-5 kept ten personality disorder categories but placed an alternative model of personality disorders in Section III. Criterion A rates level of personality functioning, meaning impairment in self (identity, self-direction) and interpersonal life (empathy, intimacy), on a five-point scale. Criterion B describes pathological traits in five domains: negative affectivity, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition and psychoticism. Six disorders (antisocial, avoidant, borderline, narcissistic, obsessive-compulsive, schizotypal) are defined as typical combinations of impairment and traits. It closely resembles the ICD-11 model.

Key. The DSM and ICD diagnose individuals. The Research Domain Criteria are a research framework built from basic brain and behavioural systems. The Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology is a data-driven dimensional hierarchy. Neither of the last two is a "new diagnostic manual".

4.11 Culture and diagnosis

Diagnostic manuals were written mostly by Western clinicians about Western patients. The psychiatrist and anthropologist Arthur Kleinman named the central danger in 1977: the category fallacy, applying a category from one culture to another where it may not fit, then treating any fit as proof it is universal. He also taught clinicians to elicit the patient's explanatory model: what they think is wrong, why, and what should be done.

Older DSM editions listed "culture-bound syndromes", implying that only non-Western cultures shape distress. The DSM-5 replaced this with cultural concepts of distress, of three kinds. Cultural syndromes are symptom clusters recognised locally as a pattern. Cultural idioms of distress are ways of expressing suffering, such as speaking of "tension". Cultural explanations attribute distress to a cause, such as spirit possession or the evil eye.

For Indian students the key example is dhat syndrome, named by the Indian psychiatrist N. N. Wig in 1960. Mainly young South Asian men attribute weakness, fatigue, poor concentration, anxiety, low mood and sexual worries to loss of semen, believed to pass in the urine or through nocturnal emissions, drawing on traditional ideas of semen as a precious vital substance. A clinician who says "nothing is wrong with you" loses the patient to a quack; one who takes the belief seriously, gives accurate sex education gently, and looks for the depression or anxiety that often accompanies it, can help.

Case. Farhan, 21, visits a college counselling centre with weakness and worry that semen is draining away in his urine, having already bought tonics from roadside clinics. The interview also finds two months of low mood and lost interest after a failed examination. The psychologist asks what he believes is happening, explains the physiology plainly, and treats the depression; respecting his explanation is what let her find it and keep him in treatment.

The DSM-IV's Outline for Cultural Formulation became, in the DSM-5, the Cultural Formulation Interview: 16 questions, with an informant version and supplementary modules. It covers the cultural definition of the problem in the person's own words; cultural perceptions of cause, context and support, including family, religion and community; cultural factors affecting self-coping and past help seeking, including healers, prayer and temples; and cultural factors affecting current help seeking, including expectations of the clinician.

Misdiagnosis across cultures takes predictable forms. Over-pathologising reads normal experience as symptom: hearing God's voice in prayer, or a dead parent's voice while mourning, is not thereby psychosis, and possession at a religious festival is not in itself a dissociative disorder. Under-recognition misses disorder expressed unfamiliarly: in India depression often presents as headaches, body aches, weakness and "gas" rather than sadness. Clinician bias produces systematic error; in the United States, Black patients have repeatedly been found to receive schizophrenia diagnoses more often than white patients with similar presentations. The safeguards: ask about cultural and religious context, judge experiences against the person's own group's norms, use interpreters properly, and ask whether the person and community see a problem.

4.12 Case formulation versus diagnosis, and a case walked through

A diagnosis answers "what kind of problem is this?", placing the person in a category shared with others; it is nomothetic, concerned with general laws, a term Gordon Allport brought into personality psychology. A case formulation answers "why does this person have this problem, now, and what keeps it going?"; it is idiographic, a set of theory-based hypotheses linking history, circumstances, thoughts, feelings and behaviour, revised as information arrives. A common structure is the "five Ps": presenting problems; predisposing factors (long-standing vulnerabilities); precipitating factors (triggers of this episode); perpetuating factors (what keeps it going); and protective factors (strengths). These are filled in at biological, psychological and social levels, following the biopsychosocial model of the American psychiatrist George Engel (1977). A clinician needs both: the diagnosis links the case to research, treatments, prognosis and records; the formulation explains this person, sets priorities and restores the detail the label strips away.

Case. Lakshmi, a 38-year-old schoolteacher in Chennai, is brought to a hospital clinic by her husband. For three months she has felt low most of the day, lost interest in teaching and her children, woken at 3 in the morning unable to sleep again, lost weight, felt exhausted and unable to concentrate. She says she is "a burden to everyone", complains of headaches and body aches, and has missed three weeks of work. Her mother died ten months ago after a long illness in which Lakshmi was the main carer. A similar low period followed her second child's birth and lifted over several months. Her mother-in-law, who lives with them, criticises her often. She is close to her sister and finds comfort at the temple.

Step one: is this abnormal? Her state deviates sharply from her usual self; she is distressed and dysfunctional; and "a burden to everyone" raises danger, so the clinician asks calmly and directly about thoughts of death (asking does not plant the idea). She reports passive wishes not to wake, without plan or intent, and a safety plan is made with her and her husband.

Step two: alternatives. Thyroid function and haemoglobin are checked, since physical illness can mimic depression. Normal grief would not explain pervasive loss of interest, worthlessness and early waking. Prolonged grief disorder would centre on yearning for her mother and, in the DSM-5-TR, needs twelve months since the death. No history of mania is found, which for now rules out bipolar disorder.

Step three: working diagnosis. She meets at least five of the nine criteria for a major depressive episode, including depressed mood and loss of interest, for over two weeks, with clear impairment. With the earlier episode, the DSM-5-TR diagnosis is major depressive disorder, recurrent episode, moderate; the hospital record uses recurrent depressive disorder with a current moderate episode (F33 in ICD-10, a Chapter 6 code in ICD-11). It stays a working diagnosis pending the blood tests. Her bodily complaints are a typical Indian presentation of depression.

Step four: formulation. Presenting: low mood, loss of interest, insomnia, passive death wishes, withdrawal from work. Predisposing: a previous episode; possibly a long habit of putting others first and judging herself harshly. Precipitating: her mother's death after years of caregiving, removing a relationship and a role. Perpetuating: withdrawal, which removes achievement and pleasure; the "burden" belief, which stops her seeking support; criticism at home; poor sleep. Protective: a supportive husband, a close sister, her faith, a valued job, past recovery.

Step five: plan. The diagnosis says cognitive-behavioural therapy, including behavioural activation, and antidepressants both have good evidence for moderate depression, so a psychiatric opinion is sought. The formulation says where to start: graded activities with her children and a planned return to school, examining the "burden" belief, space to grieve her mother, a family session to reduce criticism, drawing on her sister and temple, and checking risk every session. None of this could be read from the diagnosis alone.

Recap.

No single yardstick defines abnormality, so clinicians combine rarity, norm violation, distress and dysfunction as the four Ds, while Jerome Wakefield requires both a failed natural mechanism and harm, and Marie Jahoda describes positive mental health. Diagnosis aids communication, research, treatment, prognosis and access to services, but brings stigma, labelling, reification and lost detail; Thomas Szasz called mental illness a myth, and David Rosenhan's 1973 study has been seriously undermined by Susannah Cahalan's 2019 investigation. Classifications are categorical, dimensional or prototype-based, judged by reliability (Cohen's kappa) and validity, and troubled by comorbidity. The DSM gained explicit criteria and axes in 1980, lost the axes in 2013 and added prolonged grief disorder in 2022; the ICD, which India's public health system and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 rely on, moved from Chapter V F codes to Chapter 6 in the ICD-11. The Research Domain Criteria and the Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology offer dimensional research alternatives, and the Cultural Formulation Interview guards against cultural misdiagnosis. A diagnosis names the kind of problem; a case formulation explains this person, and good practice needs both.

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Critically evaluate the four classic yardsticks of abnormality. Why do clinicians combine them, and how do the "four Ds" attempt this?
  2. Explain Jerome Wakefield's harmful dysfunction analysis. How does it deal with normal grief and with drapetomania, and what are its weaknesses?
  3. Discuss the advantages and costs of psychiatric classification, with reference to labelling theory, stigma, reification and Thomas Szasz's critique.
  4. Describe Rosenhan's "On being sane in insane places". What did it claim, what were the early criticisms, and how has Cahalan's investigation changed its standing?
  5. Distinguish categorical, dimensional and prototype classification. Explain reliability and validity of diagnosis, including the calculation of Cohen's kappa, and the significance of comorbidity.
  6. Trace the DSM from DSM-I to DSM-5-TR, and compare the DSM-5-TR with the ICD-11, explaining why India relies on the ICD in public health.
  7. Distinguish diagnosis from case formulation. Using a clinical example, show how cultural factors and the five Ps shape understanding and treatment.

5 The clinical interview and the mental status examination

Before any test is given, any diagnosis written or any therapy begun, a clinician sits with a person and talks. That disciplined talk, the clinical interview, is the most used assessment method in clinical psychology and psychiatry, and the mental status examination is the part of it that records, in a precise shared vocabulary, how the person's mind is working at the moment of meeting. This chapter teaches both from the ground up, from interviewing skills and structured instruments to the Indian case history format and every heading of the mental status examination.

Every viva and clinical posting will ask you to take a history and write a mental status examination, and the interview is where assessment most often goes wrong. The chapter ends with a worked mini-interview and a written summary.

5.1 What a clinical interview is, and why it dominates assessment

A clinical interview is a planned, purposeful conversation between a trained professional and a person seeking help (or someone who knows that person well), held to understand the person's difficulties, gather what is needed for diagnosis and formulation, decide on care, and begin a working relationship. It is planned, because the clinician enters with goals and an outline; purposeful, because every question has a reason; a conversation, because the relationship is itself being built; and clinical, because it runs under professional rules of consent, confidentiality and responsibility.

The clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) explain why it is the most used tool. It needs no equipment and works anywhere. It is flexible, so the clinician can follow an unexpected lead at once. It alone gathers history, observes behaviour live and builds the relationship simultaneously. And it frames every other method: tests are chosen, interpreted and explained through interviews. A clinician can practise without a particular test; none practises without interviewing.

Interview versus conversation

  • A conversation has no set goal, while an interview is directed towards specific information and decisions.
  • In conversation disclosure is mutual; in an interview the roles are asymmetric, and the clinician does not answer the client's disclosures with personal ones.
  • Politeness steers conversation away from painful topics; the interviewer deliberately and tactfully asks about suicide, sexuality, substance use and family conflict.
  • The interviewer, unlike a friend, is responsible for structure, time, safety and the information, and attends as much to how things are said as to what.
Who gave us this. Harry Stack Sullivan, the American psychiatrist, set out the modern view in The Psychiatric Interview (published in 1954, after his death). He called the interviewer a participant observer: someone whose own manner shapes what the patient says, and who must therefore observe the interaction, including his own part in it, as data.
Trap. Rapport is not friendship, and the interview is not "just talking". It is an assessment instrument with its own reliability and validity (section 5.10). A warm interview that misses a suicide plan has failed.

5.2 Kinds of interview by purpose

One meeting often serves several purposes, but naming them tells the clinician what must be achieved.

The intake interview (admission interview in hospitals) is the first formal contact with a service. It asks why the person has come and why now, how urgent the problem is, and whether this is the right service. It screens for risk, explains services and confidentiality, and ends with a disposition: accept, refer or admit. In India it also settles the legal basis of admission under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, which distinguishes an independent admission (the person consents) from a supported admission (the person needs very high support in decision-making and specified risk conditions are met).

The case history interview builds a chronological account of the person's life and illness from the patient and informants, often over several sessions (section 5.8). The diagnostic interview asks whether the person meets criteria for disorders in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11, working systematically through symptoms, duration, impairment and exclusions such as substance use; structured instruments (section 5.5) belong here. The mental status examination interview records present functioning, observed and tested in the room: the history is longitudinal, the story over time; the mental status examination is cross-sectional, a snapshot of now (section 5.9). The crisis interview is taken up in section 5.3.

The termination or exit interview closes therapy or a hospital stay. It reviews goals and progress, consolidates learning, names early warning signs of relapse, arranges follow-up and makes room for the feelings endings bring; at discharge it also covers medication and whom to call in an emergency.

5.3 The crisis interview and suicide risk assessment

A crisis is a time-limited state in which a stressful event, such as a suicide attempt, bereavement or assault, overwhelms a person's usual coping. The crisis interview is therefore short, centred on the here and now, and directive. The clinician makes the setting safe for both parties, establishes calm contact, defines the precipitating problem concretely, assesses risk to self, to others and from others (such as domestic violence), identifies strengths and supporters, agrees a specific plan for the next hours and days, and arranges follow-up. The full history waits.

Suicide risk assessment

Every interview includes suicide risk, and the first rule is to ask directly. Research has consistently found that asking does not plant the idea or increase suicidal thinking; many people are relieved to be asked. Questions move from general to specific.

  1. Hopelessness comes first: "Have you felt life is not worth living?"
  2. Passive death wishes follow: "Have you wished you could sleep and not wake up?"
  3. Active ideation is then asked: "Have you thought of ending your life?"
  4. Plan and intent are explored: "Have you thought how, when and where? How likely is it that you would act?"
  5. Means and preparation are checked: "Is there pesticide or medicine at home? Have you written a note or given things away?"
  6. Past attempts are reviewed, including how the person was found and how he or she felt about surviving.

Risk factors include a previous attempt (the strongest single predictor), depression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia and substance use, hopelessness (which Aaron Beck and colleagues showed predicts eventual suicide better than depression severity), recent loss, chronic painful illness, isolation, family history of suicide, impulsivity and access to lethal means, including stored pesticide in rural India. Protective factors include children, religious objections and family support. The outcome is a reasoned level of risk and a matching plan: safety planning, removal of means, involving family, closer follow-up or admission.

Trap. No scale can predict whether a particular individual will die by suicide; the event is rare enough that even good predictors throw up many false alarms. Assessment guides management, it does not forecast. A denial of intent does not cancel a high-risk picture.
Why it matters. Section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 presumes, unless proved otherwise, that a person who attempts suicide was under severe stress and shall not be tried and punished under Section 309 of the Indian Penal Code, and directs the government to provide care and rehabilitation. The clinician's stance after an attempt is care, not reporting a crime.

5.4 Interviewing children, adolescents, couples, families, and through interpreters

Children are brought by adults, so there are always several informants: child, parents and often teachers. They often disagree usefully: parents tend to report visible behaviour such as defiance better, children their own fears and sadness. The interviewer uses short, concrete questions in the child's words, time anchors a child can use ("before or after Diwali?"), shorter sessions, and for young children play and drawing. Confidentiality is explained simply, with its key limit: if someone is hurting the child, the clinician will tell someone who can keep the child safe.

Adolescents are often brought unwillingly and are alert to adults "taking the parents' side". See them alone for part of the session, say plainly what will and will not be shared with parents, and stay non-judgemental. A widely taught framework is HEADSS: home, education, activities, drugs, sexuality, and suicide and depression. In India, board examination and coaching pressure, phones and relationships deserve direct questions.

Couples and families seen together (a conjoint interview) make the relationship pattern the focus: who speaks first, who speaks for whom, who stays silent, which alliances appear. The clinician stays neutral, gives everyone a voice, sets ground rules and turns blame into description. Circular questions ("When your father gets angry, what does your mother do?") reveal patterns rather than accusations, and a genogram maps three generations. Screening for domestic violence is always done with each partner alone.

Through interpreters: a trained interpreter is better than a relative, and a child must never interpret for a parent. Brief the interpreter to translate everything literally, including disorganised speech; speak to the patient directly, in short units; debrief afterwards. Interpreters may make thought-disordered speech sound coherent, so formal thought disorder is judged cautiously across languages.

5.5 Structured, semi-structured and unstructured interviews

In the unstructured interview the clinician chooses the questions, wording and order. It offers flexibility, freedom to follow leads and easy rapport, but poor reliability: two clinicians may cover different ground and disagree. The United States and United Kingdom Diagnostic Project (1972) showed this: New York psychiatrists diagnosed schizophrenia far more readily than London ones, a gap that largely vanished under a standard interview and definitions. Robert Spitzer and colleagues in the 1970s named two sources of disagreement: information variance (clinicians obtain different information) and criterion variance (they apply different rules to the same information). Structured interviews reduce the first; the operational criteria of DSM-III (1980) reduce the second.

In the fully structured interview every question is scripted word for word in fixed order, with fixed response options and scoring, so trained lay interviewers can give it in population surveys. The Diagnostic Interview Schedule, built for the Epidemiologic Catchment Area study in the United States, and the World Health Organization's Composite International Diagnostic Interview are examples. They are highly reliable but rigid, and blind to what they do not ask. The semi-structured interview fixes the ground and core questions but lets the clinician rephrase, probe and judge whether a symptom is truly present, trading a little reliability for much clinical sense.

FeatureUnstructuredSemi-structuredFully structured
QuestionsChosen freelyCore fixed, probes freeAll scripted
ReliabilityLowestGoodHighest
Flexibility and rapportHighestModerateLowest
Administered byClinicianTrained clinicianTrained lay interviewer
ExamplesRoutine intakeSCID-5, ADIS-5, SCAN, K-SADSDiagnostic Interview Schedule, Composite International Diagnostic Interview, MINI

The named instruments

The Structured Clinical Interview for DSM-5 (SCID-5), by Michael First, Janet Williams, Rhonda Karg and Robert Spitzer, is the standard semi-structured interview for DSM diagnoses, descended from the version built for DSM-III. It opens with an open-ended overview, then works through diagnostic modules, each question printed beside the criterion it tests; the interviewer rates the criterion, not merely the answer, and skips out of a module once an essential criterion is absent. Clinician, Research and personality disorders versions exist.

The Anxiety and Related Disorders Interview Schedule for DSM-5 (ADIS-5), by Timothy Brown and David Barlow, is semi-structured and centred on anxiety, obsessive-compulsive and trauma-related disorders, with mood sections and screening for other conditions. Its hallmark is dimensional rating: a Clinician's Severity Rating from 0 to 8 for each diagnosis, with 4 conventionally the threshold of clinical significance.

The Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview (MINI), by David Sheehan, Yves Lecrubier and colleagues (1998), is a short structured interview for DSM and ICD diagnoses. Each module opens with screening questions; a "no" skips the module, and most items are yes or no. Often completed in about a quarter of an hour, it suits drug trials and busy clinics, at the cost of depth.

The Schedules for Clinical Assessment in Neuropsychiatry (SCAN) were developed under the World Health Organization around the tenth edition of the Present State Examination, originally devised by the British psychiatrist John Wing. The clinician questions flexibly, then rates each experience against precise definitions in a glossary, an approach designed for cross-cultural use.

The Kiddie Schedule for Affective Disorders and Schizophrenia (K-SADS), the child version of the adult Schedule for Affective Disorders and Schizophrenia, was originated by Joaquim Puig-Antich and colleagues; its widely used Present and Lifetime version was published in 1997 by Joan Kaufman, Boris Birmaher and colleagues for ages 6 to 18. Parent and child are interviewed separately and the clinician reconciles the accounts into summary ratings, opening diagnostic supplements only where screening is positive.

Trap. The SCID is called "structured" but is semi-structured, because its ratings rest on clinical judgement. The fully structured, lay-administered instruments are the Diagnostic Interview Schedule and the Composite International Diagnostic Interview; the MINI is described by its authors as structured.

5.6 Core interviewing skills

Rapport is a working relationship of trust in which the client feels safe to say difficult things. Carl Rogers argued in 1957 that therapist empathy, unconditional positive regard and congruence (genuineness) are necessary conditions for change; they are the raw material of rapport. Attending is the base of the "microskills" hierarchy that Allen Ivey began teaching in 1968: posture, eye contact, a calm voice and verbal tracking (staying with the client's topic). Gerard Egan, in The Skilled Helper (1975), summarised physical attending as SOLER: face the client squarely, open posture, lean slightly forward, eye contact, relaxed; in India sustained gaze with elders or across genders may need softening. Active listening means hearing content, feeling and meaning, and showing it.

An open question invites an extended answer ("What has been troubling you?"); a closed question asks for a fact or a yes or no ("Did you sleep last night?"). Open questions give rich, self-directed accounts; closed ones pin down details and help with rambling or crisis-stricken clients. The funnel moves from open to closed within each area. Facilitation keeps the client talking without steering: nodding, "go on", repeating a key word. Paraphrasing restates content in fewer words ("So the fights began after your brother's marriage"). Reflection of feeling names the emotion beneath the words ("You felt humiliated"). Clarification makes a vague statement concrete ("When you say tension, what happens?"); in Indian clinics "tension", "weakness" and "gas" must always be clarified.

Confrontation is not aggression but the tentative pointing out of a discrepancy ("You say home is fine, yet your husband no longer speaks to you"). Interpretation offers a hypothesis linking patterns beyond what the client said; it comes from the psychodynamic tradition and rarely belongs in a first interview. Summarising draws together a longer stretch, at transitions and the end, inviting correction. Silence gives time to think and feel, though a long pause may also signal thought block or depressive slowing. Empathy is understanding the client's experience from within his or her frame of reference and communicating it; it differs from sympathy, feeling sorry from outside ("I can see how frightening that was" versus "Poor you").

What to avoid

  • Leading questions ("You've been depressed, haven't you?") invite agreement from anxious or deferential clients.
  • "Why" questions ("Why did you drink again?") sound accusatory; "What was happening before you drank?" gets the same information without blame.
  • Premature advice and reassurance ("Just think positively") closes exploration and signals that distress is not being taken seriously.
  • Jargon ("Any anhedonia?") confuses; "Do you still enjoy things?" works.
  • Double questions, interruptions, judgemental remarks and heavy note-taking also damage the interview.

5.7 The phases of an interview and its setting

The setting is private, quiet and uninterrupted, with chairs at an angle rather than across a desk; where violence is possible, neither person's exit is blocked. Notes are brief and explained ("I'll jot down a few points"), stopped during emotional disclosures, and written up soon after with verbatim samples of key statements.

The opening sets the frame: greeting, introducing oneself and one's role, the purpose and time available, and a confidentiality statement, that what is said stays within the treating team, with limits for serious risk of harm to self or others, child abuse and legal orders. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 lists confidentiality among the rights of persons with mental illness. The clinician then opens broadly: "What brings you here today?" The body explores the problem, history and mental status, funnelling from open to closed and signposting transitions ("Now I'd like to ask about your family"). The closing is announced ahead ("We have ten minutes left") and includes a summary, "Is there anything important I haven't asked?", next steps and a safety check.

5.8 Case history taking

Indian training follows a fixed sequence, and the order itself prevents omissions. The record opens with the informants, their relationship to the patient, and whether their information is reliable (consistent, credible) and adequate (enough for a picture).

  • Identifying data cover name, age, sex, education, occupation, income, marital status, religion, language, urban or rural domicile and socioeconomic status.
  • Chief complaints are listed in the patient's and informant's own words, chronologically, each with its duration ("Suspiciousness towards neighbours, five months").
  • History of present illness records onset (abrupt, acute or insidious), precipitants, course (continuous, episodic, fluctuating), progress (improving, static, deteriorating), each symptom's effect on functioning, sleep, appetite and weight, treatment tried (including faith healers), and relevant negatives such as no head injury or substance use.
  • Past history covers earlier psychiatric episodes and their outcome, and medical illnesses, head injuries and seizures.
  • Family history is drawn as a three-generation genogram: consanguinity, family type (nuclear, joint, extended), mental illness, suicide and substance use in relatives, and dynamics such as authority, communication and attitude to the patient's illness.
  • Personal history follows the life course: pregnancy, birth and milestones; childhood problems such as bed-wetting, tantrums or school refusal; schooling, performance and reason for stopping; jobs, changes and satisfaction; menstrual history for women; sexual knowledge, orientation, experiences and worries; marital history (arranged or chosen, adjustment, children, in-law conflict); and substance use, with age at first use, pattern, quantity, last use, tolerance and withdrawal.
  • Premorbid personality describes the person before the illness, mainly from informants: attitudes to self, others and work, social relationships, moral and religious standards, predominant mood, leisure, fantasy life, habits and reaction to stress.

The write-up then adds examination findings, diagnosis, formulation and a management plan.

Trap. Premorbid personality is not a personality disorder, and must not be described from the present state. A withdrawn patient with schizophrenia may have been outgoing before; revealing that change is the section's purpose.

5.9 The mental status examination

The mental status examination is a systematic description of observable behaviour and reported inner experience at the time of the interview. It rests on the descriptive phenomenology of Karl Jaspers, whose General Psychopathology (1913) taught that an experience must first be described exactly as the patient lives it, before its cause is guessed. It combines observation with questioning and testing, written in plain terms with verbatim samples.

Appearance, behaviour, psychomotor activity and attitude

General appearance and behaviour are observed from the first moment: build, apparent versus stated age, grooming, dress for season and occasion, scars (including self-harm marks), expression, eye contact and ease of rapport. Self-neglect suggests depression, schizophrenia or dementia; flamboyant dress suggests mania; muttering, smiling to oneself or turning towards an empty corner is hallucinatory behaviour. Psychomotor activity may be increased (agitation, to be distinguished from akathisia, the drug-induced restlessness of antipsychotics) or decreased (retardation in depression; stupor, a conscious but mute, immobile state). Abnormal movements include tremor, tics and tardive dyskinesia. Catatonic signs include posturing, waxy flexibility (a limb stays where the examiner places it, tested by gently moving it), negativism, echopraxia, mannerisms (odd flourishes on purposeful acts) and stereotypies (purposeless repeated movements). Attitude towards the examiner is recorded as cooperative, guarded, suspicious, hostile, over-familiar or indifferent.

Speech

Speech is described for rate, volume, tone, quantity, reaction time, spontaneity, relevance and coherence. Pressure of speech (rapid, loud, hard to interrupt) is typical of mania; poverty of speech (little speech) and poverty of content (enough words, little information) occur in schizophrenia. Dysarthria is faulty articulation; dysphasia is a disorder of language itself, such as word-finding difficulty, and points to brain disease.

Mood and affect

Mood is the sustained emotional state the person reports; affect is the observable, moment-to-moment expression of emotion: mood is the climate, affect the weather. Mood is asked ("How have you been feeling in your spirits?"), recorded in the patient's words and rated (euthymic, depressed, elated, irritable, anxious). Affect is described for range (full, restricted, blunted, flat), reactivity, intensity, stability, appropriateness to content, congruence with mood and communicability. Flat or inappropriate affect (laughing while describing a death) suggests schizophrenia; labile affect, shifting rapidly, occurs in mania and organic states.

Thought: stream, form, content, possession

Stream is the speed and flow of thinking. In flight of ideas (mania) speech leaps between topics with traceable links such as rhymes (clang associations) and distractions; in retardation (depression) thoughts are slow and few; thought block is a sudden stop with the mind going blank (schizophrenia); perseveration, repeating an idea beyond relevance, suggests organic disease. Form is logical organisation, and its disturbance is formal thought disorder: loosening of associations (ideas slide with no understandable link), tangentiality (never reaching the point), circumstantiality (excessive detail, but the point is reached), neologisms and word salad. It is judged from a recorded sample of free talk.

Content covers preoccupations, worthlessness, guilt, suicidal and homicidal ideas, phobias, overvalued ideas and delusions. A delusion is a false belief, firmly held, not correctable by evidence or reasoning, and not explained by the person's culture or religion. Types include persecutory, referential (neutral events carry special meaning), grandiose, guilt, nihilistic, somatic, jealous and erotomanic. An overvalued idea is an understandable, emotionally charged belief that dominates life without a delusion's fixity. Delusions are elicited gently ("Do you feel anyone is against you?"), their conviction tested ("Could there be another explanation?"), and checked against the informant and the culture.

Possession concerns whether thoughts feel like one's own. An obsession is a recurrent, intrusive, unwanted thought, image or impulse recognised as one's own and resisted, often linked to a compulsion performed to relieve anxiety. In thought alienation thoughts feel alien: insertion (put in by an outside agency), withdrawal (taken out) and broadcasting (spreading so others know them), which Kurt Schneider counted among the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia.

Trap. An obsession is "my own thought, which I do not want"; thought insertion is "not my thought, someone put it there". Confusing them confuses obsessive-compulsive disorder with schizophrenia. Textbooks also differ on whether flight of ideas belongs under stream or form.

Perception

A hallucination is a perception without an external stimulus, experienced as real, located outside and not under voluntary control; an illusion is a misperception of a real stimulus, such as a rope taken for a snake in dim light. Auditory hallucinations, commonest in schizophrenia, may address the patient (second person) or discuss him (third person), comment on his actions, argue, echo his thoughts, or give commands, which raise risk. Visual hallucinations suggest delirium or alcohol withdrawal; tactile ones, such as insects crawling under the skin, occur in withdrawal and stimulant use; olfactory and gustatory ones suggest temporal lobe disease. Hypnagogic and hypnopompic hallucinations, on falling asleep and waking, are normal. Depersonalisation and derealisation (self or world feeling unreal) are recorded here. Questions are normalised ("Some people under stress hear voices when no one is there; has that happened?"), and any voice is characterised by number, content, location and response.

Cognitive functions

Consciousness ranges from alert through drowsy and clouded to stupor and coma; impairment or fluctuation suggests delirium. Orientation is tested for time, place and person; in organic states time is lost first and personal identity last, and with unschooled patients time may be judged by season or festival. Attention (directing the mind) and concentration (sustaining it) are tested by serial sevens from 100, serial threes for those with less schooling, days or months backwards, and digit span. Memory is tested at three spans: immediate (digit span forward, normally about seven digits, or repeating three words), recent (recalling the three words after five minutes; the last day's events, checked with the informant) and remote (verifiable personal and public events). Recent memory usually fails before remote memory in dementia, and confabulation fills gaps in Korsakoff syndrome. Intelligence is estimated from general knowledge suited to the background, simple arithmetic and vocabulary, compared with education. Abstract thinking is tested with similarities ("How are an orange and a banana alike?"), differences ("a lie and a mistake") and proverbs ("Empty vessels make the most noise"; or the Hindi "Ek machhli saare talaab ko ganda kar deti hai", one fish dirties the whole pond). Concrete answers suggest organic disease, intellectual disability or schizophrenia, but also limited schooling; bizarre answers suggest thought disorder.

Judgement and insight

Judgement is the capacity to assess a situation and act appropriately. Personal judgement is the realism of one's own plans; social judgement is behaviour in social situations, observed and reported; test judgement uses hypothetical situations ("What would you do if you found a stamped, addressed envelope on the road?"). Test judgement is the weakest guide, since a correct textbook answer can coexist with disastrous real conduct. Insight is awareness of being ill, of the illness's nature and of the need for treatment, graded on the six-point scale of the Kaplan and Sadock tradition used in Indian training.

GradeDescription
1Complete denial of illness.
2Slight awareness of being sick and needing help, while denying it at the same time.
3Awareness of being sick, but blaming others, external factors or physical causes.
4Awareness that the illness is caused by something unknown in oneself.
5Intellectual insight: admitting illness due to one's own irrational feelings, without applying this to future behaviour.
6True emotional insight: emotional awareness of one's motives and feelings, leading to real change.
HeadingWhat is assessedHowExamples of abnormality
Appearance and behaviourGrooming, dress, eye contact, rapportObservation from first contactSelf-neglect, flamboyance, hallucinatory behaviour
Psychomotor activityAmount and quality of movementObservation; moving a limbAgitation, retardation, stupor, catatonic signs
AttitudeRelation to the examinerObservationGuarded, hostile, over-familiar
SpeechRate, volume, quantity, coherenceListening; verbatim samplePressure, poverty, mutism, dysarthria
Mood and affectReported and expressed emotionAsking; observationDepressed, elated, flat, labile, inappropriate
ThoughtStream, form, content, possessionFree talk; targeted questionsFlight of ideas, block, derailment, delusions, obsessions, insertion
PerceptionHallucinations, illusionsNormalised questions; observationThird-person or command voices, visual hallucinations
CognitionConsciousness, orientation, attention, memory, intelligence, abstractionBedside testsClouding, disorientation, poor recall, concreteness
JudgementPersonal, social, testPlans, conduct, hypothetical questionsUnrealistic plans, inappropriate acts
InsightAwareness of illness and need for help"What do you think is happening to you?"Grades 1 to 3 in psychosis

5.10 Reliability, validity, bias and culture

As an instrument, the interview is judged for reliability: whether two interviewers agree about the same person (inter-rater) and whether conclusions hold across occasions (test-retest), with diagnostic agreement usually expressed by the chance-corrected kappa statistic. Validity asks whether it measures what it claims to; self-report is distorted by memory, insight, social desirability and denial, so informants and records are safeguards. Two clinicians can agree and both be wrong: reliability does not guarantee validity.

The halo effect, named by Edward Thorndike in 1920, is the spread of one salient impression to unrelated judgements: a well-dressed, articulate patient may be judged less ill or less at risk than he is. Confirmatory bias is seeking and remembering evidence for an early hypothesis while overlooking evidence against it; a clinician who decides in five minutes that it is "just stress" may never ask about voices. Related errors are anchoring on first information, premature closure, and diagnostic overshadowing, attributing new symptoms to an existing diagnosis. Remedies are structured coverage, a deliberate differential diagnosis, seeking disconfirming evidence, and supervision.

Cultural factors in India

  • Family presence: patients usually come accompanied and relatives answer for them; this gives collateral history and support, but suicidal ideas, substance use, sexual concerns and ill-treatment may stay hidden, so the patient is also seen alone as routine.
  • Language: distress is often voiced through idioms and bodily complaints ("tension", weakness, burning in the head, the Punjabi "sinking heart"); Dhat syndrome, distress over supposed semen loss, was named by N. N. Wig in 1960. Record the patient's words and clarify rather than translating straight into psychiatric terms.
  • Stigma: fear of the "mental" label and its effect on marriage and family standing delays help-seeking, sends many first to faith healers, and leads to minimising; framing questions around sleep, stress and functioning helps.
  • Gender: women may not speak freely before husbands or in-laws, or discuss menstrual, sexual or marital matters with a male clinician, and domestic violence may surface only in private; offer a chaperone or same-gender clinician. Men may hide distress behind physical complaints or anger.
  • Beliefs and hierarchy: possession, black magic and the evil eye are shared cultural explanations, not delusions unless they exceed what the person's community believes; the DSM-5 Cultural Formulation Interview (16 questions) explores the person's own explanation. Schooling strongly affects serial sevens and proverbs.

5.11 A worked mini-interview and mental status summary

Case. Mr S, 26, a science graduate and data-entry operator living in a joint family in a Karnataka town, is brought by his father and brother with five months of withdrawal to his room, reduced talk, suspiciousness towards neighbours, muttering and smiling to himself, poor sleep, and six weeks off work. Onset was insidious, course continuous and worsening; the father is a reliable, adequate informant.

Seeing him alone, the clinician introduces herself as a trainee clinical psychologist, explains that what he says stays within the team unless someone's safety is at risk, in which case she will tell him first, and says they have forty minutes (opening and confidentiality statement). He says, "I don't know why they brought me. I'm not mad." She replies, "It sounds as though coming here wasn't your idea, and you're worried what people might think" (reflection of feeling). "Everyone on our street is already talking." "Talking?" (facilitation). "About me. When I pass, they cough." "Could you tell me about that from when it began?" (open question).

He describes a quarrel over a water tank five months ago, after which neighbours cough as a signal whenever he passes and have "joined with the office people" to get him dismissed. She paraphrases this back and he agrees. "How sure are you, out of a hundred?" "A hundred." "Could there be another reason people cough?" "No, it's too regular." She does not argue; she has established fixity. Then a normalised closed question: "When you're alone, do you hear people talking when no one is there?" After a long pause: "At night. Two men, outside the wall. One says 'he's finished', the other laughs." No commands. A gentle confrontation: "You said work was fine, but your father says you haven't gone for six weeks?" "They're all in it." "That sounds frightening and exhausting" (empathy). Then directly: "Have you thought life isn't worth living, or of ending it?" "Sometimes it would be easier to be dead, but I won't do anything; my mother would suffer." No plan or past attempt. Asked what is happening to him: "Nothing is wrong with my mind; the neighbours are the problem. Maybe I need a sleeping tablet." She summarises, asks what she has missed, and arranges for him to meet the team psychiatrist (closing), without debating the delusion.

Written mental status summary

Appearance and behaviour: young man of stated age, thin, poorly groomed, seasonally dressed; poor, intermittent eye contact; rapport established with difficulty; seen muttering and glancing at the window while waiting. Psychomotor activity: mildly decreased; no catatonic signs. Attitude: guarded, later cooperative. Speech: relevant, coherent, low volume, reduced quantity, increased reaction time. Mood: "fed up and tired"; objectively anxious. Affect: anxious, restricted, reactive, appropriate and congruent. Thought: normal stream, no formal thought disorder; persecutory delusion of a neighbour and colleague conspiracy; delusion of reference (coughing as signals); passive death wishes without plan or intent; no obsessions or thought alienation. Perception: third-person auditory hallucinations, two male voices from outside at night, commenting on him; no commands. Cognition: alert, oriented in all three spheres; serial sevens to 65 with one error; six digits forward; three of three words recalled at five minutes; remote memory intact; intelligence clinically average; concrete proverb interpretation ("An empty pot makes more sound"), significant for a graduate. Judgement: personal and social impaired (stopped work on delusional grounds); test intact. Insight: grade 2, denies illness but accepts help for sleep.

This is a psychotic disorder of five months without prominent mood disturbance. Under ICD-11, one month of such symptoms allows a diagnosis of schizophrenia; under DSM-5-TR, with under six months of total disturbance, it would currently be schizophreniform disorder. Delusional disorder is less likely given the hallucinations, and substance and medical causes must still be excluded.

Recap.

The clinical interview is a planned, purposeful, professional conversation and the most used assessment method. Interviews differ by purpose and are adapted for children, adolescents, families and interpreted sessions. Unstructured interviews maximise flexibility and rapport, fully structured ones maximise reliability, and semi-structured instruments such as the SCID-5, ADIS-5, SCAN and K-SADS balance the two, with the MINI as a brief structured option. Skilled interviewing rests on rapport, attending, the open-to-closed funnel and the listening skills, and avoids leading and "why" questions, premature advice and jargon. The case history gives the longitudinal story under fixed headings; the mental status examination, grounded in Jaspers' phenomenology, gives the cross-sectional picture from appearance to insight. Interviews must be checked for reliability, validity, the halo effect and confirmatory bias, and adapted to Indian realities of family presence, language, stigma and gender. Suicide risk is always asked about directly, to guide management rather than to predict.

NameWhat they gave us
