Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Define the clinical interview and explain how it differs from an ordinary conversation. Why is it the most widely used assessment method in clinical psychology?
  2. Compare structured, semi-structured and unstructured interviews with reference to reliability, flexibility and rapport, and describe any two of the SCID-5, ADIS-5, MINI, SCAN and K-SADS.
  3. Describe the core skills of clinical interviewing with examples, and discuss the errors an interviewer should avoid.
  4. Outline the headings of a psychiatric case history as used in Indian clinical training, stating what is asked under each.
  5. Describe the mental status examination in detail, explaining how thought, perception and cognitive functions are assessed and what abnormal findings indicate.
  6. How would you conduct a crisis interview with a person who has just attempted suicide? Discuss suicide risk assessment and the relevant provision of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017.
  7. Discuss the reliability and validity of the clinical interview, the biases that affect it, and the cultural factors that shape interviewing in India.

6 Psychological assessment: tests, observation, judgement and the report

Almost every decision a clinical psychologist makes rests on assessment. Before anyone is offered therapy, certified as having an intellectual disability, or told whether their forgetfulness is an early dementia, somebody has to gather information, understand the person and tell whoever will act on it. In Indian hospitals much of a clinical psychologist's week goes into exactly this: intelligence testing for disability certificates, personality testing on psychiatry wards and neuropsychological testing after head injury.

This chapter builds that work from the ground up, from psychometrics through the main families of instruments and clinical judgement to the report, the ethics of testing and a worked case: ground both clinical textbooks on your list (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) cover at length.

6.1 Assessment versus testing, and what assessment is for

A psychological test is a standardised procedure for obtaining a sample of behaviour and describing it with scores or categories. It observes only a small, carefully chosen slice of behaviour (answers to forty vocabulary questions, say) and infers something larger. Psychological assessment is the broader process of gathering and integrating information from many sources to answer a question about a person and support a decision. Its sources include interview, history, observation, reports from family or teachers, records and, usually, tests. The test produces a number; the clinician produces the meaning.

Good assessment is multi-method and multi-informant, because every method has blind spots; agreement across methods builds confidence, and disagreement is information to explain.

Assessment serves several purposes.

  • Screening is a brief first pass over many people to find those who need fuller evaluation, such as a short depression questionnaire given to every primary care patient.
  • Diagnosis classifies a person's difficulties using the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM) or the International Classification of Diseases (ICD), including ruling out alternatives.
  • Treatment planning decides which problems to target first, which strengths to build on and what may get in the way.
  • Outcome evaluation repeats measurement during and after treatment to show whether the person is actually improving.
  • Research uses instruments to select participants and measure variables.
  • Forensic questions come from the legal system: fitness to stand trial, mental state at the time of an offence, risk of violence, custody or compensation.

Indian practice adds disability certification, which brings concessions in education and employment.

The organiser of every assessment is the referral question: what the referrer actually needs to know, and what decision rides on it. Referrals often arrive vague ("psychometry please", "IQ and personality"). A psychiatrist who writes "personality assessment" may really be asking, "Is this patient's odd thinking psychotic or a personality style, because I must decide whether to start an antipsychotic?" The clinician turns such requests into answerable questions, often by phoning the referrer, and those questions dictate which tests are chosen and what the report must say.

Key. Tests are tools; assessment is the clinician's reasoning, using those tools and much else, to answer a specific referral question. No test score on its own is a diagnosis, and running the same "psychometry" battery on every client, whatever the question, is testing, not assessing.

6.2 Psychometric foundations: standardisation, norms and reliability

Standardisation means a test is given, scored and interpreted the same way every time. The manual fixes instructions, item order, time limits, permitted prompts and scoring, so that score differences reflect differences between people, not between sessions.

A raw score (34 items correct) means nothing until compared with a relevant group, the normative sample. Raw scores are converted into derived scores: percentile ranks (the percentage of the norm group scoring below the person), z scores (mean 0, standard deviation 1), T scores (mean 50, standard deviation 10) and deviation IQ (mean 100, standard deviation 15). Such tests are norm-referenced; a criterion-referenced test instead compares performance with a fixed standard of mastery.

Norms are only as good as their sample, which should be large, recent and representative. This is why Indian norms matter. An American-normed test may contain unfamiliar content and, above all, ignores education, which strongly affects scores and varies enormously across India; a rural adult with five years of schooling judged against American norms may look impaired when he is not. Translation is not adaptation: items must be checked for cultural meaning and fresh norms collected. Norms also age. Intelligence test scores rose across the twentieth century (the Flynn effect, documented in many countries by James Flynn), so a test with old norms compares today's client with a lower-scoring population and overestimates ability, a real issue with Indian adaptations normed decades ago.

Reliability is consistency of measurement. Classical test theory treats every observed score as a true score plus random error; reliability, a coefficient from 0 to 1, indexes how little error there is, and each type detects a different source of error.

TypeHow it is estimatedError it detects
Test-retestSame test given twice, some interval apart; scores correlated.Fluctuation over time. Suits stable traits, not passing states.
Parallel formsTwo equivalent versions with different items; scores correlated.The particular items chosen; also avoids practice effects.
Split-halfOne test split into halves (often odd versus even items); halves correlated, then corrected upward with the Spearman-Brown formula because each half is shorter.Inconsistency of content within one sitting.
Internal consistencyAverage interrelation of all items, usually coefficient alpha, introduced by Lee Cronbach in 1951.Whether the items measure the same thing.
Inter-raterTwo trained raters score the same responses independently; agreement indexed by correlation, kappa or the intraclass correlation.The scorer. Essential for projective tests, observation and interviews.

By a common rule of thumb, coefficients in the 0.70s suffice for group research, but decisions about an individual call for about 0.90 or above.

6.3 Validity, measurement error and the accuracy of decisions

Validity is the degree to which evidence supports a particular interpretation and use of scores. It belongs to an interpretation, not to a test in the abstract: a reading test can be valid for finding poor readers and invalid as a measure of intelligence in a child with dyslexia.

Content validity asks whether the items adequately sample the domain, as judged by experts; a depression scale asking only about sadness, ignoring sleep, appetite and hopelessness, has poor content validity. It differs from face validity, merely whether the test looks relevant to the test-taker. Criterion validity asks whether scores relate to an outside criterion. If the criterion is measured at about the same time, this is concurrent validity (a new depression questionnaire against clinicians' diagnoses that week); if it lies in the future, it is predictive validity (entrance scores predicting first-year marks).

Construct validity concerns theoretical attributes that cannot be observed directly, such as anxiety or extraversion. Lee Cronbach and Paul Meehl argued in 1955 that it is established by deriving predictions from the theory of the construct (a "nomological network") and checking whether scores behave as predicted. Two kinds of evidence are central: convergent validity, high correlation with other measures of the same construct, and discriminant validity, low correlation with measures of different constructs. Donald Campbell and Donald Fiske formalised this in 1959 in the multitrait-multimethod matrix: an anxiety questionnaire should correlate more with an interview rating of anxiety than with a questionnaire on another trait, even though that shares its method.

Key. Reliability sets a ceiling on validity: a test cannot correlate with anything more strongly than it correlates with itself. An unreliable test cannot be valid, but a reliable test can still be invalid.

The standard error of measurement

The standard error of measurement sizes the margin of error around a score: the test's standard deviation multiplied by the square root of (1 minus the reliability). For an intelligence test with standard deviation 15 and reliability 0.91, one minus 0.91 is 0.09, whose square root is 0.3, so the standard error is 4.5 points. About 95 percent of the time the true score lies within roughly two standard errors, about 9 points, of the observed score. A child scoring 72 therefore has a true score most plausibly between about 63 and 81, a band that straddles the region used for intellectual disability. So current diagnostic guidance allows for measurement error and weighs adaptive functioning rather than treating 70 as a sharp line.

Sensitivity, specificity and base rates

Sensitivity is the proportion of people with a condition whom the test correctly identifies; specificity is the proportion without it whom the test correctly clears. Moving a cut-off trades one for the other. But the clinician's real question is: given this positive result, how likely is the condition? That is the positive predictive value, and it depends heavily on the base rate, how common the condition is among the people tested. Paul Meehl and Albert Rosen showed in 1955 that ignoring base rates makes even good tests mislead.

1,000 people; sensitivity and specificity both 90 percentBase rate 2 percent (community)Base rate 30 percent (specialist clinic)
People with the condition20300
True positives18270
False positives (10 percent of those without it)98 of 98070 of 700
Chance a positive result is correct18 of 116, about 16 percent270 of 340, about 79 percent

When a condition is rare, most positives are false alarms, so screening must be followed by full assessment.

Trap. "90 percent sensitivity" does not mean a positive result is 90 percent likely to be correct; that depends on the base rate.

6.4 Intelligence assessment

In 1905 Alfred Binet and Théodore Simon, asked by French authorities to identify children needing special schooling, published graded tasks arranged by the age at which typical children pass them, giving a mental age. In 1912 William Stern proposed dividing mental age by chronological age, and Lewis Terman's 1916 Stanford revision, the Stanford-Binet, multiplied that ratio by 100 to give the intelligence quotient (IQ). This ratio IQ failed for adults, whose mental age stops rising while their chronological age does not. Modern tests use the deviation IQ, comparing a person with others of the same age on a scale with mean 100 and standard deviation 15: about two thirds of people score between 85 and 115, and roughly 2 percent below 70. The Stanford-Binet fifth edition (2003) measures five factors (fluid reasoning, knowledge, quantitative reasoning, visual-spatial processing and working memory), each through verbal and nonverbal tasks.

The Wechsler scales

David Wechsler, at Bellevue Hospital in New York, found the Binet too verbal and child-centred for adults; his Wechsler-Bellevue scale (1939) introduced verbal and performance scores alongside a full-scale score, and the adult deviation IQ. It led to the Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC, 1949) and the Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (WAIS, 1955), each built from short subtests such as vocabulary, block design and digit span. Recent editions replaced the verbal and performance split with factor-based index scores: four in the adult fourth edition (2008), five in the children's fifth edition (2014).

The adult indexes are Verbal Comprehension (verbal reasoning), Perceptual Reasoning (analysing visual designs and solving novel problems), Working Memory and Processing Speed; the children's edition splits Perceptual Reasoning into Visual Spatial and Fluid Reasoning. Interpretation moves from the most reliable, most general score downward: Full Scale IQ, then index differences larger than chance, and only cautiously single subtests; reading pathology into subtest "scatter" has proved unreliable.

Raven's Progressive Matrices

John C. Raven published the Progressive Matrices in 1938. Each item is a pattern with a piece missing, and items grow progressively harder; there are Standard, Coloured (for young children, older adults and people with intellectual disability) and Advanced forms. Needing no speech or reading, it is used across languages as a measure of general reasoning, but it is "culture-reduced", not "culture-free": familiarity with abstract printed puzzles, a product of schooling, still matters.

Indian tests and adaptations

The Binet-Kamat Test, V. V. Kamat's adaptation of the Stanford-Binet for Indian children, was among the earliest. Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence (C. M. Bhatia, 1955) was built for Indian conditions and low literacy, with five subtests: Koh's Block Design, Alexander's Passalong, Pattern Drawing, Immediate Memory for digits, and Picture Construction. Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children (Arthur J. Malin, 1969) adapts the Wechsler children's scale, yields verbal, performance and full-scale quotients, and remains common in child guidance clinics. The Vineland Social Maturity Scale (Edgar Doll, 1935; adapted for India by Malin) is not an intelligence test: it is an interview with a carer about everyday competence in self-help, locomotion, communication, occupation, socialisation and self-direction, giving a social age and social quotient. It matters because intellectual disability requires adaptive as well as intellectual deficits. All these norms are old and regional, and a report should say so.

Interpreting IQ and its limits

An IQ summarises performance on particular tasks, at one time, against one norm group. It predicts school achievement reasonably well but is affected by schooling, language, motivation, anxiety and fatigue, and it does not capture creativity, practical wisdom or adaptive functioning. Report it as a range with a descriptive category, never as a fixed, innate quantity: two tests normed decades apart can give one child noticeably different IQs.

6.5 Objective personality assessment

"Objective" describes the format, structured items and mechanical scoring, not objective truth. Inventories are built by rational construction (items theory says should tap a trait), empirical criterion keying (keeping whatever items separate groups) or factor analysis (letting item intercorrelations reveal the dimensions).

The Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory

The Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI) was published in 1943 by the psychologist Starke Hathaway and the psychiatrist J. Charnley McKinley at the University of Minnesota. Its innovation was empirical criterion keying. They gave a large pool of statements to diagnosed psychiatric patients and to "Minnesota normals" (largely visitors to the University hospitals). An item went onto, say, the depression scale if depressed patients answered it differently from normals, whatever its content, so some items seem unrelated to their scale. The scales rest on how patients actually respond, but they overlap heavily and reflect 1930s diagnostic groups.

Its second great contribution was the validity scales, which check how the person approached the test. Cannot Say counts omitted items. The L (Lie) scale detects naive attempts to look virtuous ("I have never told a lie"). The F (Infrequency) scale contains items rarely endorsed by normal people; high scores suggest random responding, exaggeration or very severe disturbance. The K (Correction) scale detects subtler defensiveness, and a fraction of it is added to several clinical scales. Later editions added scales for inconsistent and yea-saying responding.

ScaleNameScaleName
1Hypochondriasis6Paranoia
2Depression7Psychasthenia
3Hysteria8Schizophrenia
4Psychopathic Deviate9Hypomania
5Masculinity-Femininity0Social Introversion

Scores are T scores; on the second edition, 65 or above is clinically significant. Because the names are dated, clinicians use numbers and interpret code types, the two or three highest scales: a "2-7" profile suggests an anxious, depressed, self-critical person; a "4-9" profile impulsive, rule-breaking behaviour. The MMPI-2 (1989, 567 items) restandardised the test on a new American sample. The MMPI-2-Restructured Form (2008) introduced Restructured Clinical scales that strip out a common "demoralisation" factor which had inflated the old scales together, and the MMPI-3 (2020) updated norms and scales again.

The Millon, the NEO, the Sixteen Personality Factor and the Eysenck questionnaires

Theodore Millon's Millon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory (1977) was built from his own theory of personality and aligned with the DSM personality disorders, with further scales for clinical syndromes. It uses base rate scores calibrated to each disorder's expected prevalence among patients and, being normed on patients, overstates pathology if given to community members.

The NEO Personality Inventory of Paul Costa and Robert McCrae (revised edition 1992, 240 items) measures normal personality on the five-factor model: Neuroticism, Extraversion, Openness to experience, Agreeableness and Conscientiousness, each divided into six facets. The model grew from the lexical hypothesis, that important personality differences become encoded as words in ordinary language. It has few checks on test-taking attitude.

Raymond Cattell factor-analysed trait words and ratings to produce the Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire (1949), measuring sixteen primary "source traits" such as warmth, dominance and emotional stability, which combine into global factors such as extraversion and anxiety. Hans Eysenck argued instead for a few broad, biologically based dimensions. The Eysenck Personality Questionnaire (1975), written with Sybil Eysenck, measures Extraversion, Neuroticism and Psychoticism, plus a Lie scale.

Trap. Eysenck's Psychoticism scale measures tough-mindedness, hostility and impulsivity, not psychosis, and MMPI scale 8 does not diagnose schizophrenia. Interpret scales by their items and research, not their labels.

6.6 Projective personality assessment

The projective hypothesis holds that when people make sense of an ambiguous stimulus with no right answer, they must organise it using their own needs, conflicts and ways of perceiving, and so reveal them. Lawrence Frank named these "projective methods" in 1939, drawing on psychoanalytic ideas about unconscious processes. Many now call them performance-based measures.

The Rorschach Inkblot Test

The Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach published ten inkblots in Psychodiagnostik (1921): five black and grey, two with red, three multicoloured. In the response phase the person says what each blot might be; in the inquiry the examiner asks where each percept was seen and what made it look that way. Responses are coded for location (whole blot, common detail, unusual detail, white space), determinants (form, colour, shading, movement), content, form quality (how well the percept fits the blot's shape) and popular responses. Rival scoring systems made research incomparable until John Exner merged them into the Comprehensive System (1974), with standard administration, coding, norms and indices (for disordered thinking, depression and suicide risk, for example). Concerns about its norms and weaker variables led Gregory Meyer and colleagues to publish the Rorschach Performance Assessment System (2011), which keeps the best-supported variables, uses international reference data, and standardises response numbers (the examiner prompts for two or three per card and stops at four).

The Thematic Apperception Test

Christiana Morgan and Henry Murray introduced the Thematic Apperception Test in 1935 at the Harvard Psychological Clinic. Its cards show ambiguous scenes, plus one blank card, and the person tells a story for each: what is happening, what led up to it, what the characters feel, and how it ends. Murray read stories through his theory of needs (inner motives such as achievement or affiliation) and press (environmental forces), usually through the "hero" the storyteller identifies with. Clinicians typically use eight to twelve cards and interpret qualitatively, which limits reliability. Uma Chowdhury produced Indian adaptations of it and of the Children's Apperception Test.

Sentence completion and drawings

Sentence completion tests offer stems ("My mother...", "I feel guilty when...") for the person to complete; the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (Julian Rotter, 1950) has forty stems and an adjustment score. Drawing tests ask for a person, a house and tree, or the family. Karen Machover (1949) turned the Draw-a-Person into a personality test, reading features such as large eyes as signs of suspiciousness. Drawings help rapport with children, but sign-by-sign interpretation has the weakest research support of any projective method.

The scientific debate

Scott Lilienfeld, James Wood and Howard Garb's review in 2000 crystallised the criticisms: inconsistent administration and scoring, weak inter-rater reliability for some methods, norms that made ordinary people look disturbed, poor validity for many specific signs, and little incremental validity (added accuracy beyond interviews and questionnaires). Defenders countered that properly coded Rorschach variables are reliable, and a 2013 meta-analysis by Joni Mihura and colleagues supported a middle position: variables tied to perceptual accuracy and disordered thinking were well supported, many others were not. The defensible summary, important because these tests remain standard in Indian training and hospitals, is that projective tests are not all equal and systematically scored variables beat impressionistic reading.

6.7 Behavioural assessment

Behavioural assessment grew from behaviour therapy in the 1960s. Traditional tests treat a response as a sign of an underlying trait; behavioural assessment treats behaviour as a sample of what the person does in particular situations. It is situational, repeated over time and tied directly to treatment. Frederick Kanfer and George Saslow (1965) proposed such behavioural analysis as an alternative to diagnostic labels, since it points straight at what to change.

A functional analysis identifies what causes and maintains a behaviour. The ABC model records Antecedents (setting, people and demands just before), the Behaviour defined observably, and its Consequences (what follows and may reinforce it). Problem behaviours usually serve a function: gaining attention, escaping a demand, obtaining something tangible, or sensory stimulation. Treatment targets the function.

Case. An eight-year-old boy with autism bangs his head when a worksheet is presented (antecedent), and the teacher then removes it to comfort him (consequence): the behaviour serves escape. He is taught to ask for a break, worksheets are shortened, and head-banging no longer removes them. A child seeking attention by the same behaviour would need a different plan.

Naturalistic observation watches behaviour where it occurs (classroom, home, ward). Analogue observation recreates the situation in the clinic: a role play, or a behavioural avoidance test in which a person with a phobia approaches the feared object step by step. Target behaviours are defined operationally, and recording may be by frequency, duration, latency, interval recording (noting whether the behaviour occurred in each short interval) or time sampling. Observers are trained and their agreement checked, because they drift from definitions over time.

In self-monitoring clients record their own behaviour, thoughts or feelings as they occur, such as cigarettes smoked or the thought records of cognitive behaviour therapy; smartphone ecological momentary assessment prompts recordings several times a day. Rating scales and checklists gather structured reports. The best known in child work is the Child Behavior Checklist from the system developed by Thomas Achenbach: parents rate over a hundred problem behaviours (teacher and adolescent forms exist), and scores form syndrome scales grouped as internalising (anxiety, depression, withdrawal, somatic complaints) and externalising (aggression, rule-breaking). Informants often disagree, which shows whether a problem is confined to one setting.

Reactivity is the change in behaviour caused by observing it: children behave better with a stranger in the classroom, smokers smoke less while counting. It is reduced by unobtrusive observation and habituation, and in therapy it can even help, since self-monitoring alone often improves behaviour.

6.8 Neuropsychological assessment and cognitive screening

Neuropsychological assessment measures cognitive functions to understand how brain damage or dysfunction affects behaviour. It is requested after head injury or stroke, when dementia is suspected, and around brain surgery. It samples attention, processing speed, memory, language, visuospatial skills and executive functions (planning, flexibility, inhibition, working memory) against age- and education-appropriate norms.

Fixed batteries give everyone the same comprehensive set. The Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery, begun by Ward Halstead and developed by Ralph Reitan, includes the Category Test, Tactual Performance Test, Seashore Rhythm Test, Speech-Sounds Perception Test, Finger Tapping Test and Trail Making Test, summarised in an Impairment Index. The Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery standardised the qualitative bedside examination of the Russian neuropsychologist Alexander Luria into scored scales. Flexible approaches instead choose tests to fit the referral question. In India, the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery, developed at the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences, Bengaluru, by Shobini Rao and colleagues, provides norms by age, education and sex, with a separate children's battery, using tests such as digit vigilance, verbal fluency, the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, the Tower of London and verbal learning tests.

The Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE), published by Marshal Folstein, Susan Folstein and Paul McHugh in 1975, is a ten-minute bedside screen scored out of 30: orientation, registration and recall of three words, attention and calculation (serial sevens), language tasks and copying intersecting pentagons. A score below about 24 has often been used to suggest impairment, but scores depend heavily on education, and the test misses mild and executive impairment, for which the Montreal Cognitive Assessment is often preferred.

Trap. A low MMSE score in someone with four years of schooling, or in delirium or severe depression, does not establish dementia, and a normal score does not rule out mild or frontal impairment. A screen is not a diagnosis.

6.9 Clinical judgement: clinical versus statistical prediction, and clinicians' biases

Data must be combined into a judgement, such as whether a patient will relapse. In clinical prediction the clinician combines the information in their head. In statistical or actuarial prediction it is fed into an explicit rule, a formula or table derived from past cases, which works the same way every time. The distinction concerns how data are combined, not what data are used; a clinician's interview rating can be an input to a formula.

Who gave us this. Paul Meehl, in Clinical versus Statistical Prediction (1954), reviewed about twenty studies and found the mechanical method as accurate or more accurate in nearly all. A meta-analysis of 136 studies by William Grove and colleagues in 2000 found mechanical prediction equal or superior in the large majority of comparisons and clearly worse in only a small minority.

Formulas win because they apply the same weights every time, are never tired or swayed by vivid details, and derive their weights from real outcomes; clinicians rarely get systematic feedback, so experience does not correct their errors. Meehl allowed a "broken leg" exception: a formula may predict a man goes to the cinema every Tuesday, but if he has just broken his leg you should override it. The danger is seeing broken legs everywhere. Clinicians remain essential for deciding what to measure, gathering good data and handling rare circumstances. In risk assessment, structured professional judgement is a middle path: tools such as the Historical Clinical Risk Management-20 (HCR-20) make the clinician rate a fixed list of evidence-based risk factors before judging.

BiasHow it appears in assessmentRemedy
Confirmation biasForming an early hypothesis and then noticing only information that fits it.List alternatives; seek evidence that would disconfirm the favoured one.
Availability heuristicJudging a diagnosis likely because a memorable similar case comes easily to mind.Check base rates, not memorable examples.
OverconfidenceConfidence exceeding accuracy, often growing with experience while accuracy does not.Seek outcome feedback; state uncertainty; use actuarial aids.
Illusory correlationSeeing a link between a test sign and a trait that is not really there.Rely on research evidence, not clinical lore.
Barnum effectAccepting vague statements true of almost everyone as a personal description.Ask of each report sentence whether it fits most people.

Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman described the availability heuristic in 1973. Loren Chapman and Jean Chapman showed in 1967 that students shown drawings randomly paired with symptoms "discovered" the same links clinicians believed in (large eyes with suspiciousness) although the pairings contained none. Bertram Forer (1949) found students rated one identical, horoscope-style "personal" description as highly accurate; Meehl later named this the "Barnum effect", after the showman P. T. Barnum. Bias can be narrowed, never removed, by structured instruments, base rates, deliberately considering alternatives, actuarial tools, written reasoning and consultation.

6.10 Integrating the data, writing the report, and the ethics of testing

Integration builds one coherent account from all sources instead of reporting tests one by one. The clinician looks for convergence (low mood in the interview, on the MMPI depression scale and in a parent's report), explains discrepancies (a client who denies anxiety on a questionnaire but trembles throughout testing), sets findings in the context of history and culture, and weighs each source by its reliability and validity, producing a formulation that answers the referral question.

A standard report runs through identifying information; the reason for referral; procedures used; background history; behavioural observations, including effort and how far results can be trusted; results and interpretation, integrated by domain or question with scores as ranges; and a summary answering the referral question, with diagnostic impressions and specific, feasible recommendations. Write for the referral source. A teacher needs to know what to do in class, not what a Perceptual Reasoning Index is; a psychiatrist needs a clear diagnostic answer; a court needs conclusions tied to the legal question with their limits stated. Avoid jargon or explain it at once, describe behaviour concretely, include strengths, state uncertainty honestly, and avoid Barnum statements. The client, too, deserves feedback in plain language, checked for understanding. Stephen Finn's Therapeutic Assessment goes further, making clients collaborators who pose their own questions, so that assessment itself becomes therapeutic.

  • Competence. Use only tests you are trained in; publishers restrict complex instruments to qualified users. In India the Rehabilitation Council of India registers clinical psychologists, whom the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 recognises as mental health professionals.
  • Informed consent. The client or guardian should understand the purpose, who will see the results and the limits of confidentiality, which matter most in forensic, employment and school referrals.
  • Test security. Items, manuals and scoring keys must not be circulated or coached, since a test whose answers are known measures nothing.
  • Cultural fairness. Tests must suit the person's language, education and culture; results for people unlike the normative sample need cautious interpretation and a stated limitation.
  • Responsible use. Avoid obsolete tests and norms, and never adopt computer-generated interpretations uncritically.

6.11 A case from referral question to report

Case.

Arjun, nine, in class 4 of an English-medium school in Pune, is referred by his teacher: "Slow learner. Please test IQ." His family speaks Marathi at home.

Clarifying the question. A call reveals the teacher's real question: why is a lively boy who follows class discussion failing English and written work, and does he need a special school? The working questions become: is his intellect average or below; if average, is there a specific learning, attention or emotional problem; and what help does he need? Hypotheses include intellectual disability, specific learning disorder, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, anxiety, sensory problems and simple lack of English exposure.

Test selection. A paediatric check excludes vision and hearing problems. After interviewing parents and teacher, the psychologist selects Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children (Indian norms, though old), the Vineland Social Maturity Scale, standardised reading, spelling and arithmetic tests, the Child Behavior Checklist (parent and teacher forms), interval-recorded observation in an English and a mathematics period, and a sentence completion form read aloud.

Results. Arjun tried hard, so scores can be trusted. His full-scale quotient was 101 (verbal 97, performance 106), average allowing for measurement error, as was his social quotient. He read at about a class 2 level, slowly, stumbling over sounding out words; his Marathi reading was also behind, which argues against mere lack of English exposure. Arithmetic was at class level except for word problems he could not read. Parent ratings were normal apart from worry about school; the teacher rated borderline attention problems. He was off-task in about half the observed intervals in English but about one tenth in mathematics, and asked to sharpen his pencil or visit the toilet whenever reading aloud was due, which let him escape it. He completed one stem: "I feel stupid when... I read in front of everyone."

Report summary. "Arjun is a friendly, hard-working boy of average intelligence. He does not have an intellectual disability and does not need a special school. His difficulties are specific to reading and spelling in both English and Marathi and fit a specific learning disorder with impairment in reading. His inattention appears mainly when he must read and seems to be avoidance of a humiliating task rather than a general attention disorder; review this once his reading improves. We recommend structured, phonics-based remedial teaching; examination accommodations such as extra time and having questions read to him; not asking him to read aloud before the class for now; praise for effort; and review in one year. He may be eligible for certification as having a specific learning disability."

Recap.

Assessment answers a referral question by many methods, of which testing is one; its purposes run from screening and diagnosis to treatment planning, outcome evaluation, research and forensic work. A score means something only if the test is standardised, reliable, valid for the purpose and normed on relevant, recent samples (a real issue in India); every score carries a standard error, and a positive result's value depends on the base rate. Intelligence is assessed with the Wechsler scales, the Stanford-Binet, Raven's matrices and Indian tests such as Bhatia's battery and Malin's scale. Personality is assessed with objective inventories such as the MMPI and the NEO and with projective methods such as the Rorschach, whose support is variable-specific. Behavioural assessment rests on functional analysis and observation; neuropsychological assessment on batteries such as the Halstead-Reitan and the NIMHANS battery, with the MMSE only a screen. Statistical prediction usually matches or beats clinical judgement, which is prone to predictable biases. Findings end in a clear report for the referral source, governed by rules on competence, consent, test security and cultural fairness.

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Distinguish assessment from testing, describe the purposes of clinical assessment, and explain why the referral question should organise every assessment.
  2. Define reliability and validity and their main types, and explain with worked examples how the standard error of measurement, sensitivity, specificity and base rates affect decisions about individuals.
  3. Trace intelligence testing from Binet to the current Wechsler scales, describe the main Indian tests, and evaluate the uses and limits of IQ.
  4. Describe the construction, validity scales and clinical scales of the MMPI and its revisions, and compare it with other objective inventories.
  5. Explain the projective hypothesis, describe the Rorschach and the Thematic Apperception Test, and evaluate the scientific debate about them.
  6. Discuss clinical versus statistical prediction and the biases in clinical judgement, and explain how to reduce them when integrating data and writing a report.

7 Psychotherapy: what it is, the main approaches, and whether it works

Chapter 3 set out the theories behind the main models of clinical psychology. This chapter walks into the consulting room: what psychotherapy is, who comes and what they expect, how a course of therapy runs, and what a therapist of each school actually does. It then asks the question every viva asks: does any of this work, how do we know, and does it matter which therapy a person receives?

Psychotherapy is the clinical psychologist's core skill, and the outcome debate is among the most examined areas in the clinical textbooks. The chapter closes with one client followed through a full course of cognitive behaviour therapy.

7.1 What psychotherapy is, and how a course of therapy runs

No definition satisfies every school, but a working one covers them all: psychotherapy is a planned, collaborative relationship in which a trained professional uses psychological methods, grounded in a theory of how problems arise and how people change, to help a person reduce distress and change troubling patterns of feeling, thinking, behaving or relating. "Trained professional" separates therapy from a wise elder's help; therapy is not "just talking". "Psychological methods" means conversation, the relationship and structured practice rather than medicines. "Grounded in a theory" means a rationale explains the problem and justifies the procedure. "Collaborative" makes the client an active partner. All of it sits inside an ethical frame of consent, confidentiality and boundaries. Counselling overlaps heavily; by convention it is shorter and aimed at adjustment problems in people functioning reasonably well.

People come with depression, anxiety, trauma, marital conflict, grief or a sense of being stuck; some are sent by a parent, college or court and may not believe they have a problem. All bring expectations. Role expectations concern what each party will do: many first-time clients expect a doctor who questions and then prescribes advice, and are puzzled when asked to explore feelings or keep a diary. Outcome expectations concern whether therapy will help, and duration expectations how long it will take. Badly mismatched expectations predict dropout, so many therapists begin with role induction: a plain explanation of how therapy works and what the client's part is.

Most courses of therapy pass through four phases.

  1. Assessment. The first one to three sessions gather the problem, its history, background, strengths and any risk to self or others, often with standardised questionnaires. Consent is taken and the limits of confidentiality explained. Assessment ends in a case formulation: a working hypothesis about how the problem developed and what keeps it going.
  2. Goal setting. Good goals are specific and observable ("attend classes three days a week"), not vague ("feel better").
  3. The working phase. Techniques are applied and most change happens; setbacks and strains in the relationship are expected.
  4. Termination. A planned ending reviews what changed and how, prepares for future difficulties (relapse prevention), and gives room to feelings about ending, sometimes followed by spaced "booster" sessions.

Sessions usually last 45 to 50 minutes, often weekly, within a stable frame of time, place, fees and boundaries. A psychodynamic session is deliberately unstructured so the client's material can emerge. A cognitive behavioural session is structured; the sequence taught by Judith Beck runs: mood check, bridge from the last session, agenda set together, homework review, agenda items, new homework, and a closing summary with the client's feedback.

Homework means tasks carried out between sessions: recording thoughts, practising relaxation, facing a feared situation. The logic is arithmetic: one hour a week with the therapist against more than a hundred waking hours without. Reviews find that therapies using homework tend to do better, and clients who complete more improve more. Undone homework is treated as information (too hard, badly explained, blocked by a belief such as "nothing will help"), not defiance.

7.2 Common factors and the therapeutic alliance

Therapies with incompatible theories all seem to help. A psychoanalyst explains depression through loss and anger turned inward, a behaviourist through lost reinforcement, a cognitive therapist through negative thinking. If all succeed, perhaps what heals is what they share. This is the common factors argument.

Rosenzweig and the Dodo bird verdict

Saul Rosenzweig made the argument in a short 1936 paper. The success of different methods, he suggested, owed more to "implicit common factors" than to their theories: the therapist's personality, the release of feeling, and above all a consistent, believable way of understanding one's difficulties, whose coherence mattered more than its literal truth. He borrowed a line from Lewis Carroll's Alice's Adventures in Wonderland, where after a chaotic race the Dodo declares: "Everybody has won, and all must have prizes." In 1975 Lester Luborsky and colleagues reviewed comparative studies, found few differences in outcome, and put the Dodo's line in their title; the label "Dodo bird verdict" (genuine therapies produce roughly equal benefit) dates from that review.

Trap. Rosenzweig introduced the Dodo analogy in 1936; Luborsky and colleagues popularised the "Dodo bird verdict" in 1975. The verdict concerns average comparisons between competently delivered therapies, not every therapy for every problem: for obsessive-compulsive disorder and specific phobias, exposure-based methods appear to have a specific advantage, and the verdict remains contested.

Jerome Frank and the shared features of healing

The psychiatrist Jerome Frank, in Persuasion and Healing (1961), compared psychotherapy with religious healing, shamanism and placebo. People seek help, he argued, less for particular symptoms than because they are demoralised: helpless, hopeless and unable to cope. Every healing tradition combats demoralisation through four shared features: (1) an emotionally charged, confiding relationship with a helper; (2) a healing setting that marks the helper as a source of help; (3) a rationale or "myth" that plausibly explains the sufferer's problems; and (4) a ritual or procedure based on that rationale, requiring the active participation of both and believed by both to restore health. "Myth" did not mean falsehood; the explanation heals because it is shared and believed.

Wampold's contextual model

Bruce Wampold, in The Great Psychotherapy Debate (2001), set two models against each other. In the medical model, therapy works like a drug: a specific ingredient corrects a specific deficit. In the contextual model, therapy is a social healing practice: once a bond forms, benefit flows through the real relationship, a genuine connection that heals in itself; through expectations, as the client accepts an explanation and believes the treatment will work; and through specific ingredients, which matter because they lead the client to take health-promoting actions such as facing fears. Wampold reads small differences between therapies and larger ones between therapists as favouring the contextual model; critics say he underweights conditions where specific procedures clearly matter.

Client, therapist and expectancy factors

Client factors include severity, chronicity, motivation, social support and readiness to change. Therapist factors matter too: some therapists consistently outperform colleagues delivering the same manual. Expectancy and hope probably explain much of the rapid improvement that often precedes any technique. Michael Lambert offered a widely reproduced estimate in 1992 of each factor's contribution; it came from his reading of the literature, not a statistical analysis, and must be quoted as an estimate.

Source of improvementLambert's estimateWhat it includes
Extratherapeutic (client and life) factorsAbout 40 percentClient strengths, support, chance events, spontaneous recovery
Common factorsAbout 30 percentEmpathy, warmth, acceptance, the alliance
Expectancy (placebo)About 15 percentHope and belief in the treatment
Specific techniquesAbout 15 percentProcedures unique to a school

The therapeutic alliance

The most studied common factor is the therapeutic alliance. The idea began in psychoanalysis, but in 1979 Edward Bordin gave it a form any school could use, with three components:

  • Goals: agreement on what the therapy aims to change.
  • Tasks: agreement on the activities through which change will happen (exploring childhood, keeping thought records, exposure) and a shared sense that they are relevant.
  • Bond: the emotional tie of trust, liking and respect.

This made the alliance measurable, and large meta-analyses consistently find a moderate correlation between alliance and outcome, roughly 0.25 to 0.30, across therapies. Being a correlation, it could partly reflect early improvement strengthening the alliance, though the link largely survives when prior change is controlled. Research on ruptures, strains shown through withdrawal (going quiet, agreeing too readily) or confrontation (complaining), suggests that ruptures openly repaired can strengthen the alliance.

7.3 Psychodynamic therapy in practice

Psychodynamic therapy descends from Sigmund Freud's psychoanalysis: unconscious conflicts and early relationship patterns are helped to surface in the room. Classical analysis meant four or five couch sessions a week for years; modern psychodynamic therapy is usually weekly and face to face.

Free association is the "fundamental rule": the client says whatever comes to mind without censoring it as trivial or embarrassing. Interpretation, the main intervention, offers a hypothesis about hidden meaning, built up gradually. The therapist first clarifies ("You've mentioned your manager three times today"), then confronts an avoidance ("Each time you reach how angry you were, you change the subject"), then interprets ("Perhaps anger at someone in authority feels dangerous, as it did with your father"). Good interpretations are timely, tentative and close to what the client can almost see.

Transference is the client's unconscious transfer onto the therapist of feelings belonging to earlier figures: a client harshly criticised as a child may be sure the therapist is silently judging him. Because the pattern appears live, it can be examined as it happens. Countertransference is the therapist's emotional reaction to the client. Freud saw it as an obstacle; in 1950 Paula Heimann argued it is also an instrument, since what the therapist feels (boredom, protectiveness, irritation) may reveal how the client affects others. Resistance is any way the client unknowingly opposes painful material: lateness, forgotten sessions, silence, intellectualising, or a sudden "everything is fine". It is explored, not fought, because what is avoided usually matters. Working through, from Freud's 1914 paper "Remembering, Repeating and Working-Through", is the slow revisiting of an insight across many situations until it changes how the person feels and acts. One insight does not cure.

Brief psychodynamic therapy limits the number of sessions, agrees a single focus such as one core conflict, makes the therapist more active, and selects clients who can form a relationship quickly and tolerate strong feeling. David Malan's "triangle of conflict" (hidden feeling, the anxiety it arouses, the defence against it) and "triangle of person" (the pattern with the therapist, current others and past figures) remain standard teaching tools. James Mann's time-limited psychotherapy uses exactly twelve sessions and makes the approaching ending part of the work.

7.4 Client-centred therapy in practice

Carl Rogers developed client-centred (later person-centred) therapy from the 1940s. His 1957 paper "The necessary and sufficient conditions of therapeutic personality change" claimed that if six conditions hold over time, constructive change follows, and nothing else is needed:

  1. Two persons are in psychological contact.
  2. The client is in a state of incongruence, vulnerable or anxious.
  3. The therapist is congruent (genuine) in the relationship.
  4. The therapist experiences unconditional positive regard for the client.
  5. The therapist experiences empathic understanding of the client's internal frame of reference and tries to communicate it.
  6. The client perceives, at least minimally, that empathy and regard.

Diagnosis, interpretation, advice and technique are all absent; the relationship is the treatment. The visible activity is reflection: restating the client's meaning, especially the feeling behind it, so the client can hear it and go further. It is not parroting; late in life Rogers disliked "reflection of feelings" as a mechanical label and preferred to call it testing his understanding.

Case.

Client: My parents keep asking when I'll get a proper job. I just nod. Then I go to my room and can't do anything.

Therapist: You nod along, and afterwards there's a heaviness, as if the energy has drained out.

Client: Yes. I feel I've failed them. But also... I'm angry. It's my life.

Therapist: Two feelings side by side: guilt at letting them down, and anger that the decision isn't left to you.

The therapist does not reassure, advise or interpret the parents' motives, and never runs ahead of what the client feels. Feeling understood rather than judged, he reaches the anger he had kept out of awareness: incongruence beginning to resolve.

Research partly supports Rogers: meta-analyses find therapist empathy moderately related to outcome across therapies, and genuineness and positive regard more modestly so. But "necessary and sufficient" has not held in its strong form: for some disorders structured methods outperform the relationship alone. The core conditions are powerful facilitative factors, not a complete treatment.

7.5 Behaviour therapy techniques

Behaviour therapy applies learning principles directly to problem behaviour, working on what can be observed and measured now and testing its methods experimentally.

Relaxation training. Edmund Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation has the client tense and release muscle groups in turn, learning to notice and let go of tension. With slow breathing, it is used for stress and generalised anxiety and as a building block of desensitisation.

Systematic desensitisation. Joseph Wolpe described it in Psychotherapy by Reciprocal Inhibition (1958): since a person cannot be deeply relaxed and anxious at once, relaxation can gradually replace anxiety. The client learns relaxation; builds an anxiety hierarchy of feared scenes rated on a 0 to 100 scale of subjective units of distress; then, while relaxed, imagines the lowest scene, stopping if anxiety rises, and moves up only when a scene can be held calmly.

Exposure and flooding. Later research showed the active ingredient to be chiefly exposure: prolonged, repeated contact with the feared stimulus without escape, graded or intense, imagined or in real life (in vivo). Flooding places the client at once in the most feared situation until anxiety falls; it can work fast but is distressing and needs full consent. The older explanation is habituation, fear declining with sustained contact; newer inhibitory-learning accounts hold that the person learns something new ("the disaster did not happen") that competes with the old fear.

Exposure and response prevention, the main behavioural treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder, was pioneered by Victor Meyer in 1966 and developed extensively by Edna Foa. The client touches a "contaminated" doorknob and then does not wash. Compulsions relieve anxiety briefly, which is exactly what keeps the obsession alive; blocking them teaches that anxiety subsides and the feared outcome does not occur.

Contingency management and token economies. Contingency management systematically reinforces wanted behaviour; in substance use treatment, clients earn vouchers for drug-free urine tests. A token economy applies this to a whole ward: Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin described one at Anna State Hospital, Illinois, in 1968, where long-stay patients earned tokens for self-care and ward tasks, exchangeable for privileges. Behaviour improved, but gains often faded when tokens stopped, and critics questioned making basic comforts conditional.

Behavioural activation draws on Peter Lewinsohn's view that depression is maintained by low positive reinforcement: withdrawal reduces reward, deepening low mood and further withdrawal. The client monitors activity and mood, sees the link, and schedules activities that bring pleasure or mastery. A 1996 component study found activation alone did about as well as full cognitive therapy, establishing it as a treatment in its own right. Social skills training teaches interpersonal behaviour (starting a conversation, making a request, refusing) through instruction, modelling, role-play, feedback and homework, in schizophrenia, social anxiety and developmental conditions.

Trap. Systematic desensitisation is graded, uses relaxation and is usually imaginal; flooding is ungraded, uses no relaxation and starts at the top of the hierarchy. Exposure and response prevention adds a second element: blocking the compulsion.

7.6 Cognitive therapy and cognitive behaviour therapy techniques

Cognitive therapies, developed by Aaron Beck from the 1960s and Albert Ellis from 1955, rest on the principle that interpretations of events shape feelings and actions. Cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT) combines these methods with behavioural ones. Beck called the stance collaborative empiricism: therapist and client test the client's beliefs together, like scientists.

Identifying automatic thoughts. These are quick evaluations that arise in a situation and feel simply true: "They think I'm stupid", "I'll never cope". Beneath them lie intermediate beliefs (rules such as "If I'm not perfect, I'm a failure") and core beliefs ("I am worthless"). The classic cue, at a moment of mood change, is "What was going through your mind just then?" Clients learn to spot thinking errors: all-or-nothing thinking, catastrophising, mind reading, overgeneralisation, personalisation and "should" statements.

Thought records are written forms with columns for the situation, emotion and intensity, the automatic thought and belief in it, evidence for, evidence against, a balanced alternative, and a re-rating of emotion. The aim is accurate, not positive, thinking; the balanced thought must be believable.

Socratic questioning replaces telling with asking: "What is the evidence for and against?", "Is there another explanation?", "What would you tell a friend?", "What is the worst, best and most realistic outcome?". This is guided discovery: a conclusion the client reaches is more convincing than one handed over. The downward arrow asks repeatedly "If that were true, what would it mean about you?" to reach the core belief.

Behavioural experiments test beliefs by action. The client states a prediction ("If I speak in the seminar, people will laugh"), rates belief in it, tries it and records what happened. Experiments often drop safety behaviours, small precautions (avoiding eye contact, rehearsing every sentence) that stop the person learning the feared outcome would not have happened anyway.

Disputing irrational beliefs. Ellis's rational emotive behaviour therapy uses the ABC model: an activating event (A) does not itself cause the emotional and behavioural consequences (C); the beliefs (B) about it do. Disturbance comes from rigid demands ("I must succeed") and their offshoots: awfulising, low frustration tolerance ("I can't stand it") and global self-rating ("I am a failure"). Therapy adds D, disputing, and E, an effective new belief. Disputes are empirical ("Where is the evidence you must succeed?"), logical ("Does wanting it mean you must have it?") and pragmatic ("Where does this belief get you?"). Ellis was famously forceful and set "shame-attacking" homework.

Trap. Beck treats beliefs as hypotheses tested collaboratively through questions and experiments; Ellis vigorously disputes irrational "musts". Beck speaks of automatic thoughts and schemas, Ellis of irrational beliefs and the ABC model.

7.7 The third wave: acceptance, mindfulness and dialectics

In 2004 Steven Hayes described a "third wave" of behavioural and cognitive therapy. The first wave changed behaviour; the second changed the content of thoughts; the third changes the person's relationship to thoughts and feelings through acceptance, mindfulness, values and dialectics.

Acceptance and commitment therapy

Acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT), developed by Hayes and colleagues, holds that suffering is magnified by experiential avoidance, the struggle to escape unwanted inner experiences, and cognitive fusion, taking thoughts as literal truths. It builds psychological flexibility through six processes: acceptance, cognitive defusion (seeing thoughts as thoughts), contact with the present moment, self-as-context (a stable observing self), values, and committed action. Techniques include prefacing a thought with "I'm having the thought that..." and the "passengers on the bus" metaphor, where thoughts are noisy passengers who can shout without taking the wheel. A cognitive therapist asks "Is this thought true?"; this therapist asks "Does holding on to it help you live the life you value?"

Dialectical behaviour therapy

Marsha Linehan developed dialectical behaviour therapy (DBT) for chronically suicidal women with borderline personality disorder, publishing her manuals in 1993. Pure change-oriented therapy left these clients feeling invalidated; pure acceptance left dangerous behaviour unchanged. The dialectic holds both: "You are doing the best you can, and you need to do better." Her biosocial theory sees the disorder as biological emotional vulnerability meeting an invalidating environment. Treatment has four modes: individual therapy, a skills group, telephone coaching and a therapists' consultation team. The group teaches mindfulness, distress tolerance, emotion regulation and interpersonal effectiveness. Individual work targets life-threatening behaviour first, then therapy-interfering behaviour, then behaviour damaging quality of life, using a daily diary card and chain analysis, a step-by-step reconstruction of the events, thoughts and feelings leading to a problem behaviour.

Mindfulness-based cognitive therapy

Zindel Segal, Mark Williams and John Teasdale set out mindfulness-based cognitive therapy in a 2002 book, combining Jon Kabat-Zinn's mindfulness-based stress reduction with cognitive therapy as an eight-week group course with daily practice (body scan, sitting meditation, a "three-minute breathing space"). It targets relapse in people recovered from recurrent depression, in whom a small dip in mood can reactivate old negative thinking and spiral into a new episode. Mindfulness teaches decentring, seeing thoughts as passing mental events, and a shift from a driven "doing" mode to an accepting "being" mode. Trials found it reduced relapse in people with three or more previous episodes, but not clearly in those with fewer.

Trap. Mindfulness-based cognitive therapy prevents relapse in people currently well after recurrent depression; it is not a first-line treatment for acute severe depression. Acceptance and commitment therapy does not dispute thoughts; it changes how the person relates to them.

7.8 Group, family and couples therapy

Group therapy treats several clients, often six to ten, with one or two therapists, from structured skills groups to interpersonal process groups where members' relationships with each other are the material. Irvin Yalom, in The Theory and Practice of Group Psychotherapy (first edition 1970), named the therapeutic factors through which groups help.

Yalom's therapeutic factorWhat it means
Instillation of hopeSeeing others improve shows change is possible.
UniversalityDiscovering others share one's "unique" shame.
Imparting informationTeaching and advice from therapist or members.
AltruismHelping others raises self-worth.
Corrective recapitulation of the primary family groupOld family patterns re-emerge and are reworked.
Development of socialising techniquesLearning social skills through feedback.
Imitative behaviourModelling oneself on the therapist or members.
Interpersonal learningLearning how one affects others; trying new ways of relating.
Group cohesivenessBelonging and acceptance; the group's alliance.
CatharsisExpressing strong feeling, joined to understanding.
Existential factorsFacing responsibility, mortality and the limits of others.

Family therapy treats the family as a system: a symptom in the "identified patient" is part of patterns in the whole, and causation is circular rather than linear. Salvador Minuchin's structural family therapy reshapes boundaries and hierarchy, for example between an enmeshed parent and adolescent; Murray Bowen stressed differentiation of self and mapped generations in a genogram. The strongest evidence concerns schizophrenia: high expressed emotion in relatives (critical comments, hostility, emotional over-involvement) predicts relapse, and family psychoeducation that lowers it reduces relapse. Couples therapy treats the relationship itself: behavioural couples therapy trains communication and problem-solving, while emotionally focused therapy works on attachment needs beneath cycles of blame and withdrawal.

7.9 Does it work? From Eysenck to meta-analysis

"Does therapy work?" hides two questions. Efficacy asks whether a treatment works under ideal research conditions: selected clients with one diagnosis, trained therapists following a manual, random allocation. Effectiveness asks whether it works in ordinary practice, with clients who have several problems and therapists of varied skill. Efficacy studies maximise internal validity (confidence the treatment caused the change); effectiveness studies maximise external validity (generalisability).

The main efficacy design is the randomised controlled trial: random assignment to the treatment or a comparison (waiting list, treatment as usual, a psychological placebo, or another therapy) spreads unknown differences evenly, so an outcome difference can be attributed to treatment. Assessors can be blind to group, but therapist and client cannot. On the effectiveness side, Martin Seligman's 1995 analysis of a Consumer Reports survey found readers reported substantial benefit, more with longer treatment, though it relied on retrospective self-report without a control group.

Eysenck's 1952 challenge

In 1952 Hans Eysenck published "The effects of psychotherapy: an evaluation". Pooling existing reports, he set improvement rates for neurotic patients treated by psychoanalysis (about 44 percent) and eclectic therapy (about 64 percent) against state hospital discharges and insurance disability claimants seen by general practitioners, of whom about 72 percent, roughly two-thirds, improved within two years. He concluded that psychotherapy had not been shown to beat spontaneous remission. Critics, notably Allen Bergin, showed the comparison to be flawed: the baseline groups differed in diagnosis and severity, improvement criteria varied, and psychoanalytic patients who left early were counted as failures. Reanalyses gave far lower spontaneous remission rates. Still, the paper forced the field to test its claims.

Smith and Glass: meta-analysis and effect size

In 1977 Mary Lee Smith and Gene Glass applied meta-analysis to 375 controlled studies: instead of counting wins and losses, each result is converted into a common metric, the effect size, and averaged. They found an average of about 0.68, with small differences between types of therapy; an expanded 1980 analysis of 475 studies found about 0.85. Eysenck called the 1977 study "an exercise in mega-silliness" for mixing good and poor studies; this "garbage in, garbage out" objection made later meta-analyses weigh study quality, but the central finding has held.

The effect size here, Cohen's d after Jacob Cohen, is the difference between treated and control averages divided by the standard deviation (the typical spread of scores): how many standard deviations apart the groups sit. Cohen's rough benchmarks are 0.2 small, 0.5 medium and 0.8 large. An effect of 0.68 means the average treated person is better off than about 75 percent of untreated people; 0.85 raises this to about 80 percent.

Case. Untreated clients average 30 on an anxiety scale with a standard deviation of 10; treated clients average 23. The difference of 7, divided by 10, gives an effect size of 0.7: the average treated client scores better than roughly three-quarters of untreated clients. This does not mean 75 percent of clients improved; it locates the average treated person within the untreated distribution.

The dose-effect relationship

In 1986 Kenneth Howard and colleagues pooled earlier outcome data and plotted percentage improved against sessions attended. The curve rose steeply, then flattened: roughly half of clients had improved measurably by about eight sessions and roughly three-quarters by about 26. This negatively accelerating curve is the dose-effect relationship; acute distress responded faster than long-standing personality problems. Howard's group later proposed a phase model (1993): remoralisation (restored hope, often within a few sessions), then remediation (symptom relief), then rehabilitation (changing long-standing patterns).

Trap. Efficacy is ideal-conditions evidence; effectiveness is real-world evidence. An effect size is not a percentage of people helped. Eysenck claimed improvement did not exceed spontaneous remission, not that therapy harmed.

7.10 Empirically supported treatments, harm and dropout

Which therapies work for which problems? In 1995 a task force of the American Psychological Association's clinical psychology division, chaired by Dianne Chambless, listed "empirically validated" treatments, later renamed empirically supported treatments, with criteria refined in later papers.

  • A well-established treatment needed at least two good between-group experiments, by different research teams, showing it superior to placebo or another treatment, or equivalent to an established one; or a large series of well-designed single-case experiments; with a treatment manual and a clearly described sample.
  • A probably efficacious treatment needed, for example, two experiments showing superiority to a waiting list, or strong experiments from only one team.

The lists shaped training and guidelines and were dominated by cognitive behavioural treatments. Critics said they favoured short, manualised therapies easy to trial, treated diagnosis as the unit of treatment, and ignored relationship and therapist. The American Psychological Association's 2006 policy on evidence-based practice in psychology broadened the frame: best available research integrated with clinical expertise, in the context of the client's characteristics, culture and preferences.

Deterioration and harm. Bergin named the deterioration effect in the 1960s, noticing that some treated clients got worse. Commonly cited estimates associated with Lambert put deterioration at roughly 5 to 10 percent of adult clients. Harm comes from poor therapy (ruptured alliance, boundary violations, missed risk) or harmful treatments. Scott Lilienfeld (2007) listed potentially harmful therapies, including "Scared Straight" programmes for young offenders (linked to more, not less, offending), critical incident stress debriefing straight after trauma, and "recovered memory" techniques that can create false memories. Routine outcome monitoring, with a brief measure every session and alerts when a client is not on track, has been shown to reduce deterioration.

Dropout means leaving before goals are reached, usually without a planned ending. Estimates depend on definition: a widely cited 1993 meta-analysis found about 47 percent; a larger 2012 meta-analysis with stricter definitions found about 20 percent. Younger clients, clients with personality disorders and those seen by trainees drop out more; clear explanation, agreed goals and early attention to expectations reduce it. Some dropouts simply feel better already, as the dose-effect curve predicts.

7.11 Psychotherapy in India

Textbook psychotherapy grew up in Europe and North America and assumes an individualistic client, comfortable confiding in a stranger and aiming at autonomy.

Family involvement. In India the family is usually inside the treatment: relatives bring the client, attend the first interview, hold important history and share decisions about treatment, marriage and work. An early example is Vidya Sagar's work at the Amritsar Mental Hospital in the 1950s, where families stayed with patients in tents on the hospital grounds and joined in their care. Family involvement brings resources (support, help with homework, a wider view) and confidentiality dilemmas, especially with young adults.

Directive expectations. Many Indian clients expect an authority who diagnoses and advises, and a purely non-directive stance can seem evasive. The psychiatrist J. S. Neki, in a 1973 paper, proposed the guru-chela relationship as a therapeutic paradigm. Western therapy, he argued, treats dependence as something to resolve quickly in pursuit of autonomy, whereas Indian tradition accepts and values dependence on a guide. The guru is warm but authoritative, guides actively, and leads the disciple through trust towards eventual self-realisation. Neki suggested Indian therapists use a more directive, dependent early relationship as a route to growth rather than resisting it.

Cultural adaptation changes delivery while keeping the active ingredients: the client's language and idioms of distress (many describe psychological suffering through the body, as "tension", weakness or pain); family involvement in formulation and homework; stories and philosophical ideas the client already values; respect for spiritual or karmic explanations alongside a psychological one; and shorter courses where travel and cost demand it. Given the shortage of professionals, task-sharing has grown: in the Healthy Activity Program in Goa, developed by Vikram Patel and colleagues, lay counsellors delivered brief behavioural activation-based treatment for depression that outperformed enhanced usual care in a randomised controlled trial.

Yoga and meditation are indigenous to the subcontinent, and mindfulness itself comes from Buddhist meditation. Indian centres, notably the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences in Bengaluru, have researched yoga as an add-on to standard treatment for conditions including depression and schizophrenia. Clinicians integrate breathing, relaxation and meditation as complements to evidence-based care, not replacements. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 recognises clinical psychologists with qualifications recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India as mental health professionals.

7.12 A case: one client across a course of cognitive behaviour therapy

This composite case shows the chapter's pieces working together over fourteen sessions.

Sessions 1 and 2: assessment, formulation and goals

Karan, 23, is brought by his mother three months after failing, for the second time, to win a postgraduate seat. He sleeps until noon, has stopped studying and seeing friends, and feels worthless. He admits passing thoughts that his family "would be better off" without him; the therapist asks directly, finds no plan or intent, and agrees a safety plan. His score of 19 on the nine-item Patient Health Questionnaire indicates moderately severe depression. The therapist explains confidentiality and how the therapy works, and, with Karan's consent, invites his mother in for the last ten minutes. In session 2 they build a formulation: praise in childhood came mainly for marks, and Karan formed the rule "If I don't succeed, I'm worthless" over a core belief "I am a failure". The rejection activated it; low mood led to withdrawal, withdrawal to no achievement, which seemed to prove the belief. Goals: study three hours a day, five days a week; meet a friend weekly; bring his score below 10. Homework: an hourly activity and mood diary.

Sessions 3 and 4: behavioural activation

The diary shows mood lowest when lying in bed scrolling his phone and highest on an evening walk and when a cousin visited. They schedule a morning walk, one hour of study and one call to a friend, building up gradually; his mother agrees to notice effort rather than mention the exam. By session 4 his score is 15: early remoralisation.

Sessions 5 to 7: automatic thoughts and Socratic questioning

Sitting down to study, Karan catches the thought "What's the point, I'll fail again", believed at 90 percent. On a thought record he lists evidence for (two failures) and against (he missed the cut-off narrowly, his score rose between attempts, he cleared another entrance). Asked what he would tell a friend, he says bad luck plus a study plan that needed changing. His balanced thought, "I've fallen short twice by small margins, which says my preparation needs changing, not that I'm incapable", cuts belief in the original to 50 percent. He names his errors: all-or-nothing thinking and fortune-telling. By session 7 his score is 11.

Sessions 8 to 10: experiments, the core belief and a rupture

Karan predicts that friends told about the second failure will look down on him (belief 80 percent). He tells two; one describes his own failed attempt and they form a study group. Belief falls to 20 percent. In session 9 he says the forms "feel like school"; the therapist acknowledges this and switches to spoken thought records, repairing the rupture. The downward arrow reaches "I am a failure", and Karan notices he would never apply that label to his cousin for the same result.

Sessions 11 to 14: relapse prevention and ending

Sessions move to fortnightly. Karan writes a plan of what helped, his warning signs (sleeping late, skipping walks, "what's the point" thoughts) and what he will do if they return; his mother joins part of session 13 to learn the signs. At session 14 his score is 6, he studies daily, meets friends and has registered for the next entrance. Boosters are set for one and three months. Common factors (alliance, hope, a believable rationale, family support) and specific techniques (activation, thought records, Socratic questioning, experiments) worked together, as the contextual and phase models predict.

Recap.

Psychotherapy is a planned, collaborative, theory-based relationship that moves through assessment, goal setting, a working phase and termination, with homework carrying change into daily life. The common factors tradition, from Rosenzweig's Dodo through Frank and Wampold, locates much of therapy's power in what all therapies share, above all Bordin's alliance of goals, tasks and bond. Each school still has distinctive practices: interpretation of transference and resistance, Rogers' core conditions, exposure and reinforcement, thought records and experiments, and third-wave acceptance and mindfulness. Groups add Yalom's therapeutic factors; family work treats the system. Eysenck's 1952 challenge was answered by Smith and Glass's 1977 meta-analysis, and Howard's dose-effect curve showed most improvement arriving early. Therapy can also harm, and clients drop out. In India, family involvement, directive expectations and Neki's guru-chela model shape delivery.

NameWhat they gave us
