Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Define psychotherapy and describe the phases of a course of therapy, explaining the role of client expectations and homework.
  2. Critically discuss the common factors position with reference to Rosenzweig, Frank, Wampold, Lambert and Bordin's alliance. Is the Dodo bird verdict justified?
  3. Compare the techniques of psychodynamic, client-centred and cognitive behavioural therapy, with a clinical example of each.
  4. Describe the behavioural techniques used for anxiety disorders and obsessive-compulsive disorder, and explain why they work.
  5. Trace the outcome debate from Eysenck (1952) to Smith and Glass (1977), explaining effect size, the dose-effect relationship and efficacy versus effectiveness.
  6. How should psychotherapy be adapted for Indian clients? Discuss family involvement, directive expectations and Neki's guru-chela paradigm.

8 Ethics, law and the profession

Every other chapter of this book is about what a clinical psychologist knows and does: how to assess, how to diagnose, how to treat, how to think about well-being. This chapter is about the rules that govern the doing. It covers the ethics codes that the profession writes for itself (above all the American Psychological Association's code, which both clinical textbooks, Pomerantz and Trull and Phares, use as their frame), the core ethical areas of competence, informed consent, confidentiality and boundaries, a stepwise method for reasoning through a dilemma, the special ethics of testing and research, the Indian laws that bind a clinical psychologist practising in India, and the professional debates about training, licensing, evidence-based practice, prescribing, online therapy and burnout.

It matters for two reasons. The first is practical: ethical and legal mistakes are the commonest way a competent clinician harms a client, loses a registration or ends up in court, and most of them are made not by bad people but by good people who never learned to see the dilemma coming. The second is academic: ethics questions appear on every entrance and university paper, and examiners like them because they reward precise knowledge (which principle, which standard, which section of which Act) and punish vague good intentions. Read this chapter as a set of tools you will actually use on your first day in a clinic.

8.1 Why ethics codes exist, and the shape of the American Psychological Association code

A profession is an occupation that society allows to regulate itself. Society grants psychologists a privileged position: people tell them things they tell no one else, allow them to test their minds, and sometimes accept their judgement on questions of liberty, custody or employment. In exchange, the profession promises to police its members. An ethics code is the written form of that promise. It does three jobs at once. It protects the public by stating what clients are entitled to expect. It guides practitioners by turning vague values into concrete rules for recurring situations. And it protects the profession itself, because a discipline that disciplines its own members is less likely to have rules imposed on it from outside.

The American Psychological Association adopted its first code in 1953, and that code was unusual because it was built empirically: members were asked to send in real ethical incidents they had faced, and the rules were written around those incidents rather than deduced from philosophy. The version in force today is the Ethical Principles of Psychologists and Code of Conduct, adopted in 2002 and amended in 2010 and 2016. Both amendments grew out of the controversy over psychologists' involvement in the interrogation of detainees by the United States military. The 2010 amendment changed the standards on conflicts between ethics and law and between ethics and an employer's demands, so that a psychologist can no longer use a law or an order to justify violating basic human rights. The 2016 amendment added to the standard on avoiding harm an explicit statement that psychologists do not participate in torture or in cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment.

General principles versus enforceable standards

The code has two layers, and confusing them is a classic examination error. The first layer is the five General Principles. These are aspirational: they describe the ideals the profession aims at, and they are explicitly not enforceable rules. No psychologist is disciplined for "violating Principle C". The second layer is the Ethical Standards, which are enforceable rules of conduct; a complaint to the Association or to a licensing board is judged against them.

General PrincipleWhat it asks of the psychologist
A. Beneficence and NonmaleficenceStrive to benefit those you work with and take care to do no harm; guard against your own personal, financial, social or political interests leading to misuse of your influence, and stay aware of how your own physical and mental health affects your work.
B. Fidelity and ResponsibilityEstablish relationships of trust, uphold professional standards, accept responsibility for your behaviour, manage conflicts of interest, and care about the ethical conduct of colleagues.
C. IntegrityPromote accuracy, honesty and truthfulness; do not steal, cheat, commit fraud or misrepresent facts. Where deception is ever justified (as in some research), weigh its necessity and repair its effects.
D. JusticeRecognise that all people are entitled to access and benefit from psychology, and take care that your biases or the limits of your competence do not lead to unjust practice.
E. Respect for People's Rights and DignityRespect privacy, confidentiality and self-determination; take special care to protect people whose capacity for autonomous decisions is impaired; respect cultural, individual and role differences, including those of age, gender, gender identity, race, ethnicity, culture, national origin, religion, sexual orientation, disability, language and socioeconomic status.

If these five look familiar, it is because they echo the four principles of biomedical ethics that Tom Beauchamp and James Childress set out in Principles of Biomedical Ethics (first edition 1979): respect for autonomy, beneficence, nonmaleficence and justice. Karen Kitchener, in a 1984 paper, adapted this framework for psychology and added fidelity (keeping promises and being trustworthy), and her version shaped how the profession teaches ethical reasoning.

The ten standard areas

The enforceable standards are grouped into ten numbered sections: (1) Resolving Ethical Issues; (2) Competence; (3) Human Relations; (4) Privacy and Confidentiality; (5) Advertising and Other Public Statements; (6) Record Keeping and Fees; (7) Education and Training; (8) Research and Publication; (9) Assessment; and (10) Therapy. Individual standards are cited by number, so that "Standard 3.05" is the rule on multiple relationships and "Standard 10.05" forbids sexual intimacy with current therapy clients. You do not need to memorise every number, but knowing the ten headings lets you place any dilemma quickly: a question about test manuals is a Section 9 question, a question about a friend's request for free sessions is a Section 3 question.

Trap. The General Principles are aspirational and unenforceable; the Ethical Standards are enforceable. A question that asks "which part of the code forms the basis for sanctions?" wants the Standards. Also remember that ethics and law are different things: a behaviour can be legal but unethical (a romantic relationship with a client's adult sibling may break no law), or ethically required yet in tension with law. The code asks the psychologist to follow the law but, where law conflicts with ethics, to make the conflict known and try to resolve it consistently with the code.

8.2 Competence: knowing the edge of your skill

Competence is the most basic ethical duty because every other duty depends on it. A therapist who keeps perfect confidentiality but uses a technique they do not understand still harms the client. The code's boundaries of competence standard (2.01) says psychologists provide services only within the limits of their education, training, supervised experience, consultation, study or professional experience. Competence is therefore not a single licence to "do psychology"; it is specific to populations, problems and methods. A psychologist trained with adults is not automatically competent with young children; one trained in cognitive behaviour therapy is not automatically competent to run neuropsychological assessment or family therapy.

The standard includes two important nuances. First, competence includes cultural competence: where an understanding of factors such as age, gender, ethnicity, language, disability or socioeconomic status is essential to effective work, the psychologist must have or obtain that understanding, or refer. In India this has direct meaning: a clinician from an urban English-medium background working with a rural Bhojpuri-speaking family needs to understand how that family explains illness, who makes decisions, and what words carry stigma. Second, there are emergency exceptions: when no competent provider is available and a person urgently needs help, a psychologist may provide services beyond their usual competence to ensure the person is not denied care, but only until the emergency ends or appropriate services become available.

A psychologist extending into a new area must do it properly, through relevant education, training, supervised experience and consultation. Competence also decays, so the code requires maintaining competence (2.03) through ongoing effort. This is why licensing bodies require continuing education: in the United States, most state boards require a set number of continuing education hours per renewal period, and in India the Rehabilitation Council of India ties the renewal of registration to continuing rehabilitation education credits (see section 8.8).

Impairment and self-care

Competence can be lost not only through ignorance but through the psychologist's own condition. Standard 2.06, on personal problems and conflicts, asks psychologists not to begin an activity when they know or should know that their personal problems are likely to prevent them from performing competently, and, when they become aware of such problems, to take appropriate measures such as obtaining professional consultation or help, and deciding whether to limit, suspend or end their work. Impairment is the technical word for this: a decline in functioning, from depression, substance misuse, grief, illness or burnout, severe enough to affect work. Impairment is not the same as distress. Every clinician has bad weeks; the ethical question is whether the distress has begun to reduce the quality of care, and whether the clinician is honest enough to notice.

Self-care is therefore an ethical duty, not a luxury. It includes supervision and peer consultation, one's own personal therapy, reasonable caseloads, and a life outside work. The idea of the "wounded healer" (that some people enter the helping professions partly because of their own suffering) is useful here: personal experience can deepen empathy, but unexamined wounds can also lead a therapist to over-identify with clients, rescue them, or use them to meet their own needs.

Case. A newly qualified clinical psychologist in a district hospital is asked by the medical superintendent to assess a nine-year-old for a suspected specific learning disability, because the usual child psychologist is on leave. Her training was mainly with adults and she has administered the relevant tests only twice under supervision. The competence standard does not require her to refuse outright, but it asks what she can honestly do. Reasonable options are to arrange telephone supervision with an experienced child psychologist, to do the assessment under that supervision, or to schedule the child for the returning colleague if the matter is not urgent. What she should not do is administer and interpret the battery alone and sign a certificate that may decide the child's school provisions.

8.3 Informed consent: permission that means something

Informed consent is the process by which a person, having understood what is proposed, freely agrees to it. It rests on Principle E, respect for autonomy. The idea came into psychology from medicine and research law: after the Second World War, the Nuremberg Code of 1947 declared the voluntary consent of the human subject "absolutely essential" in research, and medical law gradually established that treating a competent adult without consent is a wrong in itself, however well-intentioned. Informed consent has three essential elements: the person must have adequate information, must have the capacity to understand and decide, and must decide voluntarily, free of coercion or undue influence.

What consent to therapy must include

The code (Standard 10.01, building on the general consent standard 3.10) asks psychologists to inform clients as early as feasible about the nature and anticipated course of therapy, fees, the involvement of third parties, and the limits of confidentiality, and to give sufficient opportunity to ask questions. When a treatment is new or its techniques are not well established, the client must be told this, told about the potential risks, told about alternative treatments, and told that participation is voluntary. When the therapist is a trainee, the client must be told so and given the supervisor's name. In practice a good consent conversation covers:

  • What the therapy involves, roughly how long it will last, and what the client will be asked to do.
  • The likely benefits and the possible risks, including the fact that therapy can temporarily make people feel worse.
  • Alternatives, including medication, other therapies and no treatment.
  • Fees, cancellation policy and how sessions are arranged.
  • Confidentiality and each of its limits, stated plainly before the client discloses anything.
  • The right to ask questions and to withdraw at any time without penalty.

Consent is a process, not a signature. A signed form is evidence that a conversation happened, not proof that the client understood. Consent must be revisited when the plan changes, for example when a therapist proposes exposure work that will be distressing, or wants to record sessions for supervision.

Capacity

Capacity is the ability to make a particular decision at a particular time. Paul Appelbaum and Thomas Grisso, in their influential 1988 work, described four abilities that together make up decisional capacity: the ability to communicate a choice, to understand the relevant information, to appreciate how that information applies to one's own situation, and to reason about the options. Their framework underlies the MacArthur Competence Assessment Tool for Treatment. Three features of capacity are often misunderstood. It is decision-specific: a person with dementia may lack capacity to manage investments but retain capacity to consent to a counselling session. It is time-specific: capacity may be lost during an acute manic episode and regained a week later. And a diagnosis alone never removes capacity: having schizophrenia does not mean a person cannot decide. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 builds on exactly this logic, as section 8.9 explains.

Children and adolescents: consent and assent

Minors cannot usually give legal consent; in India the age of majority is 18. A parent or legal guardian therefore gives consent. But ethics asks for more than the guardian's signature. The code asks psychologists working with people who are legally incapable of consent to provide an appropriate explanation, seek the person's assent (their affirmative agreement, at their level of understanding), consider their preferences and best interests, and obtain permission from a legally authorised person. Assent treats the child as a participant rather than an object: a seven-year-old can be told in simple words what will happen and asked whether that is all right; a fifteen-year-old can take part in setting goals. Adolescents raise a special tension around confidentiality. Many will not speak freely if they think everything goes to their parents, yet parents hold the legal right to consent. The common solution is an explicit agreement at the start, with parent and adolescent together, that the content of sessions stays private except where there is serious risk of harm, in which case parents will be told. Some jurisdictions go further: English law, after the Gillick case in 1985, allows a child under 16 to consent to treatment if they have sufficient understanding and intelligence to understand fully what is proposed; this is known as Gillick competence.

Consent in research

Research consent is stricter than therapy consent, because the research participant is not seeking help and the research may offer them no benefit. The code's research consent standard (8.02) requires informing participants about the purpose, expected duration and procedures; their right to decline and to withdraw once participation has begun; the foreseeable consequences of declining or withdrawing; factors that might influence their willingness, such as risks, discomfort or adverse effects; any prospective benefits; the limits of confidentiality; incentives for participation; and whom to contact with questions. Consent must also be free of coercion, which matters when the researcher has power over the participant: a professor recruiting his own students, or a hospital psychologist recruiting inpatients who may fear that refusing will affect their care, must build in genuine freedom to say no, for example an equally attractive alternative to earning course credit.

Key. Valid consent needs information, capacity and voluntariness. Capacity is decision-specific and time-specific, and is never removed by diagnosis alone. Children give assent; guardians give consent.

8.4 Confidentiality, privilege and their limits

Confidentiality is the professional and ethical duty not to disclose what a client reveals in the course of the professional relationship, except with the client's consent or where law or ethics requires or permits it. It is the foundation of therapy: people disclose shameful memories, illegal acts, suicidal thoughts and fantasies only if they trust that those disclosures will go no further. The code's Section 4 asks psychologists to protect confidential information, to discuss the limits of confidentiality at the outset (Standard 4.02), to record and disclose only what is necessary for the purpose, and to discuss confidential information with colleagues only for professional purposes and without identifying the client where possible.

Privilege is not the same thing

Privilege (often called testimonial privilege) is a legal concept. It is the client's right to prevent their therapist from being compelled to disclose confidential communications in legal proceedings, such as testifying in court. Three differences separate it from confidentiality. Confidentiality is an ethical duty of the professional, applying everywhere; privilege is a rule of evidence, applying only in legal proceedings. Confidentiality comes from professional codes; privilege exists only if a statute or court creates it. And privilege belongs to the client, not the therapist, so it is the client who may waive it, for example by bringing a lawsuit in which their own mental state is at issue. In the United States, the Supreme Court recognised a psychotherapist and patient privilege in federal courts in Jaffee v. Redmond (1996). Indian evidence law has traditionally recognised a statutory privilege for communications with a legal adviser but no comparable one for doctors or therapists, so an Indian court can order a psychologist to produce records or testify. The ethical response to such an order is to disclose only what is required, and where possible to ask the court to limit the disclosure.

The duty to warn and protect: Tarasoff

The most famous limit to confidentiality comes from a Californian case. In 1969 Prosenjit Poddar, a graduate student from India at the University of California, Berkeley, told his psychologist at the university health service, Lawrence Moore, that he intended to kill a young woman, readily identifiable as Tatiana Tarasoff, who had rejected him. Moore judged Poddar dangerous and asked the campus police to detain him; they questioned him, found him rational and released him after he promised to stay away from her. Moore's supervising psychiatrist then directed that no further action be taken. No one warned Tatiana or her family. Two months later Poddar killed her. Her parents sued the university and the clinicians.

In Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California, the California Supreme Court ruled first in 1974 that the therapists had a duty to warn the intended victim. In an unusual step, the court reheard the case, and in 1976 it restated the obligation more broadly as a duty to protect: when a therapist determines, or by the standards of the profession should determine, that a patient presents a serious danger of violence to another, the therapist must use reasonable care to protect the intended victim. Warning the victim is one way; others include informing the police, arranging hospitalisation or intensifying treatment. The court's most quoted line is that "the protective privilege ends where the public peril begins."

After Tarasoff, American states went different ways. Some made the duty mandatory, some made disclosure merely permissive (the therapist may break confidentiality without being sued for it, but is not required to), and a few declined to adopt it. Typical triggering conditions are a serious threat of physical violence, against a reasonably identifiable victim, communicated to the therapist. The lesson for any clinician, in any country, is not a particular legal rule but a sequence: assess the risk carefully, consult, document the reasoning, choose the least intrusive action that genuinely protects, and tell the client what you are doing wherever that is safe. India has no statute laying down a Tarasoff duty in these terms. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017, however, creates a right to confidentiality for persons with mental illness whose listed exceptions include disclosure where necessary to protect another person from harm or violence, so an Indian clinician who acts to protect an identifiable person at serious risk is acting within the law's own logic.

Trap. Tarasoff was decided twice. The 1974 ruling created the duty to warn; the 1976 rehearing broadened it into the duty to protect, which can be discharged in ways other than warning. Also note that the clinician at the centre was a psychologist, not a psychiatrist.

Child abuse, risk to self, and other limits

The second major limit is mandated reporting of child abuse. In most jurisdictions, including every American state, professionals who reasonably suspect that a child is being abused or neglected must report it to child protection authorities; they need not be certain, and they are not the investigators. In India the key statute is the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012 (POCSO), which imposes a reporting duty for sexual offences against children (section 8.9 covers it). The third limit is serious risk of harm to self. When a client is at imminent risk of suicide, most ethics codes and laws permit the clinician to disclose what is necessary to protect the client's life, for example to family members who can supervise, or to emergency services. The fourth set of limits includes a court order, a client's written consent to release, and in some settings the need to share information within a treatment team. Every one of these limits must be explained to the client at the start, in plain language, so that the client decides what to disclose knowing the rules.

Record keeping

Records serve the client (continuity of care), the clinician (memory and protection if challenged) and the law. Standard 6.01 asks psychologists to create and maintain records to facilitate later services, to allow replication of research, to meet institutional requirements, to ensure accuracy of billing and to comply with law. A good clinical record includes identifying information, the consent obtained, assessment findings, diagnosis, the treatment plan, a dated note of each contact, risk assessments and the reasoning behind any decision to break confidentiality. Records must be stored securely, whether on paper or electronically, and disposed of securely. The Association's record keeping guidelines suggest, in the absence of a stricter legal rule, keeping full records for seven years after the last service for an adult, and for a minor until three years after they reach adulthood if that is later. The clinical maxim is blunt: if it is not documented, from a legal point of view it did not happen.

Key. Confidentiality is the clinician's ethical duty; privilege is the client's legal right in court. The limits (danger to others, child abuse, danger to self, court orders) must be explained before disclosure, not after.

8.5 Multiple relationships and boundaries

A multiple relationship (older texts say dual relationship) exists when a psychologist is in a professional role with a person and at the same time is in another role with that person, or with someone closely associated with them, or promises to enter one in future. Standard 3.05 says a psychologist refrains from a multiple relationship if it could reasonably be expected to impair their objectivity, competence or effectiveness, or risks exploitation or harm to the person. Crucially, the same standard states that multiple relationships which would not reasonably be expected to cause impairment or risk exploitation or harm are not unethical. The code does not forbid every overlap; it forbids the risky ones.

Why are overlapping roles dangerous? Therapy is an unequal relationship. The client reveals vulnerabilities; the therapist does not. The client may idealise the therapist, depend on them, or feel unable to refuse them. When a second relationship is added, such as business partner, employer, friend, landlord or lover, the power the therapist holds in the first role leaks into the second, and the client can no longer freely say no in either. The therapist's judgement also suffers: it is hard to confront a client's avoidance when that client is also your tenant.

Boundary crossings versus boundary violations

Thomas Gutheil and Glen Gabbard, in a 1993 paper, drew the distinction that now organises the field. Boundaries are the edges of the professional role: where and when sessions happen, how long they last, what is paid, what is touched, what is self-disclosed. A boundary crossing is a departure from the usual role that is harmless, sometimes helpful, and usually done openly for the client's benefit: accepting a small gift, a brief supportive touch on the shoulder at a funeral, conducting a session in the client's home for an agoraphobic client doing exposure. A boundary violation is a departure that harms or exploits the client, typically serving the therapist's needs: sexual contact, borrowing money, recruiting the client into the therapist's business, extensive self-disclosure that turns the session towards the therapist. The same outward act can be either, depending on context, intention and effect.

The worry about crossings is the slippery slope: violations rarely start dramatically, and case histories of therapist sexual misconduct often show a sequence of small crossings (longer sessions, first names, meetings outside the office, personal confidences) that gradually erased the frame. Against this, Arnold Lazarus argued that rigid, defensive boundaries can themselves be harmful, making therapy cold and depriving clients of helpful human contact. The balanced position is that crossings should be rare, deliberate, clinically justified, discussed with the client where appropriate, and documented, and that the therapist should ask whom the crossing serves.

Sexual relationships with clients

Here there is no grey zone. Standard 10.05 prohibits sexual intimacy with current therapy clients. Standard 10.06 prohibits it with people the psychologist knows to be close relatives, guardians or significant others of current clients. Standard 10.07 forbids accepting as a client someone with whom the psychologist has had sexual intimacy. And Standard 10.08 prohibits sexual intimacy with former clients for at least two years after therapy ends, and even after two years permits it only in the most unusual circumstances, placing the burden on the psychologist to show there is no exploitation, considering factors such as the nature and length of therapy, the client's history and mental state, and whether the therapist ever suggested such a future relationship during therapy. Kenneth Pope's research on therapist and patient sexual contact documented how severely such relationships harm clients, with effects including guilt, mistrust, emptiness and increased suicide risk, which is why the prohibition is absolute.

Gifts, and practice in small communities

Gifts are a classic crossing. A box of sweets at Diwali or a card at the end of therapy is usually a culturally ordinary expression of gratitude, and refusing it may insult the client and damage the relationship. An expensive watch, cash, or repeated gifts are different: they may create obligation, reflect a client's attempt to buy affection, or signal a boundary problem worth exploring in therapy. Useful questions are the monetary value, the timing (a small gift at termination differs from one in the first week), the client's motive, the cultural meaning, and whether the therapist would be comfortable having a colleague know.

In small communities (a village, a hill town, a military base, a religious congregation, a university campus, or a close-knit minority community) multiple relationships are often unavoidable. The psychologist's children may attend school with the client's children; the client may be the only pharmacist in town. The code accepts this reality. The expected response is not to refuse all such clients, which would deny them care, but to discuss the overlap openly at the start, agree how to handle chance meetings (for example, the therapist will not greet the client in public unless the client greets first), keep the extra-therapy contact minimal, consult colleagues, and document.

8.6 A stepwise model for ethical decision-making, with worked dilemmas

Codes cannot anticipate every situation, and real dilemmas usually involve two good principles in conflict: confidentiality against protection, autonomy against beneficence. What a clinician needs is a reliable procedure. Karen Kitchener's 1984 model distinguished two levels of ethical reasoning. The intuitive level is our immediate moral sense, shaped by upbringing and experience; it works well in ordinary situations but can mislead under pressure. The critical-evaluative level is deliberate reasoning from rules, principles and theory, used when intuition is unclear or when principles clash. Stepwise models, such as the one taught by Gerald Koocher and Patricia Keith-Spiegel in their text Ethics in Psychology, turn the critical-evaluative level into a checklist. The following composite version is the one to learn and apply.

  1. Identify the problem. State in one sentence what the dilemma is and why it is a dilemma: which principles or duties pull in different directions.
  2. Identify the stakeholders. List everyone affected: the client, third parties, family, the institution, the profession, and yourself.
  3. Identify the relevant rules. Check the ethics code standards, the law of your jurisdiction, and institutional policy. Some dilemmas end here, because the law settles the question.
  4. Examine yourself. Ask what feelings, biases or interests (fear of being sued, liking or disliking the client, financial pressure) might be shaping your judgement.
  5. Consult. Talk to a supervisor or experienced colleague, and where necessary seek legal advice, protecting the client's identity.
  6. Generate options. List every realistic course of action, including the ones you dislike.
  7. Evaluate the options against the principles and against their likely consequences for each stakeholder, and prefer the option that honours the most important duties with the least intrusion.
  8. Act, document and review. Carry out the decision, tell the client what you are doing wherever safe, record the reasoning, and afterwards evaluate the outcome and what you would do differently.
Case 1: a threat of violence. Ravi, a 34-year-old man in weekly therapy for anger problems, arrives agitated after his business partner cheated him out of his share. He says, "I have bought a knife. Tomorrow I will finish him at the shop." Problem: confidentiality and the therapeutic alliance versus the life of an identifiable person. Stakeholders: Ravi, the partner, Ravi's family, the therapist. Rules: the therapist explained at intake that confidentiality ends where someone is at serious risk; the Tarasoff logic, the Mental Healthcare Act's exception for protecting a person from harm or violence, and Principle A all point towards protection. Self-examination: the therapist notices she likes Ravi and wants to believe he is only venting; she must not let that decide. Consultation: she calls her supervisor during the session break. Options: do nothing; explore the threat further; ask Ravi to hand over the knife and agree a safety plan; arrange an urgent psychiatric review or admission; inform the partner; inform the police. Evaluation: a specific victim, a stated means, a time frame and a history of anger make this a serious threat, so doing nothing is ruled out. She first explores it with Ravi; he repeats the intent. She tells him she is obliged to act and involves his wife and the duty psychiatrist, and when he refuses a safety plan she informs the police and the partner, disclosing only what is necessary. Documentation and review: she records the exact words, her risk assessment, the consultation and each action, and continues therapy with Ravi afterwards if he returns, working through the rupture.
Case 2: an adolescent's disclosure and the law. Meera, 16, seen at a school counselling service for low mood, mentions that she and her 17-year-old boyfriend have been sexually active, and begs that no one be told. Problem: her confidentiality, autonomy and trust versus a legal reporting duty. Stakeholders: Meera, the boyfriend (also a minor), her parents, the school, the psychologist. Rules: this is where the dilemma is largely settled by law. Under the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012 anyone under 18 is a child, consent does not make a sexual act lawful, and section 19 requires anyone with knowledge that such an offence has been committed to report it; section 21 makes failure to report an offence. Several Indian High Courts have voiced concern about criminalising consensual adolescent relationships, but the statutory duty remains. Self-examination: the psychologist feels the law is harsh here, and she must separate that view from her obligation. Consultation: her supervisor and the school's child protection lead. Options: say nothing (unlawful); report without telling Meera (legal but damaging to trust); tell Meera honestly, explain what will happen, involve her parents supportively, report, and stay alongside her through the process. Evaluation and action: the last option meets the legal duty while honouring respect and beneficence as far as possible. Review: the psychologist realises her intake talk had not mentioned this specific limit to adolescents, and she revises it so that future students know the rule before they disclose.
Case 3: a small town and a gift. Shalini is the only clinical psychologist in a hill town. Mr Negi, whom she has treated for depression, is in his final session; he runs the town's only hardware shop, which she also uses, and he presents her with an expensive silk shawl and an offer of "lifetime discount". Problem: gratitude and cultural norms versus a multiple relationship that could create obligation. Stakeholders: Mr Negi, Shalini, and future clients who hear about it. Rules: Standard 3.05 forbids only multiple relationships likely to impair objectivity or risk exploitation; small gifts are ordinarily acceptable crossings; the code accepts that overlap is unavoidable in small communities. Self-examination: she wants to avoid offending him, and a discount is attractive. Consultation: a colleague by telephone. Options: accept both; refuse both; accept a token and decline the rest. Evaluation: the shawl's value and the ongoing discount would create a continuing financial tie with a former client who might return to therapy, while a flat refusal might feel like rejection at the moment of ending. Action: she thanks him warmly, explains that professional rules do not let her accept costly gifts or discounts, accepts a small box of sweets he also brought, and agrees that she will continue to pay the ordinary price at his shop. Documentation and review: she notes the exchange and her reasoning.
Key. Problem, stakeholders, rules, self, consult, options, evaluate, act and document. If the law settles the question at the rules step, the remaining steps decide not whether to comply but how to comply humanely.

8.7 Ethics in assessment and research

Assessment

Psychological tests are powerful because they are standardised and because the person taking them does not know the items in advance. Both properties create ethical duties. Test security (Standard 9.11) requires psychologists to make reasonable efforts to maintain the integrity and security of test materials: manuals, instruments, protocols and stimuli such as the Rorschach inkblots or intelligence test items. If items circulate publicly, people can rehearse answers and the test loses its validity for everyone. The code distinguishes these from test data (Standard 9.04), meaning the client's raw scores, responses and the psychologist's notes about them. Test data are ordinarily released to the client or to someone the client names, on request, unless release would cause substantial harm or misuse, whereas test materials are protected. When a lawyer demands "the whole file including the test", the psychologist releases the data as the law requires but asks that materials go only to another qualified psychologist or under a protective court order.

The second duty is fairness. Psychologists must use instruments whose reliability and validity have been established for members of the population being tested (Standard 9.02), and must interpret results taking account of factors such as the person's language and culture that might affect the scores (Standard 9.06). They must not base decisions on obsolete tests (Standard 9.08). In India this is a daily problem, because many instruments were developed and normed in Western populations. A translation is not an adaptation: items about snowfall or baseball, or vocabulary that is common in one language and rare in another, change what the test measures. This is why Indian adaptations and norms matter, for example the Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children (an Indian adaptation of the Wechsler scale for children), the Bhatia Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence, and the Binet-Kamat Test. Finally, clients are entitled to informed consent before assessment and to an explanation of results in language they understand (Standard 9.10).

Research

Research ethics grew out of scandal. The Nuremberg Code of 1947 answered the experiments of Nazi doctors. The World Medical Association's Declaration of Helsinki (1964) extended the principles to medical research. In the United States, the Tuskegee study (1932 to 1972), in which Black men with syphilis were left untreated to observe the disease, led to the Belmont Report of 1979, which named three principles: respect for persons (autonomy and consent), beneficence (maximise benefit, minimise harm) and justice (fair distribution of research burdens and benefits). In psychology the defining controversy was Stanley Milgram's obedience experiments, published in 1963, in which participants believed they were giving painful electric shocks; Diana Baumrind's 1964 critique argued that the stress inflicted and the deception used were not justified.

Today, proposals must be approved in advance by an independent committee: in the United States an Institutional Review Board (IRB), and in India an Institutional Ethics Committee working under the Indian Council of Medical Research's National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical and Health Research Involving Human Participants (2017). The committee weighs risks against benefits, checks consent procedures, and protects vulnerable groups such as children, prisoners and psychiatric inpatients.

Deception (Standard 8.07) is permitted only when it is justified by significant prospective scientific, educational or applied value and non-deceptive alternatives are not feasible. It is never permitted about aspects of the research that would be expected to cause physical pain or severe emotional distress. Any deception must be explained as early as feasible, preferably at the end of the person's participation and no later than the end of data collection, and participants must be allowed to withdraw their data. This explanation is part of debriefing (Standard 8.08): after participation, the researcher gives an accurate account of the nature, results and conclusions of the study, corrects any misconceptions, and takes steps to reduce any harm. Debriefing has two functions often confused: dehoaxing (revealing the deception) and desensitising (repairing any distress or damaged self-image the study caused).

Trap. Test data (the client's answers and scores) are generally releasable to the client; test materials (manuals, items, stimuli) are protected. And deception is never allowed about anything expected to cause physical pain or severe emotional distress, however valuable the study.

8.8 The Indian legal and professional frame

The Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992

In India the profession of clinical psychology is regulated not by a psychology council but by the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI), a statutory body set up under the Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992, working under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. The Act was passed to regulate the training of rehabilitation professionals and to standardise their courses, and clinical psychologists are one of the professional categories it covers. The Council recognises training institutions and courses, and it maintains the Central Rehabilitation Register, the official list of qualified professionals. Only a person with a recognised qualification who is enrolled on the Register may practise as a rehabilitation professional, including as a clinical psychologist, and the Act makes practising without registration a punishable offence. Registration must be renewed periodically, and renewal is tied to continuing rehabilitation education credits. For decades the standard qualification was the two-year M.Phil in Clinical Psychology offered at institutions such as the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences in Bengaluru and the Central Institute of Psychiatry in Ranchi. With the National Education Policy 2020 discontinuing the M.Phil degree, the Council has been shifting the professional qualification to an RCI-approved professional master's programme in clinical psychology. The practical point for a student is simple: an ordinary MA in psychology does not by itself make you a registered clinical psychologist.

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 replaced the Mental Health Act 1987 and came into force in May 2018. Where the 1987 Act was mainly about admission and custody, the 2017 Act is built on rights, and was written to bring Indian law into line with the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (2006), which India ratified in 2007. Its main features for a clinical psychologist are these.

  • A presumption of capacity. Every person, including a person with mental illness, is deemed to have capacity to make mental healthcare decisions if they can understand the relevant information, appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision, and communicate it. This mirrors the Appelbaum and Grisso logic described in section 8.3.
  • Rights of persons with mental illness. The Act lists rights including a right to access mental healthcare from services run or funded by government, a right to community living, protection from cruel, inhuman and degrading treatment, equality and non-discrimination (including a requirement that insurers cover mental illness on the same basis as physical illness), a right to information, a right to confidentiality, a right to access one's medical records, and a right to legal aid. It prohibits certain practices outright, such as chaining and electroconvulsive therapy without anaesthesia and muscle relaxants.
  • Advance directives. Under section 5, an adult may make an advance directive stating how they wish, and do not wish, to be cared for and treated for mental illness, and whom they appoint as their nominated representative, to take effect if they later lose capacity.
  • The nominated representative. Under section 14, a person may appoint a nominated representative to support them in making decisions; where none is appointed, the Act sets out who takes that role, starting with the person named in an advance directive and then relatives or carers.
  • Decriminalisation of attempted suicide. Section 115 provides that a person who attempts suicide is presumed, unless proved otherwise, to have severe stress and shall not be tried and punished under section 309 of the Indian Penal Code, and places a duty on government to provide care, treatment and rehabilitation to reduce the risk of recurrence. The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023, which replaced the Indian Penal Code from July 2024, does not carry over a general offence of attempted suicide.
  • Recognition of the clinical psychologist. The Act defines a clinical psychologist in terms of a recognised qualification in clinical psychology from an institution approved by the Rehabilitation Council of India, and names clinical psychologists among the mental health professionals it recognises, alongside psychiatrists, psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses. This gives the profession statutory standing in mental health services.

The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 and the POCSO Act 2012

The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 replaced the Persons with Disabilities Act 1995 and expanded the list of recognised disabilities to 21 specified disabilities. It expressly includes mental illness, alongside intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder. A person with at least 40 percent of a specified disability, certified by the designated authority, has a benchmark disability, which brings entitlements such as reservation in government employment and in higher education. For clinical psychologists this means that assessments they carry out can feed directly into certification and hence into a person's legal entitlements, which raises the stakes of accurate, fair testing.

The Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012 (POCSO) is a gender-neutral law defining a child as anyone under 18 and creating specific sexual offences against children. Section 19 requires any person who knows or apprehends that such an offence has been or is likely to be committed to report it to the Special Juvenile Police Unit or the local police, and section 21 makes failure to report punishable with imprisonment of up to six months, a fine, or both. For a psychologist, this is a statutory limit to confidentiality that must be stated clearly when working with children, adolescents and their families, as Case 2 showed.

Key. The Rehabilitation Council of India registers clinical psychologists; the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 recognises them and governs rights, capacity, advance directives and nominated representatives; section 115 decriminalised attempted suicide in effect; the 2016 disability Act counts mental illness as a disability; POCSO sections 19 and 21 create a reporting duty.

8.9 Professional issues: training, credentialing, evidence-based practice and prescribing

Training models, briefly revisited

Ethical competence depends on how clinicians are trained, and the training models met earlier in this book carry ethical assumptions. The scientist-practitioner model agreed at the Boulder Conference in 1949 holds that clinicians should be both producers and consumers of research, and leads to the research doctorate. The practitioner-scholar model endorsed at the Vail Conference in 1973 emphasises practice informed by research, and leads to the Doctor of Psychology degree. The clinical scientist model, set out in Richard McFall's 1991 "Manifesto for a science of clinical psychology", argues that only scientifically grounded practice is ethical practice. In India, training has followed a strongly practice-based route through supervised hospital work, regulated by the Rehabilitation Council of India.

Licensure and credentialing

A credential is any formal recognition of qualification. Licensure is the strongest form: a state law that restricts both the title and the practice of psychology to people who meet set requirements, so that practising without a licence is illegal. Certification, in the strict sense, restricts only the use of a title. In the United States, licensure typically requires a doctoral degree from an accredited programme, a set period of supervised experience, a pass on the national Examination for Professional Practice in Psychology, and often an examination on the state's laws. Beyond licensure, board certification by the American Board of Professional Psychology is a voluntary advanced credential in a specialty. In India, enrolment on the Central Rehabilitation Register performs the licensing function. The purpose in every system is the same: to give the public a way of knowing that the person they consult has met a minimum standard, and to give regulators a way of removing those who fall below it.

Evidence-based practice

The movement began in medicine. David Sackett and colleagues defined evidence-based medicine in 1996 as the conscientious, explicit and judicious use of current best evidence in making decisions about the care of individual patients. In psychology, a task force of the American Psychological Association's Division of Clinical Psychology in the mid-1990s began listing empirically supported treatments, therapies shown effective in controlled trials, and Dianne Chambless and Steven Hollon published criteria for these in 1998. Critics objected that such lists privileged manualised, brief therapies studied in selected samples, and neglected the therapist and the relationship. The Association's 2006 Presidential Task Force answered with a broader definition: evidence-based practice in psychology is the integration of the best available research with clinical expertise in the context of patient characteristics, culture and preferences. The three components are often pictured as a three-legged stool. Research evidence tells you what generally works; clinical expertise lets you assess this client, build the relationship and adapt; and the client's characteristics, culture and preferences decide what is acceptable and meaningful to this person.

Trap. Evidence-based practice is not the same as using an empirically supported treatment. Empirically supported treatments are a list of therapies; evidence-based practice is a decision-making process that integrates three sources, only one of which is research.

The prescription privileges debate

Should psychologists with extra training be allowed to prescribe psychiatric medication? Supporters argue that many people, especially in rural and underserved areas, cannot reach a psychiatrist; that psychologists already understand diagnosis and behaviour; and that a prescriber who is also a therapist is well placed to avoid over-reliance on drugs. The United States Department of Defense trained a small group of military psychologists to prescribe in a demonstration project in the 1990s. Opponents, including many psychiatrists and also many psychologists, argue that the required medical training is inadequate for safe management of side effects and physical illness, and that prescribing would pull the profession away from its distinctive strength, psychological treatment. New Mexico became the first American state to grant prescriptive authority to specially trained psychologists in 2002, followed by Louisiana in 2004, Illinois in 2014, Iowa in 2016 and Idaho in 2017, with a small number of others since. In India, psychologists cannot prescribe medication; prescribing is restricted to registered medical practitioners.

8.10 Telepsychology and burnout

Telepsychology

Telepsychology is the provision of psychological services through telecommunication technologies: video, telephone, text and online platforms. The American Psychological Association published Guidelines for the Practice of Telepsychology in 2013, developed jointly with the Association of State and Provincial Psychology Boards and the Association's Insurance Trust, and remote practice expanded enormously during the COVID-19 pandemic. In India, the government launched Tele-MANAS, the national tele mental health programme, in October 2022. The ethical principles do not change online, but each takes a new form. Competence now includes competence with the technology and knowing which clients are suitable for remote work. Informed consent must cover the specific risks, such as a data breach or a dropped connection, and what will happen if the call fails. Confidentiality requires a secure platform and attention to who else is in the client's room. Emergency planning requires knowing the client's physical location at each session and having local emergency contacts, because a therapist in Delhi cannot personally reach a suicidal client in Guwahati. And jurisdiction matters: licensing rules generally follow where the client is, not where the therapist sits.

Burnout

Herbert Freudenberger introduced the term burnout in 1974 to describe the exhaustion he saw in staff at a free clinic. Christina Maslach developed the concept into its standard three-part form: emotional exhaustion (feeling drained), depersonalisation (a cynical, detached attitude towards clients) and reduced personal accomplishment (feeling ineffective). The Maslach Burnout Inventory, published by Maslach and Susan Jackson in 1981, measures these three dimensions. Related ideas describe the specific cost of trauma work: compassion fatigue, associated with Charles Figley, is the reduced capacity for empathy that follows sustained exposure to others' suffering, and vicarious traumatisation, described by Lisa McCann and Laurie Anne Pearlman in 1990, is the lasting change in a therapist's own beliefs about safety, trust and the world that can come from repeatedly hearing clients' trauma. Burnout is driven by heavy caseloads, lack of control, poor supervision, isolation and a mismatch between values and workplace demands. It is an ethical issue, not just a personal one, because a burnt-out clinician becomes an impaired clinician (section 8.2): detached, error-prone and at greater risk of boundary lapses. Prevention is therefore part of professional responsibility: manageable caseloads, regular supervision, peer support, personal therapy and a protected life outside work.

Why it matters. The same ethical principles run through every setting, from a hospital ward to a video call. What changes is the form the risk takes, and a good clinician learns to ask, in each new setting, how competence, consent, confidentiality and boundaries will be protected here.
Recap.

Ethics codes exist because society lets professions regulate themselves, and the American Psychological Association's code (2002, amended 2010 and 2016) does this through five aspirational General Principles and ten sections of enforceable Standards. Competence means working within the limits of one's training, maintaining it through continuing education, and recognising when personal impairment threatens it. Informed consent requires information, capacity and voluntariness; capacity is decision-specific and time-specific, children give assent while guardians consent, and research consent is stricter still. Confidentiality is the clinician's ethical duty, privilege is the client's legal right in court, and both have limits, most famously the duty to protect established in Tarasoff (1974 and 1976), along with child abuse reporting, risk to self and court orders. Multiple relationships are forbidden only when they risk impairment or exploitation, boundary crossings differ from violations, and sexual relationships with clients are prohibited. A stepwise decision model (problem, stakeholders, rules, self-examination, consultation, options, evaluation, action and documentation) turns dilemmas into manageable reasoning, and assessment and research carry their own duties of test security, cultural fairness, ethics committee review, and limits on deception with full debriefing. In India, the Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992 governs registration, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 governs rights, capacity, advance directives and nominated representatives and in effect decriminalised attempted suicide in section 115, the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 counts mental illness as a disability, and the POCSO Act 2012 creates a duty to report; the profession's wider debates concern training models, licensure, the Association's 2006 definition of evidence-based practice, prescription privileges, telepsychology and burnout.

NameWhat they gave us
