Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Distinguish the General Principles from the Ethical Standards of the American Psychological Association's code. Describe the five principles and explain why only the Standards are enforceable.
  2. What are the essential elements of informed consent? Discuss capacity, consent with children and adolescents, and the special requirements of consent in research.
  3. Discuss the limits of confidentiality with reference to the Tarasoff decisions, child abuse reporting and risk of harm to self. How does confidentiality differ from privilege?
  4. Explain the distinction between boundary crossings and boundary violations. How should a psychologist manage multiple relationships and gifts, particularly in small communities?
  5. Describe a stepwise model of ethical decision-making and apply it to a dilemma in which a client threatens to harm an identifiable person.
  6. Critically examine the legal framework governing clinical psychology practice in India, with reference to the Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 and the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012.
  7. Define evidence-based practice in psychology as stated by the American Psychological Association in 2006, and discuss the ethical issues raised by prescription privileges, telepsychology and burnout.

9 Specialties, prevention and culture in clinical psychology

The earlier chapters built the core of clinical psychology: assessment, diagnosis, psychotherapy and research. This chapter carries that core outward. A specialty is a recognised area of practice that rests on the shared foundation but adds its own knowledge and tools for a particular population (children and adolescents), setting (the medical ward, the court, the community) or problem (the injured brain). The chapter then widens the frame twice: from treating one person at a time to preventing problems across whole communities, and from assuming that one culture's categories fit everyone to asking how culture shapes symptoms, help seeking and treatment.

In India these are rarely separate careers: one clinical psychologist in a general hospital may meet all of these problems in a single day. The clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) cover this ground, and examiners return often to the stages of change, the general adaptation syndrome, section 84 of the Indian Penal Code, the levels of prevention and emic versus etic approaches.

9.1 Clinical child and adolescent psychology

Clinical child and adolescent psychology assesses and treats psychological problems from infancy through adolescence, always within the family and school. Its guiding framework is developmental psychopathology, shaped in the early 1980s by Dante Cicchetti, Alan Sroufe and Michael Rutter. Its central idea is that normal and abnormal development must be studied together: a disorder is a developmental pathway that has gone off course, not a fixed thing a child simply "has". Equifinality means different starting points can lead to the same outcome (one adolescent becomes depressed through genetic risk and family conflict, another through bullying). Multifinality means the same starting point can lead to different outcomes (three neglected children may develop depression, conduct problems or good adjustment). Risk factors such as poverty and parental mental illness raise the chance of a poor outcome; protective factors such as one warm adult relationship lower it. Resilience is good adaptation despite serious adversity: Emmy Werner's study of children born on the Hawaiian island of Kauai found that roughly one third of those judged high risk grew into competent, caring adults.

Why children are not small adults

First, development changes what is normal: a tantrum at two is expected, at twelve it is a concern; bedwetting at four is ordinary, at ten it is a referral problem. Second, children rarely refer themselves. Parents or teachers bring them, so the referral reflects the adult's tolerance and stress as much as the child's behaviour. Third, disorders look different: the DSM allows irritable rather than sad mood to count toward depression in children and adolescents, and anxiety often appears as stomach aches, clinging or refusing school. Fourth, young children cannot easily describe inner states, so clinicians use play, drawings and observation. Fifth, informants disagree. Thomas Achenbach, who developed the Child Behavior Checklist and the distinction between internalising problems (anxiety, depression, withdrawal) and externalising problems (aggression, defiance), showed that parents, teachers and children agree only modestly. This is not mere error: children genuinely behave differently in different settings.

Common referrals include attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder; anxiety, school refusal and depression; autism spectrum disorder, intellectual disability and specific learning disorder; enuresis; and, in adolescents, self-harm and substance use. In India a frequent complaint is "not studying", which may conceal a learning disorder, ADHD, anxiety or depression. Parents are informants, clients and the main agents of change, and schools supply teacher reports and accommodations. The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 recognises specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder, and certification can bring examination concessions such as extra time or a scribe.

Parent training

Behavioural parent training is among the best supported interventions for disruptive behaviour in young children. Its theory comes largely from Gerald Patterson's coercive cycle. A parent gives an instruction, the child whines and defies, and the parent gives up: the child learns that defiance removes demands, and the parent learns that giving in ends the scene, so both behaviours are negatively reinforced. At other times the parent escalates until the child yields, teaching both that escalation pays. Parent training reverses this by teaching parents to praise good behaviour (often in daily child-led play), ignore minor misbehaviour, give clear one-step instructions, apply consistent consequences and use brief time out from positive reinforcement. In Parent-Child Interaction Therapy, developed by Sheila Eyberg, the therapist coaches the parent live, often through an earpiece from behind a one-way mirror.

Case. Aarav, seven, has daily tantrums and hits his sister. At home his mother repeats instructions then gives up when he screams, his grandmother calms him with sweets, and his father shouts. The formulation is a coercive cycle kept alive by three inconsistent adults. All three attend sessions, begin ten minutes of daily praised play, agree on two clear rules and use a short time out for hitting. The tantrums fall mainly because the adults now respond alike. Offering parent training does not mean the parents caused the problem; it means they are the strongest lever for change.

9.2 Health psychology and behavioural medicine: the biopsychosocial model and stress

Health psychology applies psychology to promoting health, preventing and treating illness and improving health care. Behavioural medicine is the related interdisciplinary field joining psychologists, physicians and nurses, needed because today's chronic diseases are shaped by tobacco, diet, inactivity and adherence. In 1977 the psychiatrist George Engel argued in the journal Science that the biomedical model, which reduces disease to biological deviation and separates mind from body, cannot explain why patients with identical test results differ so much in suffering and recovery. His biopsychosocial model treats illness as the product of interacting biological, psychological and social systems. In type 2 diabetes, the biological level includes genes and insulin resistance; the psychological level includes beliefs about the illness, depression and self-efficacy (confidence that one can carry out a behaviour); the social level includes family food customs, festival sweets and the cost of medicines.

The endocrinologist Hans Selye began the modern study of stress. In 1936 he reported that rats exposed to very different harmful agents all showed enlarged adrenal glands, shrunken thymus and lymph nodes, and stomach ulcers. He concluded that the body makes a non-specific response to any demand, the general adaptation syndrome. In the alarm reaction, the sympathetic nervous system and the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis release adrenaline and cortisol. In the stage of resistance, the body copes with the continuing stressor at the cost of sustained arousal. In the stage of exhaustion, resistance collapses and "diseases of adaptation" such as ulcers and high blood pressure appear. The model's weakness is that it treats every stressor and every person alike; later research showed that psychological factors such as uncertainty and lack of control drive much of the response.

Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman, in Stress, Appraisal, and Coping (1984), put the person back in. In their transactional model, stress lies neither in the event nor in the person but in the transaction between them: it arises when a person appraises demands as taxing or exceeding their resources. In primary appraisal the person asks "What is at stake for me?"; a stressful answer takes the form of harm or loss (already done), threat (anticipated) or challenge (a chance for growth). In secondary appraisal the person asks "What can I do about it?", weighing skills, money and support. Reappraisal follows as things change. Two students facing the same entrance examination can differ completely: one sees a threat and doubts her preparation; the other sees a challenge and trusts hers.

Trap. Primary and secondary appraisal are not first and second in time; they are two different questions that can occur in either order and shape each other. And keep the contrast clean: Selye's model is physiological and non-specific; Lazarus and Folkman's is cognitive and individual.

9.3 Coping and the health behaviour models

Lazarus and Folkman defined coping as changing cognitive and behavioural efforts to manage demands appraised as taxing; it is defined by effort, not success. Problem-focused coping acts on the problem: gathering information, planning, negotiating. Emotion-focused coping manages the distress: seeking comfort, reappraisal, distraction, prayer, acceptance, and also avoidance and denial. What works depends on goodness of fit. For controllable stressors, such as a failing grade, problem-focused coping usually works best; for uncontrollable ones, such as bereavement, acceptance and finding meaning are often more adaptive. Most people use both.

Trap. Emotion-focused coping is not the same as maladaptive coping. Acceptance, positive reappraisal and seeking support are emotion-focused and often healthy; only some forms, such as persistent denial and substance use, tend to harm.

The health belief model was developed in the 1950s by social psychologists in the United States Public Health Service, among them Godfrey Hochbaum and Irwin Rosenstock, to explain why people ignored free tuberculosis screening. A person acts when they believe they are vulnerable (perceived susceptibility), that the illness would be serious (perceived severity), that action helps (perceived benefits) and that the costs are bearable (perceived barriers), and when something prompts them (cues to action, such as a relative's diagnosis). Self-efficacy was added in 1988. The model is intuitive but assumes rational calculation and neglects habit and emotion.

The theory of planned behaviour, proposed by Icek Ajzen in 1985 and stated fully in 1991, extends the theory of reasoned action developed by Martin Fishbein and Ajzen in 1975. Behaviour follows from intention, and intention is shaped by attitude toward the behaviour, subjective norms (what people who matter want) and perceived behavioural control, which can also predict behaviour directly. A man will likely intend to quit chewing gutka if he thinks quitting is good for him, his wife and doctor want it, and he believes he can manage cravings. The weakness is the intention-behaviour gap: many sincere intentions are never carried out.

James Prochaska and Carlo DiClemente developed the transtheoretical model from studies of how smokers quit on their own, with an influential paper in 1983. Its stages of change are precontemplation (no intention to change in the next six months), contemplation (intending to change within six months but ambivalent), preparation (intending to act within a month and taking small steps), action (changed within the past six months) and maintenance (change sustained beyond six months). Relapse is normal, and people often cycle through the stages several times. The practical message is to match the intervention to the stage: information in precontemplation, resolving ambivalence in contemplation (for which motivational interviewing, developed by William Miller and Stephen Rollnick, is well suited), planning in preparation and skills in action. Critics note that the stage boundaries are arbitrary time cut-offs and that stage-matched interventions have not consistently beaten unmatched ones; its lasting contribution is the idea of meeting people where they are.

ModelWho and whenCore componentsMain weakness
Health belief modelHochbaum, Rosenstock and colleagues, 1950sSusceptibility, severity, benefits, barriers, cues to action, self-efficacyAssumes rational weighing
Theory of planned behaviourIcek Ajzen, 1985 and 1991Attitude, subjective norm and perceived control lead to intention, then behaviourIntention-behaviour gap
Transtheoretical modelProchaska and DiClemente, early 1980sFive stages of change, decisional balance, self-efficacyArbitrary stage boundaries

9.4 Adherence, pain and psychoneuroimmunology

Adherence is the extent to which a person's behaviour (taking medicines, following a diet, attending appointments) matches recommendations agreed with a health provider; the older word "compliance" was dropped because it implies obedience rather than agreement. A World Health Organization report in 2003 estimated that adherence to long-term therapies in developed countries averages only about 50 percent. Non-adherence may be unintentional (forgetting, a confusing regimen, cost) or intentional (side effects, a belief the medicine is unnecessary), and it is worst for symptomless conditions such as high blood pressure. Psychologists simplify regimens, use reminders, check understanding with the teach-back method (the patient explains the plan in their own words), correct beliefs and involve family.

Case. Mr Ramesh, fifty-two, a shopkeeper with type 2 diabetes, takes tablets irregularly and refuses insulin. In health belief model terms, he thinks diabetes is "only sugar" (low perceived severity), fears insulin means he is dying (a barrier) and has had no symptom (no cue to action). In stages of change terms he is in contemplation. Motivational interviewing explores his ambivalence, his doctor corrects the insulin belief, and his tablets are tied to a fixed habit (opening the shop). He moves to preparation and agrees to try insulin.

Chronic pain, persisting beyond normal healing (commonly more than three months), is where psychology helps most. The gate control theory of Ronald Melzack and Patrick Wall (1965) proposed a "gate" in the dorsal horn of the spinal cord. Small pain fibres open it; large touch fibres tend to close it (which is why rubbing a bumped elbow helps); and messages descending from the brain, carrying attention, anxiety, mood and expectation, open or close it. An operant approach notices that pain behaviours such as grimacing and resting can be reinforced by sympathy and escape from work, and instead rewards activity set by planned quotas. Cognitive-behavioural therapy (CBT) for pain targets catastrophising, teaches pacing and relaxation, and treats depression and poor sleep. Referral to a psychologist does not mean the pain is imaginary: psychological factors act on real pain transmission.

Psychoneuroimmunology studies how the mind, nervous system and immune system influence each other. Robert Ader and Nicholas Cohen (1975) gave rats saccharin water together with cyclophosphamide, an immune-suppressing drug; later, saccharin alone suppressed their immune responses, so brain and immunity must communicate. In humans, Janice Kiecolt-Glaser and Ronald Glaser showed that medical students' immune function dips at examination time and that small wounds heal more slowly in people caring for a relative with dementia. The effects are real but modest; the claim that positive thinking alone cures cancer is not supported.

9.5 Clinical neuropsychology

Clinical neuropsychology applies knowledge of brain-behaviour relationships to assessing and rehabilitating people with known or suspected brain dysfunction. In 1861 Paul Broca linked the left inferior frontal lobe to speech production; in 1874 Carl Wernicke linked the left posterior temporal lobe to comprehension. Alexander Luria argued that complex functions depend on functional systems spread across the brain rather than single centres. Henry Molaison (known for decades as H.M.) had both medial temporal lobes removed in 1953 to control epilepsy and could no longer form new memories of events, yet Brenda Milner showed he could learn a mirror-drawing skill without remembering practising it: the hippocampus is needed for new memories, and there is more than one memory system. Now that brain imaging shows structure directly, the neuropsychologist's main task is describing function: what the person can do, and what that means for daily life, work and legal decisions.

DomainWhat it coversExamples of tests
Attention and speedFocusing, sustaining, dividing and switching attentionDigit span, Trail Making Test Part A
MemoryLearning new material, delayed recall, recognitionWechsler Memory Scale, Rey Auditory Verbal Learning Test
Executive functionPlanning, flexibility, inhibition, abstract reasoningWisconsin Card Sorting Test, Stroop test, Trail Making Test Part B
LanguageNaming, comprehension, repetition, fluencyBoston Naming Test, verbal fluency
Visuospatial abilityPerceiving spatial relations, copying and constructingBlock design, clock drawing, Rey-Osterrieth Complex Figure

A full assessment also estimates premorbid (pre-injury) ability, screens mood (depression lowers scores) and uses performance validity tests to check effort. Patterns count more than single scores: someone who cannot freely recall a word list but recognises the words has a retrieval problem, typical of frontal and subcortical conditions, while someone who can neither recall nor recognise has a storage problem, typical of hippocampal damage and early Alzheimer's disease. Indian tests must suit language, schooling and literacy, which is why the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery and the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction were built.

Three referral questions dominate. In dementia, the question is whether decline exceeds normal ageing and which type is likely: early Alzheimer's disease shows a storage memory deficit, frontotemporal dementia begins with behavioural and executive change, and depression can mimic dementia but improves when the depression is treated. In traumatic brain injury, severity is judged from the Glasgow Coma Scale and the length of post-traumatic amnesia; since the frontal and temporal lobes are vulnerable, common problems are slowed processing, poor attention and memory, executive difficulty and personality change. In epilepsy, the neuropsychologist assesses effects of seizures and medication and, before surgery, maps memory and language so that surgery does not create another Henry Molaison. Rehabilitation may be restorative (retraining a function, with limited evidence) or compensatory (diaries, phone alarms, checklists). Errorless learning helps people with amnesia: because they cannot remember which attempts were wrong, trial and error ingrains mistakes, so the correct answer is supplied from the start.

Case. Kiran, twenty-four, a software engineer, had about a week of post-traumatic amnesia after a motorcycle accident. Three months later his scans look almost normal, but he forgets meetings and works slowly. Testing shows average reasoning (his premorbid level), slow processing, poor free recall with good recognition, and poor set shifting on the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, with valid effort. The report explains that his problems are real despite normal scans, and recommends a graded return to work with written task lists and phone reminders.

9.6 Forensic psychology

Forensic psychology applies psychology to questions raised by the law. The client is often the court or a lawyer, not the person assessed, so the evaluator explains at the start who will read the report, considers malingering (deliberately faking symptoms for gain) and should not also be the person's therapist, because helper and neutral assessor are conflicting roles.

Competence to stand trial concerns the accused person's present state. The United States standard, from Dusky versus United States (1960), requires sufficient present ability to consult a lawyer with a reasonable degree of rational understanding, and a rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings. Indian criminal procedure (the Code of Criminal Procedure 1973, now the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita 2023) similarly provides for inquiry into whether an accused of unsound mind is incapable of making a defence.

The insanity defence concerns the mental state at the time of the offence. In 1843 Daniel M'Naghten, acting on persecutory delusions, shot and killed Edward Drummond, secretary to the Prime Minister Robert Peel, apparently believing he was shooting Peel. After his acquittal, the English judges set out the M'Naghten rules: a person is not responsible if, at the time of the act, he was labouring under such a defect of reason, from disease of the mind, as not to know the nature and quality of the act, or, if he did know it, that he did not know it was wrong. This is a purely cognitive test of knowledge, not of self-control. India follows it closely. Section 84 of the Indian Penal Code 1860 states that nothing is an offence which is done by a person who, at the time of doing it, by reason of unsoundness of mind, is incapable of knowing the nature of the act, or that he is doing what is either wrong or contrary to law. The Indian Penal Code has been replaced by the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023, in force from 1 July 2024, where the same provision appears as section 22. Indian courts distinguish legal insanity from medical insanity: a diagnosis of schizophrenia does not itself establish the defence; the disorder must have made the person incapable of knowing the nature or wrongness of the act at that moment, and the burden of proving this lies on the accused. The psychologist reconstructs the mental state from records, interviews and testing; the decision belongs to the court.

Trap. Competence is about the present (can the person take part in the trial now); insanity is about the past (was the person responsible at the time of the act). Someone can be insane at the time of the offence and competent now, or the reverse. Also, contrary to public belief, the insanity defence is raised rarely, succeeds rarely, and those who succeed are usually detained for treatment, not set free.

Risk assessment estimates the likelihood of violence, reoffending or self-harm and how it can be managed. Unstructured clinical judgement performs poorly, largely because violence is rare and clinicians overpredict it. Actuarial methods combine fixed, mostly historical factors statistically; they are more accurate but say little about reducing risk. Structured professional judgement, now dominant, rates evidence-based items and leaves the final judgement to the clinician; the best known tool is the HCR-20, whose twenty items cover Historical, Clinical and Risk management factors. Static factors (past violence) cannot change; dynamic factors (active psychosis, substance use) can, and are the targets of intervention. As an expert witness, the psychologist may give opinions within their expertise (section 45 of the Indian Evidence Act 1872, now replaced by the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam 2023), must stay within the data and leaves the ultimate issue to the court. In child custody evaluations the governing principle is the welfare of the child, treated as paramount under the Guardians and Wards Act 1890 and the Hindu Minority and Guardianship Act 1956; a sound evaluation interviews both parents and the child, observes each parent with the child and focuses on parenting capacity rather than diagnosis.

9.7 Community psychology and the logic of prevention

Community psychology shifts attention from the individual to the settings people live in: families, schools, workplaces, neighbourhoods. It is often dated to a 1965 conference at Swampscott, Massachusetts. Its values are prevention, an ecological view of person and environment, strengths rather than deficits, social justice and collaboration. Julian Rappaport's writing on empowerment (1981) argued for enhancing people's control over their own lives rather than treating them as passive recipients of expert help. In parallel, the community mental health movement produced the United States Community Mental Health Act of 1963, which funded local centres as large hospitals were emptied; underfunded services left many discharged patients homeless, a warning against shrinking institutions without building community care.

Gerald Caplan, in Principles of Preventive Psychiatry (1964), applied public health prevention to mental disorder. Primary prevention stops new cases, reducing incidence (emotional skills programmes for all schoolchildren). Secondary prevention detects disorder early and treats it promptly, reducing prevalence (screening mothers for depression after childbirth). Tertiary prevention reduces disability and relapse in established disorder (rehabilitation for schizophrenia). Because secondary and tertiary prevention are really treatment, Robert Gordon (1983) proposed levels defined by who is targeted, and the Institute of Medicine (United States) adopted them in 1994, reserving "prevention" for work before onset. Universal prevention targets a whole population; selective prevention targets subgroups with above-average risk, such as children of depressed parents; indicated prevention targets individuals with early signs but no full disorder. In suicide prevention, restricting highly lethal pesticides (followed in Sri Lanka by a large fall in suicides) is universal, support for those bereaved by suicide is selective, and follow-up after self-harm is indicated.

Caplan (1964)AimInstitute of Medicine (1994)Target
PrimaryReduce incidenceUniversalWhole population
SecondaryReduce prevalence by early detection and treatmentSelectiveHigher-risk subgroups
TertiaryReduce disability and relapseIndicatedIndividuals with early signs
Trap. The rows are not equivalents. Caplan's levels follow the stage of disorder, and two come after onset; the Institute of Medicine levels follow the risk status of the target group, and all three come before onset. Indicated prevention sounds like secondary prevention, but it works with people who do not yet have the disorder.

9.8 Community mental health in India and task sharing

India has far too few mental health professionals for its population. The National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, carried out by the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS), found a treatment gap (the share of people with a disorder receiving no treatment) of roughly 70 to over 90 percent depending on the disorder. India launched the National Mental Health Programme in 1982, aiming to make minimum mental health care available and accessible to all, especially the most vulnerable, to apply mental health knowledge in general health care and social development, and to promote community participation, chiefly by integrating mental health into primary health care. A pilot in Bellary district, Karnataka, begun in 1985, became the model for the District Mental Health Programme, launched in 1996. Its district team (typically a psychiatrist, clinical psychologist, psychiatric social worker and psychiatric nurse) runs clinics, trains primary care staff, supplies medicines and runs outreach, though evaluations have found vacancies and underused funds in many districts.

Task sharing means training people without professional mental health qualifications (community health workers, lay counsellors, nurses) to deliver specific psychological tasks under supervision, while specialists handle complex cases, training and supervision. The Indian psychiatrist Vikram Patel championed it, notably in Where There Is No Psychiatrist (2003); in Goa, the Healthy Activity Program trained lay counsellors in brief behavioural activation for depression, and a randomised trial found it effective. Similar programmes include the Thinking Healthy Programme for mothers with depression in Pakistan and the Friendship Bench in Zimbabwe, where trained older women deliver problem-solving therapy.

Why it matters. Task sharing changes the psychologist's question from "How many patients can I treat?" to "How many people can my training and supervision reach?" The psychologist becomes a designer of simple protocols, a trainer and a supervisor. Without ongoing supervision, lay workers burn out and drift from the protocol, so task sharing works only inside a supporting system.

9.9 Culture and what counts as a symptom

Culture is the shared beliefs, values, practices, language and ways of making meaning that a group passes on. First, it shapes what counts as a symptom: hearing a dead parent's voice during mourning, or a possession state at a festival, may be expected in some communities. The DSM defines a delusion as a belief not ordinarily accepted by the person's culture, so a clinician ignorant of the culture cannot apply the definition. Second, it shapes how distress is expressed. Many people communicate distress through the body (fatigue, aches, "gas") or through words such as "tension". These are idioms of distress. Dhat syndrome, named by N. N. Wig in 1960, is a well known Indian example: young men attribute weakness, fatigue and anxiety to loss of semen, drawing on a traditional belief that semen is concentrated vital essence. The distress is real, and dismissing the belief usually ends the relationship. Third, culture shapes help seeking and treatment, taken up in 9.11.

The psychiatrist and anthropologist Arthur Kleinman gave two tools. The explanatory model is the patient's own account of what the problem is, what caused it, why now and what treatment they expect; eliciting it is the first step to a plan the patient will accept. The category fallacy (1977) is the error of taking a diagnostic category built in one culture and applying it to another where it lacks coherence. The DSM-5 (2013) responded by replacing "culture-bound syndromes" with cultural concepts of distress and adding the Cultural Formulation Interview, sixteen questions about the person's own view of the problem, its causes, their cultural identity, supports and past help seeking.

The emic and etic distinction comes from the linguist Kenneth Pike (1954), from phonemics (sound distinctions meaningful within one language) versus phonetics (a universal system for all speech sounds). An emic approach studies a culture from the inside, using its own categories; an etic approach uses categories assumed to be universal so that cultures can be compared. John Berry (1969) warned against the imposed etic, applying a test built in one culture elsewhere as though it were universal, and recommended a derived etic, reached by studying each culture emically first and then identifying what is truly shared. Translating a Western depression questionnaire into Hindi and scoring villagers on it is an imposed etic; building a measure from how Tamil women themselves describe distress is emic. Tests therefore need linguistic, conceptual and metric equivalence.

Trap. Emic does not mean "Indian" and etic does not mean "Western". The terms describe the researcher's stance, inside or outside the culture's own categories. An Indian researcher using an unadapted Western questionnaire in an Indian village is working in an imposed etic manner.

9.10 Cultural competence, cultural humility and working across difference in India

Cultural competence is the capacity to work effectively with clients whose backgrounds differ from one's own. Derald Wing Sue and colleagues set out its best known tripartite model in the early 1980s, elaborated in 1992 with Patricia Arredondo and Roderick McDavis. Awareness is knowing one's own assumptions, values, biases and privileges. Knowledge is understanding the client's worldview, history and social situation, including discrimination and barriers to care. Skills are interventions and communication styles that fit the client. Critics note that "competence" suggests something that can be mastered, and that facts about groups harden into stereotypes ("Indian clients want advice"). Melanie Tervalon and Jann Murray-Garcia (1998) proposed cultural humility: lifelong self-critique, redressing the power imbalance between clinician and client, and partnership with communities.

Key. Competence without humility slides into stereotype; humility without knowledge slides into good intentions that help no one. Hold Sue's awareness, knowledge and skills with an attitude of cultural humility.

Differences in India combine; Kimberlé Crenshaw (1989) called this intersectionality, so that a poor Dalit woman experiences caste, class and gender as one position, not three.

  • Gender. Women's distress is often shaped by marriage, dowry and domestic violence, so violence should be screened for privately; men more often present through alcohol.
  • Caste. Discrimination in schools, colleges, work and marriage is a real and often unspoken stressor, which a client may not raise unless the therapist shows it is safe.
  • Class. Fees, travel, lost wages and the lack of a private room shape whether therapy is possible at all.
  • Religion. Faith is often a source of coping and should not be pathologised or overridden.
  • Language. Therapy in a second language can lose emotional depth, and many tests are normed only in English.
  • Sexuality and gender identity. The Supreme Court recognised transgender persons as a third gender in 2014 and decriminalised consensual same-sex relations in 2018 (Navtej Singh Johar versus Union of India), and affirmative practice treats these as normal variations while helping clients with family pressure and discrimination.

9.11 Stigma, help seeking and traditional healers in India

Erving Goffman, in Stigma (1963), described stigma as a deeply discrediting attribute that reduces a person, in others' eyes, from a whole person to a tainted, discounted one. Patrick Corrigan later distinguished public stigma (the population's stereotypes, prejudice and discrimination) from self-stigma, in which the person internalises the stereotypes and loses hope, a "why try" effect. Structural stigma is built into law: mental disorder is a ground for divorce under the Hindu Marriage Act 1955. Label avoidance, not seeking treatment to escape the label, is one of stigma's main harms. In India stigma attaches to the whole family and to siblings' marriage prospects, so illness is kept secret, called "tension" or "weakness", and treatment is delayed.

Help seeking follows explanatory models: illness may be blamed on stress, weakness, karma, the evil eye (nazar), black magic or spirit possession, often several at once. Indian pathways-to-care research repeatedly finds that many patients see faith healers or general physicians before mental health services. This is medical pluralism: families use temple, dargah and hospital side by side. Traditional healers offer an explanation that fits the family's worldview, family involvement, ritual, hope, low cost and no psychiatric label. They can also harm: chaining, beating and starvation have been documented, and delayed treatment of psychosis or epilepsy worsens outcome. In 2001 a fire at a shelter linked to the dargah at Erwadi in Tamil Nadu killed 28 chained people with mental illness; the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 now prohibits chaining. Collaboration has also been tried: in Gujarat, the Dava-Dua ("medicine and prayer") programme placed a psychiatric clinic at the dargah of Mira Datar.

Case. Fatima, twenty-two, is brought to a district hospital after three months at a dargah, where her family was told she was possessed by a jinn. She is suspicious, hears voices and barely sleeps. The psychologist asks what the family believes is happening, acknowledges the hope the dargah gave them, and explains that medicine can calm the illness while they continue praying. Framing care as "both, not either" keeps them engaged; the one firm line is that Fatima must never be chained or beaten.
Recap.

Specialties apply the shared core of clinical psychology to particular populations, settings and problems. Child psychology rests on developmental psychopathology, treats children as more than small adults and uses parent training to break the coercive cycle. Health psychology applies the biopsychosocial model, explains stress through Selye's physiological and Lazarus and Folkman's cognitive models, and explains health behaviour through the health belief model, the theory of planned behaviour and the stages of change. Neuropsychology maps cognitive domains to answer questions about dementia, head injury and epilepsy. Forensic psychology separates present competence from past insanity, judged in India by section 84 of the Indian Penal Code, now section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita. Community psychology turns from treatment to prevention, which in India means the national and district programmes and task sharing. Culture shapes symptoms and help seeking, calling for emic thinking, competence held with humility, and respectful engagement with traditional healers.

NameWhat they gave us
