Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Explain developmental psychopathology and why children are not small adults. How does parent training reverse the coercive cycle?
  2. Compare Selye's general adaptation syndrome with Lazarus and Folkman's transactional model, and distinguish problem-focused from emotion-focused coping.
  3. Critically evaluate three models of health behaviour and apply them to improving adherence in chronic illness.
  4. Describe what a clinical neuropsychologist assesses and the role of assessment and rehabilitation in dementia, head injury and epilepsy.
  5. Distinguish competence to stand trial from the insanity defence, with reference to the M'Naghten rules and section 84 of the Indian Penal Code (now section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita).
  6. Compare Caplan's levels of prevention with universal, selective and indicated prevention, and review India's community mental health programmes.
  7. Discuss how culture shapes symptoms, help seeking and treatment in India, with reference to emic and etic approaches, cultural humility and traditional healers.
Part II: Positive psychology

10 What positive psychology is, and where it came from

This chapter opens the second part of the book. Part I was about clinical psychology: how psychologists understand, assess and treat distress and disorder. Positive psychology asks a different question. Instead of "what has gone wrong with this person, and how do we repair it?", it asks "what goes right in human lives, why, and how can more of it be made to happen?" This chapter teaches what the field claims to be, why it appeared in the late 1990s, which older ideas it grew from, how it organises its subject matter, how it gathers evidence and what its critics say.

It matters for two reasons. The 1998 presidential address, the January 2000 special issue, the three pillars, Marie Jahoda and Corey Keyes' two continua model are standard long-answer topics. More importantly for a future clinician, positive psychology changes what "mental health" means. If health is more than the absence of illness, a psychologist who only removes symptoms has done half the job. The last section builds the bridge back to clinical practice, which chapter 16 crosses in detail.

10.1 Defining positive psychology

The simplest definition, the one Martin Seligman (the American psychologist who launched the field under that name) repeats most often, is that positive psychology is the scientific study of what makes life worth living. Each word does work. "Scientific" means the field claims the same methods as the rest of psychology: measurement, hypothesis testing, experiments, longitudinal follow-up and replication. "Study" means it is first a research enterprise and only second a set of techniques. "What makes life worth living" names the subject matter: happiness and satisfaction, but also meaning, engagement, relationships, character, achievement and the social conditions that support them. Other definitions stress different aspects.

SourceDefinition (paraphrased)What it emphasises
Martin SeligmanThe scientific study of what makes life worth livingA good life, not merely a symptom-free one
Martin Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi (2000)The science of positive subjective experience, positive individual traits and positive institutions, aimed at improving quality of life and preventing pathologyThree levels, from feelings to character to society
Kennon Sheldon and Laura King (2001)The scientific study of ordinary human strengths and virtuesStrengths are common, not the property of saints and geniuses
Shelly Gable and Jonathan Haidt (2005)The study of the conditions and processes that contribute to the flourishing or optimal functioning of people, groups and institutionsProcesses, and groups as well as individuals
Christopher PetersonThe scientific study of what goes right in lifeA plain contrast with the study of what goes wrong

Two phrases need explaining. Optimal human functioning means operating at or near one's best: not merely coping, but using one's capacities fully, relating well, learning and contributing. It is a standard above "normal" in the statistical sense, because the average person may function well below their best. Strengths and virtues are the positive side of personality. A virtue, in the older philosophical sense, is a morally valued excellence of character, such as wisdom or courage. A strength is a more specific psychological route by which a virtue shows itself: curiosity is a route to wisdom, perseverance a route to courage. Christopher Peterson and Martin Seligman organised these in 2004 as the Values in Action (VIA) classification of six virtues and 24 strengths, presented as a "manual of the sanities" to sit beside the DSM.

Positive psychology is an umbrella, not a single theory. Research on subjective well-being, optimism, hope, flow and gratitude existed before 2000 in scattered corners of psychology; the new field gathered it under one name and built the journals, funding and training that made it a recognised discipline.

Key. Positive psychology is the scientific study of what makes life worth living: positive experiences, positive traits and the institutions that enable them. The two words that carry the definition are "scientific" (empirical method, not inspiration) and "worth living" (the presence of a good life, not only the absence of disorder).

10.2 What positive psychology is not

It is not "happiology". Critics sometimes call it a science of feeling cheerful, but feeling good is only one part of its subject. Seligman's Authentic Happiness (2002) separated pleasure, engagement and meaning, and his Flourish (2011) replaced happiness with well-being, described by the PERMA model (positive emotion, engagement, relationships, meaning, accomplishment). A person caring for a dying parent may not feel happy, yet may be living a life the field counts as going well.

It is not positive thinking. Positive thinking is a popular self-help tradition that grew from the nineteenth century American "New Thought" or "mind-cure" movement and reached a mass audience through Norman Vincent Peale's The Power of Positive Thinking (1952). It claims that cheerful thoughts, and refusing negative ones, will by themselves bring health and success. Positive psychology tests claims with data rather than testimonials, does not hold that optimism is always good (unrealistic optimism leads people to ignore real risks), and does not tell people to suppress negative feelings.

It is not a denial of the negative, nor a replacement for clinical psychology. The founding complaint was that psychology had become unbalanced, not that suffering should be ignored. Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi said plainly that treating mental illness should continue; positive psychology was a complement, useful for prevention, recovery and the many people who are not ill but not doing well.

It is not humanistic psychology renamed, and it is not self-help. Humanistic psychology discussed growth and potential decades earlier, but the two differ in method (section 10.7). A large commercial market also sells coaching and books under the positive psychology label; some of it is faithful to the research and much is not.

Trap. A common exam answer says positive psychology "teaches people to think positively". That is wrong twice over: it is a research field, not a doctrine, and it explicitly separates itself from Peale-style positive thinking. The key contrasts are evidence versus testimonial, realistic versus unconditional optimism, and acceptance versus suppression of negative emotion.

10.3 Why it arose: the disease model and psychology's lost missions

Before the Second World War, Seligman argued, psychology had three missions: to cure mental illness, to make the lives of all people more productive and fulfilling, and to identify and nurture high talent. The second and third were real programmes; Lewis Terman at Stanford University, for example, began in the 1920s a famous long-term study of gifted children.

After 1945 the United States had very large numbers of returning soldiers with psychological injuries. From 1946 the Veterans Administration funded the training and employment of clinical psychologists on a large scale, and the National Institute of Mental Health, created under the National Mental Health Act of 1946, became a major research funder. Both paid for work on mental illness. Seligman's argument was economic: psychologists found they could make a living treating disorder, and researchers could win grants studying it. Over the following decades the first mission swallowed the other two.

The result was the disease model (also called the medical or deficit model). Distress is understood by analogy with physical illness: it is caused by an underlying defect located inside the individual; the psychologist diagnoses it, finds its cause and applies a treatment that removes it; success is symptom reduction; the ideal endpoint is the absence of disorder.

Seligman credited its achievements: diagnosis became reliable once explicit criteria arrived with the third edition of the DSM in 1980, and effective treatments were found for disorders such as depression, panic disorder and phobias. But he named three costs. Psychology came to see people as passive victims of forces acting on them, neglecting choice and strength. The missions of improving normal lives and nurturing talent were forgotten. And prevention had no science to draw on: the most effective prevention works not by fixing damage but by building competencies (courage, optimism, interpersonal skill, hope, perseverance) that buffer against disorder, and psychology knew little about them.

A teaching metaphor captures the imbalance. Picture a scale from minus five (severe disorder) through zero (no disorder) to plus five (flourishing). The disease model works on the left half, moving people towards zero. Positive psychology asks what moves people from zero towards plus five, and whether the same forces prevent a slide below zero. Section 10.9 shows the metaphor's limit: illness and well-being are better seen as two scales than one line.

The complaint carried weight because of who made it. In 1967, with Steven Maier, Seligman had published experiments on learned helplessness: animals exposed to inescapable shocks later failed to escape shocks they could have avoided, a passivity that became an influential model of depression. His work then turned from how helplessness is learned to how it is unlearned, leading to research on explanatory style and Learned Optimism (1990). That personal shift from helplessness to optimism is a small version of the shift he later proposed for the whole discipline.

10.4 The founding moment: the 1998 address and the January 2000 special issue

Positive psychology's date of birth is usually given as 1998. Martin Seligman was President of the American Psychological Association that year, and the presidency gives its holder an annual theme and a presidential address. Seligman chose positive psychology. His address, delivered in 1998 and published in the American Psychologist in 1999, argued that psychology had become a science of damage and repair and should now build a science of human strength, with particular weight on prevention: the way to prevent depression, substance misuse or violence in the young is to build the strengths that protect against them.

The second landmark is the special issue of the American Psychologist published in January 2000, edited by Martin Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi, on happiness, excellence and optimal human functioning. Its introduction, "Positive psychology: An introduction", is the field's most cited founding text. It set out the three levels of the field (section 10.8), told the story of the disease model and criticised humanistic psychology (section 10.7). The issue included Ed Diener on the science of subjective well-being and Richard Ryan and Edward Deci on self-determination theory.

Who gave us this. Martin Seligman (born 1942), University of Pennsylvania: learned helplessness (with Steven Maier, 1967), learned optimism (1990), President of the American Psychological Association in 1998 and founder of positive psychology as a named movement. Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi (1934 to 2021), Hungarian-born psychologist at the University of Chicago, best known for flow, complete absorption in a challenging activity (Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience, 1990). He recalled watching, as a child in wartime Europe, many admired adults fall apart once their supports were removed while a few kept their integrity and purpose; he wanted to know what those few drew on.

Institutions followed quickly: the Journal of Happiness Studies (2000); C. R. Snyder and Shane Lopez's Handbook of Positive Psychology (2002), which in later editions became the Oxford Handbook of Positive Psychology, one of this course's references; Peterson and Seligman's classification of strengths (2004); the Journal of Positive Psychology (2006); and the International Positive Psychology Association (2007).

Trap. Keep the founding events apart. The presidential address is 1998 (Seligman alone). The special issue is January 2000 (Seligman and Csikszentmihalyi). Neither coined the phrase: Abraham Maslow used "positive psychology" in 1954.

10.5 The oldest roots: philosophy and the wisdom traditions

Positive psychology is young as a named field, but its questions (what is a good life, is happiness the same as pleasure, which qualities of character are worth cultivating) are among the oldest in human thought. When Peterson and Seligman built their classification, they surveyed Confucian, Taoist, Buddhist, Hindu, Athenian, Jewish, Christian and Islamic texts and found six virtues recurring across them: wisdom, courage, humanity, justice, temperance and transcendence.

Hedonism and eudaimonia

Greek philosophy supplied the field's most important distinction. Hedonism holds that the good life is the pleasant life; Epicurus refined this into a quiet ideal in which the best pleasure is freedom from pain and mental disturbance. Aristotle, in the Nicomachean Ethics, argued instead that the highest human good is eudaimonia, usually translated "happiness" but closer to "flourishing". For Aristotle eudaimonia is not a feeling but an activity: the exercise of distinctively human capacities, above all reason, in accordance with virtue over a complete life. Just as a good knife cuts well, a good human life uses human capacities well. Virtues are dispositions built by practice, each lying at a mean between extremes; courage lies between cowardice and recklessness.

Modern research turned this contrast into two traditions. The hedonic tradition studies subjective well-being: life satisfaction and the balance of pleasant over unpleasant emotion. The eudaimonic tradition studies psychological functioning: meaning, purpose, growth, autonomy and relationships, whether or not they feel pleasant at the moment. Richard Ryan and Edward Deci set out the distinction in a 2001 review, and Carol Ryff's six-dimension model of psychological well-being (1989) is the classic eudaimonic measure. Later chapters teach both.

Buddhist and Indian traditions

Buddhism begins with dukkha, the unsatisfactoriness of ordinary life, which it traces to craving and clinging. Its ideal of happiness (sukha) is a stable well-being that does not depend on getting what one wants, reached through ethical conduct, meditation and wisdom. The four "immeasurable" states it cultivates (loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy and equanimity) read like a list of positive psychology topics, and mindfulness entered Western psychology from Buddhist practice.

Indian thought has its own vocabulary. The Sanskrit word for health, swastha, means being established in oneself, and the Sushruta Samhita, a classical Ayurvedic text, describes the healthy person as one whose bodily functions are in balance and whose self, senses and mind are clear and content: health as a positive state, centuries before the World Health Organization. The Bhagavad Gita describes the sthitaprajna, the person of steady wisdom who stays even-minded in pleasure and pain, and teaches nishkama karma, wholehearted action without attachment to its rewards.

Why it matters. One criticism of positive psychology (section 10.11) is that it universalised a Western, individualist idea of the good life. Knowing that Indian and Buddhist thought hold older, more relational and more equanimity-centred accounts of well-being lets you answer that criticism and apply the field sensibly with Indian clients.

10.6 Psychology before the name: William James, Gordon Allport and Marie Jahoda

William James (1842 to 1910), the founder of American psychology, cared about flourishing throughout his career. In The Varieties of Religious Experience (1902) he contrasted the healthy-minded temperament, which sees the world as good and passes quickly over evil, with the sick soul, acutely aware of suffering. He took both seriously and warned that healthy-mindedness could be shallow if it ignored real suffering, a warning later critics turned on positive psychology. In "The Energies of Men" (1907) he asked why most people use only a small part of their resources: a question about optimal functioning in all but name.

Gordon Allport (1897 to 1967), a founder of personality psychology, insisted that psychology study healthy people and not only neurotic ones. In Pattern and Growth in Personality (1961) he described the mature personality: an extended sense of self that includes others and causes beyond oneself; warm relating to others; emotional security and self-acceptance; realistic perception; absorbing skills and tasks; insight into oneself with a sense of humour; and a unifying philosophy of life. Several of these reappear almost unchanged in later models of well-being.

The most direct forerunner is Marie Jahoda (1907 to 2001), an Austrian-born social psychologist. In 1958 she wrote Current Concepts of Positive Mental Health for the Joint Commission on Mental Illness and Health in the United States. Her starting point was that the absence of illness is not a sufficient definition of mental health: many people have no disorder yet function poorly, so mental health must be defined by the presence of something positive. She proposed six criteria.

Jahoda's criterionWhat it means
Attitudes towards the selfSelf-acceptance, self-awareness and a stable sense of identity
Growth, development and self-actualisationA continuing movement towards realising one's potential
IntegrationA balance of inner forces, a unifying outlook and resistance to stress
AutonomyIndependence from social pressure; regulating behaviour from within
Perception of realitySeeing the world undistorted by one's own needs, and empathy for others
Environmental masteryCompetence in love, work and play; adequacy in relationships; meeting demands and solving problems

Jahoda admitted that each criterion carries value judgements and offered them as a starting point for research. The list was largely ignored for decades as psychiatry moved to symptom checklists, but it anticipated Carol Ryff's model, which shares self-acceptance, growth, autonomy and environmental mastery with it almost directly.

10.7 Maslow, Rogers and humanistic psychology: ancestors and rivals

Abraham Maslow (1908 to 1970) actually used the phrase first. The final chapter of the first edition of Motivation and Personality (1954) was titled "Toward a Positive Psychology". In it he complained that psychology had revealed much about human shortcomings and illness and little about potential and virtue: Seligman's complaint, 44 years early. Maslow's hierarchy of needs (1943) places physiological, safety, belonging and esteem needs beneath self-actualisation, the realisation of one's full potential. He described self-actualising people as realistic, self-accepting, autonomous, creative and prone to peak experiences, moments of intense joy and wonder, but his method (a small, hand-picked sample judged by Maslow himself) was weak by modern standards.

Carl Rogers (1902 to 1987) developed client-centred therapy (Client-Centered Therapy, 1951; On Becoming a Person, 1961). He held that every organism has an actualising tendency, a built-in drive towards growth that flourishes under unconditional positive regard, empathy and genuineness. Therapy aimed at helping the client become a fully functioning person: open to experience, living in the present and trusting their own judgement. Rogers was also an empirical pioneer, among the first to record therapy sessions for research.

Maslow, Rogers and others formed humanistic psychology in the late 1950s and 1960s, calling it the "third force" against psychoanalysis (pessimistic and pathology-focused) and behaviourism (mechanistic). It stressed free will, subjective experience, meaning and growth: much of what positive psychology later claimed.

Why, then, is positive psychology not humanistic psychology revived? In the 2000 article Seligman and Csikszentmihalyi argued that humanistic psychology had not built a cumulative empirical base, had been wary of conventional scientific method, and had spawned self-help movements whose emphasis on the self encouraged self-centredness. The historian Eugene Taylor replied in 2001 that positive psychology was repackaging humanistic ideas without credit, that humanists had done research (Rogers' recorded sessions being the obvious case), and that a narrow science of questionnaires could not capture the lived, existential side of flourishing, where meaning often comes through confronting suffering and death.

The quarrel has softened. Self-determination theory's needs for autonomy, competence and relatedness, and Ryff's personal growth dimension, are close cousins of Rogers and Maslow, and the second wave (section 10.11) accepted much of the humanist point about the value of the negative. The fair summary: humanistic psychology asked many of positive psychology's questions first; positive psychology brought stronger conventional measurement and a far larger research infrastructure.

Trap. Do not write that Maslow was a positive psychologist or that Seligman was a humanist. Maslow was a humanistic psychologist who used the phrase "positive psychology" in 1954; Seligman founded positive psychology as an organised field in 1998 and criticised humanistic psychology's empirical base. Related movements, not the same movement.

10.8 The three pillars: experiences, traits and institutions

The 2000 article organised the field into three levels, which Seligman's Authentic Happiness (2002) called the three pillars.

Positive subjective experiences are the first pillar: what a good life feels like from the inside. Seligman and Csikszentmihalyi divided it by time. About the past: well-being, contentment and satisfaction. In the present: happiness, pleasure and flow. About the future: hope and optimism. The time division lets you place almost any positive state: gratitude and forgiveness change one's relation to the past, savouring and mindfulness deepen the present, hope and optimism shape the future.

Positive individual traits are the second pillar: stable qualities such as the capacity for love and vocation, courage, interpersonal skill, perseverance, forgiveness, originality, future-mindedness, spirituality, high talent and wisdom, later formalised in the VIA classification. Traits matter because they are more stable than moods and more open to the person's own influence than circumstances.

Positive institutions are the third pillar: families, schools, workplaces and communities, and the civic virtues they foster, such as responsibility, nurturance, altruism, civility, tolerance and work ethic. Strengths develop and are expressed within institutions, and some make this easier than others. This has been the least developed pillar, though it has grown through positive education and research on organisational and national well-being.

The pillars connect: positive institutions nurture positive traits, which make positive experiences more likely. A child in a supportive school (institution) develops curiosity and persistence (traits), which bring more frequent flow and satisfaction (experiences).

Trap. Three different "threes" appear in this chapter. The three missions are psychology's pre-war goals (cure illness, improve all lives, nurture talent). The three pillars are the levels of positive psychology (experiences, traits, institutions). The three time frames sit inside the first pillar (past, present, future). PERMA, from 2011, has five elements.

10.9 Health as more than the absence of illness

The constitution of the World Health Organization, in force from 1948, defines health as "a state of complete physical, mental and social well-being and not merely the absence of disease or infirmity." It includes mental and social well-being, defines health by the presence of well-being, and denies that the absence of disease is enough. Critics note that "complete", taken literally, makes almost nobody healthy; it was an aspiration, not a criterion. In practice psychiatry and clinical psychology long worked with the opposite rule: a person is mentally healthy if they have no diagnosable disorder. What was missing was a way to measure the positive side.

Corey Keyes and the mental health continuum

Corey Keyes, an American sociologist and psychologist, set out in 2002 to treat mental health the way the DSM treats mental illness: as a syndrome of symptoms, a set number of which must be present for a diagnosis. His "symptoms" of mental health come from three areas.

  • Emotional well-being (hedonic): positive feelings, interest in life and life satisfaction.
  • Psychological well-being (eudaimonic, personal, taken from Carol Ryff): self-acceptance, personal growth, purpose in life, environmental mastery, autonomy and positive relations with others.
  • Social well-being (Keyes' own addition): social acceptance, social actualisation (belief that society can improve), social contribution, social coherence (finding society understandable) and social integration (belonging to a community).

That gives three emotional signs and eleven signs of positive functioning. The Mental Health Continuum Short Form, a 14-item questionnaire, asks how often in the past month each was felt. A person is flourishing if they report at least one of the three emotional signs "every day" or "almost every day" and at least six of the eleven functioning signs at that frequency; languishing if at least one emotional and six functioning signs occurred "never" or "once or twice"; and moderately mentally healthy otherwise.

Languishing is the most useful clinical idea here: a state of emptiness and stagnation in which life feels hollow, though the person meets criteria for no disorder. Keyes' research with large United States samples found that flourishing adults were a minority, that languishing carried real impairment in daily functioning and work, and that languishing people were at higher risk of later mental illness.

Trap. Languishing is not mild depression and is not a DSM or ICD diagnosis. It is defined by the absence of well-being, not the presence of depressive symptoms. A person can languish without being depressed.

The two continua model

Keyes' more radical claim is the two continua model. The single-line view assumes that less illness means more health. Keyes showed, through factor analysis of survey data, that mental illness and mental health behave as two separate but correlated dimensions: one axis runs from high to low symptoms of illness, the other from languishing to flourishing. People with mental illness are more likely to languish, but the correlation is only moderate, so position on one axis does not tell you position on the other. The ideal Keyes called complete mental health: flourishing and free of mental illness.

Low illness symptomsHigh illness symptoms
FlourishingComplete mental health: well and not illCoping with a disorder while meaning, relationships and purpose stay intact
LanguishingIncomplete mental health: not ill, but not wellIll and not well: the most impaired group

Labels for the off-diagonal cells vary between authors, so learn the logic. Three implications follow. Removing illness does not produce health: a treated patient may become symptom-free yet still languish, and still carry raised risk of relapse. People with a diagnosis can flourish, which fits the recovery movement's definition of recovery as a satisfying, hopeful life even with ongoing symptoms. Treatment and promotion are different jobs: services must reduce illness and also build well-being.

Case. Ananya, 24, has bipolar I disorder; on medication she has been episode-free for two years, is finishing a master's degree she cares about and is close to her sister. She scores as flourishing. Rohit, 26, has never met criteria for any disorder, but works long hours in a job he finds pointless, has drifted from friends and describes his days as "grey". He scores as languishing. On a single illness-to-health line Rohit looks healthier. The two continua model says each is healthy on one axis only: Ananya needs continued treatment, and Rohit, who would never be referred under a disease model, is exactly who positive psychology argues services should reach before he slides into disorder.
Key. The World Health Organization (1948) said health is more than the absence of disease; Marie Jahoda (1958) proposed positive criteria for mental health; Corey Keyes (from 2002) made it measurable as flourishing, moderate mental health and languishing, and showed that mental illness and mental health are two related but separate continua.

10.10 How positive psychology does its research

Self-report scales. Most constructs are measured by questionnaires: the Satisfaction With Life Scale (Ed Diener and colleagues, 1985), five statements such as "In most ways my life is close to my ideal"; the Positive and Negative Affect Schedule, which rates twenty emotions; the Mental Health Continuum Short Form; and the VIA Inventory of Strengths. National surveys use single items such as the Cantril ladder (rate your life from zero, the worst possible, to ten, the best possible), which underlies the annual World Happiness Report. Self-report suits a construct that is partly subjective by definition, but it is vulnerable to social desirability, to current mood colouring global judgements and to cultural differences in scale use, so good studies cross-check with informant reports and behaviour.

Experience sampling. Global questions ("how happy are you in general?") depend on memory. The experience sampling method, developed by Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi and colleagues from the 1970s, signals people at random moments in daily life (once by pager, now by smartphone) to record what they are doing, with whom and how they feel. It is how flow was studied in real life, and it measures well-being as lived rather than as remembered.

Longitudinal studies. To know whether positive states predict good outcomes, researchers follow people over time. In the Nun Study analysis by Deborah Danner and colleagues (2001), autobiographies written decades earlier by young Catholic nuns were coded for positive emotion, and nuns whose writing expressed more positive emotion lived longer on average. Because the nuns shared diet, housing and health care, many alternative explanations are reduced, but the design is still correlational.

Intervention trials. The strongest test of cause is experimental change. Positive psychology interventions are brief deliberate activities meant to raise well-being, tested in randomised controlled trials. In a landmark 2005 study, Seligman and colleagues randomly assigned several hundred online participants to one of several exercises or to a placebo (writing about early memories). Writing down three good things each day and why, and using one's signature strengths in a new way, raised happiness and lowered depressive symptoms for up to six months relative to placebo. A 2009 meta-analysis by Nancy Sin and Sonja Lyubomirsky concluded that such interventions work on average; later, more conservative meta-analyses, weighting larger and better-designed studies and correcting for the publication of small striking studies, found the effects considerably smaller.

Why it matters. When asked to "critically evaluate" a positive psychology claim, look at method: is the evidence only self-report, cross-sectional or longitudinal, was there an active placebo, how large was the effect, and has it replicated outside North American university students?

10.11 Criticisms, and the second wave

The tyranny of the positive attitude. The psychologist Barbara Held argued in the early 2000s that American culture imposes a "tyranny of the positive attitude", under which struggling people fail twice: once in their problem, and again in not being positive about it. Positive psychology risked lending scientific authority to this pressure. The danger is sharpest in illness: a cancer patient told that optimism aids survival may blame herself when the disease progresses, though reviews have not supported claims that positive attitudes improve cancer survival.

Neglect of the value of negative emotions. Fear signals danger, anger signals injustice, sadness slows us after loss and draws support, guilt repairs relationships. Julie Norem's research on defensive pessimism showed that some anxious people perform better when they imagine what could go wrong and prepare; making them "think positive" worsened performance.

Cultural bias. Much of the research was done in the United States, often with students, and critics argue its good life reflects Western individualism: the individual as the unit of well-being and happiness as excited personal achievement. Jeanne Tsai's research on "ideal affect" found that European Americans tend to value high-arousal positive states such as excitement, and East Asians low-arousal states such as calm. In many cultures happiness is relational, found in harmony and fulfilled duty, and the Indian ideals of equanimity in section 10.5 are a case in point. Measures and interventions cannot be assumed to travel.

Replication problems. The clearest casualty is the positivity ratio. In 2005 Barbara Fredrickson and Marcial Losada claimed, using a model borrowed from fluid dynamics, that people flourish when positive emotions outnumber negative ones by about 2.9 to 1. In 2013 Nicholas Brown, Alan Sokal and Harris Friedman showed that the mathematics was invalid, and the journal formally withdrew the modelling; Fredrickson accepted the flaw while maintaining that more positive relative to negative emotion is broadly beneficial. The popular claim that about 40 percent of happiness is under intentional control (the "happiness pie") has also been criticised as a misreading of what variance estimates can say about an individual, and intervention effects shrink in better studies.

Commercialisation. The writer Barbara Ehrenreich, in Bright-Sided: How the Relentless Promotion of Positive Thinking Has Undermined America (2009, published in Britain as Smile or Die), described the pressure during her breast cancer treatment to see illness as a gift and stay cheerful. She traced this culture from New Thought through Peale to corporate motivational speaking and academic positive psychology, which she accused of lending respectability to a profitable industry. A related political charge is that the field places responsibility for well-being on the individual, encouraging people to adjust their attitudes to poverty or overwork rather than change the conditions.

The second wave

The field's main response is the second wave. Its best-known early statement is Paul Wong's 2011 proposal of "positive psychology 2.0". Wong, a Canadian psychologist and founder of meaning therapy, argued that the good life comes not from maximising positive and minimising negative but from integrating both; meaning often emerges precisely through confronting suffering. In 2016 Itai Ivtzan, Tim Lomas and colleagues published Second Wave Positive Psychology: Embracing the Dark Side of Life, studying courage (which requires fear), forgiveness (which requires an injury), post-traumatic growth (which requires trauma) and awareness of mortality. The second wave rests on what Lomas and Ivtzan call the dialectical nature of well-being: a "positive" quality can harm in the wrong context (forgiveness that keeps someone in an abusive relationship), a "negative" one can help (anger driving resistance to injustice), and the two often mingle, as in a bittersweet farewell.

10.12 Positive psychology and clinical psychology

The relationship has moved from rivalry towards integration. At first positive psychology was presented as the other half of psychology: clinical psychology repairs, positive psychology builds. Alex Wood and Nicholas Tarrier, in a 2010 article in Clinical Psychology Review, called this divide artificial. Many characteristics are one dimension seen from two ends (gratitude and ingratitude, hope and hopelessness), so studying one end gives an incomplete picture. They proposed a positive clinical psychology giving equal weight to positive and negative functioning in research, assessment and treatment. Note the tension with Keyes: for some traits positive and negative form one continuum, while mental illness and overall mental health form two. Both can be true of different constructs.

In practice positive psychology enters clinical work in four ways. Assessment can record strengths and well-being, not only symptoms, so the formulation shows what the client has to work with. Treatment can include structured positive interventions: positive psychotherapy, developed by Tayyab Rashid and Martin Seligman, combines exercises such as using signature strengths, gratitude and savouring with work on symptoms; well-being therapy, developed by the Italian psychiatrist Giovanni Fava on the basis of Ryff's dimensions, is used late in the treatment of depression and anxiety to build well-being and reduce relapse. Relapse prevention gains from the finding that a remitted but languishing patient has not fully recovered. Prevention and promotion programmes in schools and communities apply the founding argument that strengths buffer against disorder.

In India this has particular force. Clinical psychology here, regulated through the Rehabilitation Council of India, faces a large treatment gap, and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, chiefly a rights-based law on the care of people with mental illness, also places a duty on government to run programmes for promoting mental health and preventing mental illness. Indian clients often draw on religious, family and community resources that a strengths-based assessment notices and a deficit-based one misses, while the cultural criticisms of section 10.11 warn against importing Western interventions unadapted.

DimensionDisease modelPositive psychology perspective
Core questionWhat is wrong, and how can it be fixed?What is right, and how can it be built on?
Definition of healthAbsence of disorderPresence of well-being (flourishing) as well as absence of disorder
View of the personA patient acted upon by pathologyAn active agent with strengths, choices and potential
Main focusSymptoms, deficits and damageStrengths, positive emotions, meaning and relationships
ClassificationDSM and ICD categories of disorderVIA character strengths; flourishing and languishing
Goal of interventionReduce symptoms towards zeroBuild well-being beyond zero; optimal functioning
Who is servedPeople with diagnosable disordersEveryone, including those not ill but not well
PreventionIdentify and reduce risk factorsBuild protective strengths that buffer against disorder
Main riskReducing people to diagnoses and ignoring their resourcesIgnoring real suffering and blaming people for not being positive
Why it matters. This is the bridge to chapter 16. Modern practice combines both columns; they are not camps to choose between. A clinician works on two axes, reducing illness and building well-being. Clearing the first while ignoring the second leaves a client symptom-free but languishing, and at risk.
Recap.

Positive psychology is the scientific study of what makes life worth living: positive subjective experiences, positive individual traits and the institutions that enable them; it is not happiology, positive thinking, a denial of suffering or a replacement for clinical psychology. It arose from Martin Seligman's argument that post-war psychology, funded to treat mental illness, adopted a disease model and neglected its missions of improving all lives and nurturing talent; he made positive psychology the theme of his 1998 presidential year at the American Psychological Association, and he and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi edited the founding special issue of the American Psychologist in January 2000. Its roots run through Abraham Maslow (who used the phrase in 1954), Carl Rogers and humanistic psychology (ancestors with whom it quarrelled over method), Marie Jahoda's six criteria of positive mental health (1958), William James and Gordon Allport, and back to Aristotle's eudaimonia and Buddhist and Indian ideas of well-being. The field is mapped by three pillars: experiences (past satisfaction, present happiness and flow, future hope and optimism), traits and institutions. The World Health Organization's 1948 definition and Corey Keyes' model of flourishing, moderate mental health and languishing establish that health is more than the absence of illness, and the two continua model shows illness and well-being are related but separate dimensions. The field works through self-report scales, experience sampling, longitudinal studies and intervention trials, and has been criticised for the tyranny of positivity, neglect of negative emotion, Western bias, replication failures such as the positivity ratio and commercialisation, to which the second wave responded by integrating the dark and the light. For clinical psychology the lesson is to work on two axes: reduce illness and build well-being.

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Define positive psychology, drawing on at least three definitions, and discuss what it is not, with particular reference to the difference between positive psychology and positive thinking.
  2. Explain Martin Seligman's argument that post-war psychology adopted a disease model and neglected two of its three missions, and evaluate it.
  3. Trace the roots of positive psychology from ancient philosophy through William James, Gordon Allport, Marie Jahoda and humanistic psychology, and discuss the tension between humanistic and positive psychology.
  4. Describe the three pillars of positive psychology, with examples of the past, present and future forms of positive subjective experience.
  5. "Health is more than the absence of illness." Discuss with reference to the World Health Organization definition, Jahoda's criteria and Corey Keyes' mental health continuum and two continua model, and draw out the implications for clinical practice.
  6. Describe the main research methods of positive psychology and critically evaluate the evidence for positive psychology interventions.
  7. Critically evaluate positive psychology, discussing its major criticisms and the response of second wave positive psychology.

11 Happiness and well-being

Clinical psychology was built to understand and reduce suffering. Positive psychology asks the other half of the question: what does it mean for a life to go well, and how would we know? This chapter starts with the two philosophical traditions that still divide the field, well-being as pleasure and well-being as living well. It then covers the four models examiners ask about most: Ed Diener's subjective well-being, Carol Ryff's psychological well-being, Corey Keyes's social well-being, and Martin Seligman's move from authentic happiness to PERMA. Finally it asks what predicts happiness and how honestly we can measure it.

The positive psychology texts (Baumgardner and Crothers; the Oxford Handbook of Positive Psychology) cover this ground. It matters to a clinician because having no disorder is not the same as being healthy. A patient whose depression has remitted may still be flat, purposeless or lonely, and knowing how well-being is built tells you what is still missing.

11.1 Two old answers: hedonic and eudaimonic well-being

The hedonic tradition (from the Greek hedone, pleasure) holds that a good life contains as much pleasure and as little pain as possible. Its best known ancient voice is Epicurus (341 to 270 BCE), who called pleasure the beginning and end of the blessed life. He did not mean feasting. He prized ataraxia (a mind free of disturbance and fear) and aponia (a body free of pain), reached through simple living, moderation and friendship. The modern word "epicurean", meaning a lover of rich food, distorts his teaching.

The English philosopher Jeremy Bentham systematised the hedonic view in An Introduction to the Principles of Morals and Legislation (1789). He argued that pain and pleasure govern everything humans do, and that the right action produces the greatest happiness for the greatest number (the principle of utility). His "felicific calculus" weighed a pleasure by its intensity, duration, certainty, nearness and similar properties. In modern psychology this tradition survives as the study of how pleasant people find their experiences and lives, which in practice means subjective well-being (section 11.3).

The eudaimonic tradition comes from Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics (4th century BCE). Eudaimonia joins eu (good) and daimon (a guiding spirit, often read as one's true self). For Aristotle it was not a feeling but an activity: living, over a complete life, in accordance with virtue. His "function argument" runs like this. A good knife cuts well. The distinctive function of a human being is reason. So a good human life is one in which reason and the virtues (courage, temperance, justice, practical wisdom) are exercised well. Each virtue is a mean between two vices, as courage lies between cowardice and rashness. Aristotle thought a life of pleasure alone was fit for grazing animals.

The American psychologist Alan Waterman brought eudaimonia into empirical psychology in 1993. He read the daimon as a person's true potentials and called the experience of acting in line with them personal expressiveness: the sense that "this is who I really am" and "this is what I was meant to do", with a feeling of being intensely alive. When students rated their own important activities, personal expressiveness correlated strongly with hedonic enjoyment, but the relationship was lopsided. Expressive activities were almost always enjoyable, yet many enjoyable activities were not expressive. The expressive ones involved challenge, effort and developing one's best abilities, while the merely enjoyable ones were more often about relaxing.

Richard Ryan and Edward Deci's 2001 review in the Annual Review of Psychology ("On happiness and human potentials") organised the field around these two traditions. They argued that well-being is multidimensional and placed eudaimonia inside their self-determination theory, which says people flourish when three basic psychological needs are met: autonomy (acting by one's own choice), competence (being effective) and relatedness (connection with others). Pleasant feeling then follows as a by-product. They also warned that some pleasure-bringing goals, such as chasing wealth or fame, do not bring wellness.

FeatureHedonic well-beingEudaimonic well-being
Philosophical rootsEpicurus, Jeremy BenthamAristotle
Core questionHow good does my life feel?Am I living well, as the person I could be?
What counts as well-beingPleasure, satisfaction, absence of painVirtue, growth, meaning, realising potential
Main psychological modelSubjective well-being (Ed Diener)Psychological well-being (Carol Ryff); self-determination theory

11.2 Why the distinction matters, and where it blurs

The distinction matters because pleasure and wellness can come apart. Someone who drinks every evening to feel good may report plenty of pleasant feeling while their life is shrinking. It works the other way too: raising a child, caring for a sick parent or getting through a demanding clinical internship is often stressful in the moment, yet deeply meaningful. A purely hedonic scorecard would rate these lives badly, and a eudaimonic one would not. The distinction also guides intervention. Someone lacking pleasant emotion might be taught savouring, while someone lacking direction needs work on values and purpose.

In the data, though, the two blur. Corey Keyes, Dov Shmotkin and Carol Ryff (2002) found in a national American sample that subjective and psychological well-being were related but separable: most people high on one were high on the other, but a meaningful minority were not. Todd Kashdan, Robert Biswas-Diener and Laura King (2008) argued that eudaimonia has been defined in many incompatible ways, that eudaimonic theories mix the causes of well-being with the outcome itself, and that the overlap in data is so large that splitting the two may confuse more than it clarifies. Veronika Huta and Richard Ryan (2010) offered a fix: separate a person's motive for an activity (seeking pleasure versus seeking excellence and meaning) from the outcome. Hedonic motives brought more immediate positive feeling and eudaimonic motives brought more meaning, and people who pursued both tended to report the richest well-being.

Trap. "Eudaimonic" does not mean "unpleasant but good for you", and "hedonic" does not mean "selfish". Epicurus recommended a quiet, moderate life full of friendship. The distinction is about what well-being consists of (feeling good versus functioning well).

11.3 Subjective well-being: Diener's model and how it is measured

Warner Wilson's 1967 review pictured the "happy person" as young, healthy, well educated, well paid, extraverted, optimistic, religious and married, a portrait later research largely qualified. Ed Diener's 1984 review "Subjective well-being" gave the field its central definition: a person's cognitive and emotional evaluation of their own life. It has three components.

  1. Life satisfaction is the cognitive component: a considered judgement of how one's life measures up to one's own standards.
  2. Positive affect is how often a person feels pleasant emotions and moods, such as joy, interest and contentment.
  3. Negative affect is how often a person feels unpleasant emotions and moods, such as sadness, anxiety and anger.

A person high in subjective well-being is satisfied with life, often feels positive emotion and seldom feels negative emotion. Diener stressed three hallmarks. The construct is subjective: the person, not an expert, judges whether their life is going well. It includes positive measures, not only the absence of distress. And it is a global assessment of life as a whole. In 1999 Diener, Eunkook Suh, Richard Lucas and Heidi Smith added a fourth element, domain satisfactions: satisfaction with work, family, health, finances, leisure and oneself.

The Satisfaction With Life Scale

Life satisfaction is usually measured with the Satisfaction With Life Scale by Ed Diener, Robert Emmons, Randy Larsen and Sharon Griffin (1985). It has five statements, each rated from 1 (strongly disagree) to 7 (strongly agree), such as "In most ways my life is close to my ideal" and "If I could live my life over, I would change almost nothing". Scores run from 5 to 35. No item names a life domain. That is deliberate: each respondent weighs whatever matters to them, so a monk and a businessman are each judged against their own ideals.

The Positive and Negative Affect Schedule

The emotional components are most often measured with the Positive and Negative Affect Schedule (PANAS) by David Watson, Lee Anna Clark and Auke Tellegen (1988). It lists twenty emotion words. Ten measure positive affect (such as interested, enthusiastic, inspired, alert) and ten measure negative affect (such as distressed, guilty, hostile, afraid). Each is rated from 1 (very slightly or not at all) to 5 (extremely), giving two separate scores that each run from 10 to 50. The instructions can ask about "right now", "the past week" or "in general", so the same schedule can measure a passing state or a stable trait. High positive affect means energy and enthusiasm, low positive affect means lethargy, high negative affect means distress, and low negative affect means calm.

The independence of positive and negative affect

The most surprising finding here is that positive and negative affect are not two ends of one line. In 1969 Norman Bradburn found that his positive and negative affect items correlated only weakly and were predicted by different life circumstances. Ed Diener and Robert Emmons (1984) showed when this holds. In any single moment the two are inversely related, because it is hard to feel intense joy and intense misery at once. But when people report how often they have felt each over weeks or months, the two frequencies become largely independent. Knowing how much sadness someone has had tells you little about how much joy they have had.

The clinical payoff is direct. Lee Anna Clark and David Watson's tripartite model (1991) proposed that anxiety and depression share high negative affect, while low positive affect is specific to depression. So treatment that reduces negative affect does not automatically bring positive affect back. After cognitive behaviour therapy a patient may no longer feel hopeless yet still feel very little: the negative affect has gone but the anhedonia (loss of pleasure) remains. That is the case for adding interventions that build positive emotion.

Trap. Low negative affect is not the same as high positive affect. Over time they are separate dimensions, which is why the PANAS gives two scores and never one combined "mood" score.

11.4 Psychological well-being: Ryff's six dimensions

Carol Ryff's 1989 paper "Happiness is everything, or is it?" criticised subjective well-being research for lacking theory: its measures had been adopted because they were convenient, not because anyone had worked out what positive functioning is. Ryff went back to the theorists of the healthy personality: Abraham Maslow's self-actualisation, Carl Rogers's fully functioning person, Carl Jung's individuation, Gordon Allport's maturity, Erik Erikson's psychosocial stages and Marie Jahoda's criteria of positive mental health (1958). Where their accounts converged she found six dimensions, measured by her Scales of Psychological Well-Being. Ryff and Corey Keyes (1995) supported the six-factor structure in a national American sample.

DimensionWhat it meansA high scorerA low scorer
Self-acceptanceA positive, realistic attitude towards oneself and one's pastAccepts both good and bad qualities; feels positive about the life lived so farDissatisfied with self; wishes to be different
Positive relations with othersWarm, trusting, close relationshipsCapable of empathy, affection and intimacyFew close relationships; finds it hard to be warm; isolated
AutonomySelf-determination and independence of judgementResists social pressure; evaluates self by personal standardsRelies on others' judgements for important decisions; conforms
Environmental masteryThe capacity to manage one's life and surroundingsHandles everyday affairs competently; creates settings that suit personal needsStruggles with daily life; feels unable to change the surroundings
Purpose in lifeGoals, direction and a sense that life is meaningfulHas aims and a sense of directionLacks meaning; few goals
Personal growthContinued development and realisation of potentialSees self as growing; open to new experienceFeels stagnant, bored and uninterested in life

The six dimensions do not move together across the life span. In Ryff's cross-sectional data, environmental mastery and autonomy tended to be higher in midlife than in young adulthood, while purpose in life and personal growth tended to be lower in old age, a pattern one happiness score would hide. Giovanni Fava built well-being therapy (1998) on the model: a short, structured psychotherapy that targets the problems left behind after depression or anxiety has otherwise responded to treatment.

There are two criticisms. Some later factor analyses found self-acceptance, environmental mastery, purpose and growth so intertwined that it is unclear they are four separate things. And the model leans individualist. Autonomy, defined as resisting social pressure, may matter less in collectivist settings, including much of India, where acting in harmony with family and community is itself a sign of maturity.

Trap. Ryff's model is psychological well-being (eudaimonic); Diener's is subjective well-being (hedonic). Both rely on self-report, so "subjective" refers to the hedonic content, not to the fact that people answer questionnaires.

11.5 Social well-being: Keyes's five dimensions and the mental health continuum

Corey Keyes noticed that both Diener's and Ryff's models are private: they describe how a person feels and functions as an individual. But people live in communities, and sociology had long described the harm of anomie (normlessness) and alienation. In "Social well-being" (1998) Keyes defined social well-being as a person's appraisal of how they are doing in society, with five dimensions.

  • Social integration is the sense of belonging to a community. A high scorer feels part of their neighbourhood or workplace; a low scorer feels like an outsider.
  • Social acceptance is a trusting attitude towards people in general, as opposed to seeing others as unreliable or hostile.
  • Social contribution is the belief that one has something of value to give to society.
  • Social actualisation is the belief that society has potential and is moving in a good direction.
  • Social coherence is the sense that the social world is understandable and predictable, as opposed to baffling.

Keyes then combined all three traditions. His mental health continuum (2002) treats emotional well-being (the hedonic components), psychological well-being (Ryff's six) and social well-being (his five) as the symptoms of mental health, just as low mood and loss of interest are symptoms of depression. Someone who reports high emotional well-being and most of the functioning items nearly every day is flourishing, languishing is the mirror image, and those in between are moderately mentally healthy. Behind this sits the two continua model, in which mental illness and mental health are related but distinct dimensions. A person can have no diagnosable disorder and still be languishing, which Keyes linked with more impairment, while a person with a chronic condition can still flourish in many respects. In Indian practice, where family, neighbourhood and religious community shape so much of daily life, the social dimensions are often where a patient's well-being is won or lost.

Key. Emotional (Diener), psychological (Ryff) and social (Keyes) well-being are three families of positive symptoms. Flourishing is a positive diagnosis, not just the absence of a negative one.

11.6 Seligman: from authentic happiness to PERMA

Martin Seligman, who launched positive psychology during his 1998 presidency of the American Psychological Association, has offered two theories of well-being. Authentic Happiness (2002) described three routes. The pleasant life means maximising positive emotion about the past (gratitude), the present (savouring) and the future (optimism and hope). The engaged life means absorption, the state Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi called flow, reached by using one's signature strengths in work, love and play. Seligman separated pleasures, raw feelings with shortcuts such as chocolate, from gratifications, absorbing activities with no shortcut. The meaningful life means using one's strengths in the service of something larger than oneself. Christopher Peterson, Nansook Park and Seligman (2005) found that engagement and meaning predicted life satisfaction more strongly than pleasure.

In Flourish (2011) Seligman replaced this with well-being theory, for three reasons. In everyday speech "happiness" means a cheerful mood, so the theory kept being read as a theory of mood. Its gold-standard measure, life satisfaction, is itself coloured by mood at the moment of answering. And the three routes left out things people pursue for their own sake, especially achievement and relationships. The goal changed from raising life satisfaction to increasing flourishing. Well-being became a construct with several measurable elements, each of which had to contribute to well-being, be pursued by many people for its own sake, and be measurable independently of the others.

Element of PERMAWhat it meansExample
Positive emotionJoy, gratitude, contentment, hopeSavouring tea with a friend
EngagementAbsorption and flow, usually judged afterwardsLosing track of time on a hard case formulation
RelationshipsPositive connections; Peterson's summary of the field was "other people matter"Having someone to call at two in the morning
MeaningBelonging to and serving something bigger than the selfVolunteering at a community mental health camp
AccomplishmentAchievement and mastery pursued for their own sakeClearing an entrance examination after sustained effort

PERMA is usually measured with the PERMA-Profiler by Julie Butler and Margaret Kern (2016), which asks three questions per element. Critics note that the choice of five elements is somewhat arbitrary (why not health, or autonomy?), that PERMA describes what well-being contains rather than what causes it, and that a 2018 comparison involving Todd Kashdan found PERMA and standard subjective well-being measures nearly indistinguishable, raising the question of whether PERMA is a new construct or a new label.

Trap. Authentic happiness theory (2002) has three elements and its outcome is life satisfaction. Well-being theory (2011) has five (PERMA) and its outcome is flourishing. The additions are relationships and accomplishment.

11.7 Can happiness change? Adaptation, set points and genes

Harry Helson's adaptation-level theory says we judge a stimulus against a neutral point set by recent experience, which is why lukewarm water feels hot to a hand just out of cold water. Philip Brickman and Donald Campbell applied this to happiness in 1971. Good and bad events cause temporary swings, but people adapt and return to neutral, so individuals and societies trying to become happier are on a hedonic treadmill: every gain raises the neutral point.

The famous test was the 1978 study by Brickman, Dan Coates and Ronnie Janoff-Bulman, "Lottery winners and accident victims: Is happiness relative?". Big winners of the Illinois state lottery were not significantly happier than a comparison group, and they took less pleasure in everyday things such as talking with a friend. The authors proposed contrast (the high of winning makes ordinary pleasures seem smaller) and habituation (the thrill fades as wealth becomes normal). People recently paralysed in accidents rated their present happiness lower than the comparison group, though still above the midpoint of the scale.

Trap. The study is often retold as "people with paralysis are as happy as everyone else". It did not show that. The accident victims were less happy than the controls, only less unhappy than people would predict, and the samples were small.

Set point theory and twin studies

David Lykken and Auke Tellegen's "Happiness is a stochastic phenomenon" (1996) used the Minnesota Twin Registry. Identical twins, even those reared apart, were far more alike in well-being than fraternal twins, and roughly half the variation in a single well-being measurement was attributed to genes. Income, education, marital status and religious commitment explained very little. They suggested that trying to be happier might be as futile as trying to be taller, a pessimism Lykken later softened. A 2015 meta-analysis by Meike Bartels put heritability nearer a third.

Trap. Heritability describes variation across a population in a given environment. It says nothing about how "genetic" one person's happiness is, and it does not mean fixed: height is highly heritable, yet average heights rose as nutrition improved.

The revision: beyond the hedonic treadmill

In "Beyond the hedonic treadmill" (2006) Ed Diener, Richard Lucas and Christie Napa Scollon revised adaptation theory in five ways. Set points are not neutral: most people are mildly happy. People have different set points, partly because of temperament. A person may have several set points, since life satisfaction, positive affect and negative affect move independently. Set points can change. And people differ in how much they adapt. The key evidence came from panel studies that follow the same people every year, especially the German Socio-Economic Panel. After unemployment, life satisfaction fell and did not fully recover even after re-employment. Widowhood took years to recover from, severe disability produced lasting drops, and marriage gave only a brief average boost.

Key. Adaptation is real but incomplete and uneven. A set point is better thought of as a range with a default position than as a thermostat that cannot be reset.

11.8 The "happiness pie" and why it has been criticised

Sonja Lyubomirsky, Kennon Sheldon and David Schkade's "Pursuing happiness: The architecture of sustainable change" (2005) was an answer to the treadmill. Their sustainable happiness model said long-term happiness comes from a genetic set point (about 50 percent), life circumstances (about 10 percent) and intentional activity (about 40 percent). The 50 percent came from twin studies, the 10 percent from reviews showing demographic circumstances explain little, and the 40 percent was what remained. Intentional activities could be behavioural (acts of kindness), cognitive (counting blessings) or volitional (pursuing meaningful goals). Such activities resist adaptation, they argued, because they are episodic, effortful and can be varied, and their effect depends on timing and on how well the activity fits the person.

Nicholas Brown and Julia Rohrer's critique "Easy as (happiness) pie?" (2019) set out the main problems.

  • Variance percentages describe differences between people in a population. They cannot tell any one person that 40 percent of their happiness is under their control.
  • The 40 percent was never measured. It is what was left after subtracting 50 and 10 from 100, so it also contains measurement error and every unmeasured influence.
  • The 10 percent is too low, because only a few circumstances were measured, each with error, and circumstances interact with genes instead of simply adding to them.

In a 2021 reappraisal Sheldon and Lyubomirsky accepted much of this criticism but maintained that deliberate activities can raise well-being. Meta-analyses of positive psychology interventions support that modest claim: the effects are real but small.

Trap. Do not write "40 percent of your happiness is under your control" as a finding. The defensible statement is that well-being is partly heritable, partly shaped by circumstances, and can be modestly raised by sustained activity.

11.9 Money and happiness

Richard Easterlin's 1974 paper identified the Easterlin paradox. Within a country, richer people report being happier. Yet as national income in the United States rose substantially after the Second World War, average happiness did not. His explanation was relative income and rising aspirations: what matters is how your income compares with others' and with what you expect, and when everyone grows richer together, those comparisons cancel out. Betsey Stevenson and Justin Wolfers (2008) challenged this with larger international data, finding that life satisfaction rises with the logarithm of income and never levels off. A logarithmic relationship means each doubling brings about the same gain: going from 20,000 to 40,000 rupees a month matters about as much as going from 2 lakh to 4 lakh. Easterlin replied that over a decade or more national growth and happiness still did not move together.

Daniel Kahneman and Angus Deaton (2010) analysed about 450,000 survey responses from American residents. Life evaluation, measured on Hadley Cantril's ladder (0 for the worst possible life, 10 for the best), rose steadily with income. Emotional well-being, measured by questions about yesterday's emotions, flattened above roughly 75,000 dollars a year. Low income also made misfortunes such as divorce and ill health more painful. Matthew Killingsworth (2021) challenged the plateau with experience sampling: more than a million momentary reports from over 30,000 employed American adults showed experienced well-being still rising above 75,000 dollars. Killingsworth, Kahneman and Barbara Mellers, as a neutral arbiter, then ran an adversarial collaboration (2023), in which opponents design an analysis together. For the unhappiest minority, happiness rose with income up to about 100,000 dollars and then levelled off. For the majority it kept rising. The earlier plateau appeared partly because Kahneman and Deaton's yes-or-no questions mainly captured the absence of unhappiness.

Erzo Luttmer (2005) found that, with one's own income held constant, higher earnings among neighbours went with lower happiness, which is direct evidence for relative income. Tim Kasser and Richard Ryan (1993) showed that people who prioritise extrinsic goals (wealth, fame, image) report lower well-being than those who prioritise intrinsic goals (growth, relationships, community), because extrinsic goals do little for basic psychological needs and invite endless comparison.

11.10 Other correlates of happiness

Diener's 1999 review concluded that demographic factors together explain only a small share of differences in well-being. Most findings are correlations, and the arrow can point both ways, since happy people are more likely to marry, stay healthy and get hired.

Relationships and health. Ed Diener and Martin Seligman's "Very happy people" (2002) found that all of the happiest tenth of a student sample had strong social relationships, which the authors called necessary but not sufficient for high happiness. Married people report higher well-being on average, partly because happier people are more likely to marry, and the boost from marriage fades. The quality of a relationship matters more than marital status, and loneliness is among the strongest negative correlates. Self-rated health predicts well-being better than doctors' ratings, because how people cope with illness matters as much as the illness.

Age. David Blanchflower and Andrew Oswald (2008) reported a U-shape, with well-being falling to a low point in middle age and then rising. This is contested: the U is clearest after statistically controlling for income, marriage and health, some longitudinal studies find a flatter pattern, and generational differences can pass for effects of age. Laura Carstensen's socioemotional selectivity theory explains later-life gains: as people see their remaining time as limited, they prioritise emotionally meaningful goals. Well-being often drops steeply in the last years before death, which is called terminal decline.

Religion, work and personality. Religiosity has a modest positive link with well-being, which Ed Diener, Louis Tay and David Myers (2011) found stronger in religious societies and in countries with hard living conditions. Unemployment is one of the few events with lasting effects on life satisfaction. Personality is among the strongest predictors: Paul Costa and Robert McCrae (1980) found that extraversion predicted positive affect and neuroticism predicted negative affect, mapping two traits onto the two independent affect dimensions.

Culture. In individualist cultures happiness is often understood as personal achievement and excited positive feeling. In collectivist cultures it more often means relational harmony and calm. Eunkook Suh, Ed Diener, Shigehiro Oishi and Harry Triandis (1998) found that emotions strongly predicted life satisfaction in individualist nations, while in collectivist nations social approval also mattered. Indian thought distinguishes sukha (pleasure and ease) from ananda (a deeper bliss), and among the four aims of life it places kama (pleasure) alongside artha (material security), dharma (duty and virtue) and moksha (liberation), a balance that resembles the hedonic and eudaimonic traditions held together. The World Happiness Report, first published in 2012 and edited by John Helliwell, Richard Layard and Jeffrey Sachs among others, ranks countries by average Cantril ladder scores and explains the differences through income, social support, healthy life expectancy, freedom, generosity and perceived corruption. Nordic countries regularly top it. India has usually been placed in the lower part of the ranking, sometimes below some of its neighbours, and that position is debated because a single ladder question may not capture Indian ideas of well-being.

11.11 Measurement problems and better methods

Because subjective well-being is a person's own evaluation, self-report cannot be avoided. Its validity is respectable: self-reports agree with ratings by family and friends and with how often people smile genuinely. But it has three weaknesses.

The first is mood and context. Norbert Schwarz and Gerald Clore (1983) telephoned people on sunny and rainy days. Those called on sunny days reported higher life satisfaction, unless the interviewer first mentioned the weather, in which case the difference vanished because people then put their mood down to the weather. People often build a satisfaction judgement on the spot from whatever comes to mind most easily. Later large-sample studies, however, found the weather effect to be very small.

The second is cultural response styles. Some groups tend to agree with any statement, some favour the ends of a scale and others the midpoint, modesty norms differ, and words such as "happy" do not translate cleanly. Comparing cultures therefore requires testing whether a questionnaire measures the same thing in each group (measurement invariance).

The third is the gap between the remembering self and the experiencing self, which Kahneman emphasised. Memories are dominated by an episode's most intense moment and its ending (the peak-end rule) and largely ignore its length (duration neglect).

Two methods get closer to experience. The experience sampling method, developed by Reed Larson and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi, signals people at random moments to report what they are doing and feeling. With it, Matthew Killingsworth and Daniel Gilbert (2010) found that minds wandered nearly half the time and that mind-wandering predicted lower happiness. The day reconstruction method of Kahneman, Alan Krueger and colleagues (2004) is less demanding: people reconstruct the previous day as a diary of episodes, noting what they did, with whom and how they felt. In their study of 909 working women in Texas, socialising and intimate relations were the most pleasant activities and commuting among the least.

Key. A global satisfaction score, a momentary report and a reconstructed day can paint different pictures of one life, so good assessment combines methods.

11.12 Case: the same score, two different lives

Case.

Rohan, 34, is a bank manager in Pune with a good salary, a comfortable flat, a stable marriage and weekends of films and dinners out. Meera, 29, is a clinical psychology trainee in a district mental health programme. She earns little, travels long hours and is often exhausted, but she chose this path against family pressure and says she has never felt more herself. Both score 27 on the Satisfaction With Life Scale.

Diener's model shows the first difference. On the PANAS, Rohan scores high on positive affect and low on negative affect, while Meera is moderate on positive affect and fairly high on negative affect. They reach the same satisfaction by different routes: Rohan judges his life against comfort and security, Meera against doing work she believes in.

Ryff's model shows the second. Rohan is high on environmental mastery and self-acceptance but low on purpose and personal growth, and he admits his major choices were made to meet his parents' expectations (low autonomy). Meera is high on purpose, growth and autonomy and has warm relations with colleagues, but she is low on environmental mastery.

The case teaches three things. A single satisfaction score hides profiles that differ in clinically important ways. The two traditions capture different strengths: Rohan's well-being is mainly hedonic and Meera's mainly eudaimonic. And the profiles point to different interventions. Rohan might benefit from clarifying his values and setting self-chosen goals; without them he risks drifting towards what Keyes calls languishing. Meera needs protection against burnout (supervision, a manageable workload, recovery time and scheduled pleasure) so that her negative affect does not wear away the purpose that sustains her.

Recap.

The hedonic tradition (Epicurus, Bentham) equates well-being with pleasure and satisfaction, and the eudaimonic tradition (Aristotle, Waterman, Ryan and Deci) equates it with virtue, growth and living in line with one's true self; the two overlap heavily in data but are worth separating. Diener's subjective well-being combines life satisfaction, positive affect and negative affect, measured with the Satisfaction With Life Scale and the PANAS, and the two kinds of affect are largely independent over time. Ryff's psychological well-being has six dimensions, Keyes's social well-being has five, and Keyes combined all three into a continuum from languishing to flourishing. Seligman moved from three routes to authentic happiness (2002) to the five PERMA elements (2011). Adaptation is real but incomplete, well-being is partly heritable, and the happiness pie has been heavily criticised. Money predicts life evaluation and, for most people, emotional well-being too, while relative income and materialism complicate the picture. Relationships, health, personality, work, religion, age and culture all correlate with well-being, and measurement must deal with mood, response styles and the gap between remembered and experienced happiness.
