Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Distinguish the hedonic and eudaimonic views of well-being, tracing each to its philosophical roots and modern models. Why has the distinction been criticised, and why is it still useful?
  2. Describe Diener's model of subjective well-being and how each component is measured. What does the independence of positive and negative affect mean clinically?
  3. Explain Ryff's six dimensions and Keyes's five. How does the mental health continuum combine them, and what does the two continua model imply for practice?
  4. Critically compare Seligman's authentic happiness theory with PERMA. Why did he change it, and what criticisms has PERMA faced?
  5. "Happiness is largely fixed." Evaluate this with reference to the hedonic treadmill, the lottery study, twin research, the revised set point theory and the happiness pie.
  6. Discuss money and happiness, covering the Easterlin paradox, Kahneman and Deaton, the 2023 adversarial collaboration, relative income and materialism.
  7. What are the problems with measuring happiness by self-report, and how do experience sampling and the day reconstruction method address them?

12 Positive emotions, emotional intelligence and savouring

For most of the twentieth century the psychology of emotion was really the psychology of fear, anger, sadness and disgust. Those emotions cause visible trouble and fill clinics, while joy, gratitude and calm were treated as pleasant extras. This chapter covers the correction of that imbalance. It starts from what an emotion is, then teaches Barbara Fredrickson's broaden-and-build theory of why positive emotions exist, the "positivity ratio" that grew out of it and was later shown to rest on invalid mathematics, the evidence linking positive emotion to health, ten specific positive emotions (with gratitude and awe in depth), and savouring, the skill of making good experiences last.

The second half moves from feeling emotions to handling them: emotional intelligence and its critique, James Gross's process model of emotion regulation, and cultural differences in which feelings are prized, including the Indian theory of rasa. The positive psychology texts (Baumgardner and Crothers; the Oxford Handbook) treat this material as core.

12.1 What an emotion is

An emotion is a brief, coordinated response of the whole person to an event that matters for his or her goals or well-being. It is about something (you are afraid of the dog, grateful to your teacher), it is brief (seconds to minutes, unless the situation continues or you keep re-living it), and it is coordinated, meaning several systems change together. Researchers usually name five components.

  • Subjective feeling is the conscious experience of the emotion, measured by self-report.
  • Physiological change is the shift in heart rate, breathing, muscle tension and stress hormones that prepares the body for action.
  • Expressive behaviour is the change in face, voice and posture that signals the emotion to others.
  • Cognitive appraisal is the evaluation of what the event means for you. Richard Lazarus distinguished primary appraisal (is this relevant to my well-being: a harm, a threat or a challenge?) from secondary appraisal (can I cope?). A friend's silence appraised as rejection produces sadness; appraised as rudeness, anger.
  • Action tendency, emphasised by Nico Frijda, is the readiness to act in a particular way: fear urges escape, anger confrontation, disgust expulsion.

At night you see a coiled shape on the path, appraise it as a snake, and your heart pounds, your eyes widen, you feel fear and you jump back. Then you see it is a rope: the appraisal changes and every other component collapses into relief. Change the meaning of an event and the whole response changes, the idea behind "cognitive change" in section 12.10.

Emotion, mood and affect

A mood is a longer-lasting, lower-intensity state with no particular object; you can be irritable without being irritated at anyone. Affect is the umbrella term for any pleasant or unpleasant feeling state. James Russell's "core affect" describes it by two dimensions, valence (pleasant to unpleasant) and arousal (activated to deactivated), arranged in a circle, the circumplex model: excitement is pleasant and high in arousal, calm pleasant and low, anxiety unpleasant and high, sadness unpleasant and low.

TermDuration and intensityObjectExample
EmotionSeconds to minutes; can be intenseA specific event or personAnger when a classmate takes credit for your work
MoodHours to days; usually mildDiffuse, often no clear causeA low, flat Sunday evening
AffectUmbrella term covering bothDescribed by valence and arousalPositive and negative affect scores on a questionnaire

The most used measure is the Positive and Negative Affect Schedule (PANAS), published in 1988 by David Watson, Lee Anna Clark and Auke Tellegen: people rate how strongly they have felt ten positive adjectives (such as "interested" and "enthusiastic") and ten negative ones (such as "distressed" and "hostile"). Its key finding is that positive and negative affect behave as two relatively independent dimensions, not opposite ends of one line.

Trap. Positive affect is not simply the absence of negative affect. A person can be high on both (an exciting but stressful first week in a new job) or low on both (a flat, empty week). Removing distress does not automatically produce joy, which is why positive psychology argues that positive emotions need study, and sometimes cultivation, in their own right.

12.2 Basic emotions, and why positive emotions were neglected

Paul Ekman took up a question raised by Charles Darwin in The Expression of the Emotions in Man and Animals (1872): are some emotional expressions universal products of evolution? In the late 1960s Ekman and Wallace Friesen studied the Fore people of Papua New Guinea, who had had very little contact with outside media; told a short story and shown faces, they chose the face Western observers would pick. Ekman argued for a small set of basic emotions, each with a universal facial signal, rapid onset, short duration and automatic appraisal: happiness, sadness, fear, anger, surprise and disgust, with contempt later proposed as a seventh. He and Friesen also built the Facial Action Coding System (1978), which describes any expression by the muscle movements that compose it.

Only one of the six, happiness, is clearly positive, and all positive emotions share one signal, the smile. A genuine enjoyment smile also recruits the muscle around the eyes; Ekman called it the Duchenne smile after Guillaume Duchenne, the nineteenth-century French neurologist who described it, and polite smiles usually lack it. Ekman later widened his list to include positive states such as amusement, contentment, pride and relief.

Barbara Fredrickson's 1998 paper "What good are positive emotions?" explained the neglect. First, positive emotions are fewer and less differentiated, without distinct faces or physiological patterns. Second, theories built around specific action tendencies (fear means flee) gave positive emotions only vague ones such as "aimless activation", making them look pointless. Third, post-war psychology concentrated on mental illness. Fourth, as Roy Baumeister and colleagues showed in "Bad is stronger than good" (2001), negative events generally have larger effects than positive ones of equal size, so researchers attended first to the bad. The launch of positive psychology in a 2000 special issue of the American Psychologist edited by Martin Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi gave positive emotion research a home, and Fredrickson's theory became its flagship.

12.3 Broaden-and-build theory

Who gave us this. Barbara Fredrickson: "What good are positive emotions?" (Review of General Psychology, 1998) and "The role of positive emotions in positive psychology: The broaden-and-build theory of positive emotions" (American Psychologist, 2001). Popular books: Positivity (2009) and Love 2.0 (2013).

Fredrickson's answer was that positive emotions work on a longer timescale. Negative emotions solve immediate problems: facing a predator, it pays to narrow thought and action to escape. Positive emotions usually arise when we are safe, when it pays to explore, play and bond, building skills and relationships that help later. The theory has two central hypotheses, broadening and building, and two extensions, undoing and the upward spiral.

The broadening hypothesis

A thought-action repertoire is the range of thoughts and actions that come to mind as possible in a given moment. Negative emotions narrow it; positive emotions broaden it. Joy creates the urge to play and be creative, interest the urge to explore, contentment the urge to savour and integrate one's circumstances into a new view of self and world, and pride the urge to share news and imagine further achievements.

The key experiments were published by Fredrickson and Christine Branigan in 2005. Participants were randomly assigned to watch a short film clip inducing amusement, contentment, a neutral state, anger or fear. In a global-local visual processing task, participants saw a figure such as a triangle made of small squares and chose which of two figures it more resembled: one matching its overall shape (a triangle of small triangles) or its small elements (a square of small squares). Choosing the global match reflects broader attention. Participants who had watched the positive clips chose global matches more often than those in the neutral condition. In an adapted Twenty Statements Test, participants completed as many "I would like to..." statements as they could; the positive groups listed more actions than the neutral group, the negative groups fewer. Positive emotion widened both what people noticed and what they felt like doing, extending Alice Isen's earlier findings that even mild positive affect, induced by a small gift, made people more creative problem-solvers.

The building hypothesis

Broadened moments are fleeting, but the building hypothesis states that what they prompt accumulates into durable personal resources that outlast the emotions: physical resources (strength, coordination, health, built for example through the rough-and-tumble play that joy prompts in children), intellectual resources (knowledge and flexible problem-solving, built through exploration), social resources (friendships and support networks, built through shared amusement, gratitude and love) and psychological resources (resilience, optimism, a sense of identity and purpose). In the strongest test (2008), Fredrickson and colleagues randomly assigned working adults to a loving-kindness meditation workshop or a waiting list. Meditators' daily positive emotions rose gradually, predicting growth in resources such as mindfulness, purpose in life and social support, which in turn predicted greater life satisfaction and fewer depressive symptoms: the predicted chain from emotions to resources to well-being.

The undoing hypothesis

Positive emotions can also loosen the hold negative emotions have on body and mind. In a study published in 2000, Fredrickson and colleagues told participants they had one minute to prepare a speech that would be videotaped and judged by peers, which reliably raises anxiety, heart rate and blood pressure. Participants then watched a calming film (such as waves on a beach), an amusing one, a neutral one or a sad one, and were told they would not have to speak after all. Those shown the calming or amusing films returned to their resting cardiovascular level fastest; those shown the sad film were slowest. The positive films did little to heart rate at rest, so they were undoing prior arousal rather than merely sedating. This links to resilience: Michele Tugade and Fredrickson (2004) found that resilient people recovered faster from stress partly because they felt more positive emotion during it, and resilient American students after the attacks of 11 September 2001 felt grief but also gratitude and love, which helped protect them from depression.

The upward spiral

Broadening and building feed each other. In 2002 Fredrickson and Thomas Joiner assessed students twice, five weeks apart. Positive emotion predicted later "broad-minded coping" (stepping back to consider different ways to handle a problem), and broad-minded coping predicted later positive emotion. This upward spiral mirrors the downward spiral of depression.

Trap. The theory does not say negative emotions are bad; narrowing is adaptive under real threat and helpful for detail work such as proofreading. Nor does every positive state broaden: Philip Gable and Eddie Harmon-Jones (2008) showed that positive states high in approach motivation, such as desire on seeing pictures of desserts, narrowed attention. The clearest broadening comes from low-urgency states such as amusement and contentment.
Key. Negative emotions narrow the thought-action repertoire to meet immediate threats; positive emotions broaden it, and the broadened moments build durable physical, intellectual, social and psychological resources. Positive emotions also undo lingering negative arousal and can start upward spirals.

12.4 The positivity ratio: the rise and fall of a magic number

In 2005 Fredrickson and Marcial Losada, a management consultant who had coded the conversations of business teams, published "Positive affect and the complex dynamics of human flourishing" in the American Psychologist. They took differential equations devised by the meteorologist Edward Lorenz to model atmospheric convection (the equations behind the "butterfly" diagrams of chaos theory) and claimed they described the dynamics of human emotion. From this model they derived a critical positivity ratio: people whose ratio of positive to negative emotions exceeded about 2.9 to 1 (stated to four decimal places, 2.9013) would flourish, and those below would languish, with an upper limit of about 11.6 to 1 beyond which flourishing broke down. Fredrickson's Positivity (2009) popularised a "three to one" rule, which spread through coaching and management training.

In 2013 Nicholas Brown, a mature student on a master's course in applied positive psychology at the University of East London, doubted that a human emotional tipping point could be known to four decimal places. With Alan Sokal, a physicist known for a 1996 hoax exposing the careless use of scientific language in parts of cultural studies, and the psychologist Harris Friedman, he published "The complex dynamics of wishful thinking: The critical positivity ratio" in the same journal. They showed that nothing justified applying equations of fluid convection to emotions; that the model's settings appear to have been carried over from Lorenz's own work rather than estimated from psychological data, so the ratio came from arbitrary choices; that the data could never have tested such a model; and that the precision was absurd, since self-report cannot distinguish 2.9013 from 3.

The journal then issued a formal correction declaring the modelling element of the 2005 paper, and the ratio and upper limit derived from it, invalid and withdrawn. Fredrickson replied the same year ("Updated thinking on positivity ratios"), setting aside the tipping point while maintaining that, within bounds, more positive relative to negative emotion goes with better outcomes. What survives is the broader programme on positive emotions; what does not is any magic number.

Trap. Do not confuse the discredited ratio with John Gottman's observation that stable couples discussing conflict in his laboratory showed roughly five positive interactions for every negative one. That is a descriptive finding from his samples, not a mathematical law, and neither figure is a clinical target.
Why it matters. A famous claim passed peer review in a flagship journal and was overturned by a student who questioned a result that seemed too neat. Distrust precise numbers wrapped in unchecked mathematics.

12.5 Positive emotions and health: the Nun Study and its cautions

The best-known evidence linking positive emotion to longer life comes from the Nun Study, a long-running study of ageing and Alzheimer's disease among the School Sisters of Notre Dame in the United States, led by the epidemiologist David Snowdon. The order's archives held short autobiographies that each sister had written on taking her vows, in the 1930s and 1940s, mostly in her early twenties. Deborah Danner, David Snowdon and Wallace Friesen (2001) coded 180 of these for positive and negative emotional content (expressions of happiness, gratitude, love and hope, or of sadness and fear) and related them to survival some six decades later. Positive content was strongly associated with longevity: the quarter of sisters with the most positive writing lived several years longer on average than the quarter with the least, and had a markedly lower risk of death at any given age. Negative content was not reliably related to survival.

The convent acted as a natural experiment: the sisters shared housing, diet, medical care and work, did not marry or bear children, and rarely smoked or drank, and the writing came decades before death, so later illness could not have shaped it. But it is correlational: temperament, early health or verbal ability could explain both cheerful writing and long life (Snowdon's earlier work had linked idea-dense writing in the same autobiographies with lower later risk of Alzheimer's disease). The sample was all female, Catholic and American, and counting emotion words is crude.

The wider literature is supportive but cautious. Sarah Pressman and Sheldon Cohen's 2005 review linked positive affect with less illness and longer survival among older people in the community, but found mixed results among the seriously ill. In Cohen's quarantine studies, volunteers with a more positive emotional style were less likely to develop a verified cold after exposure to the virus. The strongest caution is reverse causation: illness makes people unhappy. The Million Women Study, a very large British study reported in The Lancet in 2016, found that once health at the start was taken into account, unhappiness itself did not predict higher mortality.

Trap. Never write that the Nun Study "proves" positive emotions cause longer life; it shows a strong prospective association in an unusually well-controlled sample. It is also unsupported, and cruel, to imply that patients who are not cheerful enough are to blame for their illness.

12.6 A map of positive emotions: Fredrickson's ten, with awe in focus

In Positivity (2009) Fredrickson described ten common positive emotions, each with its own trigger and form of broadening.

EmotionTypical triggerUrge it createsResource it tends to build
JoyThings going better than expected in a safe settingTo play and join inSkills gained through experience
GratitudeRecognising a benefit someone gave youTo give backSocial bonds and skills of care
SerenityCircumstances feel right and effortlessTo savour and integrateNew priorities and views of oneself
InterestSomething new or mysterious in a safe settingTo explore and learnKnowledge
HopeDire circumstances, fearing the worst yet yearning for betterTo plan for a better outcomeResilience and optimism
PrideCredit for a socially valued achievementTo share news and dream biggerAchievement motivation
AmusementNon-serious social incongruityTo share a laughFriendships
InspirationWitnessing human excellenceTo reach one's own higher groundMotivation for growth
AweGoodness or greatness on a grand scaleTo absorb and take it inSense of being part of something larger
LoveAny of the above within a safe, close connectionThe combined urges of the other nineSocial bonds and community

Hope is unusual because it arises inside adversity; it complements C. R. Snyder's cognitive hope theory of agency and pathways. Love, in Love 2.0 (2013), becomes "positivity resonance": micro-moments of shared positive emotion, synchrony and mutual care. Jessica Tracy and Richard Robins split pride into authentic pride ("I worked hard"), which predicts healthy outcomes, and hubristic pride ("I am brilliant"), linked to narcissism.

Awe

Dacher Keltner and Jonathan Haidt, in "Approaching awe, a moral, spiritual, and aesthetic emotion" (2003), proposed two central appraisals. Perceived vastness is encountering something much larger than oneself or one's usual frame of reference, whether physical (the Himalayas), social (a person of great power) or conceptual (a grand idea such as evolution). Need for accommodation, a term taken from Jean Piaget, means the experience cannot fit existing mental structures, which must be stretched or rebuilt. Further appraisals "flavour" awe: threat, beauty, ability, virtue and the supernatural. Arjuna's vision of Krishna's cosmic form in the Bhagavad Gita fits closely: vastness mixed with terror that shatters his existing understanding. Awe seems to shrink the self: in one study from Keltner's group, people who gazed for a minute at very tall trees, rather than a tall building, were then more helpful to an experimenter who dropped a handful of pens. Haidt also described elevation, the warm, uplifting feeling of witnessing moral beauty, which carries the urge to become a better person.

12.7 Gratitude in depth

As an emotion, gratitude arises when a person perceives a benefit from another agent that is valuable, intentionally given and costly to the giver: a senior who goes out of the way to find you a textbook evokes more gratitude than finding the book abandoned on a bench. As a trait, the grateful disposition described by Michael McCullough, Robert Emmons and Jo-Ann Tsang (2002) varies in intensity, frequency, span (across how many life areas) and density (toward how many people), measured with their six-item Gratitude Questionnaire. As a practice, it includes journals, letters and noting three good things daily. McCullough and colleagues (2001) called gratitude a moral affect: a moral barometer signalling kindness, a moral motive prompting good behaviour in return, and a moral reinforcer, since thanks makes the benefactor likelier to help again.

Who gave us this. Robert Emmons and Michael McCullough, "Counting blessings versus burdens: An experimental investigation of gratitude and subjective well-being in daily life", Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 2003.

The 2003 paper reported three studies.

  1. Study 1: university students kept a weekly record for ten weeks, listing either up to five things they were grateful for, hassles (daily irritations) or neutral life events. The gratitude group rated their lives as a whole more favourably, were more optimistic about the coming week, reported fewer physical complaints and spent more time exercising.
  2. Study 2: a daily two-week version compared gratitude with hassles and downward social comparison. Gratitude produced more positive affect than hassles and more reports of helping others, fitting the moral motive function.
  3. Study 3: adults with neuromuscular diseases practised gratitude daily for three weeks or served as controls. The gratitude group reported more positive and less negative affect, more life satisfaction and optimism, more connection with others, and longer, more refreshing sleep; their spouses independently rated them as having higher well-being.

In an online trial, Martin Seligman and colleagues (2005) found that the "gratitude visit" (delivering in person a letter of thanks) gave the largest immediate boost, fading within about a month, while "three good things" (writing nightly three things that went well and why) gave gains still present six months later. Sonja Lyubomirsky and colleagues found counting blessings once a week helped while three times a week did not, probably because it became a chore.

Gratitude seems to work by countering hedonic adaptation (noticing benefits we stop seeing), crowding out envy and building bonds. Three cautions: later meta-analyses find the effects real but modest, shrinking against well-designed alternative activities; gratitude differs from indebtedness, an uncomfortable obligation that motivates avoidance; and in many Indian families formally thanking a parent can feel oddly distant, so the practice may take the form of private appreciation or reciprocal care.

Trap. Gratitude practice is not a way to silence legitimate grievances. Telling someone in an abusive or exploitative situation to "be grateful for what you have" misuses research that studied ordinary people noticing genuine benefits.

12.8 Savouring

Who gave us this. Fred Bryant and Joseph Veroff, Savoring: A New Model of Positive Experience (2007), building on Bryant's earlier research and his Savoring Beliefs Inventory.

Bryant and Veroff define savouring as the capacity to attend to, appreciate and enhance the positive experiences in one's life. It is not the same as pleasure. Pleasure is the good feeling itself; savouring is awareness that one is feeling good combined with deliberate attention that prolongs or deepens it. Two people eat the same mango: one bolts it while scrolling on a phone, the other notices the smell, the texture and the childhood summers it recalls. Both had the pleasure; only the second savoured it. Savouring is the positive counterpart of coping, and it needs relative freedom from social and self-esteem worries, a present focus and mindful attention.

Savouring runs in three directions: reminiscence (the past), savouring the moment (the present) and anticipation (the future). The Savoring Beliefs Inventory measures perceived ability in each.

Bryant and Veroff classified savouring processes on two dimensions: experiential absorption versus cognitive reflection, and focus on the world versus the self.

Focus on the worldFocus on the self
Cognitive reflectionThanksgiving: reflecting on a blessing received (gratitude)Basking: receiving praise or congratulation (pride)
Experiential absorptionMarvelling: losing oneself in wonder (awe)Luxuriating: absorption in bodily pleasure (physical enjoyment)

Their Ways of Savoring Checklist identifies ten strategies.

  • Sharing with others: telling others or enjoying it together, a strong predictor of enjoyment.
  • Memory building: taking deliberate mental photographs or keepsakes.
  • Self-congratulation: telling oneself how proud one is.
  • Comparing: contrasting the present with worse times.
  • Sensory-perceptual sharpening: focusing on particular sensations.
  • Absorption: becoming completely immersed and trying not to think.
  • Behavioural expression: laughing, jumping or otherwise showing the feeling physically.
  • Temporal awareness: reminding oneself that the moment is fleeting.
  • Counting blessings: reminding oneself of one's good fortune.
  • Avoiding killjoy thinking: refusing to let thoughts about what is wrong or still to be done intrude.

The opposite of savouring is dampening: finding fault, worrying ahead or feeling undeserving. Dampening is common in depression and feeds anhedonia, the loss of pleasure, so savouring matters clinically. Temporal awareness is double-edged: graduating students prompted to think how little time remained made more of their last weeks and felt happier, because an ending can make a moment precious.

Key. Pleasure is the feeling; savouring is the deliberate attention that makes it last and count. It runs toward past, present and future, through four processes (thanksgiving, basking, marvelling, luxuriating) and learnable strategies.

12.9 Emotional intelligence: the scientific model, the popular models and the critique

The idea of intelligence about people is older than it seems: Edward Thorndike wrote of "social intelligence" in 1920, and Howard Gardner's Frames of Mind (1983) included interpersonal and intrapersonal intelligences. Emotional intelligence carries this into the domain of emotion.

Who gave us this. Peter Salovey and John Mayer, "Emotional intelligence", Imagination, Cognition and Personality, 1990; revised in 1997 into the four-branch model; the test was built with David Caruso.

Salovey and Mayer defined emotional intelligence as a genuine ability: perceiving emotions in oneself and others, telling them apart, and using that information to guide thinking and action. Their 1997 model has four branches, from basic to integrated: perceiving emotions in faces, voices, pictures and oneself; using emotions to facilitate thought, such as harnessing mild sadness for careful analysis or cheerfulness for brainstorming; understanding emotions, including how they blend and change (unaddressed annoyance can grow into rage); and managing emotions in oneself and others. Because this is an ability, it is measured by performance: the Mayer-Salovey-Caruso Emotional Intelligence Test (MSCEIT) has 141 items across eight tasks, two per branch (faces and pictures; facilitation and sensations; changes and blends; emotion management and emotional relationships). Answers are scored against either the consensus of a large normative sample or a panel of emotion experts, and the two methods agree closely.

Daniel Goleman, a psychologist and science journalist, made the concept famous with his bestseller Emotional Intelligence: Why It Can Matter More Than IQ (1995) described five domains: self-awareness, managing emotions, self-motivation, empathy and handling relationships. It is a mixed model, blending emotional abilities with personality traits and motivation such as optimism and persistence. Reuven Bar-On's model of "emotional-social intelligence", measured by his self-report Emotional Quotient Inventory (1997), popularised the term "emotional quotient" and is also mixed, with fifteen subscales in five areas: intrapersonal, interpersonal, stress management, adaptability and general mood.

K. V. Petrides, with Adrian Furnham, clarified that the models measure two different things. Ability emotional intelligence is actual emotion-related cognitive ability, measured by maximum-performance tests with right and wrong answers, such as the MSCEIT. Trait emotional intelligence, or trait emotional self-efficacy, is a set of self-perceptions about one's emotional life, measured by self-report, and belongs within personality. Petrides's Trait Emotional Intelligence Questionnaire groups fifteen facets under well-being, self-control, emotionality and sociability. On this analysis the mixed-model questionnaires measure trait emotional intelligence, and ability and trait measures correlate only weakly.

ModelTypeCore contentTypical measure
Salovey and Mayer (1990, 1997)AbilityPerceiving, using, understanding, managing emotionsMayer-Salovey-Caruso Emotional Intelligence Test (performance)
Goleman (1995)MixedSelf-awareness, self-management, motivation, empathy, social skillsRatings by self and colleagues
Bar-On (1997)MixedIntrapersonal, interpersonal, stress management, adaptability, general moodEmotional Quotient Inventory (self-report)
PetridesTraitSelf-perceived emotional abilities as part of personalityTrait Emotional Intelligence Questionnaire (self-report)

The scientific critique, set out by Gerald Matthews, Moshe Zeidner and Richard Roberts in Emotional Intelligence: Science and Myth (2002), makes five points. Popular definitions sprawled to include nearly every virtue other than IQ, and a concept covering everything explains nothing. Self-report scores overlap heavily with the Big Five personality traits and self-esteem. Once intelligence and personality are controlled, emotional intelligence adds only modestly to predicting performance. Consensus scoring defines "correct" as what most people say, and management items test knowing what to do, not doing it; self-report is easily faked in hiring. And the claim that it matters more than IQ was never established; general cognitive ability remains among the best predictors of academic and occupational success. Ability emotional intelligence, narrowly defined, is a real but modest construct; the popular promises outrun the evidence.

Trap. Emotional intelligence is not the same as being warm, nice or extraverted. In the ability model it is skill at reading and handling emotion accurately, which a quiet, reserved person may have in abundance and a charming, sociable person may lack.

12.10 Emotion regulation: James Gross's process model

Who gave us this. James Gross, "The emerging field of emotion regulation: An integrative review" (Review of General Psychology, 1998); with Oliver John, the 2003 study of individual differences that introduced the Emotion Regulation Questionnaire.

Emotion regulation is the set of processes by which people influence which emotions they have, when they have them, and how they experience and express them. It can be deliberate or automatic, and target oneself or others. Gross built his model on the sequence in which an emotion unfolds: a situation occurs, attention goes to it, it is appraised, and a response follows. Regulation can intervene at each step, giving five families of strategies.

FamilyWhat it doesExample for an anxious student
Situation selectionChoosing which situations to enter or avoidStudying in the library with a calm friend rather than among panicking classmates
Situation modificationChanging a situation to alter its emotional impactAsking the teacher to clarify the exam pattern, removing uncertainty
Attentional deploymentDirecting attention, as in distraction or concentrationFocusing on the first question rather than the clock
Cognitive changeChanging the meaning of the situation; reappraisal is the main form"This exam is a chance to show what I know, not a verdict on my worth"
Response modulationActing directly on the response once it has arisenSlow breathing; or hiding every sign of nervousness

The first four families are antecedent-focused; the fifth is response-focused. Cognitive restructuring in cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT) is a structured form of cognitive change.

Reappraisal versus suppression

Cognitive reappraisal changes how one thinks about a situation to change its emotional impact; expressive suppression inhibits the outward signs of an emotion already felt. In a 1998 experiment, Gross had participants watch a disgusting medical film (an amputation and the treatment of burns) while reappraising (watching in a detached, clinical way, as a doctor might), suppressing (hiding their feelings from anyone watching) or simply watching. Both regulation groups showed less facial disgust, but only reappraisal reduced felt disgust; suppression left the feeling intact and increased sympathetic activation. Later studies found that suppression impairs memory for the episode and, in conversation, lowers rapport and raises the partner's stress. On Gross and John's ten-item Emotion Regulation Questionnaire (six reappraisal, four suppression items), habitual reappraisers showed more positive emotion, closer relationships and higher well-being; habitual suppressors showed the reverse and often felt inauthentic.

Two refinements matter. Suppression appears less costly where cultural values prize emotional restraint, including many East Asian settings. And reappraisal helps most when a situation cannot be changed; when it can, changing it is often better. Modern theory therefore stresses regulatory flexibility. Savouring, finally, is regulation aimed at positive emotion.

Key. The five families track the unfolding of an emotion: situation selection, situation modification, attentional deployment, cognitive change and response modulation. Reappraisal changes the feeling at low cost; suppression hides the expression but not the feeling, at a cost to physiology, memory and relationships.

12.11 Emotion across cultures: ideal affect and the Indian theory of rasa

Jeanne Tsai's affect valuation theory shows that treating excitement as the ideal positive state is a cultural assumption. Tsai distinguishes actual affect (how people feel) from ideal affect (how they would like to feel), and contrasts high-arousal positive states (excitement, enthusiasm) with low-arousal positive states (calm, peacefulness). In her studies European Americans valued excitement more than Hong Kong Chinese did, who valued calm more, with Asian Americans in between; cultures differed more in ideal affect than in actual affect. Cultural products teach these ideals: American children's storybooks show wider, more excited smiles than Taiwanese ones. Cultures that emphasise influencing others favour excitement; those that emphasise adjusting to others favour calm. Ideal affect shapes the holidays and even the doctors people choose, and a large gap between actual and ideal affect is associated with depressive symptoms.

Indian traditions have long prized low-arousal states: the Bhagavad Gita's ideal of samatva (equanimity in success and failure) and its portrait of the sthitaprajna, the person of steady wisdom, and santosha (contentment) among the niyamas of Patanjali's Yoga Sutras. This warns against importing an excitement model of happiness uncritically into work with Indian clients.

Rasa theory

India also produced one of the oldest systematic theories of emotion, though it is a theory of aesthetic experience rather than modern psychology. The Natyashastra, a Sanskrit treatise on drama traditionally attributed to the sage Bharata and usually dated to the early centuries around the start of the common era, distinguishes bhava, emotion as it exists in life and in a play's characters, from rasa ("juice" or "flavour"), the aesthetic relish tasted by the sensitive spectator, the sahridaya (one "with heart"). Bharata listed eight rasas, each grounded in a stable emotion, the sthayibhava.

RasaEnglish senseStable emotion (sthayibhava)
ShringaraThe erotic, romantic loveRati (love, delight)
HasyaThe comicHasa (mirth)
KarunaThe pathetic, compassionShoka (sorrow)
RaudraThe furiousKrodha (anger)
ViraThe heroicUtsaha (energy, enthusiasm)
BhayanakaThe terribleBhaya (fear)
BibhatsaThe disgustingJugupsa (disgust)
AdbhutaThe marvellousVismaya (wonder)

A ninth rasa, shanta (the peaceful), was added later and influentially defended by the Kashmiri philosopher Abhinavagupta, around the tenth to eleventh centuries. Bharata's rasa sutra states that rasa arises from the combination of vibhavas (determinants: causes and settings, such as the beloved and a moonlit garden), anubhavas (consequents: visible expressions such as glances and trembling) and vyabhicharibhavas (transient accompanying states, thirty-three in number, such as anxiety and fatigue), a componential scheme that parallels section 12.1. In aesthetic experience the emotion is generalised and detached from personal stakes, which is why even karuna can be relished. Adbhuta resembles awe, hasya amusement and shanta serenity, but rasa theory is an aesthetic philosophy, not an empirically tested model.

12.12 Putting it together: a student's exam week

Case. Meera, a first-year MA Clinical Psychology student, faces five papers in seven days. By Sunday night she has studied for eleven hours and is re-reading the same page on personality disorders without taking it in. Her thoughts have narrowed to one loop, "I will fail Psychopathology", and she plans to stay up all night and cancel her weekly call with her grandmother.

Anxiety has narrowed Meera's repertoire to "keep re-reading". She begins with situation selection, moving to the library with a calm friend. Instead of an all-nighter she takes short breaks of genuine positive emotion: laughing with her roommate, a walk under monsoon clouds, the call with her grandmother. That is the undoing effect in practice. Back at her notes she suddenly sees how attachment theory connects to the personality disorders material, which is broadening at work. She does not count emotions to reach a three-to-one ratio; she simply knows positive moments are not wasted study time.

Outside the hall her heart races. Her impulse is to suppress ("don't let anyone see you're nervous"), which, as Gross's research shows, would leave the anxiety intact, add physiological strain and impair memory. Instead she uses cognitive change, reappraising the racing heart as her body preparing to perform, an approach supported by Jeremy Jamieson's research in which students told that arousal can aid performance did better on quantitative test questions. When one paper goes badly she reappraises it as one piece of a long programme.

After the last paper she savours: sharing the moment over chai, building a memory of the relief, some self-congratulation, and anticipating her mother's cooking on the train home. Had her anxiety become panic attacks or persistent hopelessness, the right step would be the counselling service: positive emotions work alongside honest attention to problems, not instead of it.

Recap.

An emotion is a brief, object-directed, coordinated response with five components (feeling, physiology, expression, appraisal and action tendency); moods are longer and objectless, affect is the umbrella term, and positive and negative affect are relatively independent. Positive emotions were long neglected, a gap filled by Barbara Fredrickson's broaden-and-build theory: positive emotions broaden thought-action repertoires (shown with film clips, global-local tasks and the Twenty Statements Test), build durable physical, intellectual, social and psychological resources, undo lingering negative arousal and fuel upward spirals. The 2.9 to 1 positivity ratio proposed with Marcial Losada in 2005 was shown by Nicholas Brown, Alan Sokal and Harris Friedman in 2013 to rest on invalid mathematics and its modelling claims were formally withdrawn; positive emotions matter, but there is no magic ratio. The Nun Study linked positive emotion in young nuns' writing with longer life, a powerful but correlational finding that must be weighed against reverse causation. Fredrickson's ten positive emotions, awe as vastness plus accommodation, and gratitude as emotion, trait and practice show that "happiness" is many things, and savouring in Fred Bryant and Joseph Veroff's model is the deliberate enhancement of good experience across past, present and future. Emotional intelligence exists as an ability model, mixed models and a trait model, and the popular claims outrun the evidence. James Gross's process model classifies regulation into five families and favours reappraisal over suppression in most contexts, while Jeanne Tsai's ideal affect research and the rasa tradition show that cultures differ in which feelings they prize.

NameWhat they gave us
Richard LazarusAppraisal theory: primary and secondary appraisal
Nico FrijdaAction tendencies as a component of emotion
James RussellCore affect and the circumplex of valence and arousal
