Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Define emotion and distinguish it from mood and affect. Describe the five components of an emotion and explain, with an example, why appraisal is considered central.
  2. Critically evaluate Barbara Fredrickson's broaden-and-build theory, describing the broadening, building and undoing hypotheses and the experimental evidence for each.
  3. Trace the history of the critical positivity ratio from its proposal in 2005 to its withdrawal. What does the episode teach about scientific self-correction, and what survives?
  4. "Positive emotions lengthen life." Evaluate this statement with reference to the Nun Study and the wider literature on positive affect and health.
  5. Discuss gratitude as an emotion, a trait and a practice, with reference to the counting blessings studies, and explain how savouring differs from simple pleasure.
  6. Compare the ability, mixed and trait models of emotional intelligence and critically evaluate the claim that emotional intelligence matters more than IQ.
  7. Explain James Gross's process model of emotion regulation and compare reappraisal with suppression, taking cultural differences in ideal affect into account.

13 Character strengths, wisdom, flow, motivation and mindfulness

For most of the twentieth century clinical psychology had a precise vocabulary for what goes wrong with people and almost none for what goes right. A clinician could separate panic disorder from generalised anxiety disorder with a structured interview, but had no agreed way to describe a patient's honesty, curiosity or courage, although these qualities shape how therapy goes. This chapter covers the tools positive psychology built to fill that gap: the Values in Action (VIA) classification of six virtues and 24 character strengths and how strengths are measured; two strengths with their own research traditions, wisdom and courage; and then four ideas about how people engage with life: flow, self-determination theory, self-efficacy with goal-setting, and mindfulness.

These ideas are part of everyday clinical work: strengths-based assessment belongs in case formulation, mindfulness-based cognitive therapy helps prevent relapse in recurrent depression, and self-efficacy predicts whether a client keeps up a behaviour change. The chapter ends with a worked case of a burned-out trainee that pulls them into one plan.

13.1 Character and virtue: why positive psychology needed a "manual of the sanities"

Character, in everyday language, means the morally valued part of personality: whether someone is honest, kind, fair or brave. Early in the twentieth century psychology largely dropped the word. Hugh Hartshorne and Mark May, in Studies in Deceit (1928), gave thousands of schoolchildren repeated chances to lie, cheat or steal in different settings and found that honesty in one setting predicted honesty in another only weakly. A child who never cheated in a class test might cheat at a party game, which suggested that "honesty" was a bundle of situation-specific habits rather than a trait. Then Gordon Allport, a founder of trait psychology, argued in 1937 that character is simply personality evaluated against a moral standard, a matter for philosophers rather than scientists.

When Martin Seligman used his 1998 presidency of the American Psychological Association to launch positive psychology, this gap became a practical problem. A science of the good needs what made the science of disorder possible: an agreed vocabulary. The Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM), especially from its third edition in 1980, gave researchers everywhere shared, reliable definitions, so that "major depressive disorder" meant the same thing in Chicago and in Bengaluru and findings could accumulate. Nothing equivalent existed for hope or perseverance; every researcher defined them differently. The answer was a classification of strengths, designed as a counterpart to the DSM and presented as a "manual of the sanities". It was led by Christopher Peterson with Seligman and published in 2004 as Character Strengths and Virtues: A Handbook and Classification.

The comparison with the DSM has limits that examiners like. The DSM is categorical (you meet criteria or you do not); the VIA scheme is dimensional (everyone has every strength to some degree). Its authors called it a classification, not a taxonomy: a taxonomy rests on a theory explaining why the categories are what they are, whereas this is an organised list justified by criteria and cross-cultural agreement, with the theory still to come. It also answers Hartshorne and May: traits predict behaviour well when behaviour is averaged across many situations, even if one situation is a poor guide.

Key. The VIA classification does for strengths what the DSM does for disorders: it gives a shared language so that research can accumulate. It is dimensional, not categorical, and a classification awaiting a theory, not a finished taxonomy.

13.2 How the VIA strengths were chosen

The scheme has three levels. Virtues are the core characteristics valued by moral philosophers and religious thinkers, such as wisdom and justice. Character strengths are the psychological routes by which a virtue is displayed: wisdom can be shown through creativity, curiosity or perspective. Situational themes are specific habits that lead a person to show a strength in a particular setting, such as the workplace. The classification concerns the first two levels.

To find the virtues, the team surveyed the major moral and religious traditions: Confucianism, Taoism, Buddhism, Hinduism, the philosophy of ancient Athens, Judaism, Christianity and Islam. Six virtues recurred across these very different traditions, sometimes under other names: wisdom, courage, humanity, justice, temperance and transcendence. To find the strengths, the team gathered candidates from philosophical, religious and popular sources and tested each against criteria. Summaries count the criteria slightly differently (usually ten to twelve), but the core ones are these:

  • Ubiquity: the strength is recognised and valued across cultures.
  • Fulfilling: using it contributes to the person's own fulfilment and living well.
  • Morally valued in its own right: people admire it for itself, not only for its results. This separates strengths from talents; we admire perfect pitch or intelligence, but we do not praise someone morally for having them.
  • Does not diminish others: watching someone display it uplifts onlookers rather than making them envious.
  • Has a clearly negative opposite: the opposite of bravery is cowardice, of honesty is deceit.
  • Trait-like: it shows some generality across situations and stability across time.
  • Measurable and distinct: it can be assessed reliably and is not just a blend of other strengths.
  • Paragons, prodigies and selective absence: some people embody it strikingly, some children show it early, and some people clearly lack it.
  • Institutions and rituals: societies build practices and institutions to cultivate it.

Not every final strength meets every criterion; the authors kept those that met most.

Trap. There are six virtues and 24 strengths. A question asking for "the six character strengths" has mixed the levels: name the six virtues, then explain that the 24 strengths sit beneath them.

13.3 The six virtues and the 24 character strengths

Each virtue has a working definition. Wisdom and knowledge are cognitive strengths of acquiring and using knowledge. Courage covers emotional strengths of exercising will to reach goals against opposition, outside or inside the person. Humanity covers interpersonal strengths of tending and befriending others. Justice covers civic strengths underlying healthy community life. Temperance covers strengths that protect against excess. Transcendence covers strengths that connect people to something larger and provide meaning. Where two names are given, both are in use.

VirtueStrengths, with brief meanings
Wisdom and knowledgeCreativity (novel and useful ways of doing things); curiosity (interest in experience for its own sake); judgement or open-mindedness (weighing evidence from all sides, willing to change one's mind); love of learning (systematically mastering new knowledge and skills); perspective (seeing the larger picture and giving wise counsel).
CourageBravery (not shrinking from threat or difficulty; acting on conviction when unpopular); perseverance or persistence (finishing what one starts despite obstacles); honesty or authenticity (speaking the truth and presenting oneself genuinely); zest or vitality (approaching life with energy and wholeheartedness).
HumanityLove (valuing close relationships in which caring is returned); kindness (doing good deeds for others and taking care of them); social intelligence (awareness of one's own and others' motives and feelings, and knowing how to fit into social situations).
JusticeTeamwork or citizenship (working well as a group member, doing one's share); fairness (treating people equally, without personal bias); leadership (organising a group to get things done while keeping good relations).
TemperanceForgiveness or mercy (giving people a second chance, not being vengeful); humility or modesty (letting accomplishments speak for themselves); prudence (being careful about choices, not taking undue risks); self-regulation or self-control (disciplining feelings, appetites and actions).
TranscendenceAppreciation of beauty and excellence (noticing beauty and skilled performance, feeling awe); gratitude (being aware of and thankful for good things); hope or optimism (expecting the best and working for it); humour or playfulness (enjoying laughter, seeing the light side); spirituality (coherent beliefs about the higher purpose and meaning of life).

Two findings are worth knowing. First, in large samples the strengths most strongly linked to life satisfaction were hope, zest, gratitude, love and curiosity, which Peterson and colleagues called the "strengths of the heart", as against more intellectual "strengths of the head" such as love of learning. Second, the six-virtue structure is not well supported. If the virtues were real underlying dimensions, factor analysis of the 24 strength scores should recover six factors; it usually recovers three to five. Large analyses by Robert McGrath found something close to three: caring, inquisitiveness and self-control. Some placements also look arbitrary (why is humour under transcendence?). The fair conclusion is that the 24 strengths are reasonably measured, but the grouping into six virtues is a philosophical organisation, not a proven psychological structure.

13.4 Signature strengths and how strengths are measured

Everyone has all 24 strengths in different amounts. Peterson and Seligman proposed that each person has a few signature strengths at the top of the profile that feel like the core of who they are. Signs include a sense of ownership ("this is the real me"), excitement and fast learning when using it, a yearning to find new ways to use it, and feeling energised rather than drained by it. The practical idea, central to Seligman's Authentic Happiness (2002), is that well-being grows when people use signature strengths regularly in work, love and leisure, rather than spending all their effort repairing weaknesses.

This was tested. In an online trial published in 2005 by Seligman and colleagues, participants were randomly assigned to brief exercises or to a placebo exercise (writing about early memories). "Using your signature strengths in a new way" daily for a week, and "three good things" (writing each day three things that went well and why), increased happiness and reduced depressive symptoms, with effects still visible about six months later. A "gratitude visit" gave the largest immediate boost but faded within about a month. Later research suggests such effects are usually modest and depend on continued practice.

The VIA Inventory of Strengths

The main measure is the VIA Inventory of Strengths, a self-report questionnaire whose original form had 240 items, ten per strength, rated on a five-point scale from "very much like me" to "very much unlike me". A youth version covers roughly ages 10 to 17, and shorter adult forms now exist. It is free online.The crucial interpretive point is that the feedback ranks a person's strengths within that person. It is a profile, not a comparison with others, so a strength ranked 24th is a "lesser strength", not a deficit. The usual limits of self-report apply (people describe who they wish to be, and self-rated humility is a paradox). A deeper criticism, drawn from Aristotle, is that strengths cannot be used in isolation: kindness collides with honesty, and practical wisdom is needed to judge which strength a situation needs and how much of it. Practitioners now speak of overuse and underuse (curiosity overused becomes intrusiveness), echoing Aristotle's idea of virtue as a mean between excess and deficiency.

The Gallup Clifton StrengthsFinder

A different tool comes from the consulting firm Gallup. Donald Clifton spent decades studying what distinguished outstanding performers at work, and Gallup released his online assessment, the Clifton StrengthsFinder (now CliftonStrengths), around the turn of the century. It identifies 34 "talent themes" (such as Achiever, Empathy and Strategic) grouped into four domains: executing, influencing, relationship building and strategic thinking. Gallup defines a talent as a naturally recurring pattern of thought, feeling or behaviour, and a strength as a talent developed through knowledge and skill. Unlike the VIA, it is commercial and its themes are not morally evaluated.

Trap. The StrengthsFinder does not measure the VIA strengths. The VIA asks "what is good about your character?"; Gallup asks "where are you most likely to perform well at work?".

13.5 Wisdom

Wisdom is one of the oldest ideals of the good life and one of the hardest to study, because it seems to mean knowledge, judgement, kindness and calm at once. Psychology studies it through implicit theories (what ordinary people mean by a wise person, which typically combines thinking, reflection and compassion) and explicit theories (formal models that experts build and test). Three explicit theories are standard.

The Berlin Wisdom Paradigm

Who gave us this. Paul Baltes, a lifespan developmental psychologist at the Max Planck Institute for Human Development in Berlin, with Ursula Staudinger and colleagues, developed the Berlin Wisdom Paradigm from the 1980s, as part of his work on what can grow, not just decline, in later life.

Baltes defined wisdom as expert knowledge about the fundamental pragmatics of life: deep knowledge and sound judgement about the conduct and meaning of life, especially its difficult and uncertain matters. "Expert" is deliberate: as a chess master has rich, organised knowledge of chess, a wise person has rich, organised knowledge of how lives go. Participants are given hard life dilemmas and asked to think aloud. The best known is: "A fifteen-year-old girl wants to get married right away. What could one consider and do?" Trained raters score the answer on five criteria, two basic and three "meta":

  1. Rich factual knowledge about human nature, development and relationships (for example, what early marriage tends to mean for education and health).
  2. Rich procedural knowledge: strategies for handling life problems, such as talking with the girl to understand why she wants this and exploring options with her family.
  3. Lifespan contextualism: seeing the problem in the context of the whole life course, culture and historical time.
  4. Value relativism and tolerance: recognising that people and communities hold different values, while keeping some universal concern for welfare.
  5. Recognition and management of uncertainty: accepting that the advisor cannot know everything (there may be special circumstances, such as a terminal illness) and planning for that.

The findings: high wisdom-related performance is rare even among educated adults. It rises steeply through adolescence and early adulthood but, on average, does not keep rising across adulthood. What helps is relevant experience and reflection; in one comparison clinical psychologists outperformed other professionals of similar education, presumably because their work is daily training in the pragmatics of life. Performance also improved when participants could discuss the dilemma with someone close to them, or merely imagine such a conversation, which suggests wisdom is partly a social achievement.

Sternberg's balance theory

Robert Sternberg (1998) defined wisdom as the application of tacit knowledge (practical know-how learned from experience), guided by values, toward a common good, by balancing intrapersonal interests (one's own), interpersonal interests (those of others directly involved) and extrapersonal interests (those of the wider community or environment), over the short and long term. It also balances three responses to a situation: adapting to the environment, shaping it, or selecting a new one. The distinctive claim is moral: a clever plan that serves only oneself is not wise, however intelligent.

Ardelt's three-dimensional model

Monika Ardelt, a sociologist, argued that wisdom is not expert knowledge but a quality of the whole personality, measured by her Three-Dimensional Wisdom Scale (2003). The cognitive dimension is a desire to know the truth and understand life deeply, accepting ambiguity and the limits of knowledge. The reflective dimension is looking at events from many perspectives, including self-examination, which reduces self-centredness and projection. The affective dimension is sympathetic and compassionate love for others. All three must be present: someone who knows much about life but lacks compassion is knowledgeable, not wise. The two camps debated the difference in print in 2004, Ardelt arguing that the Berlin method measured knowledge about wisdom rather than wisdom itself.

Wisdom and age

The popular belief that wisdom comes with age is only partly supported. In the Berlin data, older adults as a group are not wiser than middle-aged adults, though many top scorers are older. Ardelt's view is that age brings wisdom only to those who reflect on their experience. On the other side, Igor Grossmann and colleagues (2010) found that when reasoning about social conflicts, older adults more often recognised multiple perspectives, the limits of their knowledge and the need for compromise. Age supplies the raw material (experience); reflection turns it into wisdom. Indian readers may notice how close Ardelt's model is to the Bhagavad Gita's sthitaprajna, the person of steady wisdom who combines understanding, equanimity and freedom from self-centred desire.

Trap. Baltes treats wisdom as performance on dilemmas, rated by others (expert knowledge). Ardelt treats it as personality, measured by self-report (cognitive, reflective, affective). Sternberg defines it by its aim: the common good through balance.

13.6 Courage

Aristotle described courage as the mean between cowardice (too much fear) and rashness (too little). Modern research on how people define courage keeps his insight and finds three elements: an action that is voluntary and deliberate, taken despite substantial risk to the actor, for a worthy goal. Surviving a danger by accident is not courage; taking a risk for a selfish thrill is not courage either. Nor is courage the absence of fear. Stanley Rachman, studying military bomb-disposal operators, distinguished fearlessness (feeling no fear) from courage (acting well despite fear), and noted that repeated successful practice gradually turns courage into something close to fearlessness, which is exactly the logic of exposure therapy.

  • Physical courage is acting despite risk to one's body or life, as a firefighter enters a burning building.
  • Moral courage is standing up for principles despite social costs such as disapproval or loss of a job. A junior nurse who reports the illegal restraint of a psychiatric patient, knowing seniors will be angry, shows it. In India this has legal weight, since the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 gives persons with mental illness enforceable rights that staff may have to defend against colleagues.
  • Psychological courage, a term developed by the philosopher Daniel Putman (1997), is facing one's own fears, instability and destructive habits: the person with obsessive-compulsive disorder who touches a "contaminated" doorknob during exposure. Shane Lopez and colleagues described a related vital courage: persevering through serious illness or disability.
Why it matters. Therapy constantly asks clients to do what frightens them. Framing that effort as psychological courage, rather than as symptom management, gives clients a strength-based way to understand what they are doing.

13.7 Flow

Who gave us this. Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi, a Hungarian-American psychologist at the University of Chicago, introduced flow in Beyond Boredom and Anxiety (1975) and brought it to a wide public in Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience (1990).

Csikszentmihalyi began by studying painters, who worked on a canvas with total absorption, ignoring hunger and fatigue, yet often lost interest in the finished painting: the reward lay in the doing. He then interviewed rock climbers, chess players, dancers, composers and surgeons, and found the same state described across these very different activities, often with the image of being carried along by a current. Flow is a state of such complete involvement in an activity that nothing else seems to matter, so enjoyable that people pursue it for its own sake. It is usually described in nine dimensions: three conditions that make it likely and six features of the experience.

PartDimensionWhat it means
ConditionClear goalsOne knows, moment by moment, what to do next.
ConditionImmediate feedbackThe activity shows at once how well one is doing.
ConditionBalance of challenge and skillA high challenge that stretches skills without exceeding them.
ExperienceMerging of action and awarenessAction feels spontaneous, with no gap between deciding and doing.
ExperienceTotal concentrationAttention is absorbed; worries and distractions drop away.
ExperienceSense of controlA feeling of being able to handle what comes, without anxious effort.
ExperienceLoss of self-consciousnessConcern with how one appears disappears.
ExperienceTransformation of timeHours pass like minutes, or a moment stretches out.
ExperienceAutotelic experienceThe activity becomes its own reward (Greek auto, self, and telos, goal).

The experience sampling method

Interviews capture only what people remember, and memory for feelings is unreliable. So Csikszentmihalyi, Reed Larson and colleagues developed the experience sampling method (ESM) in the 1970s. Participants carried a pager (today a phone app) that signalled at random moments, about eight times a day for a week. At each signal they rated what they were doing, how challenging it was, how skilled they felt, and their concentration and mood. Its strength is ecological validity: experience is captured as it happens, in real settings. Its limits are participant burden, missed signals and the chance that being asked changes the experience. The same logic underlies ecological momentary assessment in clinical research, used to track mood or craving in daily life.

The channel model

The 1975 model placed flow in a diagonal channel between anxiety (challenge exceeding skill) and boredom (skill exceeding challenge). Experience sampling forced a correction: people with balanced but low challenge and skill did not report flow but apathy. Fausto Massimini and Massimo Carli therefore proposed a quadrant model in the late 1980s, measuring challenge and skill against each person's own average. Flow occurs only when both are above that average.

ChallengeSkillStateExample
HighHighFlowAn experienced therapist in a difficult but manageable session.
HighLowAnxietyA new trainee presenting a complex case to a consultant unprepared.
LowHighBoredomA skilled clinician spending a morning on data entry.
LowLowApathyPassive late-night scrolling or television.

The practical lesson is that flow can be engineered: when anxious, raise skill or lower the challenge for now; when bored, raise the challenge.

Trap. Flow is not simply "challenge matched to skill". Matched but low challenge and skill produce apathy. Flow needs both high, relative to the person's own usual level.

The autotelic personality, and flow in work, study and leisure

Some people enter flow far more often than others. Csikszentmihalyi called them autotelic personalities: people who do things for their own sake, show curiosity and persistence, are low in self-centredness and seek out challenges. They can find flow even in dull settings by setting small goals and watching for feedback, so flow is partly a learnable skill. The most surprising sampling finding concerns work: in a 1989 study, American workers reported flow far more often at work than in leisure, where passive activities produced mostly apathy, yet at work they more often wished to be elsewhere. This paradox of work suggests people judge work by cultural stereotype rather than by their actual experience. In study, students report flow when material is challenging and they feel competent, which is why good teaching pitches tasks just above a learner's current level. Two cautions: flow is value-neutral (gambling, compulsive gaming and even crime can produce it), and people are not consciously happy during flow, because attention is fully absorbed; the happiness is felt afterwards.

13.8 Self-determination theory

Who gave us this. Edward Deci and Richard Ryan of the University of Rochester set out self-determination theory (SDT) in Intrinsic Motivation and Self-Determination in Human Behavior (1985) and summarised it in an influential 2000 article in American Psychologist.

Self-determination theory asks not only how much motivation a person has but what kind. Intrinsic motivation is doing something because it is inherently interesting or enjoyable; extrinsic motivation is doing it for a separate outcome such as money, marks, praise or avoiding punishment. A child reading comics under the blanket at night is intrinsically motivated; a child reading a textbook to avoid a scolding is extrinsically motivated.

The undermining effect of rewards

The theory began with a surprising experiment. In 1971 Deci had university students solve an absorbing three-dimensional puzzle over three sessions; one group was paid per puzzle in the second session, and nobody was paid in the third. In a free-choice period, when students could do what they liked, the previously paid students spent less time on the puzzle than the unpaid group. In 1973 Mark Lepper and colleagues found the same with nursery children who liked drawing: those promised and given a "Good Player" certificate later drew less in free play than children who got no reward or an unexpected one.

Deci and Ryan explained this with cognitive evaluation theory. A reward experienced as controlling ("I am doing this for the money") shifts the perceived cause of behaviour from inside to outside and undermines intrinsic motivation; a reward experienced as informational ("this shows I am good at it") supports competence and can enhance it. A 1999 meta-analysis of 128 experiments by Deci, Koestner and Ryan found that expected, tangible rewards contingent on doing a task reliably undermined intrinsic motivation, while positive verbal feedback tended to enhance it. The effect is contested: Robert Eisenberger and Judy Cameron (1996) argued from their own meta-analysis that the harm was small and narrow, and the camps disputed each other's methods. The balanced conclusion: rewards do not always backfire, but expected, controlling rewards for activities people already enjoy often do. Rewards for dull tasks cannot undermine interest that was never there.

Three basic psychological needs

SDT proposes three innate needs whose satisfaction is as necessary for psychological growth as food is for physical growth:

  • Autonomy is experiencing one's actions as self-endorsed and volitional. It is not independence: a person can autonomously choose to follow family wishes if they genuinely endorse them. This matters in India, since critics call SDT individualistic; cross-cultural studies found that autonomy in this volitional sense predicted well-being in collectivist as well as individualist cultures.
  • Competence is feeling effective and experiencing mastery.
  • Relatedness is feeling connected to others, caring and being cared for.

Need-supportive parents, teachers, managers and therapists produce more intrinsic motivation, better learning and better well-being; need-frustrating settings produce passivity and alienation.

The continuum of internalisation

Much of life involves tasks that are not fun in themselves: statistics, medication, case files. Organismic integration theory, another part of SDT, explains how extrinsic motivation becomes more or less one's own through internalisation. Take a student preparing for a clinical psychology entrance examination.

TypeWhat drives itThe student's inner voice
AmotivationNo intention; no sense of value or ability."There is no point; I will not get in."
External regulation (controlled)Rewards and punishments from others."My parents will be furious if I fail."
Introjected regulation (controlled)Inner pressure: guilt, shame, ego."I would be ashamed in front of my batchmates."
Identified regulation (autonomous)Consciously valuing the goal."I need this knowledge to be a good clinician."
Integrated regulation (autonomous)The value is fully part of one's self."Helping people as a clinician is who I am; studying is part of that."
Intrinsic motivationInterest and enjoyment in the activity itself."Psychopathology fascinates me; I would read it anyway."

External and introjected regulation form controlled motivation; identified, integrated and intrinsic form autonomous motivation, which predicts better persistence, deeper learning and greater well-being even when the total amount of motivation is the same.

Trap. Integrated regulation is still extrinsic: the activity is done for its value, not its inherent enjoyment, even though it feels fully one's own.

Implications. In therapy, SDT predicts that change lasts when the client experiences it as their own choice. Lecturing and warning produce at best external or introjected regulation, which fades once the pressure is gone. An autonomy-supportive therapist acknowledges the client's view, offers choices, gives a meaningful rationale and avoids controlling language; this is why SDT is often treated as a theoretical basis for motivational interviewing, developed by William Miller and Stephen Rollnick for ambivalence about change. In education, explaining why material matters, allowing some choice and giving informational feedback produce better engagement than teaching by marks, threats and comparison. Examination-centred systems, common in India, push students toward introjected regulation, which may work short-term but costs curiosity and well-being.

13.9 Self-efficacy and goal-setting

Who gave us this. Albert Bandura introduced self-efficacy in "Self-efficacy: Toward a unifying theory of behavioral change" (Psychological Review, 1977), within his social cognitive theory, and gave the full account in Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control (1997).

Self-efficacy is a person's belief in their capability to carry out the actions needed to reach a particular outcome. Bandura distinguished outcome expectations (the belief that a behaviour leads to a result: "exposure reduces phobias") from efficacy expectations (the belief that one can perform the behaviour: "I can stay near the snake"). One can hold the first without the second, and the second decides whether a person tries. Bandura developed the idea partly from work with severe snake phobia. Self-efficacy is specific, not global (high for statistics, low for public speaking), and varies in level (how hard a task one believes one can do), strength (how firmly) and generality (how widely). It has four sources, in order of usual power:

  1. Mastery experiences: actually succeeding, especially through effort against difficulty. This is the strongest source because it is direct evidence; graded exposure for agoraphobia (the gate, then the corner, then the market) builds efficacy step by step.
  2. Vicarious experience: watching someone similar succeed ("if they can, so can I"). For a trainee, a senior trainee is a more persuasive model than a famous professor.
  3. Verbal persuasion: being told by a credible person that one can succeed. It is weaker and fragile, since one failure can undo it, but it can get a person to try, which creates mastery.
  4. Physiological and emotional states: a racing heart before a viva, read as "I cannot do this", lowers efficacy; reinterpreting arousal as readiness, or reducing it, protects efficacy.

Self-efficacy shapes which activities people choose, their effort, their persistence after setbacks and their anxiety, and it predicts outcomes in phobia treatment, smoking cessation and academic achievement. It is measured with task-specific scales and, for broad coping confidence, the General Self-Efficacy Scale. It differs from self-esteem, a global judgement of one's worth, and from locus of control (Julian Rotter), a belief about whether outcomes depend on one's actions at all: one can believe outcomes depend on action yet doubt one can perform the action.

Goal-setting theory, from Edwin Locke and Gary Latham (summarised in their 1990 book A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance), finds that specific, difficult goals produce higher performance than easy goals or vague instructions to "do your best". Goals direct attention, energise effort, increase persistence and prompt strategy development, and they work best with commitment, feedback, adequate ability and high self-efficacy. For complex tasks one does not yet know how to do, a learning goal ("find five ways to structure a formulation") beats a performance goal ("write a perfect formulation"), because pressure to perform blocks exploration. Goals link to Charles R. Snyder's hope theory, in which hope combines goals with pathways thinking and agency thinking, the last close to self-efficacy.

13.10 Mindfulness: definitions, roots and programmes

The standard definition comes from Jon Kabat-Zinn: mindfulness means "paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and non-judgementally". On purpose means attention is directed, not drifting. In the present moment means attending to what is happening now (a breath, a sound, a thought as it arises) rather than to replayed pasts or imagined futures. Non-judgementally means observing experience without labelling it good or bad or trying to fix it.

Buddhist roots

The idea is ancient and Indian in origin. "Mindfulness" translates the Pali sati (Sanskrit smriti), whose root sense is remembering or bearing in mind. The Buddha's teaching on the four foundations of mindfulness, in the Satipatthana Sutta, directs attention to the body, to feelings (pleasant, unpleasant or neutral), to states of mind and to mental phenomena. Buddhist traditions distinguish samatha, calm meditation that steadies the mind on one object, from vipassana, insight meditation that observes the changing, impermanent flow of experience. In modern India vipassana was popularised by S. N. Goenka through ten-day silent residential courses. In its home tradition mindfulness is part of an ethical path toward liberation from suffering, not a stand-alone stress technique.

Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction and its successors

Kabat-Zinn, trained as a molecular biologist and a practitioner of Zen and vipassana meditation, founded the Stress Reduction Clinic at the University of Massachusetts Medical School in 1979. His programme, Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR), was built for patients with chronic pain and stress-related conditions that standard medicine had not helped, and it removed Buddhist language so it could be offered in a hospital. The standard format is eight weekly group sessions of about two and a half hours, a day-long silent retreat and around 45 minutes of daily home practice: the body scan (moving attention slowly through the body), sitting meditation, gentle mindful movement drawn from hatha yoga, and informal practice such as mindful eating. His 1990 book Full Catastrophe Living describes it.

Its main clinical descendant is Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy (MBCT), developed by Zindel Segal, Mark Williams and John Teasdale (manual published 2002), which combines the MBSR format with cognitive therapy to prevent relapse in people who have recovered from recurrent depression. In such people a small dip in mood can reactivate old patterns of negative thinking and rumination; mindfulness teaches them to notice these early and relate to thoughts as passing mental events rather than facts. Trials found that it reduced relapse, most clearly in people with three or more previous episodes.

Ellen Langer's different view

Ellen Langer of Harvard University developed a separate concept in her 1989 book Mindfulness. For her, mindfulness is a cognitive state of actively noticing new things and drawing novel distinctions: sensitivity to context, openness to new information and awareness of more than one perspective. Its opposite, mindlessness, is acting on automatic pilot, trapped in categories formed earlier. In a classic 1978 study, people at a photocopier let someone go ahead almost as often when given a meaningless reason ("because I have to make copies") as a real one, responding to the form of a request without processing its content. Langer's mindfulness involves no meditation; it is cultivated by questioning assumptions and seeking novelty. Kabat-Zinn trains non-judgemental observation of present experience; Langer trains the active generation of new interpretations. They share a name, not a method.

Trap. Meditative mindfulness is not relaxation and not emptying the mind. Thoughts will come; the practice is noticing them and returning attention, whatever state results.

13.11 How mindfulness works, how it is measured, and evidence versus hype

A 2004 consensus definition proposed two components: self-regulation of attention to immediate experience, and an orientation of curiosity, openness and acceptance toward that experience. Three mechanisms are most often named:

  • Attention regulation: repeatedly returning attention to an anchor such as the breath trains the ability to notice where attention has gone and bring it back, directly relevant to rumination and worry, which are attention stuck on past or future.
  • Decentring: experiencing thoughts and feelings as passing events in the mind, not as truths or as the self. "I am worthless" becomes "I am having the thought that I am worthless". Teasdale and colleagues propose this as the main mechanism of MBCT.
  • Acceptance: allowing unpleasant sensations and emotions without struggling to avoid them. This works partly like exposure: staying with a difficult feeling lets it rise and fall, and the person learns it is bearable.

Two self-report measures dominate: the Mindful Attention Awareness Scale of Kirk Warren Brown and Richard Ryan (2003), measuring everyday present-moment attention, and the Five Facet Mindfulness Questionnaire of Ruth Baer and colleagues (2006), measuring observing, describing, acting with awareness, non-judging of inner experience and non-reactivity to inner experience.

The evidence is real but narrower than the publicity. A large meta-analysis led by Madhav Goyal (2014) found moderate evidence that meditation programmes improve anxiety, depression and pain, with small to moderate effects, and low or insufficient evidence for many other claims; crucially, they were not better than active treatments such as exercise or cognitive behavioural therapy. In 2018 Nicholas Van Dam and colleagues published "Mind the Hype", warning of vague and shifting definitions, small studies, weak control groups and over-interpreted brain scans. Researchers have also documented adverse effects of intensive meditation, such as anxiety, depersonalisation and resurfacing traumatic memories, which older studies rarely asked about. Critics of "McMindfulness" object to corporate programmes that teach employees to cope with stressful conditions without changing the conditions, quietly shifting responsibility from institutions to individuals.

Key. Mindfulness-based programmes help anxiety, depression and pain modestly, and MBCT has good evidence for preventing relapse in recurrent depression. They are not a cure-all, not superior to other active treatments, not free of side effects, and not a substitute for fixing a harmful environment.

13.12 Case: a strengths-based and flow-based plan for a burned-out trainee

Case.

Priya, 25, is in the second year of clinical psychology training at a large government hospital in India. She joined full of enthusiasm and now feels "on empty": exhausted by midweek, irritated and detached from patients ("they are all the same"), and convinced she is not improving despite long hours. She dreads presenting formulations to her consultant, spends hours on registers and scrolls on her phone late into the night. She still enjoys the testing sessions she runs with children and laughs easily with batchmates.

Assess first. Her complaints match Christina Maslach's three dimensions of burnout: emotional exhaustion, depersonalisation and reduced personal accomplishment. ICD-11 classifies burn-out as an occupational phenomenon, not a medical condition. Her supervisor still screens for depression, anxiety and suicidal thinking, because a strengths plan is no treatment for a depressive disorder. Here none is found.

Find her strengths. Her VIA profile shows curiosity, kindness, love of learning, humour and fairness on top, with self-regulation and prudence among her lesser strengths. Her procedural tasks give curiosity no outlet, and her kindness is being spent with no sense of effect. The late-night scrolling reflects a lesser strength under strain, not a flaw.

Map her flow. For a week she sets six random phone alarms a day and rates challenge, skill, concentration and mood. Formulation presentations fall in anxiety (high challenge, low felt skill, no feedback until the day), registers in boredom, evening scrolling in apathy. Testing with children is her one reliable flow: clear goals, immediate feedback, a stretching challenge.

Redesign the work. Formulations become a series of clear goals with feedback: fifteen minutes weekly with a senior trainee reviewing one section at a time. Register work gets a small challenge (a brief audit of referral patterns, feeding her curiosity) and is batched into one block. Using job crafting (Amy Wrzesniewski and Jane Dutton), she asks to take more child assessments.

Rebuild efficacy and motivation. Each completed section is a mastery experience, watching a senior present is vicarious experience, and specific consultant feedback is persuasion grounded in evidence; her racing heart is reframed as readiness. An SDT audit shows competence and autonomy frustrated, relatedness intact, so she gets informational feedback and some choice of cases. Her motivation moves from introjected ("I must not look stupid") toward identified ("I want to do this well for my patients").

Use strengths and mindfulness in proportion. Weekly she uses curiosity in a new way, preparing one question about each new patient's life that has nothing to do with symptoms, which also counters depersonalisation. She sets herself a phone cut-off to protect sleep and uses a three-minute breathing space before hard sessions to decentre from "I am useless at this".

Change the system too. Her supervisor raises the missing feedback and clerical load with the department, since burnout is largely a property of the job and asking a trainee to breathe through an unreasonable workload would be McMindfulness. After six weeks the sampling week and burnout ratings are repeated.

Recap.

Positive psychology needed a shared vocabulary for what is good in people, so Peterson and Seligman built the VIA classification (2004) as a dimensional "manual of the sanities": 24 strengths under six virtues, chosen by criteria such as ubiquity, fulfilment, moral value, not diminishing others, trait-likeness and measurability. The free VIA Inventory ranks strengths within a person; the Clifton StrengthsFinder is a separate, commercial tool for workplace talents; and factor analysis does not support six virtues. Wisdom has been modelled as expert knowledge about life's pragmatics (Baltes), as balancing interests toward a common good (Sternberg) and as a cognitive, reflective and affective personality (Ardelt), and it grows with reflected experience rather than age alone. Courage, physical, moral or psychological, is acting well despite fear. Flow arises when high challenge meets high skill with clear goals and immediate feedback, and the quadrant model contrasts it with anxiety, boredom and apathy. Self-determination theory explains how controlling rewards undermine intrinsic motivation, names autonomy, competence and relatedness as basic needs and traces internalisation; self-efficacy, built mainly through mastery, and specific difficult goals explain persistence and performance. Mindfulness exists in a meditative sense (Kabat-Zinn, MBSR and MBCT) and in Langer's cognitive sense, works through attention regulation, decentring and acceptance, and has real but modest evidence surrounded by hype.
