Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Why did positive psychology need a classification of character strengths? Describe how the VIA virtues and strengths were selected, list them, and critically evaluate the classification and its measurement.
  2. Compare the Berlin Wisdom Paradigm, Sternberg's balance theory and Ardelt's three-dimensional model. What does research say about wisdom and age?
  3. Explain flow, distinguishing its conditions from its experience. Describe the experience sampling method and the channel model, and discuss the paradox of work.
  4. Critically discuss self-determination theory with reference to the undermining effect, the basic psychological needs and the internalisation continuum, and its implications for therapy and education.
  5. What is self-efficacy? Explain Bandura's four sources with clinical examples and distinguish self-efficacy from self-esteem, outcome expectations and locus of control.
  6. Distinguish Kabat-Zinn's and Langer's conceptions of mindfulness, describe MBSR and MBCT and their proposed mechanisms, and evaluate the evidence against the charge of hype.
  7. Using concepts from this chapter, design and justify an intervention plan for a burned-out mental health trainee, including its limits.

14 Optimism, hope, resilience and growth after adversity

Clinical psychology grew up studying what goes wrong. This chapter asks an equally clinical question: when life delivers a blow, why do some people keep going, some fall and recover, some stay down, and some report being changed for the better? The answers come from separate research traditions: what you expect (optimism), how you explain what happened (learned helplessness and explanatory style), how you think about goals (hope), adaptation despite serious risk (resilience), protective orientations (hardiness and sense of coherence) and positive change after trauma (post-traumatic growth), all held together by Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman's model of coping.

These ideas matter clinically because they are measurable, predict real outcomes and in several cases can be taught; the controversies around them are taught here too, because examiners expect them. The positive psychology texts (Baumgardner and Crothers; the Oxford Handbook of Positive Psychology) cover this ground in detail, and the clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) draw on it when they discuss stress, coping and health.

14.1 Dispositional optimism: expecting good outcomes

Dispositional optimism is a relatively stable tendency to expect that good things, rather than bad things, will happen in one's life. The key word is generalised: confidence about one driving test is a specific expectancy, whereas dispositional optimism is a default expectation, carried across the domains of life, that the future will on the whole turn out well. Dispositional pessimism is the generalised expectation of bad outcomes.

Who gave us this. Michael Scheier and Charles Carver introduced dispositional optimism in 1985, in a paper in the journal Health Psychology that also presented the Life Orientation Test. In 1994 Scheier, Carver and a colleague published the Life Orientation Test Revised (LOT-R), the form used in most research today.

The idea came from Scheier and Carver's self-regulation model of behaviour, in which people pursue goals and keep comparing where they are with where they want to be. When an obstacle appears, the person asks, at least briefly, whether success is still likely. If yes, effort continues or increases; if no, effort is withdrawn and the goal may be abandoned. Dispositional optimism is that expectancy lifted to the level of a trait: facing almost any obstacle, the optimist assumes things will work out and keeps engaging, while the pessimist disengages sooner.

Measuring optimism

The original Life Orientation Test had twelve items, four of them unscored fillers that disguise its purpose. Because its scores overlapped heavily with neuroticism and anxiety, critics worried that "optimism" was merely the absence of negative emotion, so the 1994 revision removed items that were about coping rather than expectancy. The revised test has ten items: three optimistic (for example "In uncertain times, I usually expect the best"), three pessimistic and reverse scored (for example "If something can go wrong for me, it will") and four fillers, each rated from 0 (strongly disagree) to 4 (strongly agree), giving a total of 0 to 24. Optimism measured this way still predicted outcomes after neuroticism and anxiety were controlled.

Optimism, coping and health

Optimism works largely through coping. Optimists tend to use approach coping: planning, seeking information, acting on problems that can be solved, reframing positively and accepting what cannot be changed. Pessimists lean towards avoidant coping: denial, wishful thinking, giving up, and escape through alcohol or other substances. In Carver's studies of women treated for early breast cancer, optimism predicted less distress over the following year, largely through acceptance and positive reframing.

The best known health study concerns recovery after coronary artery bypass surgery. Scheier and colleagues (1989) measured optimism in men before bypass surgery. Optimists reached recovery milestones such as sitting up and walking sooner, returned to normal activities sooner after discharge and reported better quality of life months later; a later study by the same group found optimists less likely to be rehospitalised. Meta-analytic reviews support a modest but reliable link between optimism and physical health, working through behaviour, coping and possibly physiology.

Trap. Optimism is not denial. Research by Lisa Aspinwall and colleagues found that optimists pay closer attention to health-risk information relevant to them, because they expect acting on it to help. The optimist says "this is serious, and I can do something about it"; the person in denial says "this is not happening".

14.2 Defensive pessimism and the limits of optimism

Defensive pessimism, described by Julie Norem and colleagues in the 1980s, is a strategy in which a person anxious about a performance deliberately sets low expectations, despite usually having done well, and then mentally rehearses everything that could go wrong. A student before a viva says, "I will forget everything, they will ask about statistics, my voice will shake", and then prepares for each disaster. The low expectation cushions a possible failure, and the rehearsal turns anxiety into preparation. Norem contrasted it with strategic optimism, in which less anxious people set high expectations and avoid dwelling on the event. Defensive pessimists perform worse when prevented from using their strategy, for example when cheered up or distracted beforehand, while strategic optimists perform worse when forced to dwell on what could go wrong. Norem set out the argument in The Positive Power of Negative Thinking (2001).

Trap. The dispositional pessimist expects bad outcomes and withdraws effort; the defensive pessimist expects bad outcomes as a tactic and then works harder.

Optimism can also mislead. Neil Weinstein (1980) showed that most people rate their own chances of illness, accidents or divorce as lower than the average person's, which cannot be true of everyone; this unrealistic optimism can lead people to skip precautions such as wearing a helmet. Martin Seligman therefore argued for flexible optimism: optimistic thinking where the aim is to persist or recover morale, and pessimistic caution where being wrong would be catastrophic, as when a pilot decides whether ice on the wings needs removing.

14.3 Learned helplessness: from the laboratory to depression

In the mid-1960s at the University of Pennsylvania, Martin Seligman and Steven Maier, then graduate students, noticed that dogs that had earlier received shocks they could do nothing about later failed to learn a simple escape response, and designed an experiment to find out why.

Who gave us this. Martin Seligman and Steven Maier published the core learned helplessness experiments in 1967. The reformulated, attributional theory came from Lyn Abramson, Seligman and John Teasdale in 1978.

The triadic design used three groups. The first received shocks it could stop by pressing a panel with its head. The second was "yoked" to the first: each dog received exactly the same shocks at the same times, but nothing it did mattered, because the shock stopped only when its partner pressed the panel. The third received no shocks. Because groups one and two received physically identical shocks, any difference between them must come from control, not from pain. Next day all the dogs were placed in a shuttle box, where a signal was followed by shock through the floor and the dog could escape by jumping a low barrier. The escapable-shock and no-shock dogs quickly learned to jump. Most yoked dogs lay down, whined and passively took the shock. Seligman and Maier concluded that these dogs had learned that outcomes were independent of their responses, and that this expectation transferred to the new situation: learned helplessness.

Three deficits were described: motivational (failure to try), cognitive (difficulty learning that a response works even after it succeeds) and emotional (fear followed by passivity). Dogs given earlier experience of escapable shock were protected, which was called immunisation, and helpless dogs recovered after being dragged across the barrier repeatedly until they moved on their own.

Why the theory had to change

In the 1970s the effect was reproduced in people using inescapable noise or unsolvable problems, and Seligman proposed learned helplessness as a model of depression. But human responses varied in how widely helplessness spread, whether self-esteem fell and how long it lasted. There was also a paradox: depressed people blame themselves intensely, yet the theory said they believed they had no control. The reformulated model of Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale (1978) solved this by adding attribution: after an uncontrollable bad event people ask why it happened, and the kind of answer shapes what follows.

DimensionThe two polesWhat it determinesExample for failing an examination
Internal versus externalCaused by me, or by circumstances and othersWhether self-esteem drops"I am stupid" versus "The paper was unusually hard"
Stable versus unstableA lasting cause, or a temporary oneHow long helplessness lasts"I will never be good at this" versus "I was ill that week"
Global versus specificAffects many areas of life, or only this oneHow widely helplessness spreads"I fail at everything" versus "I am weak in statistics"

People who explain bad events with internal, stable and global causes should be most at risk of depression when bad events occur. This is a diathesis-stress model: the attributional tendency is the vulnerability, and it produces depression only together with negative events. In 1989 Abramson and colleagues extended it into the hopelessness theory of depression, in which stable, global attributions lead to hopelessness (the expectation that good outcomes will not come and nothing can be done), which then causes a subtype of depression.

14.4 Explanatory style, learned optimism and the 2016 reversal

Seligman and Christopher Peterson called a person's habitual way of explaining events explanatory style. A pessimistic explanatory style explains bad events as internal, stable and global and good events as external, unstable and specific ("I got lucky this once"); an optimistic explanatory style reverses both patterns.

In Learned Optimism (1990), Seligman translated the dimensions into the "three Ps". Permanence is stability: does the cause last ("always", "never") or pass ("lately")? Pervasiveness is globality: does it spread across life ("all teachers are unfair") or stay in its box ("this teacher is unfair")? Personalisation is internality: do I blame myself, or see the full set of causes? The healthy alternative to personalisation is not blaming others but taking appropriate responsibility without condemning one's whole self.

The standard measure is the Attributional Style Questionnaire, published in 1982 by Peterson, Seligman and colleagues. It presents twelve hypothetical events, six good and six bad, half about achievement and half about relationships. The respondent imagines each happening, writes down its one major cause and rates that cause on seven-point scales for internality, stability and globality; scores are combined separately for bad and good events. Peterson also developed the Content Analysis of Verbatim Explanations, which scores explanatory style from letters, diaries or interviews; applied to essays by young Harvard graduates, it showed that a pessimistic style predicted poorer health from middle age (Peterson, Seligman and colleagues, 1988).

Learned optimism

If explanatory style is learned, it can be relearned. Seligman adapted Albert Ellis's ABC model into an ABCDE sequence. A is the adversity; B is the automatic belief about why it happened; C is the consequence, the feelings and actions that flow from the belief rather than from the adversity itself; D is disputation, arguing with the belief as if an outside critic had said it (what is the evidence, what are the alternatives, what are the real implications, is the belief useful?); E is energisation, the lift in mood and energy that follows. This is cognitive restructuring as used in cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT), aimed at permanence, pervasiveness and personalisation. Seligman's group applied it in the Penn Resiliency Program for schoolchildren, whose preventive effects later reviews found real but small.

Learned helplessness at fifty: the reversal

In 2016 Maier and Seligman published "Learned helplessness at fifty: Insights from neuroscience" in Psychological Review, drawing on Maier's decades of work on the brain circuits involved. Passivity in the face of prolonged aversive events turned out to be the default, unlearned response, driven by strong activation of serotonin neurons in a brainstem region, the dorsal raphe nucleus. What animals learn, when shock is escapable, is control: detecting that a response ends the shock engages the medial prefrontal cortex, which inhibits the dorsal raphe and switches off the default passivity. That learned expectation of control then protects the animal in later stress, which is the real basis of immunisation.

Key. The 1967 theory said helplessness is learned. The 2016 revision says passivity is the default response to prolonged bad events, and control is what is learned. Clinically, the aim shifts to building expectations of control, which connects directly to hope and self-efficacy.

14.5 Hope theory: goals, pathways and agency

In everyday speech hope can be a passive wish ("I hope it doesn't rain"). C. R. Snyder gave the word a precise cognitive meaning: hope is the perceived capacity to produce routes to desired goals, together with the motivation to use those routes.

Who gave us this. C. R. (Rick) Snyder, a clinical psychologist at the University of Kansas, set out hope theory and the Adult Hope Scale with colleagues in 1991, in a paper that summed up the theory as "the will and the ways". He later co-edited the Oxford Handbook of Positive Psychology.

Goals are the anchor: any valued target, from finishing a thesis to walking again after injury. Hope matters most for goals of intermediate probability; if success is certain hope is unnecessary, and if truly impossible it cannot operate. Pathways thinking ("waypower") is the perceived ability to generate workable routes to goals, and another route when one is blocked: "there is a way to do this; if that fails, I can try this." Agency thinking ("willpower") is the perceived capacity to start moving along a pathway and keep going: "I can do this; I will not give up."

The components are additive and iterative: pathways without agency produce plans never carried out, agency without pathways produces energy with nowhere to go, and each feeds the other during goal pursuit. Barriers are central; high-hope people treat them as problems to route around, while low-hope people read them as signs the goal is out of reach. Emotions are by-products of perceived progress: perceived success produces positive emotion, perceived failure negative emotion, and both feed back into later hope. Snyder proposed that hope is learned in childhood through consistent, responsive caregiving.

The Adult Hope Scale has twelve items: four pathways items (for example "I can think of many ways to get out of a jam"), four agency items (for example "I energetically pursue my goals") and four fillers, rated in its common form on an eight-point scale from definitely false to definitely true. Shorter state and children's versions also exist. Higher hope has been associated with better academic and athletic performance, better adjustment to illness and injury, and greater well-being.

Hope, optimism and self-efficacy compared

Self-efficacy, from Albert Bandura (1977), is the belief that one can carry out a specific behaviour, usually in one domain; optimism expects good outcomes without saying how. Snyder argued that agency resembles self-efficacy but is broader, while pathways has no counterpart in either. The three correlate, but each predicts well-being partly independently.

ConstructWho and whenThe question it answersFocusTypical measure
Dispositional optimismMichael Scheier and Charles Carver, 1985Will things generally turn out well for me?Outcomes; silent on how they come aboutLife Orientation Test Revised
Optimistic explanatory styleAbramson, Seligman and Teasdale, 1978; Peterson and SeligmanWhy did that happen?Causal explanations of past eventsAttributional Style Questionnaire
Self-efficacyAlbert Bandura, 1977Can I perform this particular behaviour?Personal capability in one domainDomain-specific self-efficacy scales
HopeC. R. Snyder, 1991Can I find ways to my goals, and will I use them?Goals plus pathways plus agencyAdult Hope Scale

Hope in therapy

Snyder argued that hope is a common factor across psychotherapies: people arrive demoralised, and effective therapies of every school restore morale. Hope therapy, developed by Snyder and colleagues, has four overlapping parts. Hope finding uncovers existing hope through stories of past goals the client achieved. Hope bonding builds a collaborative alliance that itself supplies agency. Hope enhancing teaches the client to set clear, personally chosen goals, break them into small steps, generate several pathways with plans for likely obstacles, and use agency self-talk. Hope reminding helps the client use these skills independently.

Trap. Snyder's hope is cognitive and active, not a feeling or wish. A person who "hopes" for a good result but sees no route and has no drive to act is, in Snyder's terms, low in hope.

14.6 Resilience: what it is and how it was discovered

Resilience is positive adaptation in the context of significant adversity or risk. Both conditions are required: a genuine threat (poverty, abuse, disaster, serious loss or injury) and adaptation that is good by age-appropriate standards. A comfortable child who does well is not resilient; he or she has simply not been tested.

Is resilience a trait, a process or an outcome? Most developmental researchers treat it as a dynamic process: an interaction over time between the person and his or her relationships and environment, which can differ across domains (doing well academically while struggling emotionally) and across life. Suniya Luthar and colleagues (2000) urged language that treats resilience as a process, because talk of children who "have" resilience invites blaming those who do not cope and ignores their environment.

The pioneers

Norman Garmezy, struck that many children of mothers with schizophrenia developed well, shifted attention from what causes disorder to what allows competence; his Project Competence at the University of Minnesota trained Ann Masten.

Emmy Werner's Kauai Longitudinal Study followed all 698 children born in 1955 on the Hawaiian island of Kauai from before birth into middle adulthood. About one third were high risk, facing several adversities by age two, such as birth complications, chronic poverty, family discord or parental mental illness. Most of these children had serious learning, behaviour or mental health problems by adolescence, but roughly one in three grew into competent, confident and caring young adults. Werner's books Vulnerable but Invincible (1982) and Overcoming the Odds (1992) described what set them apart: an infant temperament that drew positive responses from caregivers; at least one stable, caring adult, often a grandparent, older sibling, aunt or teacher rather than a parent; competence in some skill; and often faith or a sense of purpose. Many who struggled as adolescents recovered later through "second chances" such as further education or a stable marriage.

Michael Rutter, the British child psychiatrist, showed in his Isle of Wight studies that single family adversities (such as severe marital discord, low social status, overcrowding, paternal criminality, maternal psychiatric disorder and placement in care) each carried little extra risk alone, but that risk rose sharply, more than additively, when several occurred together. In his 1987 paper on protective mechanisms he argued for studying mechanisms rather than lists of factors, and defined a true protective factor as one whose effect appears specifically under risk. He also described steeling effects: successfully handling manageable stress can strengthen a person against later stress, much as a vaccine does.

"Ordinary magic"

Ann Masten's 2001 article in the American Psychologist, "Ordinary magic: Resilience processes in development", drew these findings together. Earlier writers had called such children "invulnerable", implying rare, extraordinary qualities. Masten argued that resilience is common and arises from the normal functioning of basic human adaptive systems. The same short list keeps appearing: close relationships with competent, caring adults; thinking and problem-solving skills; the ability to regulate emotion and behaviour; a positive self-view and motivation to master the environment; and effective schools, communities and cultural or religious supports. Resilience fails when these systems are damaged, as when a child loses a caregiver, or overwhelmed by extreme, prolonged adversity.

Key. Resilience is ordinary, not heroic: it comes from ordinary systems working under pressure, so the way to promote it is to protect and restore those systems.

14.7 Risk, protection and adult trajectories after loss and trauma

A risk factor raises the probability of a poor outcome; cumulative risk means the number of risks often matters more than any single one. A promotive factor helps at all levels of risk, while a protective factor, strictly, reduces the effect of risk and so matters most when risk is high.

LevelCommon risk factorsCommon protective factors
IndividualDifficult temperament, chronic illness or disability, poor emotion regulation, earlier traumaEasy temperament, problem-solving skill, self-regulation, self-efficacy, hope, humour, faith or purpose
FamilyPoverty, parental conflict or violence, parental mental illness or substance misuse, abuse or neglect, loss of a parentAt least one warm, consistent caregiver; warmth with structure; supportive siblings and extended family; stable routines
CommunityViolence, discrimination, poor schools and services, disaster, displacementCaring teachers and mentors, good schools, safe neighbourhoods, religious and community organisations, accessible services

George Bonanno's trajectories

In a 2004 article in the American Psychologist on loss, trauma and human resilience, George Bonanno challenged two assumptions: that anyone who does not show intense distress after a loss is in denial and will suffer "delayed grief", and that resilience after such events is rare. From longitudinal data on bereaved spouses, disaster survivors and others, Bonanno and colleagues described four trajectories.

  • Resilience is stable healthy functioning with at most brief disturbance; in his studies it was usually the most common trajectory, often describing a majority.
  • Recovery is clearly elevated symptoms for months, then a gradual return towards earlier functioning, often over one to two years.
  • Chronic dysfunction is high distress beginning soon after the event and lasting years, as in prolonged grief, persistent post-traumatic stress or depression.
  • Delayed reactions are initial coping followed by rising symptoms; Bonanno found this uncommon, contrary to the older belief in delayed grief.

Bonanno argued that there are many routes to resilience, including hardiness and positive emotion, and later emphasised flexibility, using different coping strategies as situations demand.

Trap. The claim that resilience is the commonest response has been challenged. Frank Infurna and Suniya Luthar (2016) showed that the statistical method behind many trajectory studies (growth mixture modelling) rests on assumptions that can inflate the resilient group; with those assumptions relaxed, resilience after spousal loss, divorce and unemployment looked considerably less common. The safe answer: resilience is common, but its prevalence depends on the event, the outcome measured and the method.

14.8 Hardiness and sense of coherence

Hardiness

Suzanne Kobasa (who later published as Suzanne Ouellette) introduced hardiness in 1979 in the Journal of Personality and Social Psychology. Studying executives at a large American utility company during heavy organisational change, she compared those who reported many stressful events yet stayed healthy with those who fell ill. The healthy group differed in three attitudes, the three Cs.

  • Commitment is the tendency to involve oneself fully in what one is doing and find it worthwhile, rather than feeling alienated.
  • Control is the belief that one can influence events through one's own efforts, rather than feeling powerless.
  • Challenge is the belief that change, not stability, is normal and offers opportunities for growth rather than mainly threats.

Her colleague Salvatore Maddi described hardiness as existential courage that turns stressful circumstances into opportunities, and developed hardiness training. Critics noted that the first study measured illness and hardiness at the same time, so illness might have lowered hardiness rather than the reverse; that early scales overlapped with low neuroticism; and that challenge was usually the weakest component.

Sense of coherence and salutogenesis

Aaron Antonovsky, an American-born medical sociologist working in Israel, turned the medical question around. Medicine is pathogenic in orientation, asking what causes disease. Antonovsky proposed a salutogenic orientation (from the Latin salus, health, and Greek genesis, origin): why do people stay well despite constant stressors? His question was sharpened by a study of women in Israel that included concentration camp survivors, a substantial minority of whom were in good health despite their experiences.

Who gave us this. Aaron Antonovsky set out salutogenesis in Health, Stress, and Coping (1979) and developed the sense of coherence and its measurement in Unraveling the Mystery of Health (1987).

Antonovsky placed everyone on a health ease/dis-ease continuum rather than dividing people into healthy and sick. His metaphor: medicine stands downstream pulling drowning people out, whereas the salutogenic question is how to make people better swimmers in a river everyone is in. His answer was the sense of coherence, a pervasive, enduring though dynamic feeling of confidence with three components.

  • Comprehensibility (cognitive) is the sense that one's inner and outer world is ordered, predictable and explicable rather than chaotic.
  • Manageability (instrumental) is the sense that resources, one's own or those of trusted others, are available to meet the demands one faces.
  • Meaningfulness (motivational) is the sense that life's demands are worth investing energy in, challenges rather than burdens; Antonovsky considered it the most important of the three.

A strong sense of coherence grows from generalised resistance resources: characteristics of the person, group or environment that help with a wide variety of stressors, such as material security, knowledge, a firm identity, social support, cultural stability and faith. They build it through life experiences that are consistent, balanced between overload and underload, and allow a say in decisions. It is measured by the Orientation to Life Questionnaire, in a 29-item and a widely used 13-item version; higher scores are linked to better perceived health and quality of life. Critics note that scores correlate so strongly with low anxiety and depression that the scale may not measure anything distinct, and that the three components rarely separate in factor analysis.

Trap. Do not muddle Kobasa's commitment, control and challenge with Antonovsky's comprehensibility, manageability and meaningfulness. Hardiness came from personality psychology studying stressed executives; sense of coherence from medical sociology asking why people stay healthy. Meaningfulness resembles commitment and manageability resembles control, but comprehensibility and challenge have no exact counterparts.

14.9 Coping as the frame: appraisal, proactive coping and benefit finding

In Stress, Appraisal, and Coping (1984), Richard Lazarus and Susan Folkman proposed a transactional model: stress lies in the relationship between person and environment, arising when a person appraises demands as taxing or exceeding his or her resources. Primary appraisal asks whether the event matters, and how: irrelevant, benign, or stressful, with stressful appraisals divided into harm or loss, threat, and challenge. Secondary appraisal asks what can be done, taking stock of resources and options. Coping is the changing cognitive and behavioural effort to manage those demands, and reappraisal updates the picture as events unfold. Problem-focused coping acts on the problem; emotion-focused coping manages the distress. Neither is inherently better: problem-focused coping tends to help when the situation is controllable and emotion-focused when it is not. Folkman later added meaning-focused coping, using values and goals to sustain well-being in situations that cannot be solved, through positive reappraisal, revising goals and finding benefit. Optimism, hope and sense of coherence strengthen secondary appraisal, hardiness's challenge shapes primary appraisal, and explanatory style is a habitual reappraisal after the event.

Proactive coping, described by Lisa Aspinwall and Shelley Taylor (1997), is effort made in advance of a potentially stressful event to prevent it or change its form: building resources such as money, skills and relationships, recognising early warning signs, appraising them, making preliminary efforts and using feedback, as a student does who keeps up with reading all term.

Benefit finding is perceiving positive consequences of an adverse experience, such as closer relationships or changed priorities. Researchers distinguish it from benefit reminding, deliberately recalling those benefits as a coping strategy. A 2006 meta-analysis found benefit finding associated with less depression and more positive well-being, but also with more intrusive thoughts about the event, suggesting it is part of an active, sometimes painful process of working through. Benefit finding overlaps heavily with post-traumatic growth.

14.10 Post-traumatic growth

Post-traumatic growth is positive psychological change experienced as a result of the struggle with highly challenging life crises. The change goes beyond the person's previous level in some respect, and it comes from the struggle with the aftermath, not from the trauma itself.

Who gave us this. Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun, at the University of North Carolina at Charlotte, introduced the term in the mid-1990s and published the Post-Traumatic Growth Inventory (PTGI) in the Journal of Traumatic Stress in 1996.

The inventory has 21 items, each rated from 0 (I did not experience this change as a result of my crisis) to 5 (I experienced this change to a very great degree). Factor analysis produced five domains; a later expanded version broadened the spiritual domain, criticised as too narrowly religious, to spiritual and existential change.

DomainItemsWhat it capturesHow it might sound
Relating to others7Closer relationships, compassion, willingness to express emotion and accept help"I found out who really cares, and I let people in now."
New possibilities5New interests, a new path, openness to opportunities not previously considered"I would never have become a counsellor otherwise."
Personal strength4Feeling stronger and more self-reliant than one had realised"If I survived that, I can handle this."
Spiritual change2Deeper spiritual understanding or stronger faith"My faith became my own rather than a habit."
Appreciation of life3Changed priorities, valuing each day and small things"I no longer take an ordinary morning for granted."

How growth happens

Tedeschi and Calhoun describe trauma as a "seismic" event that shakes core beliefs, for example that the world is fair, predictable and meaningful and that the self is worthy. At first the person experiences intrusive rumination, unwanted thoughts and images of the event. Over time, especially with support and chances to talk, this can give way to deliberate rumination, a reflective effort to make sense of what happened and rebuild beliefs; growth emerges from the rebuilding and is woven into a revised life story. Growth and distress coexist: people often report growth while still grieving or symptomatic. Tedeschi and Calhoun advised clinicians to act as expert companions, noticing growth when the client raises it rather than pushing it.

Resilience means functioning stays relatively stable; recovery means it drops and returns; growth means that in some domain it ends up higher than before. Highly resilient people may report less growth, because an event that does not shake their beliefs leaves less to rebuild.

Real growth or illusion?

The main criticism concerns self-report. The inventory asks people to judge how much they have changed, which requires remembering accurately what they were like before; people are poor at such comparisons and tend to underrate their past selves, which makes the present look like growth. Patricia Frazier and colleagues (2009) measured students before and after a traumatic event and found that self-reported growth was only weakly related to actual measured change in the same qualities, and that perceived growth went with more distress, not less. The scale also offers no option for negative change, and cultural "silver lining" scripts may encourage growth reports. Andreas Maercker and Tanja Zoellner proposed a Janus-face model, after the two-faced Roman god: one face is constructive, self-transcending growth linked to better long-term adjustment; the other is illusory, self-deceptive growth that mainly soothes distress in the short term.

Key. Reported growth is meaningful to survivors, but a high inventory score is not proof of actual change. Never make growth an expectation; a client who reports none is not failing.

14.11 The Indian context

Most of this research was done in North America and Western Europe. In India the family, often extended or joint, is usually the first and largest source of support after adversity: relatives take turns at the hospital bedside, pool money for treatment and share caregiving. The same system can also strain, through expectations, conflict over decisions, or stigma that leads families to hide mental illness. Community supports, including neighbours, self-help groups, colleagues and religious institutions such as temples, mosques, churches and gurudwaras, provide material help and belonging. Help-seeking often passes through elders and religious or traditional healers before reaching mental health services, so a clinician works with these networks rather than around them.

Cultural meanings of adversity must be discussed in careful, general terms, because beliefs vary widely across individuals, communities and religions. Many Indian traditions hold, broadly, that actions have consequences that unfold over long periods (karma), and ideas of fate are common. Such beliefs can work either way. For some they support acceptance, reduce bitterness towards others and make suffering comprehensible and meaningful, echoing Antonovsky's comprehensibility and meaningfulness and Folkman's meaning-focused coping. For others they feed self-blame, much like an internal, stable attribution, or a fatalistic passivity resembling helplessness. The Bhagavad Gita's teaching of acting without attachment to the fruits of action is often read as full effort combined with acceptance that outcomes are not wholly in one's control, which resembles high agency with acceptance. The clinician's task is to explore what the belief means to this person, not to assume.

Finally, the standard scales in this chapter were developed in Western samples, so adapted versions must be validated for the group assessed. Access matters too: the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 recognises a right to mental health care, and the national tele-mental health service, Tele-MANAS, reaches people far from specialist clinics.

14.12 Case: a road accident survivor across the first year

Case.

Arjun, a 27-year-old software tester in Pune, was riding his motorcycle home on a highway at night when a truck swerved into his lane. After two operations on open fractures of his right leg, surgeons performed a below-knee amputation. He lives with his parents; his elder brother lives nearby.

Month one. In hospital he had nightmares and flashbacks of headlights, startled at loud noises and said, "My life is finished. I ruin everything I touch. I will never work properly again." His grandmother told visitors it must be his karma, which made him feel the accident was his fault. His mother and brother took turns at his bedside, and colleagues collected money for treatment.

Months two to four. During prosthetic fitting he was low, avoided road travel and refused to discuss the accident. A psychologist at the rehabilitation centre screened him for post-traumatic stress and depression; both were in the clinical range, and he began trauma-focused cognitive behavioural therapy.

Months five to eight. He set a goal with his therapist: walking into his office unaided by the first anniversary. They broke it into steps (standing with the prosthesis, walking the corridor, stairs, car journeys with his brother) and planned alternatives for setbacks such as stump pain. Disputing his early explanations, he began to say, "A truck driver swerved; my recovery is slow, not impossible; I am good at my job and my leg does not change that." His brother helped him file a claim before the Motor Accident Claims Tribunal, easing financial worry.

Months nine to twelve. He returned to work part time, joined a peer group of amputees and began talking to college students about helmets and highway safety. At one year he said, "I still hate highways and my leg hurts in the cold. But I am closer to my brother than ever, I notice small things, and I know I can handle hard things. Whatever the reason it happened, my duty now is to act well."

Trajectory. Arjun fits Bonanno's recovery trajectory, not resilience: symptoms were clearly elevated for months, then declined. Early screening mattered, because persisting symptoms would have signalled the chronic trajectory.

Explanatory style. His month-one statements are textbook pessimism: permanent ("never"), pervasive ("everything I touch") and personal ("I ruin"), with the karma comment feeding personalisation. His later statements show disputation at work (an external cause, an unstable explanation for slow progress, a bounded view of the loss), which is Seligman's ABCDE within cognitive behavioural therapy.

Hope. Rehabilitation was hope theory applied: a valued goal of intermediate probability, pathways built from small steps and plans for barriers, and agency growing with each success.

Resilience and sense of coherence. Protection came from every level: individual (skills), family (mother and brother, the adult version of Werner's caring adults) and community (colleagues, the rehabilitation centre, the peer group, the compensation system). Therapy and medical explanation raised comprehensibility, family and financial support raised manageability, and the safety talks gave his suffering meaningfulness.

Growth. His one-year statements map onto four domains: relating to others, new possibilities, personal strength and appreciation of life. The growth coexists with pain and fear, and because his reports are retrospective, the behavioural evidence (returning to work, volunteering) matters. His reworking of the karma belief, from self-blame to present duty, shows a cultural meaning becoming a resource once explored rather than dismissed.

Recap.

Dispositional optimism, from Michael Scheier and Charles Carver, is a generalised expectation of good outcomes, measured by the Life Orientation Test Revised, that works mainly through approach coping and predicts outcomes such as recovery after bypass surgery, while Julie Norem's defensive pessimism shows that low expectations can serve anxious people well. Learned helplessness began with Martin Seligman and Steven Maier's dogs, was reformulated in attributional terms by Lyn Abramson, Seligman and John Teasdale, gave rise to explanatory style (permanence, pervasiveness, personalisation) and learned optimism, and was reversed in 2016 when Maier and Seligman concluded that passivity is the default and control is learned. C. R. Snyder's hope theory defines hope as goals pursued through pathways and agency thinking, distinct from optimism and self-efficacy, and it underpins hope therapy. Resilience is positive adaptation despite significant adversity, an ordinary process (Ann Masten's "ordinary magic") built from protective factors at individual, family and community levels, grounded in Emmy Werner's Kauai study and Michael Rutter's work, with George Bonanno's trajectories describing adult responses, though how common resilience is remains debated. Hardiness (commitment, control, challenge) and Aaron Antonovsky's sense of coherence (comprehensibility, manageability, meaningfulness) are protective orientations, and Lazarus and Folkman's appraisal model, proactive coping and benefit finding frame them all. Post-traumatic growth, measured in five domains by Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun's inventory, differs from resilience and recovery, coexists with distress and may be partly illusory because it rests on retrospective self-report. In India, family, community and cultural meanings such as karma can protect or burden, and the clinician explores them, as the road accident case shows.

NameWhat they gave us
Michael ScheierDispositional optimism and the Life Orientation Test (1985, with Carver), its 1994 revision, the bypass surgery study (1989)
Charles CarverCo-founder of dispositional optimism and the self-regulation model; optimism and coping in breast cancer
Lisa AspinwallOptimists attend to relevant risk information; proactive coping with Taylor (1997)
Julie NoremDefensive pessimism versus strategic optimism; The Positive Power of Negative Thinking (2001)

Lawrence CalhounPost-traumatic growth and its inventory (1996, with Tedeschi)
