Tanja ZoellnerThe Janus-face model of growth, with Maercker

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. Define dispositional optimism and describe how it is measured. Discuss the evidence linking optimism to coping and physical health, and explain how defensive pessimism challenges the view that optimism is always adaptive.
  2. Describe the original learned helplessness experiments and the attributional reformulation. How did Maier and Seligman's 2016 review change our understanding of the phenomenon, and what follows for therapy?
  3. Explain explanatory style with reference to permanence, pervasiveness and personalisation. How is it measured, and how can a pessimistic style be changed?
  4. Critically discuss Snyder's hope theory. Distinguish hope from optimism and self-efficacy, and describe how hope can be enhanced in psychotherapy.
  5. "Resilience is ordinary magic." Discuss with reference to definitions of resilience, the Kauai Longitudinal Study, risk and protective factors, and Bonanno's trajectories after loss and trauma.
  6. Compare Kobasa's hardiness with Antonovsky's sense of coherence, placing both within Lazarus and Folkman's transactional model of stress and coping.
  7. What is post-traumatic growth? Describe its five domains and how it is thought to occur, distinguish it from resilience and recovery, and evaluate the criticism that reported growth may be illusory.

15 Love, relationships, meaning and spirituality

Earlier chapters looked at well-being mostly from the inside. This chapter turns outward and upward. Outward, because the most consistent finding in the science of well-being is that people flourish in the company of others. Upward, because people also ask why their lives matter, and many answer through meaning, religion or a personal spirituality. The positive psychology texts (Baumgardner and Crothers; the Oxford Handbook) give these topics whole chapters, and the clinical textbooks (Pomerantz; Trull and Phares) return to them in couple therapy, grief work and culturally sensitive practice. Two worked cases close the chapter.

15.1 The need to belong: why relationships sit at the centre of well-being

In 1995 Roy Baumeister and Mark Leary argued in the journal Psychological Bulletin that the need to belong is a fundamental human motivation. They set criteria that any fundamental need should meet: it should operate in nearly all people and cultures, arise readily under ordinary conditions, shape emotion and thought, and harm health and adjustment when frustrated. The desire for close bonds meets all of them. People form attachments quickly, even with strangers thrown together by chance; they resist ending bonds, even bad ones; and loss of belonging through rejection, divorce, death or exile produces anxiety, depression and loneliness.

Their key point is that the need has two parts. People need frequent, pleasant interactions with a few others, and those interactions must occur within a stable, lasting framework of mutual concern, a relationship expected to continue in which each cares about the other's welfare. Neither part alone is enough: a salesperson who chats warmly with hundreds of customers has contact without a bond, and a husband working abroad for years has a bond with little contact.

Positive psychology evidence points the same way. In 2002 Ed Diener and Martin Seligman published "Very happy people". They screened 222 university students on several happiness measures and compared the happiest tenth with average and unhappy students. The very happy group did not have more objectively good events. What set them apart was social: they spent less time alone, and all of them had good close relationships, as rated by themselves and by their friends. Good relationships looked necessary but not sufficient for very high happiness: every very happy person had them, but having them did not guarantee it.

Trap. "Very happy people" is correlational and used American undergraduates. It shows that close relationships go with very high happiness; it does not prove relationships cause it, since happy people also attract friends. Write "necessary but not sufficient" and name the design limits.

15.2 Social support, health and loneliness

Social support means the resources other people provide, or that we believe they would provide, when we need them. It may be emotional (care, reassurance), instrumental (practical help such as money or meals), informational (advice) or appraisal (feedback that helps a person evaluate their situation). Perceived support is the belief that help would be there; received support is help actually given. Perceived support is the more reliable predictor of well-being, because being helped can also make a person feel incompetent or indebted.

In 1985 Sheldon Cohen and Thomas Wills set out two models of how support protects. The main effect model says that being embedded in relationships benefits people at all times, through regular positive experience, identity and pressure towards healthy habits. The stress-buffering model says support matters most under high stress, because it makes stressors look less threatening and helps people cope. Both have evidence. The health consequences are large: in 2010 Julianne Holt-Lunstad, Timothy Smith and Bradley Layton pooled 148 studies and found that people with stronger social relationships had roughly a 50 percent greater likelihood of survival over the periods studied, an effect comparable to well-known risks such as smoking. The pathways are behavioural, physiological and psychological.

Loneliness is not the same as being alone. John Cacioppo, whose book with William Patrick, Loneliness, appeared in 2008, defined it as perceived social isolation: the painful sense that one's relationships are fewer or less satisfying than one wants. One can be lonely in a large joint family. Cacioppo offered an evolutionary account: as hunger signals the need for food, loneliness signals that bonds are threatened and pushes the person to repair them. But the signal also makes the lonely person watchful for rejection, so they read ambiguous behaviour as hostile, withdraw, and confirm their fears. This is why the most effective loneliness interventions target the lonely person's social thinking, not just their opportunities for contact.

Key. Belonging needs frequent pleasant contact inside a stable bond of mutual concern. Perceived support predicts well-being better than received support. Loneliness is perceived, not objective, isolation, and it sustains itself through hypervigilance to rejection.

15.3 Love: Sternberg's triangle, Lee's styles, passionate and companionate love

Sternberg's triangular theory

In 1986 Robert Sternberg proposed in Psychological Review that love has three components, pictured as the corners of a triangle. Intimacy is the emotional component: closeness, warmth, trust and feeling understood. Passion is the motivational component: romantic and physical attraction and the drive towards union. Commitment is the cognitive component: in the short term the decision that one loves the person, in the long term the decision to maintain that love. Each can be present or absent, giving eight kinds of love.

Kind of loveIntimacyPassionCommitmentWhat it looks like
NonloveNoNoNoCasual daily dealings with acquaintances.
LikingYesNoNoWarm friendship without desire or long-term pledge.
Infatuated loveNoYesNo"Love at first sight" for someone barely known.
Empty loveNoNoYesA marriage kept from duty after warmth and desire have gone; in some arranged marriages, the starting point from which intimacy grows.
Romantic loveYesYesNoA close, passionate affair with no decision about the future.
Companionate loveYesNoYesA long marriage or deep friendship in which passion has faded.
Fatuous loveNoYesYesA whirlwind engagement: commitment built on passion without intimacy.
Consummate loveYesYesYesThe complete form, which Sternberg said is easier to reach than to keep.

The components change over time: passion rises fast and then tends to decline, intimacy grows gradually, and commitment builds as the relationship proves itself. Sternberg argued that satisfaction depends partly on how well the partners' triangles match. Research supports the distinction, though in real couples the components overlap more than the triangle suggests.

John Alan Lee's love styles

The Canadian sociologist John Alan Lee, in Colours of Love (1973), described styles of loving, compared to colours on a wheel. The primary styles are eros (passionate love centred on strong attraction), ludus (game-playing, uncommitted love) and storge (friendship-based love that grows slowly). Mixtures give the secondary styles pragma (practical love that chooses a partner who fits set criteria), mania (possessive, jealous, dependent love) and agape (selfless, giving love). They are best seen as attitudes to love that can shift across relationships, not fixed types.

Passionate and companionate love

Elaine Hatfield drew the most widely taught distinction. Passionate love is an intense longing for union with another, with physiological arousal, intrusive thoughts about the beloved and idealisation. Companionate love is the calmer affection, trust and tenderness felt towards someone with whom one's life is deeply intertwined. Hatfield explained passion with a two-factor account: passionate love arises when a person is physiologically aroused and labels the arousal as love. The famous support is Donald Dutton and Arthur Aron's 1974 study, in which men met by an attractive interviewer on a high, swaying suspension bridge showed more attraction to her than men on a low, stable bridge, apparently misattributing fear arousal to attraction.

Trap. Sternberg's "companionate love" is intimacy plus commitment, one of eight combinations. Hatfield's "companionate love" is one of only two broad kinds. The ideas are similar, but the theories differ and must be credited separately.

15.4 Adult attachment: love as an attachment process

John Bowlby proposed that infants have an attachment system that keeps them close to a caregiver, and Mary Ainsworth's Strange Situation identified secure, anxious-ambivalent and avoidant infant patterns. In 1987 Cindy Hazan and Phillip Shaver proposed that adult romantic love is itself an attachment process run by the same system. Through a newspaper "love quiz", they asked readers which of three short paragraphs, written to mirror Ainsworth's patterns, best described them in close relationships. A little over half chose the secure description, and the rest split between avoidant and anxious.

StyleCore beliefTypical behaviour in relationships
SecureI am worthy of love; others are generally reliable.Comfortable with closeness and dependence; trusts; raises conflict openly; seeks and gives support.
AnxiousI may not be lovable enough; others may leave me.Wants more closeness than the partner offers; fears abandonment; jealous; reads small signs as rejection; seeks constant reassurance.
AvoidantDepending on others is risky or unnecessary.Uncomfortable with closeness; prizes independence; suppresses emotion; withdraws under stress; offers little support to a distressed partner.

Later work refined this. Kim Bartholomew and Leonard Horowitz (1991) crossed a positive or negative model of self with a positive or negative model of others to give four styles: secure, preoccupied, dismissing-avoidant and fearful-avoidant, the last wanting closeness yet fearing it. Shaver and colleagues then showed in 1998 that attachment is best measured on two continuous dimensions, anxiety (fear of rejection) and avoidance (discomfort with closeness), through the Experiences in Close Relationships questionnaire; a secure person is low on both. Under threat, anxious people hyperactivate the attachment system (clinging, protesting, monitoring the partner), while avoidant people deactivate it (suppressing needs, keeping distance). Pair the two and a pursue-withdraw cycle often follows: the more one seeks reassurance, the more the other retreats, confirming both partners' fears.

Why it matters. Much modern couple therapy treats conflict as protest about attachment security. Styles are moderately stable but not destiny: a reliable relationship or therapy can build "earned security".

15.5 What makes relationships last: John Gottman's research

John Gottman, working with Robert Levenson from the 1980s and later in his University of Washington laboratory, studied couples by observation rather than questionnaire alone. Couples discussed a disagreement while being videotaped; faces, words and tone were coded moment by moment, physiological signals such as heart rate were recorded, and the couples were followed for years.

The four horsemen

Four patterns predicted divorce so strongly that Gottman named them, after the biblical image, the four horsemen of the apocalypse.

HorsemanWhat it isExampleAntidote
CriticismAttacking the partner's character instead of complaining about a behaviour."You never think of anyone but yourself."Gentle start-up: state the situation, your feeling and a request.
ContemptSpeaking from superiority: sarcasm, mockery, eye-rolling, insults."Of course the great genius forgot again."Build a culture of appreciation; express fondness and respect.
DefensivenessCounter-attacking or playing the innocent victim, which blames the partner."It's not my fault, you're the one who..."Take responsibility for even part of the problem.
StonewallingWithdrawing: going silent, looking away, leaving.Staring at the phone while the partner speaks.Self-soothing: a break of at least twenty minutes, then return.

Gottman identified contempt as the single strongest predictor of divorce, because it communicates disgust. Stonewalling is tied to flooding, diffuse physiological arousal in which a person cannot take in what the partner says and so shuts down. He also stressed repair attempts that stop negativity escalating and turning towards a partner's small bids for connection.

The five to one ratio

Gottman's most quoted finding, summarised in What Predicts Divorce? (1994), is that in stable couples, during conflict discussions, positive behaviours (interest, humour, affection, validation) outnumbered negative ones (criticism, anger, contempt, defensiveness) by about five to one; in couples heading for divorce the ratio was near one to one. Good couples still fight; what matters is the balance.

Trap. Do not confuse Gottman's five to one with Marcial Losada's "positivity ratio" of about 2.9 to 1, which Nicholas Brown, Alan Sokal and Harris Friedman showed in 2013 to rest on invalid mathematics. Gottman's figure is an observed average in stable couples during conflict, not a law or a magic threshold. Publicised claims that Gottman "predicts divorce with over 90 percent accuracy" also need caution: they came from fitting models to couples whose outcomes were already known, which inflates accuracy.

15.6 Capitalisation, minding, and marriage in the Indian context

Capitalisation and active-constructive responding

Most relationship research asks how partners handle bad events. Shelly Gable asked what happens when things go right. Christopher Langston had used capitalisation in 1994 for telling others about a positive event, which boosts positive feeling beyond the event itself. Gable, Harry Reis, Emily Impett and Evan Asher (2004) showed that the benefit depends on the listener's response, which can be active or passive and constructive or destructive.

ConstructiveDestructive
ActiveActive-constructive: enthusiastic, engaged. "That's wonderful! How did they tell you?"Active-destructive: deflating. "That means longer hours; can you manage?"
PassivePassive-constructive: quiet approval. "That's nice," while reading on.Passive-destructive: ignoring or turning to oneself. "Guess what happened to me today."

Only active-constructive responding was reliably linked with greater satisfaction, intimacy, trust and daily positive feeling. Surprisingly, passive-constructive responding, which sounds supportive, behaved much like the destructive responses, because it signals that the listener does not really care. The mechanism is perceived responsiveness: the sense that the partner understands, values and cares for the self.

Minding the relationship

John Harvey and Julia Omarzu (1997) proposed that relationships are maintained by minding, which Baumgardner and Crothers present as the "mindful relationship". It has five components: knowing and being known through continued self-disclosure and attentive listening; relationship-enhancing attributions, explaining a partner's good behaviour by their character and bad behaviour by passing circumstances; acceptance and respect for what is learnt; reciprocity, so both partners do the minding; and continuity over the life of the relationship, not just in courtship.

Arranged and love marriage in India

Students often ask whether arranged marriages are happier than love marriages. Only careful, general statements are supportable. The categories blur: many Indian marriages are "semi-arranged", with families introducing and the couple choosing. Comparisons suffer from selection, since those who take each route differ in background and expectations, so differences in satisfaction cannot be credited to the type of marriage. No consistent, large difference has been shown, and the popular claim that "love grows in arranged marriages" rests on small, old studies. In Sternberg's language, arranged marriages may start with commitment and build intimacy, while love marriages start with passion and intimacy and must build commitment. A clinician should also remember that consent, coercion, dowry and family involvement in conflict affect well-being in ways satisfaction ratings miss.

15.7 Prosocial behaviour, altruism and kindness

Prosocial behaviour is any action intended to benefit another: helping, sharing, donating, comforting, volunteering. Altruism describes the motive: helping whose ultimate goal is the other's welfare rather than one's own. The same act, such as giving blood, can be altruistic or egoistic depending on why it is done. Kindness, one of the character strengths in the Values in Action (VIA) classification, is the enduring tendency to do good deeds for others.

Is any helping truly selfless? Robert Cialdini's negative-state relief model said no: we help to relieve our own sadness at another's suffering. Daniel Batson proposed the empathy-altruism hypothesis: when we feel empathic concern for a person in need (sympathy, compassion and tenderness focused on them), we become motivated to relieve their suffering for their sake; without it, we help mainly for egoistic reasons such as avoiding guilt. His key test was the ease-of-escape design. In a 1981 study, participants watched a young woman, "Elaine", apparently receiving electric shocks and could offer to take her place. Low-empathy participants helped when escape was hard but mostly left when leaving was easy, as egoism predicts; high-empathy participants helped even when they could easily walk away, as altruism predicts. Cialdini replied that empathy creates a sense of "oneness", so helping the other partly helps an expanded self. Batson's work remains the standard evidence that altruism is possible.

Helping also benefits the helper. Elizabeth Dunn, Lara Aknin and Michael Norton reported in Science in 2008 that spending more on others went with greater happiness and, experimentally, that people given five or twenty dollars and told to spend it on others that day ended up happier than those told to spend it on themselves. The amount did not matter; the use did. Larger replication efforts suggest the effect is real but smaller than first reported.

Key. Prosocial describes the act; altruism describes the motive. Batson's hypothesis says empathic concern produces genuinely other-oriented motivation, tested with the ease-of-escape design. Prosocial spending raises the giver's happiness, though modestly.

15.8 Forgiveness

What forgiveness is and is not

Definitions vary, but researchers agree on what forgiveness is not. It is not pardoning (a legal release from punishment), condoning (treating the offence as acceptable), excusing (deciding the person was not responsible), or forgetting the hurt. Nor is it reconciliation, the restoration of a relationship, which needs both people and renewed trust. A woman can forgive an abusive former partner and wisely never reconcile with him. Forgiveness is an inner change in the victim; reconciliation is an interpersonal event.

McCullough's motivational view

Michael McCullough, with Everett Worthington and colleagues (1997), defined forgiveness as a set of prosocial changes in motivation towards a transgressor. After a hurt, people are naturally motivated to avoid the offender and to seek revenge; forgiving means these motives decline and benevolence grows. McCullough's Transgression-Related Interpersonal Motivations Inventory measures these motives, making forgiveness measurable.

Worthington's REACH model

Everett Worthington, whose own mother was murdered, developed a five-step intervention that spells REACH. Recall the hurt as objectively as possible. Empathise with the offender, trying to understand the pressures behind the act, which is not excusing it. Give the altruistic gift of forgiveness, often by remembering times one was forgiven oneself. Commit publicly to forgive. Hold on to forgiveness when anger returns, knowing that recurring hurt does not mean the forgiveness was false. Worthington also separates decisional forgiveness (deciding to treat the offender without revenge) from emotional forgiveness (the slower replacement of negative feelings with empathy and compassion).

Enright's process model

Robert Enright defines forgiveness as a willed giving up of resentment towards someone who acted unjustly, with an effort to offer them compassion they may not deserve. His process model groups about twenty units into four phases. In the uncovering phase the person faces the injury and the anger and shame it caused. In the decision phase they see that current strategies are failing and consider forgiveness. In the work phase they reframe the offender in wider context, build empathy and accept the pain. In the deepening phase they find meaning in the suffering and release from resentment.

Forgiveness and health

Charlotte vanOyen Witvliet and colleagues (2001) had participants imagine a real offender in unforgiving ways (rehearsing the hurt, holding a grudge) and in forgiving ways. Unforgiving imagery raised heart rate and blood pressure. Forgiveness goes with less depression and anxiety and better self-rated health. There is a dark side: James McNulty's studies of newlyweds found that when a partner often behaved badly, a spouse's tendency to forgive predicted that the bad behaviour continued. Forgiveness should never be pressed on someone still in an unsafe relationship.

Trap. Forgiveness does not require reconciliation. Forgiveness is an inner change in the victim's motives and feelings; reconciliation restores the relationship and requires trustworthiness from the offender.

15.9 Meaning in life

Viktor Frankl and logotherapy

Viktor Frankl, a Viennese psychiatrist, survived Nazi concentration camps including Auschwitz, where members of his family died. Man's Search for Meaning, first published in German in 1946, draws a lesson from camp life: prisoners who kept a sense that something still awaited them, a loved one or an unfinished task, endured better. Frankl quoted Nietzsche's idea that one who has a why to live can bear almost any how, and argued that even when everything else is taken, a person keeps the freedom to choose their attitude to their circumstances.

From this came logotherapy (from the Greek logos, meaning), called the "third Viennese school of psychotherapy" after Sigmund Freud's psychoanalysis and Alfred Adler's individual psychology. Where Freud stressed a will to pleasure and Adler a will to power, Frankl held that the primary human motive is a will to meaning. When it is frustrated, people fall into an existential vacuum of boredom and emptiness, which can produce noogenic neurosis, distress arising from the existential dimension. Meaning, for Frankl, is discovered rather than invented, through three avenues:

  • Creative values: meaning found by creating a work or doing a deed, what we give to the world.
  • Experiential values: meaning found by experiencing beauty, truth or nature, or by encountering another person in love, what we receive from the world.
  • Attitudinal values: meaning found in the stand we take towards unavoidable suffering, the deepest source when the other two are closed.

Logotherapy's techniques include paradoxical intention (asking an anxious patient to wish for what they fear, breaking the cycle of anticipatory anxiety) and dereflection (turning attention from excessive self-observation towards people and tasks).

Steger's Meaning in Life Questionnaire

Michael Steger and colleagues (2006) built the most used modern measure, the Meaning in Life Questionnaire: ten items on a seven-point scale forming two five-item subscales. Presence of meaning is how far a person feels their life is meaningful; search for meaning is how actively they are looking for it. The two are largely independent: one can have meaning and still search. Presence is consistently linked with higher life satisfaction and lower depression. Search tends to go with lower well-being in American samples, but Steger and colleagues found search and presence positively related in Japan, so what searching signifies depends on culture.

Meaning-making after loss

Crystal Park, building on work with Susan Folkman (1997) and reviewing the field in 2010, gives the standard meaning-making model. Each person holds global meaning: broad beliefs (for example, that the world is fair), goals and a sense of purpose. A stressful event receives a situational meaning, an appraisal of what this event means. Distress comes from the discrepancy between the two. Meaning-making reduces it either by changing the appraisal to fit existing beliefs (assimilation: "it was God's plan") or by changing global beliefs to accommodate the event (accommodation: "the world is not always fair, but people can still be good"). The meanings made include acceptance, revised goals and growth. Park stresses that searching helps only when meaning is found; prolonged, unsuccessful searching goes with more distress.

Meaning versus happiness

Roy Baumeister, Kathleen Vohs, Jennifer Aaker and Emily Garbinsky (2013) surveyed about four hundred American adults and found that happiness and meaningfulness overlap substantially but have different correlates. Happiness went with satisfied needs and wants, health, enough money, being a "taker" and a focus on the present. Meaningfulness went with linking past, present and future, being a "giver", expressing oneself and, strikingly, more stress and worry. Raising children is the classic example.

15.10 Religion and spirituality

Religion usually means an organised, institutional system of beliefs, practices, rituals and community concerned with the sacred. Spirituality usually means a personal search for connection with the sacred or transcendent, inside or outside a religion. Kenneth Pargament defined spirituality as "a search for the sacred", where the sacred includes the divine and anything that takes on sacred qualities by association, such as a marriage or a calling. The two overlap heavily, but some people are "spiritual but not religious".

Intrinsic and extrinsic orientation

Gordon Allport, with J. Michael Ross in their 1967 Religious Orientation Scale, distinguished two ways of being religious. An intrinsic orientation treats religion as a master motive, internalised and lived in every area of life. An extrinsic orientation uses religion as a means to other ends, such as status, security or community. In Allport's summary, the extrinsic person uses religion while the intrinsic person lives it. It also resolved a puzzle about prejudice among churchgoers: prejudice went with the extrinsic orientation, not the intrinsic. Intrinsic religiousness is also more consistently linked with well-being.

Religious coping

Pargament's The Psychology of Religion and Coping (1997), and the short measure he developed with colleagues (the Brief RCOPE, 1998), showed that religion can help or harm coping depending on how it is used.

Positive religious copingNegative religious coping (religious struggle)
Benevolent reappraisal: seeing the stressor as part of a loving plan or a chance to grow.Punishing God reappraisal: "God is punishing me for my sins."
Collaborative coping: working with God to solve the problem.Passive deferral: waiting for God to fix it without personal effort.
Seeking spiritual support, connection and comfort.Spiritual discontent: feeling abandoned by or angry with God.
Seeking help from clergy or the religious community.Interpersonal religious discontent: conflict with the community or its leaders.

Positive religious coping goes with better adjustment and growth; negative coping goes with depression, anxiety and poorer health. In a study by Pargament and colleagues (2001), religious struggle among medically ill older patients predicted a higher risk of dying over a two-year follow-up.

Religion, spirituality and well-being

Across many studies, religiousness and spirituality show a modest positive association with life satisfaction and a modest negative association with depression, substance misuse and mortality. Three explanations dominate. Social support: religious communities give belonging and practical help. Meaning: religion offers a framework for understanding suffering and a sense of purpose, Park's global meaning in action. Health behaviour: many traditions discourage smoking, alcohol and risky conduct. Ed Diener, Louis Tay and David Myers (2011) added a qualification: the link is stronger in nations with difficult living conditions and weaker in wealthy, secure nations, where non-religious people find support and meaning elsewhere.

Trap. The link is correlational and modest. Healthier people can attend services more (reverse causation), and religion can also bring guilt and struggle. Write "religion is associated with well-being, mainly through social support, meaning and health behaviour", never "religion makes people happy".

Indian concepts of well-being

Indian traditions offer rich, if not always empirically tested, ideas about well-being. The Upanishads distinguish ananda, a bliss rooted in realising one's true self, from passing pleasure (sukha). The Bhagavad Gita teaches nishkama karma, action without attachment to its fruits, and describes the sthitaprajna, a person of steady wisdom who stays even-minded in gain and loss. Buddhist traditions describe loving-kindness (maitri) and compassion (karuna), which inspired modern compassion practices, and seva (selfless service) connects to the prosocial research above. These ideas matter clinically because Indian clients often make sense of suffering through karma, dharma or divine will, and culturally sensitive therapy works with those meanings rather than dismissing them.

15.11 Humility, compassion and self-compassion

Humility was long confused with low self-esteem. June Price Tangney (2000) argued that true humility involves an accurate assessment of one's abilities (neither inflated nor falsely low), the ability to acknowledge mistakes and limits, openness to new ideas, keeping achievements in perspective, a low focus on the self, and appreciation of the worth of all people and things.

Compassion is the feeling that arises on witnessing suffering, joined to a motivation to help. Paul Gilbert, founder of compassion-focused therapy, defines it as sensitivity to suffering in self and others with a commitment to try to relieve and prevent it. It differs from empathy (sharing or understanding another's feeling) and sympathy (feeling sorry for someone). Feeling another's pain as one's own, called empathic distress, tends to produce withdrawal and burnout, whereas compassion sustains helping. Gilbert's therapy strengthens a soothing emotion system to balance the threat and drive systems in people high in shame and self-criticism.

Kristin Neff (2003) brought self-compassion into psychology from Buddhist thought: treating oneself, in failure or suffering, with the kindness one would show a good friend. It has three components, each with an opposite, measured by her twenty-six-item Self-Compassion Scale.

ComponentMeaningIts opposite
Self-kindnessBeing warm and understanding towards oneself when failing or feeling inadequate.Self-judgement: harsh self-criticism.
Common humanitySeeing suffering and imperfection as part of shared human experience.Isolation: feeling alone in one's failure.
MindfulnessHolding painful thoughts and feelings in balanced awareness, neither suppressing nor exaggerating them.Over-identification: being swept away by the emotion.

Self-compassion is not self-esteem. Self-esteem rests on evaluating oneself positively, often by comparison, so it rises and falls with success and can slide into narcissism; self-compassion needs no comparison and is available precisely when one fails. Nor is it self-pity or self-indulgence. Juliana Breines and Serena Chen (2012) found that students encouraged to be self-compassionate after failing a hard test studied longer for a retest than those given a self-esteem boost, so self-compassion supports motivation rather than complacency. It is strongly inversely associated with depression and anxiety, though critics argue that the scale's negatively worded items partly measure distress itself, which may inflate that association.

15.12 Two cases worked through

Case.

Ananya (31) and Rohit (33), married five years after a family-introduced but mutually chosen match, come to a counselling centre in Pune saying "we fight about everything". Asked to discuss a planned visit by Rohit's parents, Ananya says, "You always side with your family; you have no spine." Rohit replies, "You make everything a drama," then looks at his phone while Ananya rolls her eyes. The therapist names Gottman's horsemen: criticism, defensiveness, stonewalling and contempt (the eye-roll). Rohit reports a racing heart, a sign of flooding, so she teaches the antidotes: a gentle start-up ("I felt left out of the decision; can we plan it together?"), taking responsibility for part of the problem, and a twenty-minute break when flooded.

When Ananya mentioned being chosen to lead a project, Rohit said "That's nice" and returned to the television, a passive-constructive response that Gable's research shows works much like a destructive one. For homework both practise active-constructive responding: asking questions, showing enthusiasm and helping the other relive good news. An attachment lens adds depth: Ananya's anxiety about coming second to Rohit's family and his avoidant retreat form a pursue-withdraw cycle. After eight sessions they escalate less and have agreed how visits will be planned.

Case.

Mr Suresh Iyer (58), a retired bank officer in Chennai, lost his wife of thirty years to a sudden heart attack. Four months later he sleeps poorly, has stopped going to the temple and says, "She never harmed anyone. We prayed every day. What was the point?" In Park's model, his global meaning (a just world in which devotion is protected) collides with the situational meaning (a sudden, undeserved death). His words also show negative religious coping, a sense of abandonment by God. On the Meaning in Life Questionnaire he scores very low on presence and high on search.

The psychologist does not push a silver lining, and screens for depression and prolonged grief disorder (a diagnosis in both DSM-5-TR and ICD-11), since meaning work does not replace treatment. Early sessions help him make sense of the loss and voice his anger at God without judgement. Gradually he accommodates his beliefs: "Perhaps prayer is not a guarantee. It was what held us together." He returns to the temple for comfort and community rather than explanation, a shift to positive religious coping. Frankl's avenues then shape a future: creatively, a scholarship in his wife's name at her school; experientially, closeness with his daughter and grandchildren; attitudinally, carrying his grief as a sign of how much the marriage meant. A year later he calls his life not happy in the old way but meaningful again, Baumeister's distinction made concrete.

Recap.

Baumeister and Leary showed that belonging is a fundamental need for frequent pleasant contact within a stable bond, and Diener and Seligman found good relationships necessary but not sufficient for very high happiness; social support protects health, and loneliness is perceived isolation. Love has been mapped as Sternberg's triangle of intimacy, passion and commitment, as Lee's six styles and as Hatfield's passionate and companionate love, while Hazan and Shaver showed that adult romance runs on the attachment system. Gottman identified criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling as predictors of breakdown and found stable couples keep about five positive behaviours for each negative one during conflict, and Gable showed that active-constructive responses to good news build intimacy. Batson argued for genuine altruism, Dunn showed that giving raises the giver's happiness, and forgiveness, distinct from reconciliation, is taught through REACH and Enright's four phases. Meaning, from Frankl's will to meaning to Steger's presence and search and Park's meaning-making after loss, is related to but distinct from happiness, and religion, especially intrinsic faith and positive coping, is modestly associated with well-being through support, meaning and health behaviour. Humility, compassion and Neff's self-compassion complete the picture of a person connected to others, to purpose and to themselves.

NameWhat they gave us

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. "Relationships are at the centre of well-being." Discuss with reference to the need to belong, social support and loneliness.
  2. Explain Sternberg's triangular theory and compare it with Lee's love styles and Hatfield's passionate and companionate love.
  3. Trace adult attachment theory from Hazan and Shaver to the two-dimensional model, showing how each style shapes relationships.
  4. What does Gottman's research tell us about why relationships last or fail, and what does capitalisation add?
  5. Define forgiveness, distinguish it from related concepts, and compare the McCullough, Worthington and Enright approaches.
  6. Discuss Frankl's contribution to the psychology of meaning and how Steger and Park extended it.
  7. Distinguish religion from spirituality, and discuss religious orientation, religious coping and the religion and well-being link.

16 Positive psychology in the clinic, and Indian perspectives on well-being

The earlier chapters ran on two tracks. Clinical psychology studied distress: how to assess, formulate and treat it. Positive psychology studied well-being: happiness, strengths, hope and meaning. This final chapter joins the tracks. It begins with the argument of Alex Wood and Nicholas Tarrier that well-being and distress lie on one continuum, then studies positive psychology interventions, the evidence for them (including the sobering recent reanalyses), and the therapies built from them: positive psychotherapy, well-being therapy, strengths-based cognitive behaviour therapy and hope therapy. It shows how strengths enter assessment and formulation, and looks briefly at schools and workplaces.

The chapter then turns to Indian ideas of well-being, treated carefully: what the texts say, what psychologists have made of them, and where the evidence is thin. It closes with the cautions every clinician must hold and a case in which positive psychotherapy runs alongside cognitive behaviour therapy for a young woman with depression.

16.1 Positive clinical psychology: well-being and distress on one continuum

When Martin Seligman launched positive psychology during his 1998 presidency of the American Psychological Association, he argued that psychology had become mainly a science of repairing damage. The new movement built its own journals and identity, and a side effect followed: positive psychology and clinical psychology came to look like two disciplines, one for the well and one for the ill. In 2010, Alex Wood and Nicholas Tarrier, both British clinical psychologists, argued in Clinical Psychology Review for a positive clinical psychology. Their claim was that the split is scientifically mistaken: clinical psychology should study well-being and distress together, with equal weight. The idea was developed in The Wiley Handbook of Positive Clinical Psychology (2016), edited by Alex Wood and Judith Johnson. Their case can be taught as four linked arguments.

  • The continuum argument. Many characteristics positive psychology studies are one pole of a dimension whose other pole clinical psychology studies: gratitude runs to ingratitude, calm to anxiety, hope to hopelessness. A gratitude questionnaire contains items about having much to be thankful for beside reverse-scored items about seeing little to be grateful for; one scale measures both ends. Studying only one half is studying half a variable.
  • The incremental prediction argument. Positive characteristics predict later disorder beyond what negative characteristics predict. Alex Wood and Stephen Joseph (2010) followed a community sample for about ten years and found that people low in psychological well-being on Carol Ryff's scales were considerably more likely to be depressed a decade later, even after allowing for earlier symptoms. Low well-being is itself a risk factor.
  • The buffering argument. Strengths are protective factors that soften the impact of adversity. Ann Masten's 2001 paper "Ordinary magic" showed that most children facing adversity develop well because of ordinary resources such as a caring adult and self-regulation. Judith Johnson, with Wood, Tarrier and colleagues, argued that certain psychological resources weaken the link between risk factors such as hopelessness and suicidal thinking. A protective factor is not merely the absence of risk; it changes what the risk does.
  • The intervention argument. If strengths predict and buffer disorder, building them is a legitimate clinical target. A therapist who reduces symptoms but leaves the client with no pleasure, purpose or close relationship has left the vulnerability in place.
Trap. Do not confuse Wood and Tarrier's single continuum with Corey Keyes's dual-continua model. Keyes showed that mental illness and positive mental health (flourishing versus languishing) are correlated but separable: a person can be free of diagnosis yet languishing. Wood and Tarrier speak of specific traits sharing one scale; Keyes speaks of two related global states. Both reach the practical point examiners want: absence of illness is not presence of well-being, so the clinician must assess both.

16.2 What counts as a positive psychology intervention

A positive psychology intervention is a deliberate activity or treatment whose primary aim is to build something positive (positive feelings, thoughts or behaviours, strengths, meaning, relationships) rather than primarily to remove something negative. Cognitive behaviour therapy for depression usually makes people happier, but its target is the disorder, so it is not a positive intervention; writing a gratitude letter targets no disorder, so it is. Acacia Parks and Robert Biswas-Diener later proposed stricter tests: the primary goal must be a positive variable, there must be evidence the activity changes it, and evidence that changing it leads to good outcomes. An unstudied pleasant activity is simply a pleasant activity.

The optimism behind these interventions drew on the 2005 "sustainable happiness" model of Sonja Lyubomirsky, Kennon Sheldon and David Schkade, which held that happiness depends on a genetic set point, life circumstances and intentional activity, the part a person can change. Its famous pie chart (50 percent set point, 10 percent circumstances, 40 percent activity) has been heavily criticised, notably by Nicholas Brown and Julia Rohrer, because population heritability figures cannot be split into one person's happiness. The safe conclusion is that intentional activity matters, not that it matters "40 percent". Positive interventions are brief, cheap, low in stigma and scalable, which makes them attractive in India, where the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016 documented a large treatment gap. That appeal is also their danger (section 16.11).

16.3 The classic exercises and the 2005 internet trial

The best-known early test is the 2005 American Psychologist study by Martin Seligman, Tracy Steen, Nansook Park and Christopher Peterson. Visitors to a website linked to Seligman's book Authentic Happiness were randomly assigned to one of five exercises or a placebo, each practised for one week, and their happiness and depressive symptoms were measured before, immediately after, and at one week, one month, three months and six months. The placebo, writing nightly about early memories, looked like an exercise but was not expected to build anything; this separated each exercise's specific effect from the general effect of expecting to feel better.

ExerciseWhat participants did for one weekWhat happened
Gratitude visitWrote a letter to someone who had been kind but never properly thanked, then delivered and read it in person.The largest immediate gains, but gone by three months.
Three good things in lifeEach night wrote three things that went well and why.Gains in happiness and fewer depressive symptoms, still present at six months.
Using signature strengths in a new wayTook the Values in Action (VIA) strengths survey, then used one top-five strength in a new way each day.Gains lasting to six months.
You at your bestWrote about a time they were at their best and reviewed the strengths shown.Short-lived change, similar to placebo.
Identifying signature strengthsTook the survey and used top strengths more often, without the "new way" instruction.Short-lived change, similar to placebo.

Two lessons followed. Exercises built on repeated daily practice with something new in it outlasted the one-off emotional event of the gratitude visit, and participants who kept practising on their own after the week showed the most lasting benefit. Positive exercises work less like a pill than like physical exercise: they help while you keep doing them. The limits matter too: participants were self-selected, educated, motivated visitors to a happiness website, and many dropped out across follow-ups.

Best possible self, kindness, gratitude and savouring

Laura King (2001) adapted James Pennebaker's expressive writing method, in which writing about trauma improved later health but was upsetting at the time. Her participants wrote for twenty minutes on four consecutive days about their best possible self: a future in which everything had gone as well as it could. Compared with controls, they gained in subjective well-being and made fewer health centre visits in the following months, without the distress of trauma writing. The exercise is now used mainly to build optimism, because it makes a hoped-for future concrete.

In acts of kindness studies reported by Lyubomirsky, Sheldon and Schkade (2005), students performed five kind acts a week for six weeks. Those who did all five on one day gained in well-being; those who spread them across the week did not, perhaps because scattered small acts blur into ordinary life. The same work found counting blessings once a week helped more than three times a week. These small early findings introduced a vital idea: dose and timing matter.

Robert Emmons and Michael McCullough (2003), in "Counting blessings versus burdens", had participants list things they were grateful for, compared with hassles or neutral events; the gratitude groups reported more positive affect and, in some conditions, better life appraisals. Savouring, set out by Fred Bryant and Joseph Veroff in Savoring: A New Model of Positive Experience (2007), means deliberately attending to, appreciating and prolonging positive experience across three time frames: anticipating, savouring the moment and reminiscing. Strategies include sharing the experience, building a memory of it, sharpening attention to sensory detail, and avoiding "kill-joy" thoughts such as "this will soon be over". Savouring suits depression, where the problem is not only fewer good events but a blunted capacity to enjoy them.

16.4 The evidence: meta-analyses, person-activity fit and who benefits

A meta-analysis pools many studies into an average effect size. Three form the core of what a student needs.

Nancy Sin and Sonja Lyubomirsky (2009) pooled 51 interventions with over four thousand participants and reported that positive interventions increased well-being (average correlation-type effect about 0.29) and reduced depressive symptoms (about 0.31), moderate effects. Effects were larger for depressed participants, for people who chose to take part, for older participants, for individual rather than group delivery, and for longer interventions. Linda Bolier and colleagues (2013) used stricter rules: only randomised controlled trials, with study quality rated. Across 39 studies and over six thousand participants, effects were smaller: roughly a third of a standard deviation for subjective well-being and about a fifth for psychological well-being and depression, still present at three to six months, with more rigorous studies tending to show smaller effects. Carmela White, Bob Uttl and Mark Holder (2019) reanalysed both, reporting errors in extracted effect sizes and strong small-study effects: the smallest studies showed the largest benefits, the signature of publication bias. After correction, the effect on well-being was small and the effect on depression smaller and uncertain. Their title said it plainly: the effects are much smaller than previously reported.

Key. Positive psychology interventions produce real but small gains in well-being and smaller, less certain reductions in depression; early enthusiasm was inflated by small, low-quality studies. They are useful additions, not replacements for established treatment.

Averages hide variation. Sonja Lyubomirsky and Kristin Layous (2013) organised it in their positive-activity model. Features of the activity matter (dose, variety, sequence, built-in social support), and so do features of the person (motivation, effort, belief that it will work, baseline mood, personality, culture). Person-activity fit is the match between them: an extravert may thrive on acts of kindness and find journalling tedious, a reflective person the reverse. People who choose activities that fit their values practise longer and gain more. Culture is part of fit. Julia Boehm, Lyubomirsky and Sheldon (2011) found Anglo American participants gained more from gratitude and optimism exercises than Asian American participants, perhaps because in interdependent cultures expressed gratitude can carry indebtedness or awkwardness. For an Indian client, reading a letter aloud to a parent may feel strange while a quiet act of service says the same thing comfortably. So who benefits most? People who are motivated and choose the activity, who practise for weeks rather than days, who have mild to moderate symptoms rather than severe illness, and for whom the activity fits their values and culture.

16.5 Positive psychotherapy (Rashid and Seligman)

Positive psychotherapy assembles positive interventions into a structured, clinician-delivered treatment, originally for depression. It was introduced by Martin Seligman, Tayyab Rashid and Acacia Parks in a 2006 American Psychologist paper and set out fully in Tayyab Rashid and Martin Seligman's Positive Psychotherapy: Clinician Manual (2018). Its premise is that depression is maintained not only by symptoms but by a lack of positive emotion, engagement, meaning and good relationships, and that building these directly can relieve symptoms and guard against relapse. It does not deny distress: clients talk about their troubles and the therapist listens empathically, but equal attention goes to what is going right. The 2006 paper reported two small pilot studies. Group treatment of mildly to moderately depressed students reduced symptoms, with gains visible at one year; individual treatment of clients with major depressive disorder did better than treatment as usual and than treatment as usual with antidepressants. The results were encouraging but small-sample, and later trials have been generally positive but mixed.

The manual runs about fifteen sessions in three phases, each session with a core concept, in-session practice and homework.

PhaseAimTypical exercises
FirstBuild a narrative of the client as a person with strengths.The positive introduction (a one-page story of the client at their best), a nightly gratitude journal, identifying signature strengths through the VIA survey and reports from family and friends, practical wisdom in using strengths.
SecondProcess negative memories and cultivate positive emotion.Open and closed memories (writing about bitter memories to process rather than ruminate), forgiveness (letting go of the wish for revenge, not excusing the wrong), maximising versus satisficing (Barry Schwartz's finding that always seeking the best option breeds regret), the gratitude letter and visit.
ThirdBuild relationships, meaning and purpose.Hope and optimism, post-traumatic growth, slowness and savouring, active constructive responding (Shelly Gable's finding that enthusiastic interest in another's good news strengthens relationships), altruism and the "gift of time", writing about meaning and legacy.
Trap. Positive psychotherapy is not "only talking about good things". Rashid insists that strengths and weaknesses, positive and negative emotions, get equal attention, and that suppressing distress is harmful.

16.6 Well-being therapy (Giovanni Fava)

Well-being therapy was developed by the Italian psychiatrist Giovanni Fava and colleagues, including Chiara Ruini, at the University of Bologna from the late 1990s. It began with an observation: many patients whose depression or anxiety had responded to treatment still carried residual symptoms and lacked psychological well-being, and these were the patients most likely to relapse. Recovery, Fava argued, requires restoring well-being, not only removing symptoms. The therapy uses Carol Ryff's (1989) six dimensions of psychological well-being as its map: environmental mastery (versus feeling overwhelmed by daily demands), personal growth (versus stagnation), purpose in life (versus directionlessness), autonomy (versus dependence on others' approval), self-acceptance (versus dissatisfaction with oneself) and positive relations with others (versus isolation).

The treatment usually runs about eight sessions in three stages. In the initial sessions, the client keeps a structured diary of episodes of well-being, however brief, and rates their intensity; formerly depressed people are often surprised to find any. In the intermediate sessions, the focus is the thoughts that cut these episodes short, borrowing directly from cognitive therapy: instead of thoughts that trigger distress, the client records thoughts that interrupt well-being ("This won't last", "I don't deserve this") and learns to challenge them. In the final sessions, the therapist identifies which Ryff dimensions are impaired and works on them, for example autonomy in a client who cannot decide anything without family approval.

Well-being therapy is mainly used sequentially: the acute illness is treated first with medication or cognitive behaviour therapy, then well-being therapy targets residual symptoms and relapse. Fava's group reported lower relapse rates in recurrent depression over several years of follow-up than with standard clinical management, and it has been studied in generalised anxiety disorder and cyclothymia. The trials were mostly small and mostly from one group, a limitation worth stating.

Why it matters. Well-being therapy is positive clinical psychology in miniature: a positive psychology model (Ryff) plus a clinical technique (cognitive restructuring) aimed at a clinical problem (relapse). It answers "Can positive psychology help people who are already ill?": yes, especially after the acute phase.

16.7 Strengths-based CBT, hope therapy and acceptance and commitment therapy

Christine Padesky and Kathleen Mooney set out strengths-based cognitive behaviour therapy in a 2012 paper subtitled "A four-step model to build resilience". Clients already show resilience somewhere, often in an area they think unimportant: a hobby, a craft, a relative they care for. The four steps follow.

  1. Search for strengths: find where the client persists despite obstacles and ask how they keep going.
  2. Construct a personal model of resilience: gather the strategies, beliefs and images they use there into their own model, in their own metaphors.
  3. Apply the model to the problem area.
  4. Practise resilience through behavioural experiments, aiming to be resilient in difficulty rather than to feel no distress.

A client overwhelmed at work may be an amateur cricketer who survives a bad over by "playing one ball at a time"; that phrase becomes the core of a model he carries into meetings. The approach fits the collaborative case conceptualisation of Willem Kuyken, Christine Padesky and Robert Dudley (2009), which builds client strengths into every cognitive behavioural formulation.

C. R. Snyder's hope theory defines hope as clear goals, pathways thinking (seeing routes to them) and agency thinking (the belief and motivation to use those routes). Hope therapy, described by Shane Lopez, Snyder and colleagues (2000), works through hope finding (discovering existing hope), hope bonding (a hopeful alliance), hope enhancing (clarifying goals, multiplying pathways, strengthening agency) and hope reminding (the client learning to catch hopeless thinking unaided). Jennifer Cheavens and colleagues (2006) reported, in a small group trial, gains in hope and self-esteem and reductions in depressive and anxious symptoms. It suits depression marked by "I see no way out" (low pathways) and "Why try?" (low agency).

Acceptance and commitment therapy, developed by Steven Hayes and colleagues, belongs to the cognitive behavioural family, not to positive psychology. It aims at psychological flexibility: accepting painful thoughts and feelings rather than struggling with them, and acting on one's values, chosen life directions ("being a caring son") as distinct from goals that can be ticked off. Clarifying values resembles identifying strengths and meaning, and committed action resembles pursuing purpose. The difference is essential: acceptance and commitment therapy does not aim to increase positive feeling, and treats the direct chase after feeling good as one of the traps that keep people stuck.

16.8 Strengths-based assessment and the fourfront

Positive clinical psychology asks that assessment gather strengths as carefully as symptoms. Strengths predict and protect (16.1); a deficit-only formulation gives the therapist nothing to build with; and a report that describes a person only as problems shapes how every later professional sees them. The key framework comes from Beatrice Wright, an American rehabilitation psychologist who worked with people with physical disabilities and noticed that reports ignored both the person's assets and the environment's part in their difficulties. With Shane Lopez she argued, in the Handbook of Positive Psychology, for widening the diagnostic focus, and Lopez and C. R. Snyder carried her approach into positive psychological assessment. It is called the fourfront because it examines four fronts at once.

Within the personIn the environment
Weaknesses1. Deficits and undermining characteristics (symptoms, skill deficits)3. Lacks and destructive factors (poverty, abuse, discrimination, no services)
Strengths2. Assets (character strengths, talents, coping skills)4. Resources and opportunities (supportive family, community, school, faith group)

The fourfront guards against two biases: locating problems inside the person when the environment is much of the story (a clinical version of the fundamental attribution error), and noticing deficits while overlooking assets. In practice, the clinician fills all four cells when writing a formulation. Measures can fill the strengths cells, among them the Values in Action Inventory of Strengths (VIA), the Hope Scale, Ed Diener's Satisfaction With Life Scale and Ryff's scales, and interviews can ask "What has helped you through hard times before?" or "When is the problem less bad?" In the report, strengths should be as specific as symptoms: not "supportive family" but "her elder sister visits weekly and is the person she would call in a crisis". A strengths section is not politeness; it names the resources the treatment plan will rely on.

Case. A 15-year-old boy in a Pune government school is referred for "aggression and poor studies". A deficit-only report lists fights and failing marks. The fourfront adds that he is the football team's most reliable player and protective of younger children (person, strengths), that his father has lost his job and home is full of quarrels (environment, weaknesses), and that his coach and an uncle take an interest in him (environment, resources). The plan changes: the coach becomes an ally, his protectiveness is channelled, and the family stress is addressed rather than "aggression" alone.

16.9 Positive education and positive institutions

Positive education teaches the skills of well-being in schools alongside academic skills. Martin Seligman and colleagues set out its case in 2009. The Penn Resiliency Program, developed by Jane Gillham, Karen Reivich and colleagues, teaches children to spot pessimistic explanations and generate alternatives, with modest effects on depressive symptoms that vary across trials; many school programmes use Seligman's PERMA model (positive emotion, engagement, relationships, meaning, accomplishment). In India, the Delhi government introduced a Happiness Curriculum in its schools in 2018; it attracted wide interest, but rigorous independent evaluation remains limited. Positive institutions apply the same thinking to workplaces, for example through Fred Luthans's psychological capital (hope, self-efficacy, resilience and optimism as developable resources at work). The caution mirrors the clinic: when positive programmes ask employees to feel better about unfair workloads instead of changing them, they blame individuals for institutional problems.

16.10 Indian perspectives on well-being

Indian traditions contain some of the oldest systematic reflection on well-being, suffering and the mind. Indian psychologists such as Anand Paranjpe, K. Ramakrishna Rao and Ajit Dalal (editors of the Handbook of Indian Psychology, 2008), and Kiran Kumar Salagame of the University of Mysore, who has written on well-being from the Hindu perspective, argue that these traditions contain psychological theory that can be studied. Two cautions apply throughout: psychology's attempts to measure these concepts are young, so claims must be modest; and "Indian thought" is not one system but many schools (Hindu, Buddhist, Jain and others) that disagree with one another.

The three gunas

The gunas come from the Samkhya school and are discussed at length in the Bhagavad Gita, especially its fourteenth chapter. All of nature (prakriti), including the mind, is made of three qualities in varying balance. Sattva is clarity, calm, goodness and knowledge; rajas is activity, passion, desire and restlessness; tamas is inertia, dullness, confusion and heaviness. Everyone has all three, and the balance shifts with time and circumstance. The Gita describes sattva as binding through attachment to happiness and knowledge, rajas through attachment to action, and tamas through heedlessness, and it holds up the person who goes beyond all three. The psychological reading treats the gunas as something like temperament dimensions: the sattvic person calm and content, the rajasic driven and restless, the tamasic lethargic and withdrawn. Self-report inventories exist, such as the Vedic Personality Inventory developed by David Wolf in the late 1990s, and studies have generally linked sattva with better well-being and tamas with more distress. The measures are still being refined, and the gunas are not a diagnostic system.

Trap. The three gunas (sattva, rajas, tamas: qualities of mind and nature) are not the three doshas of Ayurveda (vata, pitta, kapha: bodily principles).

Nishkama karma and anasakti

In the Bhagavad Gita (chapter 2, verse 47), Arjuna is told that he has a right to his action but never to its fruits, and should neither be driven by the fruits nor cling to inaction. This is nishkama karma, action without attachment to results. It does not mean not caring or not working hard; it means doing one's work wholeheartedly while holding the outcome lightly, with equanimity in success and failure, like the Gita's sthitaprajna, the person of steady wisdom. Namita Pande and Ramesh Naidu made this a psychological construct in their 1992 study "Anasakti and health". Anasakti, non-attachment, involves focusing on effort rather than outcome, relative equanimity in success and failure, and not being personally shaken by results. They distinguished it from apathy or withdrawal: the non-attached person works fully but does not stake self-worth on the result. People higher in anasakti reported less strain under stress, suggesting that non-attachment buffers stress. The clinical link is direct, since much anxiety and depression involves fierce attachment to outcomes ("If I fail, I am worthless"). Western psychology reaches related ideas through process goals, mindful decentring and acceptance and commitment therapy's values; these are parallels, not identities, but they let an Indian client see a therapeutic idea as part of their own tradition.

Sukha and ananda

Sukha is happiness or pleasure in the ordinary sense, always paired with its opposite, dukha (suffering); it depends on circumstances and the senses, and comes and goes. Ananda is bliss, described in the Upanishads as the nature of the Self, and in the Taittiriya Upanishad as the innermost of five sheaths (koshas) of the person; it does not depend on circumstances. Salagame has used this distinction to compare Indian ideas with the Western contrast between hedonic well-being (pleasure and satisfaction) and eudaimonic well-being (meaning and growth), arguing that ananda points further, to a well-being grounded in self-realisation. The Sanskrit word for health, swastha, is often explained as "established in oneself".

Yoga, meditation and the family-centred self

Patanjali's Yoga Sutras define yoga as the stilling of the mind's fluctuations and set out an eightfold path from ethical restraints and observances through posture, breath regulation and withdrawal of the senses to concentration, meditation and absorption. Yoga and meditation are widely used as well-being practices, and trials, including research at the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru, have studied yoga as an add-on treatment for depression with encouraging but methodologically mixed results. Mindfulness-based cognitive therapy, drawn from Buddhist meditation, has good evidence for preventing relapse in recurrent depression. Yoga and meditation are valuable additions, not stand-alone treatments for moderate or severe illness.

Much Western research assumes an independent self whose happiness is personal. Hazel Markus and Shinobu Kitayama (1991) described the contrasting interdependent self, defined through relationships and roles, and Alan Roland, a psychoanalyst who worked with Indian patients, described an Indian "familial self". For many Indian clients, well-being means family harmony, fulfilling one's dharma (duty) to parents and children, and the family's standing. The classical four aims of life (dharma; artha, livelihood; kama, pleasure; moksha, liberation) likewise frame the good life as balance rather than maximised personal happiness. Positive interventions for Indian clients may therefore work better when they include the family, value service and duty, and use the client's own concepts.

16.11 Critiques and cautions in clinical use

Toxic positivity is the insistence that people should always stay positive, so that sadness, anger and grief become failures. Barbara Held (2002) called it "the tyranny of the positive attitude". Telling a grieving client to "focus on the positives" invalidates her, damages the alliance and teaches her that real feelings are unwelcome. Research supports the worry: Joanne Wood and colleagues (2009) found people with low self-esteem felt worse after repeating "I am a lovable person", probably because it clashed with their beliefs, and Iris Mauss and colleagues (2011) found that valuing happiness very highly can make people less happy, because they keep judging themselves as falling short. In bipolar disorder, heightened positive emotion is itself part of the risk.

Not a substitute for treatment. A gratitude journal does not treat severe depression, psychosis, bipolar disorder, eating disorders or suicidal crisis. These need thorough assessment including risk, evidence-based psychotherapy and psychiatric referral where indicated. In India, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 gives every person a right to access mental healthcare, and clinical psychologists must be registered with the Rehabilitation Council of India; the clinician's first duty is to identify the disorder and ensure proper treatment, and only then to add positive work. The evidence in 16.4 places positive interventions in mild to moderate problems, as adjuncts, and in relapse prevention.

Cultural fit and honesty. Exercises developed with educated Western participants, such as boasting of strengths or writing effusive letters, may clash with norms of modesty and interdependence; the remedy is adaptation with the client, not abandonment. And clinicians must not oversell: promising that "three good things will cure your depression" is untrue and, when it fails, adds a sense of personal failure.

Key. Use positive interventions with distress, not instead of it: after proper assessment, alongside evidence-based treatment, fitted to the person and culture, and without promising more than the evidence supports.

16.12 Case: positive psychotherapy alongside cognitive behaviour therapy

Case.

Priya, 26, a primary school teacher in Mysuru living with her parents, is referred by a general physician with four months of low mood, tiredness and loss of interest since her engagement was broken off by the other family. She sleeps poorly, has stopped singing, feels she has "let her family down", and admits passive thoughts that "it would not matter if I were gone", without intent.

Sessions 1 and 2. The psychologist takes a full history, explores the passive thoughts, agrees a safety plan and, with consent, involves her mother. The Patient Health Questionnaire-9 and the Beck Depression Inventory, Second Edition place her in the mild to moderate range; with no mania, psychosis or suicidal plan, psychotherapy comes first, with psychiatric review if she does not improve. The formulation uses Aaron Beck's cognitive model (core belief "I am not good enough to be chosen", withdrawal, rumination) plus a fourfront grid: kindness, love of learning and perseverance; loving but anxious parents and a close cousin; gossiping relatives. Her positive introduction describes staying late for weeks to teach a struggling pupil to read.

Sessions 3 to 5. Behavioural activation begins, with activities chosen through her signature strengths: singing practice twice a week and a weekend children's reading programme. She writes three good things nightly; when it starts to feel forced, the psychologist varies it with savouring one moment in detail. Thought records tackle "Nobody will ever want to marry me" and "I have shamed my family".

Sessions 6 to 8. Through open and closed memories she writes about the broken engagement until rumination gives way to a fuller account. She resists forgiveness, and the psychologist does not push: forgiveness is explained as letting go of revenge for her own sake, and she chooses to let the anger be for now. Reading a gratitude letter aloud to her mother feels "very filmy", so she writes it privately, then cooks her mother's favourite meal and tells her simply that she knows how much she has done: person-activity fit and cultural fit in practice.

Sessions 9 to 12. She practises active constructive responding with her cousin and parents, and finds meaning in wanting to be remembered as a teacher who made children feel capable. For relapse prevention the psychologist borrows from well-being therapy: a diary of well-being moments and the thoughts that interrupt them ("I shouldn't enjoy myself when my life is a mess"). By session 12 her scores are minimal and she is singing again. The psychologist notes honestly that the cognitive and behavioural work was probably as important as the positive exercises, and schedules follow-ups at one and three months with a written plan of warning signs and helpful activities.

Recap.

Alex Wood and Nicholas Tarrier argued in 2010 that well-being and distress lie on shared continua, that low well-being predicts later disorder and that strengths buffer adversity, so clinical psychology must study and treat both; Keyes's dual-continua model reaches the same practical point. Positive psychology interventions aim primarily to build something positive; in the 2005 trial, three good things and using signature strengths in a new way gave gains lasting six months, while the gratitude visit gave a large, brief boost. Meta-analyses by Sin and Lyubomirsky and by Bolier and colleagues found moderate, then small, benefits, and White, Uttl and Holder showed that small-study bias had inflated them; benefits depend on person-activity fit. Positive psychotherapy, Fava's well-being therapy, strengths-based cognitive behaviour therapy, hope therapy and the values work of acceptance and commitment therapy turn these ideas into treatment. Beatrice Wright's fourfront puts strengths and weaknesses of person and environment into assessment. Indian thought offers the gunas, nishkama karma and anasakti, sukha and ananda, yoga and a family-centred good life, all deserving careful, modest study. Positive work must never become toxic positivity or a substitute for proper treatment.

Questions this chapter lets you answer:

  1. What is positive clinical psychology? Explain Wood and Tarrier's arguments and compare them with Keyes's dual-continua model.
  2. Describe the 2005 trial by Seligman, Steen, Park and Peterson: which exercises worked, for how long, and with what limitations?
  3. Critically evaluate the evidence for positive psychology interventions using the three major meta-analyses. What is person-activity fit, and who benefits most?
  4. Compare positive psychotherapy and well-being therapy in theory, structure, target population and evidence.
  5. Explain strengths-based assessment and Beatrice Wright's fourfront. How would you add strengths to a formulation and report?
  6. Discuss Indian perspectives on well-being (the gunas, nishkama karma and anasakti, sukha and ananda, the family-centred self). What can Indian clinical psychology learn from them, and what cautions apply?
  7. "Positive psychology in the clinic risks becoming toxic positivity." Discuss with reference to evidence, ethics and culture.

· The four references, cited correctly

The citations in the forward you got had a few slips (a wrong year, a missing co-editor, one author's name flipped). These are the correct ones, the way to write them in an assignment reference list.

  2. Trull, T. J., & Phares, E. J. (2001). Clinical psychology: Concepts, methods, and profession (6th ed.). Wadsworth/Thomson Learning.
  3. Lopez, S. J., & Snyder, C. R. (Eds.). (2009). The Oxford handbook of positive psychology (2nd ed.). Oxford University Press. The 2002 first edition is Snyder and Lopez, Handbook of positive psychology.
  4. Baumgardner, S. R., & Crothers, M. K. Positive psychology. Pearson Education India. "Steve, B." in the forward is Steve Baumgardner.
